.

Notice that, in the case of a closed string, the embedding maps have the periodicity condition
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma+2\pi)=X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
.

3.1.1
Nambu-Goto and Polyakov actions

The dynamics of a string can be described by the
Nambu-Goto action
:

S_{\text{NG}}[X]=-T\int_{\text{WS}}dA\,,

(3.2)

where
T
is the string tension and
dA
is the volume form on the worldsheet. The string’s tension
T
is canonically written as

T=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\,,

(3.3)

where
[\alpha^{\prime}]=L^{2}
gives the string scale.

Let us now write
\{\tau,\sigma\}=\sigma^{\alpha}
(with
\alpha=0,1
) and let us denote with
G_{\alpha\beta}
the induced metric on the worldsheet (where
\alpha,\beta
are worldsheet indices):

\displaystyle G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial X^{\mu}}{\partial\sigma^{\alpha}}\frac{\partial X^{\nu}}{\partial\sigma^{\beta}}=\begin{pmatrix}G_{\tau\tau}&G_{\tau\sigma}\\
G_{\sigma\tau}&G_{\sigma\sigma}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(3.4)

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\text{det}\left(G_{\alpha\beta}\right)=G_{\tau\tau}G_{\sigma\sigma}-\left(G_{\tau\sigma}\right)^{2}\,.

(3.5)

This allows to explicitly write the volume form as

dA=\sqrt{-G}\,d^{2}\sigma.

(3.6)

The Nambu-Goto action can then be rewritten as

S_{\text{NG}}[X]=-T\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-G}=-T\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-\dot{X}^{2}\left(X^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}\right)^{2}}\,,

(3.7)

where we used the convention

\cdot\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}\,,\qquad\quad^{\prime}\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma}\,.

(3.8)

As prescribed by the principle of least action, a classical solution of the EOM is a minimal area surface, the higher dimensional generalization of a geodesic. Moreover,
S_{\text{NG}}
is invariant under reparametrization of
\sigma
. Just as with the particle, this action is not the best starting point for quantization.

To ease the process of quantization, we can write a classically equivalent action which explicitly contains a
dynamical
metric tensor in 2 dimensions
h_{\alpha\beta}(\tau,\sigma)
. This gives the
Polyakov action
1
1

1

This action was originally written down independently by Deser-Zumino and by Brink-Di Vecchia, but it was used as a path integral by Polyakov.

S_{\text{P}}[X,h]=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.9)

where
h=\text{det}\left(h_{\alpha\beta}\right)
.

3.1.2
Equations of motion

Let us first variate
S_{\text{P}}
with respect to
h
:

\displaystyle\delta_{h}S_{\text{P}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\left[\delta_{h}\left(\sqrt{-h}\right)h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}+\sqrt{-h}\,\delta_{h}\left(h^{\alpha\beta}\right)\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-h}\,\delta_{h}h^{\alpha\beta}\left[-\frac{1}{2}h_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\gamma}X^{\mu}\partial^{\gamma}X_{\mu}+\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-h}\,\delta_{h}h^{\alpha\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(3.10)

where we used

\displaystyle h^{\alpha\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}=2\,,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\delta_{h}\left(h^{\alpha\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}\right)=0=\delta_{h}\left(h^{\alpha\beta}\right)h_{\alpha\beta}+h^{\alpha\beta}\delta_{h}\left(h_{\alpha\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\Longrightarrow\ \,\delta_{h}\left(h^{\alpha\beta}\right)h_{\alpha\beta}=-h^{\alpha\beta}\delta_{h}\left(h_{\alpha\beta}\right)\,,

(3.12)

\displaystyle\delta_{h}h=\delta_{h}(\text{det}\left(h_{\alpha\beta}\right))=\delta_{h}\left(e^{\text{Tr}\left\{\log(h_{\alpha\beta})\right\}}\right)=\text{det}\left(h_{\alpha\beta}\right)\text{Tr}\left\{\delta_{h}(\log(h_{\alpha\beta}))\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\!\!\!=\text{det}\left(h_{\alpha\beta}\right)\text{Tr}\left\{\frac{\delta_{h}(h_{\alpha\beta})}{\text{det}\left(h_{\alpha\beta}\right)}\right\}=hh^{\alpha\beta}\delta_{h}(h_{\alpha\beta})

\displaystyle\qquad\!\!\!=-hh_{\alpha\beta}\delta_{h}(h^{\alpha\beta})\,,

(3.13)

and where we defined the
stress-energy tensor

T_{\alpha\beta}
as

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}T_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}h_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\gamma}X^{\mu}\partial^{\gamma}X_{\mu}-\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}h_{\alpha\beta}h^{\gamma\delta}G_{\gamma\delta}-G_{\alpha\beta}\,.

(3.14)

Therefore, if we ask for
\delta_{h}S_{P}=0
, we find the
h
-EOM

T_{\alpha\beta}=0\,.

(3.15)

This equation implies that

G_{\alpha\beta}=\left(\frac{1}{2}h^{\gamma\delta}G_{\gamma\delta}\right)h_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(3.16)

meaning that the induced metric
G_{\alpha\beta}
is proportional to the free metric
h_{\alpha\beta}
through the scale parameter
\frac{1}{2}h^{\gamma\delta}G_{\gamma\delta}
.
Using the
h
-EOM (
3.15
) we then get

\displaystyle h_{\alpha\beta}=\left(\frac{1}{2}h^{\gamma\delta}G_{\gamma\delta}\right)^{-1}\,G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\ \,h=\left(\frac{1}{2}h^{\gamma\delta}G_{\gamma\delta}\right)^{-2}\text{det}\left(G_{\alpha\beta}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{2}h^{\gamma\delta}G_{\gamma\delta}\right)^{-2}G

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\ \,\sqrt{-h}=\left(\frac{1}{2}h^{\alpha\beta}G_{\alpha\beta}\right)^{-1}\sqrt{-G}\,.

(3.17)

Therefore, thanks to
h
-EOM (
3.15
), we can see that Polyakov and Nambu-Goto actions are the same:

\displaystyle S_{\text{P}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-G}\left(\frac{1}{2}h^{\gamma\delta}G_{\gamma\delta}\right)^{-1}h^{\alpha\beta}G_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-G}

\displaystyle=S_{\text{NG}}\,.

(3.18)

Moreover, recalling the definition (
3.14
), we can see that

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}{T^{\alpha}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-(\partial X\cdot\partial X)+\frac{1}{2}h^{\alpha}_{\alpha}(\partial X\cdot\partial X)

\displaystyle=-(\partial X\cdot\partial X)+\frac{1}{2}2(\partial X\cdot\partial X)=0\,.

(3.19)

Therefore, thanks to the fact that we are in two dimensions, we find that the stress-energy tensor is traceless:

{T^{\alpha}}_{\alpha}=0\,.

(3.20)

Let us now vary
S_{\text{P}}
with respect to
X
:

\displaystyle\delta_{X}S_{\text{P}}

\displaystyle\,=-\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\delta_{X}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{IBP}}{=}\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\left[\delta_{X}X^{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\right)-\partial_{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\delta_{X}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\right)\right]\,,

(3.21)

where in the last step we performed an integration by parts (IBP).

If we then ask for
\delta_{X}S_{\text{P}}=0
, the resulting
X
-EOMs are

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\right)=0\\[5.0pt]
\left[\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\delta_{X}X_{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\right]_{\partial(\text{WS})}=0\end{cases}\,,

(3.22)

where
\partial(\text{WS})
is the worldsheet boundary, which is only present in the case of open strings.

We shall now briefly focus on open strings.
Since

\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma=\int_{0}^{\pi}d\sigma\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\tau\,,

(3.23)

we can write the boundary term (
3.22
) as

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\tau\,\int_{0}^{\pi}d\sigma\,\partial_{\sigma}\left[\delta X^{\mu}\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\sigma\beta}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\right]=

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}d\tau\,\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\sigma\beta}\Big[\delta X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\Big]_{\sigma=0}^{\sigma=\pi}=0\,,

(3.24)

where
h^{\sigma\beta}
is a vector, being
\sigma
a fixed index.

Figure 3.3:
The normal and the tangent vector with respect to the boundary
\partial(\text{WS})
of the open string worldsheet.

Let us call
n
the vector normal to
\partial(\text{WS})
and
t
the vector tangent to
\partial(\text{WS})
(see fig.
3.3
)

\displaystyle n^{\beta}

\displaystyle=h^{\sigma\beta}\,,

(3.25)

\displaystyle t_{\beta}

\displaystyle=h_{\tau\beta}\,,

(3.26)

\displaystyle t_{\beta}n^{\beta}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\tau}^{\sigma}=0.

(3.27)

Then we end up with the following
X
-EOMs

\begin{cases}\partial_{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\right)&=0\qquad\text{(WS bulk eq.{})}\\[5.0pt]
\delta X^{\mu}\,\left(n^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X_{\mu}\right)\Big|_{\partial(\text{WS})}&=0\qquad\text{(WS boundary eq.{})}\end{cases}\,,

(3.28)

where the worldsheet boundary equation is for open strings only.

3.1.3
Internal symmetries

The Polyakov action
S_{\text{P}}[h,X]
is invariant under the following local transformations:

•

diffeomorphisms, namely reparametrizations of the type
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}(\sigma)
acting as follows:

\begin{cases}X^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime})=X(\sigma)\\[5.0pt]
\frac{\partial\sigma^{\prime\gamma}}{\partial\sigma^{\alpha}}\frac{\partial\sigma^{\prime\delta}}{\partial\sigma^{\beta}}h^{\prime}_{\gamma\delta}(\sigma^{\prime})=h_{\alpha\beta}(\sigma)\end{cases}\,;

(3.29)

•

Weyl symmetries, namely metric rescalings that do not affect the
X
field:

h^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}(\sigma)=e^{2\omega(\sigma)}h_{\alpha\beta}(\sigma)\,.

(3.30)

The diff-symmetry is obvious from general covariance. Let us instead check the Weyl symmetry. We have that

\displaystyle(h^{\alpha\beta})^{\prime}=e^{-2\omega(\sigma)}h^{\alpha\beta}(\sigma)\,,

(3.31)

\displaystyle h^{\prime}=\text{det}\left(h^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}\right)=e^{4\omega(\sigma)}h\quad\Longrightarrow\ \,\sqrt{-h^{\prime}}=e^{2\omega(\sigma)}\sqrt{-h}\,,

(3.32)

and hence

\sqrt{-h^{\prime}}h^{{}^{\prime}\alpha\beta}=\sqrt{-h}h^{\alpha\beta}\,.

(3.33)

Therefore we found that, being
\sqrt{-h}h^{\alpha\beta}
Weyl invariant, such a transformation is a symmetry for
S_{\text{P}}
, as claimed before.

Moreover, if we write

\displaystyle h^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=e^{2\omega}h_{\alpha\beta}=(1+2\omega+\mathcal{O}(\omega^{2}))h_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(3.34)

we can express the infinitesimal Weyl transformation as

\delta_{\omega}h_{\alpha\beta}=2\omega h_{\alpha\beta}

(3.35)

and then we can write

\displaystyle\delta_{\omega}S_{\text{P}}\propto\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{-h}\delta_{\omega}h^{\alpha\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}=0

\displaystyle\qquad\Longrightarrow\ \,h^{\alpha\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}=T_{\alpha}^{\alpha}=0\,.

(3.36)

The energy-momentum tensor is traceless as a consequence of Weyl invariance.

Besides the above gauge symmetries there is also a global Poincaré symmetry in
D
dimensions

\begin{cases}X^{{}^{\prime}\mu}={\Lambda^{\mu}}_{\,\nu}X^{\nu}+a^{\nu}\\
h^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=h_{\alpha\beta}\end{cases}\,.

(3.37)

3.2
Old Covariant Quantization

We now want to quantize the free relativistic string. To do this we have to gauge fix the Weyl+Diff gauge redundancy we have just described.
This can be done by introducing the convenient
conformal gauge
fixing, corresponding to the choice of a fiducial metric

\displaystyle\hat{h}_{\alpha\beta}=e^{-\phi}\eta_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(3.38)

\displaystyle\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}=e^{\phi}\eta^{\alpha\beta}\,,

(3.39)

where
\phi=\phi(\sigma)
is a worldsheet dependent scale factor.
In this gauge we have that

\sqrt{-\hat{h}}=e^{-\phi}\sqrt{-\eta}=e^{-\phi}

(3.40)

and therefore

\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}\sqrt{-\hat{h}}=\eta^{\alpha\beta}\,.

(3.41)

Notice that
\phi
decouples
2
2

2

At the quantum level it can be shown that this holds only for
D=26
.
from the object
\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}\sqrt{-\hat{h}}
. Therefore the gauge fixed action is

S[\hat{h},\hat{X}]=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,\eta^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\,.

(3.42)

It is now convenient to change to the
lightcone coordinates

\sigma^{\pm}=\tau\pm\sigma\,.

(3.43)

They allow us to write the integration measure as

d^{2}\sigma=\frac{1}{2}d\sigma^{+}d\sigma^{-}\,,

(3.44)

and

\tau=\frac{\sigma^{+}+\sigma^{-}}{2}\,,\qquad\sigma=\frac{\sigma^{+}-\sigma^{-}}{2}\,.

(3.45)

We can thus write

\displaystyle\eta_{++}

\displaystyle=\eta_{--}=0\,,\qquad\quad\eta^{++}=\eta^{--}=0\,,

(3.46a)

\displaystyle\eta_{+-}

\displaystyle=\eta_{-+}=-\frac{1}{2}\,,\qquad\eta^{+-}=\eta^{-+}=-2\,,

(3.46b)

namely

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{2}\\
-\frac{1}{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\,,\qquad\eta^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-2\\
-2&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.47)

Using these coordinates, the action reads

S=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d\sigma^{+}d\sigma^{-}\,\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X_{\mu}\,.

(3.48)

We may now pause for a moment and observe that, even after the gauge fixing, this action exibits a residual gauge freedom under
conformal diffeomorphisms
, defined as

\displaystyle\sigma^{{}^{\prime}+}

\displaystyle=f(\sigma^{+})=f^{+}\,,

(3.49a)

\displaystyle\sigma^{{}^{\prime}-}

\displaystyle=g(\sigma^{-})=g^{-}\,.

(3.49b)

Under such a transformation, the metric tensor is changed by a scale factor:

-d\sigma^{{}^{\prime}-}d\sigma^{{}^{\prime}+}=-\left(\partial_{+}f_{+}\partial_{-}g_{-}\right)df^{+}dg^{-}\,.

(3.50)

Therefore the metric can be recast to its original form using a Weyl transformation. This residual gauge symmetry os called conformal invariance and it will be central in the following developments.

The
X
-EOMs in the conformal gauge read

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{+}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})&=0\qquad\text{(WS bulk eq.{})}\\[5.0pt]
\delta X_{\mu}\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}\Big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}&=0\qquad\text{(WS boundary eq.{})}\end{cases}\,.

(3.51)

At the same time it is important to consider the energy momentum tensor (which is set to zero by the missing equation of motion for the metric, which is no more obtainable by varying the gauge-fixed action).

Using the convention

T_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{-h}}\frac{\delta S}{\delta h^{\alpha\beta}}=\frac{1}{2\alpha^{\prime}}h_{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\gamma}X^{\mu}\partial^{\gamma}X_{\mu}-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\,,

(3.52)

we have

\displaystyle T_{++}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{+}X_{\mu}\right)\,,

(3.53a)

\displaystyle T_{--}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\partial_{-}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X_{\mu}\right)\,,

(3.53b)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}T_{+-}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}T_{-+}=-\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}2\left(\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}\right)=0\,

(3.53c)

Notice that that (upon use of the
X
-eoms) the energy momentum tensor is conserved:

\partial^{\alpha}T_{\alpha\beta}=0\ \,\Longleftrightarrow\ \,\begin{cases}\partial_{+}T_{--}=0\\
\partial_{-}T_{++}=0\end{cases}\,.

(3.54)

This implies that

\begin{cases}T_{++}=T_{++}(\sigma^{+})\\
T_{--}=T_{--}(\sigma^{-})\end{cases}\,.

(3.55)

We are now ready to quantize the string in the “old” manner, namely through
old covariant quantization
(OCQ).

3.2.1
OCQ for the closed string

3.2.1.1
Classical solution

Let us focus first on the closed string case. The EOM will be the bulk equation

\partial_{+}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})=0\,.

(3.56)

This is a wave equation in two dimensions, whose generic solution is

X^{\mu}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})=X^{\mu}_{L}(\sigma^{+})+X^{\mu}_{R}(\sigma^{-})\,,

(3.57)

where
X^{\mu}_{L}
is the left-moving wave, while
X^{\mu}_{R}
is the right-moving one.

Recalling that the solution has to be periodic in
\sigma\rightarrow\sigma+2\pi
(namely
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma+2\pi)=X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
) we can write it as

\displaystyle X^{\mu}_{L}(\sigma^{+})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{0}^{\mu}+c^{\mu}\right)+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}P^{\mu}\sigma^{+}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\alpha_{n}^{\mu}}{n}e^{-in\sigma^{+}}\,,

(3.58a)

\displaystyle X^{\mu}_{R}(\sigma^{-})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(X_{0}^{\mu}-c^{\mu}\right)+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}P^{\mu}\sigma^{-}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}}{n}e^{-in\sigma^{-}}\,,

(3.58b)

where
c^{\mu}
is a zero-mode freedom which disappears in the sum
X_{L}+X_{R}
to which we are ultimately interested.

Hence the generic solution reads as follows:

X^{\mu}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})=X_{0}^{\mu}+\alpha^{\prime}P^{\mu}\tau+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left(\alpha_{n}^{\mu}e^{-in\sigma^{+}}+\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}e^{-in\sigma^{-}}\right)\,.

(3.59)

This is the classical closed string solution. It resembles what we already found for the free particle
3
3

3

Here, for the string, we take the worldsheet time
\tau
to be a-dimensional, while for the particle we were implicitly assuming that
\tau
had dimensions of (space-time) time
2
.
(
2.21a
), being
\alpha^{\prime}P^{\mu}\tau
the term containing the information on the center of mass (as we will see shortly,
P^{\mu}
is indeed the momentum of the center of mass). Moreover, the string solution has the additional term
i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left(\alpha_{n}^{\mu}e^{-in\sigma^{+}}+\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}e^{-in\sigma^{-}}\right)
, which describes the internal oscillatory dynamics of the string. The
i
is needed in order to implement the
X^{\mu}
reality condition:

X^{\mu}=\left(X^{\mu}\right)^{*}\,\Longleftrightarrow\overset{(\sim)}{\alpha}_{-n}=(\overset{(\sim)}{\alpha}_{n})^{*}\,\,.

(3.60)

Let us now evaluate the momentum density

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{X}_{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\dot{X}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{P^{\mu}}{2\pi}+\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\left(\alpha_{n}^{\mu}e^{-in(\tau+\sigma)}+\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}e^{-in(\tau-\sigma)}\right)\,,

(3.61)

where we used the
X
-EOM. If we integrate this density over the string extension

\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\sigma\,\mathcal{P}^{\mu}=P^{\mu}\,,

(3.62)

we see that indeed
P^{\mu}
is the momentum of the center of mass.

3.2.1.2
Quantization

In canonical quantization we now have to impose the equal time commutation relation

\left[X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma),\mathcal{P}^{\nu}(\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\right]=i\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta(\sigma-\sigma^{\prime})\,

(3.63)

on the solutions (
3.59
), (
3.2.1.1
).
At the level of oscillators this means

\displaystyle\left[\alpha_{n}^{\mu},\alpha_{m}^{\nu}\right]=\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{m}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=n\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,

(3.64a)

\displaystyle\left[\alpha_{n}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{m}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.64b)

\displaystyle\left[X_{0}^{\mu},P^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=i\eta^{\mu\nu}\,.

(3.64c)

Exercise 3.2.1

Prove this statement, using the formula
\delta(\sigma)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{e^{in\sigma}}{2\pi}
.

If we define

\displaystyle a_{n}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\alpha_{n}^{\mu}\qquad\,\text{ for $n>0$}\,,

(3.65)

\displaystyle a_{n}^{\mu\,\dagger}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\alpha_{-n}^{\mu}\qquad\text{for $n>0$}\,,

(3.66)

we then have

\left[a_{n}^{\mu},a_{m}^{\nu\,\dagger}\right]=\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{m,\,n}\,.

(3.67)

These are therefore infinitely many harmonic oscillators. The vacuum is defined as

\alpha_{n}\ket{0}=0\,,\qquad\bra{0}\alpha_{-n}=0\,,\qquad\forall n>0

(3.68)

with the reality condition

\left(\alpha_{n}^{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}=\alpha_{-n}^{\mu}\,.

(3.69)

Taking into account also the center-of-mass Heisenberg algebra
\left[X_{0}^{\mu},P^{\nu}\right]=i\eta^{\mu\nu}
, the Hilbert space will thus be generated by the vacuum at definite momentum
\ket{0,P}
, on which oscillators act. The generic basis element will thus be

\alpha_{-n_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}\dots\alpha_{-n_{k}}^{\mu_{k}}\tilde{\alpha}_{-m_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}\dots\tilde{\alpha}_{-m_{k}}^{\nu_{k}}\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.70)

Thanks to the oscillators, such a state can be in any (possibly reducible) Lorentz representation (
\{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}\}\{\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{k}\}
) of integer spin. The momentum is also not constrained in any way. This is the most general off-shell state we can write down. Next, to find the physical states, we have to impose the constraints which are the missing equation for the metric, resulting in the vanishing of the energy-momentum tensor.

3.2.1.3
Physical constraints and Virasoro algebra

Now we have to implement the missing equation of motion for
h
as constraints:

\displaystyle T_{++}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{+}X_{\mu}=T_{++}(\sigma^{+})\,,

(3.71)

\displaystyle T_{--}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X_{\mu}=T_{--}(\sigma^{-})\,.

(3.72)

Expanding them in harmonics

\begin{cases}T_{++}(\sigma^{+})=-\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}L_{n}e^{-in\sigma^{+}}\\[5.0pt]
T_{--}(\sigma^{-})=-\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{L}_{n}e^{-in\sigma^{-}}\end{cases}\,,

(3.73)

we obtain the classical expressions of the so-called
Virasoro operators

\displaystyle L_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n-k}^{\mu}\alpha_{k}^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}\,,\qquad\widetilde{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\alpha}_{n-k}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{k}^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.74)

where the zero-mode is defined as

\alpha_{0}^{\mu}=\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{\mu}=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}P^{\mu}\quad\textrm{(closed string)}\,.

(3.75)

Exercise 3.2.2

Obtain explicitly the Virasoro operators.

We have now to interepret the above classical expressions as operators. In doing so we have to pay attention that the oscillators
\alpha^{\mu}_{n}
are not always commuting and therefore the previous classical expressions may be ambiguous. In fact closer inspection reveals that
only

L_{0}
suffers from a possible ambiguity:

\displaystyle L_{n\neq 0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\,,\quad\textrm{(non ambiguos)}

(3.76)

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\,,\quad\,\,\,\textrm{(ambiguous!)}\

(3.77)

because

\displaystyle\left[\alpha_{n-k}^{\mu},\alpha_{k}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.78a)

\displaystyle\left[\alpha_{-k}^{\mu},\alpha_{k}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle\neq 0\,.

(3.78b)

A natural possibility to consider is the oscillator normal ordering
:\mathrel{\,\cdots\,}:
, where creation operators are placed to the left and annihilation operators to the right:

:\mathrel{L_{0}}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}:\mathrel{\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}}:=\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{0}^{2}+\sum_{k\geq 0}\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\equiv\hat{L}_{0}\,.

(3.79)

Since any different ordering convention will result in a constant shift in
L_{0}
, for the time being we leave this constant free and fix it a later stage from consistency. This brings to the physical state constraint

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\left(\hat{L}_{0}-a\right)\ket{\text{phys}}=0\\[5.0pt]
\left(\hat{\widetilde{L}}_{0}-\tilde{a}\right)\ket{\text{phys}}=0\end{cases}\,.

(3.80)

From now on, if not needed, we will not explicitly write the
\hat{\cdot}
on quantum operators, as well as the normal ordering.
Moreover, in the following we will always focus on
L_{n}
, since the story for the
\widetilde{L}_{n}
is the same.

Before discussing how to impose the constraints related to the
L_{n\neq 0}
’s, it is important to study their operator algebra. We may start by observing that

\left[L_{n},\alpha_{m}^{\mu}\right]=-m\alpha_{n+m}^{\mu}\,.

(3.81)

Exercise 3.2.3

Prove eq. (
3.81
).

We can then evaluate

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\left[L_{n},:\mathrel{\alpha_{m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}}:\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}\left[L_{n},\alpha_{m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\right]+\sum_{k<0}\left[L_{n},\alpha_{k}\cdot\alpha_{m-k}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}\left\{(k-m)\alpha_{n+m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}-k\alpha_{m-k}\cdot\alpha_{n+k}\right\}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+\sum_{k<0}\left\{-k\alpha_{n+k}\cdot\alpha_{m-k}+(k-m)\alpha_{k}\cdot\alpha_{n+m-k}\right\}\right)\,.

(3.82)

Let us now choose
n>0
for convenience and set
n+k=q
in the second and third terms. We then have

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}(k-m)\alpha_{n+m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}+\sum_{q\geq n}(n-q)\alpha_{n+m-q}\cdot\alpha_{q}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+\sum_{q\leq n-1}(n-q)\alpha_{q}\cdot\alpha_{n+m-q}+\sum_{k<0}(k-m)\alpha_{k}\cdot\alpha_{n+m-k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}(n-m)\alpha_{n+m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(k-n)\alpha_{n+m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.+\sum_{k\leq-1}(n-m)\alpha_{k}\cdot\alpha_{n+m-k}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)\alpha_{k}\cdot\alpha_{n+m-k}\right)\,.

(3.83)

Moreover we can write

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)\alpha_{k}\cdot\alpha_{n+m-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)\left(\alpha_{n+m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}-\left[\alpha_{n+m-k}^{\mu},\alpha_{k}^{\nu}\right]\eta_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)\left(\alpha_{n+m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}-(-k)\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{n+m,\,0}\eta_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)\left(\alpha_{n+m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}+kd\delta_{n+m,\,0}\right)\,,

(3.84)

where
d
is the target space dimension. Therefore we have

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(n-m)\alpha_{n+m-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)k\,D\,\delta_{n+m,\,0}\right)

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{1}{2}\,D\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)k\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,.

(3.85)

If we now evaluate the finite sum

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k)k=\frac{1}{6}n(n^{2}-1)

(3.86)

we find the
quantum
algebra staisfied by the constraints

\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{D}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,

(3.87)

which is the famous
Virasoro algebra
with
central

charge

c=D
.
The term

\frac{c}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,\,0}

is called a
central extension
and, as we have seen, is a (two-dimensional) quantum effect.

We immediately notice that the central extension vanishes when
n\neq-m
.

Moreover, it vanishes also when
n=-1,0,+1
, which means that
L_{-1},L_{0},L_{1}
do not have the central extension.
They obey the nice
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
algebra

\left[L_{0},L_{1}\right]=-L_{1}\,,\qquad\left[L_{0},L_{-1}\right]=+L_{-1}\,,\qquad\left[L_{1},L_{-1}\right]=2L_{0}\,,

(3.88)

also known as the
Gliozzi algebra
(who ‘re-discovered’ it in the early days of string theory) or the
small conformal algebra
.
We recall that
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
can be put in (almost) one-to-one correspondence with the group of global conformal transformations of the Riemann sphere

f(z)=\frac{\alpha z+\beta}{\gamma z+\delta}\,,

(3.89)

whose coefficients matrix has unit determinant:

\text{det}\left(\begin{matrix}\alpha&\beta\\
\gamma&\delta\end{matrix}\right)=1\,.

(3.90)

As we will see, this is no coincidence and it will be better understood when we will study the underlying conformal field theory (CFT) on the worldsheet.

Let us now consider the zero-momentum vacuum:

\ket{0,P}\Big|_{P=0}=\ket{0}\,.

(3.91)

We have that

\displaystyle P^{\mu}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.92a)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}^{\mu}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.92b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{\mu}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(3.92c)

Notice that the normal ordering
:\mathrel{\,\cdots\,}:
we introduced earlier is well-adapted to
\ket{0}
, even for the
\alpha_{0}
oscillator which obviously commutes with everything.
We then have

\displaystyle L_{+1}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.93a)

\displaystyle L_{0}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.93b)

\displaystyle L_{-1}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.93c)

since

\displaystyle L_{+1}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{1-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\ket{0}=\begin{cases}0\quad\text{for $k>0$ since $\alpha_{k}$ annihilates $\ket{0}$}\\
0\quad\text{for $k<0$ since $\alpha_{1-k}$ annihilates $\ket{0}$}\\
0\quad\text{for $k=0$ since $\alpha_{0}\big|_{P=0}=0$.}\\
\end{cases}\,,

(3.94)

\displaystyle L_{0}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}=\left(\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\neq 0}\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\right)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}\Big|_{P=0}+\sum_{k>0}\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\right)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.95)

\displaystyle L_{-1}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{-1-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\ket{0}=\begin{cases}0\quad\text{for $k>0$ since $\alpha_{-1-k}$ annihilates $\ket{0}$}\\
0\quad\text{for $k<0$ since $\alpha_{k}$ annihilates $\ket{0}$}\\
0\quad\text{for $k=0$ since $\alpha_{0}\big|_{P=0}=0$.}\\
\end{cases}\,.

(3.96)

This means that
\ket{0}
is invariant under the action of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
and therefore it is called
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum
.
From the point of view of the zero-momentum vacuum
\ket{0}
, the states
\ket{0,P}
are not vacua, but rather excited states obtained by injecting momentum
P
into
\ket{0}
by means of a plane wave operator:

e^{iP\cdot\hat{X}_{0}}\ket{0}=\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.97)

We have indeed

\displaystyle\hat{P}^{\mu}e^{iP\cdot\hat{X}_{0}}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0+\left[\hat{P}^{\mu},e^{iP\cdot\hat{X}_{0}}\right]\ket{0}

\displaystyle=iP_{\nu}\left[\hat{P}^{\mu},\hat{X}_{0}^{\nu}\right]e^{iP\cdot\hat{X}_{0}}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=iP_{\nu}(-i\eta^{\mu\nu})e^{iP\cdot\hat{X}_{0}}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=P^{\mu}e^{iP\cdot\hat{X}_{0}}\ket{0}\,.

(3.98)

Therefore
\ket{0}
is the true
unique
vacuum of our matter Hilbert space.

Now we are ready to impose the remaining Virasoro constraints in addition to (
3.80
). When
n\neq 0
, we would be tempted to define the physical states
\ket{\text{phys}}
as

L_{n}\ket{\text{phys}}\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$?$}}}{=}}0\qquad\forall n\neq 0\,.

(3.99)

However, because of the Virasoro algebra, this way of imposing the constraints would result in

\displaystyle\bra{\text{phys}}\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]\ket{\text{phys'}}=

\displaystyle\quad\qquad=0

\displaystyle\quad\qquad=\bra{\text{phys}}\left((n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{D}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,\,0}\right)\ket{\text{phys'}}\,,

(3.100)

which would imply

\innerproduct{\text{phys}}{\text{phys'}}=0\,.

(3.101)

Since this is clearly not acceptable, we take the weaker constraint

\bra{\text{phys}}L_{n}\ket{\text{phys'}}=0\qquad\forall n\neq 0\,,

(3.102)

which implies (recalling that
L_{n}^{\dagger}=L_{-n}
)

L_{n}\ket{\text{phys}}=0\qquad\bra{\text{phys}}L_{-n}=0\qquad\forall n>0\,.

(3.103)

The same holds for
\widetilde{L}_{n}
.

On the other hand, if we consider
n=0
, the physical constraint we have to consider is the one that we already wrote in (
3.80
), namely

\left(L_{0}-a\right)\ket{\text{phys}}=0\,,

(3.104)

where we recalled that
L_{0}^{\dagger}=L_{0}
.
We will see that, for a proper critical value of
a
(and of
D
, the space-time dimensions), these constraints will give rise to a consistent physical spectrum of excitations with well-defined interactions. However to fully understand the mathematical reason behind these critical values we will need the BRST quantization.

3.2.1.4
Two anticipations from BRST quantization

At this point it is worth noticing that the quantum system we are studying has two unfixed quantities: the spacetime dimension
D
and the normal ordering constant
a
. Although it is possible to examine how the string spectrum changes as we vary these two parameters, the BRST quantization method we will analyze later fixes these two numbers uniquely. Without entering yet in the details of the BRST quantization of the string it is worth anticipating the two main outcomes of this process.

1.

As for the particle and the more general example discussed in
2.3.1
we will have a BRST operator
Q_{B}
which acts on an Hilbert space which is the tensor product of the Hilbert space we are considering now (the matter Hilbert space) and the ghost Hilbert space. Because of normal ordering subtleties the nilpotency of
Q_{B}
will not be guaranteed, but we will have

Q_{B}^{2}=0\quad\leftrightarrow\quad D=26\,,

(3.105)

2.

In this matter-ghost Hilbert space we can search for physical states by analyzing the kernel of
Q_{B}
in a subspace of the form
\ket{\Psi}=\ket{\psi}^{\rm matter}\otimes\ket{\downarrow}^{\rm ghost}
, where
\ket{\downarrow}^{\rm ghost}
is a stringy analog of the corresponding ghost vacuum for the particle (
2.83
). We will then find

Q_{B}\ket{\Psi}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\begin{cases}L_{n}\ket{\psi}^{\rm matter}=0\,\,\,\qquad\forall n>0\\[5.0pt]
(L_{0}-1)\ket{\psi}^{\rm matter}=0.\end{cases}\,.

(3.106)

Which are the OCQ constraints we have been discussing above, but with the precise choice
a=1
.

3.2.1.5
Physical, spurious and null states

Continuing with our OCQ framework, it is useful to introduce some definitions.

•

A
physical
state
\ket{\text{phys}}
is a state that satisfies the physical conditions

\displaystyle L_{n}\ket{\text{phys}}=0\qquad\forall n>0\,,

(3.107a)

\displaystyle\left(L_{0}-a\right)\ket{\text{phys}}=0\,.

(3.107b)

•

A
spurious
state
\ket{\chi}
is a state generated by negatively-moded Virasoros
L_{-n}
acting an a generic state
\ket{\chi_n}
):

\displaystyle\ket{\chi}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n>0}L_{-n}\ket{\chi_n}\,.

(3.108)

Such a state has zero inner product with any physical state:

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\chi}{\text{phys}}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(3.109)

•

A
null
state
\ket{\text{null}}
is a state that is both physical and spurious:

\displaystyle\ket{\text{null}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n>0}L_{-n}\ket{\chi_n}\,,

(3.110a)

\displaystyle L_{n}\ket{\text{null}}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\forall n>0\,,

(3.110b)

\displaystyle(L_{0}-a)\ket{\text{null}}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(3.110c)

A null state has zero inner product with other null states and with physical states:

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\text{null}}{\text{null}'}=0\,,\qquad\innerproduct{\text{null}}{\text{phys}}=0\,.

(3.111)

This suggests to define an equivalence relation inside the space of physical states, stating the fact that the inner product between physical states does not change if we add null states to the physical states:

\ket{\text{phys}}\sim\ket{\text{phys}}+\ket{\text{null}}\,.

(3.112)

As we will see, the freedom of adding null states to physical states will be perceived in the target space as an (on-shell)
space-time gauge invariance
.

3.2.1.6
Physical spectrum of the closed string

Let us now define the level operator

\overset{(\sim)}{N}=\sum_{k>0}\overset{(\sim)}{\alpha}_{-k}\cdot\overset{(\sim)}{\alpha}_{k}.

(3.113)

This operator counts the level of oscillator excitation, by assigning to each oscillator
\alpha_{-n}
a level
n
. For example we have

\displaystyle N\left(\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\right)^{2}\left(\alpha_{-2}^{\nu}\right)^{3}\ket{0,P}=8\left(\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\right)^{2}\left(\alpha_{-2}^{\nu}\right)^{3}\ket{0,P}\,,

(3.114a)

\displaystyle N\left(\alpha_{-3}^{\mu}\right)\left(\alpha_{-2}^{\nu}\right)\ket{0,P}=5\left(\alpha_{-3}^{\mu}\right)\left(\alpha_{-2}^{\nu}\right)\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.114b)

We can then write (see eq. (
3.95
))

\overset{(\sim)}{L}_{0}=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+\overset{(\sim)}{N}\,.

(3.115)

We will study the Hilbert space by decomposing it in eigenspaces of
(N,\widetilde{N})
with fixed level. This is useful because these eigenspaces are finite dimensional (compared to the infinite dimensionality of the full Hilbert space).

Before we move forward with the analysis of the spectrum, it is useful to rewrite the
(L_{0},\tilde{L}_{0})
constraints as

\displaystyle\left(L_{0}+\widetilde{L}_{0}\right)\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{2}+N+\widetilde{N}\right)\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}}{2}+N+\widetilde{N}\right)\ket{0,P}=2a\ket{0,P}\,,

(3.116a)

\displaystyle\left(L_{0}-\widetilde{L}_{0}\right)\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=\left(N-\widetilde{N}\right)\ket{0,P}=0\,,

(3.116b)

where we assumed
a=\tilde{a}
(remember that
a=\tilde{a}=1
). If we have a generic state
|N,\widetilde{N}\rangle
of level
(N,\widetilde{N})
, the constraint (
3.116b
) reads as the level matching condition:

N=\widetilde{N}\,.

(3.117)

It simply establishes that a physical state has to contain the same oscillator level on both sides. Therefore, instead of
(N,\widetilde{N})
, we will simply use
N
to denote the level of the state of interest.

On the other hand, using the mass-shell condition
p^{2}=-m^{2}
the constraint (
3.116a
) reads

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}}{2}+2N=2a\,,

(3.118)

and hence, using (
3.117
), it gives the mass-shell of the state
|N,N\rangle
:

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=4(N-a)\,.

(3.119)

We can now move on and analyze the first levels.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N=0

We start from
N=0
, i.e. by
\ket{0,P}
. We have that

\displaystyle\overset{(\sim)}{L}_{n}\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\forall n>0\,,

(3.120a)

\displaystyle\overset{(\sim)}{L}_{0}\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+0\right)\ket{0,P}=a\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.120b)

The mass-shell condition (
p^{2}=-m^{2}
) together with the second constraint (i.e. (
3.120b
)) gives

-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}}{4}=a\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ m^{2}=-\frac{4a}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

(3.121)

If
a>0
, the
(0,0)
state has
m^{2}<0
, namely it is a tachyon. Since, at the end of the day
a=1
, we definitely have a tachyon in the spectrum.
This is not a good news because it is saying that we are quantizing the theory around an
unstable vacuum
. The question whether is there a new stable vacuum in the tachyon potential (just like the stable symmetry-breaking vacuum of the Higgs field of the Standard Model) is as of today an
open question
and a great mystery. Our (pedagogical) strategy in the sequel will be to
ignore
this tachyon problem and use this model to learn as much as we can in this simple bosonic setting. Later on we will perform a similar analysis for the (more complicated)
super-string
model where the tachyon will not be there and where genuine space-time fermions will arise in the spectrum. But for the time being we continue with the bosonic string.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N=1

At the level
N=1
the only possible state is

\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}

(3.122)

whose mass is
\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=4(1-a)
. This state has two Lorentz indices of undefined symmetry, therefore it is a reducible representation of the Lorentz group. If we saturate them with a spacetime polarization (depending on the momentum), we get

G_{\mu\nu}(P)\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.123)

The tensor
G_{\mu\nu}
can be split into three Lorentz irreducible representations

G_{\mu\nu}=h_{\mu\nu}+B_{\mu\nu}+\Phi\eta_{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.124)

defined as follows:

\begin{cases}h_{\mu\nu}=G_{(\mu\nu)}\underbrace{-\frac{1}{D}\eta^{\mu\nu}G_{\rho}^{\,\,\,\rho}}_{\text{remove the trace}}\qquad\quad\,\,\text{symmetric traceless}\\[20.0pt]
B_{\mu\nu}=G_{[\mu\nu]}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\text{anti-symmetric}\\[20.0pt]
\quad\Phi=\underbrace{\frac{1}{d}G_{\rho}^{\,\,\,\rho}}_{\text{trace}}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\,\text{scalar}\end{cases}\,.

(3.125)

The most generic state can be written integrating over the momentum:

\ket{\psi}=\int d^{d}P\,G_{\mu\nu}(P)\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.126)

If we now consider the
n\neq 0
Virasoro constraints (see eq. (
3.103
)), we have that only
L_{1}
gives a non-trivial contribution, while
L_{n\geq 2}\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}=0
. We find indeed

\displaystyle L_{1}G_{\mu\nu}\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\propto G_{\mu\nu}(\alpha_{0}^{\rho}\alpha_{1}^{\sigma})\eta_{\rho\sigma}\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}=

\displaystyle\qquad=G_{\rho\nu}P^{\rho}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.127)

Therefore
L_{1}
and
\widetilde{L}_{1}
impose (respectively)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}P_{\mu}G^{\mu\nu}&=0\\
G^{\mu\nu}P_{\nu}&=0\end{cases}\,.

(3.128)

On the other hand, the mass-shell constraint (
3.119
) says that

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=4(1-a)\,,

(3.129)

which means that for
a<1
we have massive states, for
a=1
we have massless states and for
a>1
we have tachyons (that are now tensors, and not scalars, as in the
(0,0)
case). Let’s analyze the situation in detail.

•

We may start considering the
a>1
case, where the states are tachyons. Since
P^{2}>0
,
P^{\mu}
is a space-like vector and therefore there is a certain reference frame where it can be written as
P^{\mu}=(0,\vec{P})
. Moreover, the condition
P_{\mu}G^{\mu\nu}=0
implies that we can take an acceptable
G^{\mu\nu}=\delta^{\mu 0}\xi^{\nu}
. We can then consider the state

\ket{\text{state}}=\xi_{\nu}\alpha_{-1}^{0}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}\,,

(3.130)

which is physical if

\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}=a-1>0\,.

(3.131)

In addition,
G^{\mu\nu}P_{\nu}=0
also implies that
\xi=(\xi^{0},\vec{\xi})
, with
\vec{P}\cdot\vec{\xi}=0
. We have thus a physical state. Now we can compute its norm, finding

\left|\left|\,\ket{\text{state}}\,\right|\right|^{2}=\eta^{00}(\xi\cdot\xi)\delta(P+P^{\prime})<0\,.

(3.132)

Therefore for
a>1
there exist negative-norm states, which are inconsistent with the basic axioms of quantum mechanics. Therefore we must have
a\leq 1
.

•

Let us now consider the
a=1
case, where
m^{2}=0
and therefore

P^{2}=0\,.

(3.133)

In this sector we have physical zero-norm states. If we indeed consider the polarization

G_{*}^{\mu\nu}=P^{\mu}\xi^{\nu}+v^{\mu}P^{\nu}

(3.134)

with
P\cdot\xi=0
and
v\cdot P=0
, the state is physical and we also have

\left|\left|\,G_{*}^{\mu\nu}\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}\,\right|\right|^{2}\sim P^{2}=0\,.

(3.135)

This is a physical state having vanishing norm, but it is spurious too because

\displaystyle P^{\mu}\xi^{\nu}\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle\propto L_{-1}(\xi\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{-1})\ket{0,P}\,,

(3.136a)

\displaystyle v^{\mu}P^{\nu}\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle\propto(v\cdot\alpha_{-1})\widetilde{L}_{-1}\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.136b)

We hence found a null state, giving an equivalence relation on the physical states’ space:

\displaystyle\ket{\text{phys}}

\displaystyle\sim\ket{\text{phys}}+\ket{\text{null}}\,,

(3.137)

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\sim G_{\mu\nu}+P^{\mu}\xi^{\nu}+v^{\mu}P^{\nu}\,,

(3.138)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\big(\text{with}\,\,P\cdot\xi=0\,,\,\,v\cdot P=0\,,\,\,P^{2}=0\big)\,.

We have that (
3.138
) is a gauge transformation of the polarization tensor
G_{\mu\nu}
. If we then split it into its three components (see (
3.125
)), we can write

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\sim h_{\mu\nu}+P_{(\mu}\theta_{\nu)}\,,

(3.139)

\displaystyle B_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\sim B_{\mu\nu}+P_{[\mu}\Lambda_{\nu]}\,,

(3.140)

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\sim\Phi\,,

(3.141)

where we defined

\theta_{\mu}=\frac{\xi_{\mu}+v_{\mu}}{2}\,,\qquad\Lambda_{\mu}=\frac{\xi_{\mu}-v_{\mu}}{2}\,.

(3.142)

We then find the following states.

–

The
graviton

h_{\mu\nu}
with its gauge transformation (see eq.
3.139
) and with its physical conditions

\begin{cases}P^{2}h_{\mu\nu}=0\\
P_{\mu}h^{\mu\nu}=0\\
{h_{\mu}}^{\mu}=0\end{cases}\,,

(3.143)

which imply (and in fact are equivalent to) the linearized Einstein equations in momentum space
4
4

4

Similarly, the photon’s EOMs are

\displaystyle\Box A_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}(\partial\cdot A)=0

\displaystyle\,\,\leftrightarrow\,\,P^{2}A_{\mu}-P_{\mu}(P\cdot A)=0\,.

Since
A_{\mu}
and
P_{\mu}
are in general two vectors pointing in different directions (an
A_{\mu}
proportional to
P_{\mu}
would be gauge trivial), the above equation is equivalent to

\begin{cases}P^{2}=0\\
P\cdot A=0\end{cases}\,.

:

P^{2}\,h_{\mu\nu}-P^{\rho}P_{(\mu}h_{\nu)\rho}-P_{\mu}P_{\nu}\,h_{\rho}^{\rho}=0\,.

(3.144)

–

The
Kalb-Ramond field

B_{\mu\nu}
, which is a 2-form

B=B_{\mu\nu}dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}\,.

(3.145)

It comes with its own gauge transformation (see eq.
3.140
) and its physical conditions are

\begin{cases}P^{2}B_{\mu\nu}=0\\
P_{\mu}B^{\mu\nu}=0\end{cases}\,,

(3.146)

which are equivalent to the EOMs of a 2-form which are briefly described as follows. We first define the field-strength

\displaystyle H=dB

\displaystyle=\partial_{[\mu}B_{\nu\rho]}dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}\wedge dx^{\rho}

\displaystyle=H_{\mu\nu\rho}dx^{\mu}\wedge dx^{\nu}\wedge dx^{\rho}\,.

(3.147)

In complete analogy with the 1-form Maxwell field
A_{\mu}
and its field-strenght
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{[\mu}A_{\nu]}
, the action functional for the 2-form field
B
is given by
5
5

5

Recall that in flat
D
-dimensional Minkowski space, the
Hodge dual
of a
p
-form
A_{p}=A_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p}}dx^{\mu_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{\mu_{p}}
, is the
(D-p)
-form
*A_{p}=A^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p}}\epsilon_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{d}}dx^{\mu_{p+1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{\mu_{D}}.
This operation needs a spacetime metric, since the indices of
A
are raised.

S\sim\int d^{D}x\,H_{\mu\nu\rho}H^{\mu\nu\rho}\sim\int H\wedge*H.

(3.148)

\begin{cases}*d*H=0\qquad\to\,\,\Box B_{\mu\nu}+\partial^{\rho}\partial_{[\mu}B_{\nu]\rho}=0\\
dH=0\qquad\quad\,\,\,\text{(Bianchi identities, no condition on $B$)}\end{cases}\,.

(3.149)

–

The
dilaton

\Phi
, which is a gauge invariant (see eq.
3.141
) scalar field which should obey the physical state
condition

P^{2}\Phi(P)=0.

(3.150)

•

Let us finally consider the
a<1
case. Here we have massive representations of the Lorentz group and therefore we do not find negative-norm physical states. In principle this would be acceptable. However, as already anticipated, the BRST quantization fixes
a=1
unambiguosly. Since, as we will see, we eventually need the BRST model to describe strings interactions, we will not consider this possibility further.

We hence found that the closed string spectrum at the
(1,1)
level is physically acceptable for
a\leq 1
. At the critical value
a=1
there are zero-norm states, corresponding to the gauge symmetries. From a purely OCQ perspective
a=\tilde{a}=1
is required to have a properly defined graviton (plus dilaton and Kalb-Ramond) with their own gauge transformations, associated to null states.
While, at this stage, this is still not sufficient to fix
a=1
by consistency it is however an indication that the correct choice
a=1
is associated with the appearance of null states in the spectrum.

\blacksquare\,
The Kalb-Ramond field and the fundamental string charge

The fact that we have found a massless two-form in the closed string spectrum is deeply related to the one-dimensional nature of the string.
We all know that when we have a charged particle of charge
q
, there is a minimal coupling between the particle world-line and the gauge field
A_{\mu}
which feels the charge
q
:

\displaystyle q\int_{WL}A=q\int d\tau\dot{X}^{\mu}(\tau)\,A_{\mu}(X)\,.

(3.151)

At the same time the charge
q
is detectable by computing the flux of the electric field
E\sim*F
over a sphere surrounding the particle:

\displaystyle q\sim\int_{S^{D-2}}*F^{(2)}\,,

(3.152)

where
*
is the Hodge dual (which, in 4 dimensions, exchanges
\overrightarrow{E}
with
\overrightarrow{B}
).
This is Gauss theorem, appropriately generalized for a particle in
D
space-time dimensions.

In the same way we can consider the Kalb-Ramond 2-form to minimally couple to the string itself via

-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{WS}B=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{WS}d^{2}\sigma\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\,B_{\mu}(X)

(3.153)

and the fundamental string charge is then given by the flux of
H=dB
through a spatial
S^{d-3}
-sphere encircling the string

\displaystyle\textrm{String charge}\sim\int_{S^{d-3}}*H^{(3)}\,.

(3.154)

\blacksquare\,
Higher levels

Having fixed
a=1
, at higher levels we find massive representations of the Lorentz group with, in general, spin higher than 2. The structure of these massive modes is quite complicated, especially for the closed string and it would not be so illuminating to discuss them in detail. We will analyze some of this massive states when we will study the easier open string spectrum. Here we would like to take the opportunity to learn that, just as the search for null states at level 1 selected
a=1
, the search for null states at level 2 will instead select the critical dimension
D=26
.

In order to see this, let us consider

\ket{\chi_2}=\underbrace{\left(2L_{-2}+3L_{-1}^{2}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{left}\\
\text{excitation}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\left(\tilde{\theta}_{-2}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{right}\\
\text{excitation}\end{subarray}}\ket{0,P}\,,

(3.155)

where
\tilde{\theta}_{-2}
is a generic operator made up of right oscillators
\tilde{\alpha}
(adding up to
\widetilde{N}=2
).

Setting
a=\tilde{a}=1
, we will assume that
\tilde{\theta}_{-2}
has been chosen so that the
\tilde{L}_{n}
Virasoro constraints are already satisfied:

\displaystyle(\widetilde{L}_{0}-1)\tilde{\theta}_{-2}\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.156a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{1}\tilde{\theta}_{-2}\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.156b)

\displaystyle\widetilde{L}_{2}\tilde{\theta}_{-2}\ket{0,P}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(3.156c)

Notice that, thanks to the Virasoro algebra, it is enough to impose
\tilde{L}_{1}=\tilde{L}_{2}=0
to guarantee that
\tilde{L}_{n>0}=0
.

Exercise 3.2.4

Prove this.

Now we would like to check whether this spurious state is also physical, i.e. it is null.
Explicit calculation shows that

\displaystyle(L_{0}-1)\ket{\chi_2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+1\right)\ket{\chi_2}=0\to m^{2}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,,

(3.157a)

\displaystyle L_{1}\ket{\chi_2}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(3.157b)

\displaystyle L_{2}\ket{\chi_2}

\displaystyle\propto(D-26)\tilde{\theta}_{-2}\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.157c)

Exercise 3.2.5

Show this.

Moreover, one can show that

\left|\left|\,\ket{\chi}\,\right|\right|^{2}\propto(26-D)\,.

(3.158)

Therefore the norm is zero for
D=26
, precisely when the state is null, as expected.

It can be shown that when
a=1
and
D=26
there is a proliferation of similar null states which, as previously argued, define equivalence relations between physical states. This is the so-called critical string theory and these null states are the hallmark of the BRST cohomology of the string which will be indeed consistently realized only for
a=1
and
D=26
.

To better appreciate the structure of level-two null states you can amuse yourself with the following exercise.

Exercise 3.2.6

In the left moving sector, consider the level two state

\ket{\Psi}=\left(\alpha_{-1}\cdot\alpha_{-1}+c_{2}\alpha_{0}\cdot\alpha_{-2}+2c_{3}(\alpha_{0}\cdot\alpha_{-1})^{2}\right)\ket{0,P}.

(3.159)

Determine
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
in such a way that the state is physical (for arbitrary value of the normal ordering constant
a
).
For the physical values of
c_{2}
and
c_{3}
show that the norm of the state is given by

\langle{\Psi}\ket{\Psi}=\frac{2(D-1)[2(a-2)(8a-21)+(2a-3)D]}{(9-4a)^{2}}\langle{0,P}\ket{0,P}.

(3.160)

Therefore (assuming
D>1
) we have that

\langle{\Psi}\ket{\Psi}\geq 0\quad\rightarrow\quad D\leq\frac{2(2-a)(21-8a)}{3-2a}.

(3.161)

3.2.2
OCQ for the open string

3.2.2.1
Classical solution and quantization

Let us now focus on the open string case. As we have seen, the EOMs are (
3.51
)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\partial_{+}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})&=0\qquad\text{(WS bulk eq.{})}\\
\delta X_{\mu}\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}\Big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}&=0\qquad\text{(WS boundary eq.{})}\end{cases}\,.

(3.162)

The open string’s endpoints (corresponding to
\sigma=0
and
\sigma=\pi
) are characterized by their boundary conditions (BC) which, in this case, can be Neumann or Dirichlet
6
6

6

In a more generic string background there can be many different, equally consistent, boundary conditions. Neumann and Dirichlet are the only possible boundary conditions for a single scalar field
X^{i}
in a non-compact space.
.

•

The Neumann (N) boundary condition is

\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}\Big|_{\text{endpoint }}=0\,,

(3.163)

which preserves Poincaré symmetry. It correspond to the statement that there is no momentum transfer along a certain direction
\mu
in spacetime.

•

The Dirichlet (D) boundary condition is

\delta X_{\mu}\Big|_{\text{endpoint }}=0\,,

(3.164)

which breaks translational symmetry, since it corresponds to the statement that the endpoint is fixed at a certain point along the direction
\mu
in spacetime.

N-N string

Let us first consider an open string having Neumann boundary conditions on both its endpoints along a certain direction
\mu
.
We may then write the solution (in the Neumann direction
\mu
) as we did for the closed string, namely by splitting it into its left and right-moving parts, using lightcone coordinates:

X^{\mu}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})=X^{\mu}_{L}(\sigma^{+})+X^{\mu}_{R}(\sigma^{-})\,.

(3.165)

We then define the auxiliary quantity

\hat{X}^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=\begin{cases}X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)\qquad\quad\,\,\text{if }\sigma\in(0,\pi)\\
X^{\mu}(\tau,2\pi-\sigma)\quad\text{if }\sigma\in(\pi,2\pi)\end{cases}\,,

(3.166)

and naturally extend it by
2\pi
-periodicity:

\hat{X}^{\mu}(\tau,2\pi+\sigma)=\hat{X}^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma).

(3.167)

In addition, by construction,
\hat{X}
also satisfies

\hat{X}(\tau,\sigma)=\hat{X}(\tau,-\sigma).

(3.168)

Notice that
\hat{X}^{\mu}
is analogous to the closed string solution. However the additional invariance
\sigma\to-\sigma
implies that this time we have only a set of oscillators

\alpha_{n}^{\mu}=\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu},

and the full solution then reads

X^{\mu}_{NN}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})=X_{0}^{\mu}+\alpha^{\prime}P^{\mu}\tau+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha_{n}^{\mu}\left(e^{-in\sigma^{+}}+e^{-in\sigma^{-}}\right)\,.

(3.169)

Exercise 3.2.7

Show that for such a solution the Neumann boundary condition
\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}\big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}=0
holds.

This time the Hilbert space will be acted by a single set of oscillators

\left[\alpha_{n}^{\mu},\alpha_{m}^{\nu}\right]=n\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,

(3.170)

in addition with the center of mass Heisenberg algebra

[X_{0}^{\mu},P^{\nu}]=i\eta^{\mu\nu}.

(3.171)

The generic state will hence be

\alpha_{-n_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}\dots\alpha_{-n_{k}}^{\mu_{k}}\ket{0,P}\,.

(3.172)

In analogy with the closed string, the physical open string states can be defined by implementing the Virasoro constraints, using the quantum operators

\displaystyle L_{n\neq 0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}\,,

(3.173a)

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}:\mathrel{\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}}:=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{0})^{2}+N=\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}+N\,,

(3.173b)

where in this case, for the NN open string, we define the zero-mode oscillator as

\alpha_{0}^{\mu}=\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}P^{\mu}\,\quad\textrm{(open NN string)}\,.

(3.174)

D-D string

Let us now consider an open string having Dirichlet boundary conditions on both its endpoints along a certain direction
i
. Since
\delta X_{i}\big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}=0
, we can fix

\displaystyle X^{i}(\sigma=0)=x^{i}\,,\qquad X^{i}(\sigma=\pi)=x^{i}\,.

(3.175)

Therefore the solution (in the Dirichlet direction
i
) can be written as

X^{i}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{i}+Y^{i}(\tau,\sigma)\,,

(3.176)

where
Y^{i}(\tau,\sigma)
is the oscillatory part and obeys

Y^{i}(\tau,\sigma)\Big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}=0\,,

(3.177)

so that
\delta Y_{i}\big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}=0
. We then define

\hat{Y}^{i}(\tau,\sigma)=\begin{cases}Y^{i}(\tau,\sigma)\quad\text{if }\sigma\in(0,\pi)\\
-Y^{i}(\tau,2\pi-\sigma)\quad\text{if }\sigma\in(\pi,2\pi)\end{cases}\,

(3.178)

and extend it by
2\pi
-periodicity. This time
\hat{Y}
is odd under
\sigma\to-\sigma
:

\hat{Y}^{i}(\tau,-\sigma)=\hat{Y}^{i}(\tau,2\pi-\sigma)=-\hat{Y}^{i}(\tau,\sigma)\,.

(3.179)

Therefore the solution for the oscillatory part can be written as a closed string with
\alpha_{n}=-\tilde{\alpha}_{n}
:

Y^{i}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})=i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha_{n}^{i}\left(e^{-in\sigma^{+}}-e^{-in\sigma^{-}}\right)\,,

(3.180)

and the complete solution reads as

X^{i}_{DD}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{i}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha_{n}^{i}\left(e^{-in\sigma^{+}}-e^{-in\sigma^{-}}\right)\,.

(3.181)

The term
\alpha^{\prime}P^{\mu}\tau
(containing the momentum of the center of mass) is absent, since
\tau
would violate the anti-symmetric property of the solution
X^{i}(\tau,\sigma)
under
\sigma\to-\sigma
. Therefore, when the open string’s endpoints are fixed, the center of mass does not move.

N-D string

Let us finally consider an open string having Neumann boundary condition on one endpoint and Dirichlet boundary condition on the other one, along a certain direction
\mu
:

\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}\Big|_{\sigma=0}=0\,,\qquad X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)\Big|_{\sigma=\pi}=x^{\mu}\,.

(3.182)

The solution can be written using half-integer moded oscillators as

X^{\mu}_{ND}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{r}\alpha_{r}^{\mu}\left(e^{-ir\sigma^{+}}+e^{-ir\sigma^{-}}\right)\,.

(3.183)

It has no momentum
P^{\mu}
since one endpoint is fixed by the Dirichlet condition and hence the center of mass cannot have definite momentum.

Analogously, if we exchange the N and D conditions for
\sigma=0
and
\sigma=\pi
we find

X^{\mu}_{DN}(\tau,\sigma)=X_{0}^{\mu}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{r}\alpha_{r}^{\mu}\left(e^{-ir\sigma^{+}}-e^{-ir\sigma^{-}}\right)\,.

(3.184)

Therefore the exchange
\text{N}\longleftrightarrow\text{D}
translates into a change of sign of the anti-holomorphic sector (i.e. the right-moving part).

Exercise 3.2.8

Prove this last statement.

We can then define (for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
)

L_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}:\mathrel{\alpha_{n-r}\cdot\alpha_{r}}:

(3.185)

together with the commutators

\displaystyle\left[\alpha_{r}^{\mu},\alpha_{s}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=r\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{r+s,\,0}\,,

(3.186a)

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{1}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,.

(3.186b)

These operators will act on a vacuum
\ket{0}_{ND}
as

\alpha_{r}\ket{0}_{ND}=0\,,\quad\forall r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\,,\,\,r\geq\frac{1}{2}\,.

(3.187)

Before we go on it is useful to analyze the stress-energy tensor and its relation with the boundary conditions of the open string:

T_{\pm\pm}(\sigma^{\pm})=-\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}L_{n}e^{-in\sigma^{\pm}}\,.

(3.188)

If we notice that

\displaystyle\partial_{+}X_{NN}(\sigma^{+})

\displaystyle=\partial_{-}X_{NN}(\sigma^{-})\Big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}\,,

(3.189a)

\displaystyle\partial_{+}X_{DD}(\sigma^{+})

\displaystyle=-\partial_{-}X_{DD}(\sigma^{-})\Big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}\,,

(3.189b)

we realize that for N-N and for D-D the stress energy tensor satisfies

T_{++}(\sigma^{+})=T_{--}(\sigma^{-})\Big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}\,.

(3.190)

This is an example of a
gluing condition
. Notice that, independently on the boundary conditions for
X
,
T
has always the same boundary conditions.
Similarly, for N-D open strings it easy to see that

\displaystyle\partial_{+}X_{ND}(\sigma^{+})

\displaystyle=\partial_{-}X_{ND}(\sigma^{-})\Big|_{\sigma=0}\,,

(3.191a)

\displaystyle\partial_{+}X_{ND}(\sigma^{+})

\displaystyle=-\partial_{-}X_{ND}(\sigma^{-})\Big|_{\sigma=\pi}\,.

(3.191b)

Nevertheless,
T
has always the same boundary condition (
3.190
).

Exercise 3.2.9

Prove eqs. (
3.191
).

3.2.2.2
Mixed boundary conditions and
D(p)
-branes

Let us now consider an open string whose endpoints have:

•

Neumann BC along
\mu=0,1,\dots,p
;

•

Dirichlet BC along
i=p+1,\dots,D-1
(where
D
is the spacetime dimension).

These endpoints can therefore move only in the
p+1
Neumann directions, since they are fixed in the
(D-p-1)
Dirichlet directions. This means that we have a sub-manifold
\mathbb{R}^{1,p}
of the spacetime
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
where the endpoints can move around (see fig.
3.4
). Such a sub-manifold is called a Dirichlet brane, more precisely a
D(p)
-brane
.
Notice in particular that if the open string has N-N endpoints in every direction, the
D(p)
-brane is identified with the whole spacetime.

The existence of a
D(p)
-brane breaks the invariance of the closed string theory under translations. As in a spontaneously symmetry breaking vacuum we then expect that Goldstone bosons arise, describing the displacement of the brane along the directions where the translational invariance is broken.

As the
D(p)
-brane moves in spacetime, it spans a
p+1
-dimensional surface, called
worldvolume
(see fig.
3.5
).

Figure 3.4:
A
D(p)
-brane embedded in the target space
\mathbb{R}^{1,D-1}
.

Figure 3.5:
The trajectory of the
D(p)
-brane in the spacetime spans the worldvolume. The
p
-dimensional momentum
P^{\mu}
lies on the
D(p)
-brane, since along Dirichlet directions we have no momentum.

3.2.2.3
Single
D(p)
-brane spectrum

Let us consider a generic state made up of Neumann oscillators
\alpha_{n}^{\mu}
and Dirichlet oscillators
\alpha_{n}^{i}
acting on the vacuum
\ket{0,P}
, where the spacetime momentum
P^{\mu}
lies on the
D(p)
-brane, since along Dirichlet directions we have no momentum (see fig.
3.5
). The zero mode Virasoro operator is in this case

L_{0}=\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}+N^{(NN)}+N^{(DD)}=\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}+N^{(\text{tot})}\,.

(3.192)

This turns into the mass formula

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=N^{(\text{tot})}-a\,.

(3.193)

We can now analyze the first energy levels of the spectrum.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N=0

At the level
N=0
we have

t(P)\ket{0,P}\,,

(3.194)

where
t(P)
is the spacetime polarization of the state.
Its mass is given by

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=-a\,.

(3.195)

If we then take

a=1\,,

(3.196)

this
N=0
state corresponds to a tachyon (whose spacetime polarization is
t(P)
) which is four times lighter than the closed tachyon (see eq.
3.121
):

m^{2}=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

(3.197)

\blacksquare\,
Level
N=1

At the level
N=1
we have to take one oscillator
\alpha_{-1}
, that can excite the vacuum along a Neumann or a Dirichlet direction. If we saturate the free indices with a spacetime polarization, we have

\Big(A_{\mu}(P)\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}+\phi_{i}(P)\alpha_{-1}^{i}\Big)\ket{0,P}\,,

(3.198)

where

•

A_{\mu}(P)
is a
p+1
-dimensional vector of
SO(1,p)
;

•

\phi_{i}(P)
are
D-p-1
scalars, assembled in the vector representation of
SO(D-p-1)
, which appears to be a global internal symmetry if we are sitting on the
D(p)
-brane.

Therefore the existence of the
D(p)
-brane breaks the spacetime Lorentz symmetry as follows:

SO(1,d-1)\longrightarrow\underbrace{SO(1,p)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Lorentz on}\\
\text{the $D(p)$-brane}\end{subarray}}\times\,\,\,\underbrace{SO(d-p-1)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{rotations in the}\\
\text{Dirichlet directions}\end{subarray}}\,.

(3.199)

If we then consider the mass, we have that
L_{0}-a=0\Longrightarrow\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=1-a
, which for
a=1
translates into

m=0\,.

(3.200)

Therefore we found a massless gauge vector
A_{\mu}
and
D-p-1
massless scalars
\phi_{i}
. These scalars are the Goldstone bosons in the directions where the translational symmetry has been broken by the
D(p)
-brane. By Goldstone theorem they should be massless and therefore the choice
a=1
we first pushed in (
3.196
) is in this case necessary.

These
\phi_{i}
can be seen as the coordinates describing the motion of the
D(p)
-brane in the transverse directions, where it is embedded.
Collectively, they can be seen as a map
\{\phi^{i}\}:\mathbb{R}^{1,p}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{D-p-1}
taking a point
x
in the worldvolume of the
D(p)
-brane and saying where it lays in the transverse space, namely in the Dirichlet directions. When
\phi^{i}=0
(i.e. the scalar fields have zero vev), the
D(p)
-brane is not deformed.

If we now consider the Virasoro constraints
L_{1}=0
and
L_{0}=1
, we find the EOMs for the massless gauge field (Maxwell’s equations) and for the massless scalars (massless Klein-Gordon equation):

\begin{cases}P_{\mu}A^{\mu}(P)=0\\
P^{2}A^{\mu}(P)=0\end{cases}\,,

(3.201)

P^{2}\phi^{i}(P)=0\,.

(3.202)

The equivalence relation (
3.112
) for the gauge field
A_{\mu}
reads as the gauge invariance

A_{\mu}(P)\sim A_{\mu}(P)+\lambda(P)P_{\mu}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ A_{\mu}(x)\sim A_{\mu}(x)+i\partial_{\mu}\lambda(x)\,,

(3.203)

where
\lambda(P)P_{\mu}\ket{0,P}\sim L_{-1}\ket{0,P}
is a null state, since
P^{2}=0
.

Again, at higher level, we find massive states, as described in the following exercise.

Exercise 3.2.10

Consider a
D(25)
-brane (Neumann conditions on all spacetime directions). Consider the most generic open string state at level
N=2

\big(\xi_{\mu}\alpha_{-2}^{\mu}+\theta_{\mu\nu}\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\alpha_{-1}^{\nu}\big)\ket{0,P}\,

(3.204)

and determine its physical conditions. Show that it is consistent to set
\xi_{\mu}=0
without loosing physical degrees of freedom and that in total we have a spin 2 massive particle (traceless symmetric rank two tensor).

3.2.2.4
Multiple
D(p)
-branes spectrum

Let us now consider a D-brane system made up of two
D(p)
-branes (
D(p)_{1}
and
D(p)_{2}
) parallel along a common Dirichlet direction
y
(see fig.
3.6
). They are both characterized by their Dirichlet moduli
y_{i}
, which are the precise values of the Dirichlet BC for the open strings attached to the
D(p)_{i}
-brane.

Figure 3.6:
The
D(p)-D(p)
system.

As already discussed in subsection
3.2.2.3
, the spectrum (at the
N=0,1
energy levels) on the two branes separately contains one tachyon (having
m^{2}=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}
), one massless gauge field and
d-p-1
massless scalars. These are the states produced by strings that start and end on the same
D(p)
-brane. On the other hand, the strings that are stretched between the two
D(p)
-branes generate different states. They have the same BC along all the Dirichlet directions except for
y
, where the Dirichlet modulus changes at the two endpoints.

Let us first focus on the string that starts on the
D(p)_{1}
-brane and ends on the
D(p)_{2}
-brane.
Recalling eq. (
3.176
), we can write its solution as
Y^{(12)}(\tau,\sigma)
. It is defined by

\displaystyle\partial_{+}\partial_{-}Y^{(12)}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=0\quad\,\,\text{(wave equation)}\,,

(3.205a)

\displaystyle Y^{(12)}(\tau,\sigma=0)

\displaystyle=y_{1}\quad\text{(BC)}\,,

(3.205b)

\displaystyle Y^{(12)}(\tau,\sigma=\pi)

\displaystyle=y_{2}\quad\text{(BC)}\,.

(3.205c)

The solution can be written as

Y^{(12)}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})=\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{\pi}\right)y_{1}+\frac{\sigma}{\pi}y_{2}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha_{n}^{(12)}\left(e^{-in\sigma^{+}}-e^{-in\sigma^{-}}\right)\,

(3.206)

and it is useful to write (with
\Delta y=y_{2}-y_{1}
being the distance between the two branes)

\left(1-\frac{\sigma}{\pi}\right)y_{1}+\frac{\sigma}{\pi}y_{2}=y_{1}+\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi}(\sigma^{+}-\sigma^{-})\,.

(3.207)

If we define the zero-mode oscillator as

\alpha_{0}^{(12)}=\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\,,

(3.208)

we can evaluate

\displaystyle\partial_{+}Y^{(12)}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\alpha_{n}^{(12)}e^{-in\sigma^{+}}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n}^{(12)}e^{-in\sigma^{+}}\,,

(3.209a)

\displaystyle\partial_{-}Y^{(12)}(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi}-\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\alpha_{n}^{(12)}e^{-in\sigma^{-}}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n}^{(12)}e^{-in\sigma^{+}}\,.

(3.209b)

We can then build the stress-energy tensor in the
y
direction (in the other directions the Dirichlet conditions are the same for both the endpoints of the stretched string) as follows:

T_{\pm\pm}^{(y)}=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\partial_{\pm}Y\partial_{\pm}Y\,.

(3.210)

We are now ready to study the spectrum of these stretched strings.
The mass formula (setting
a=1
) is

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}

\displaystyle=-1+N^{\text{(tot)}}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi}\right)^{2}\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}=-1+N^{\text{(tot)}}+\left(\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\,.

(3.211)

As
\Delta y
increases,
m^{2}
grows. Therefore the energy of the stretched string increases with the separation of the two
D
-branes.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N=0

At the level
N=0
we have a scalar particle

t^{(12)}(P)\ket{0,P}\,,

(3.212)

whose mass is given by

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=-1+\left(\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\,.

(3.213)

Therefore, if the two
D(p)
-branes are at a distance
\Delta y
such that

-1+\left(\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\geq 0\,,

(3.214)

the tachyon is not a tachyon anymore.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N=1

At the level
N=1
we have

\Big(W_{\mu}^{(12)}(P)\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}+\phi_{i}^{(12)}\alpha_{-1}^{i}\Big)\ket{0,P}

(3.215)

whose mass is given by

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=\left(\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\,.

(3.216)

Therefore we have a massive vector
W_{\mu}^{(12)}
and
D-p-1
massive scalars
\phi_{i}^{(12)}
.
If we now consider the Virasoro constraints
L_{1}=0
and
L_{0}=1
, we find the EOMs for the massive vector field (Proca’s equations) and for the massive scalars (massive Klein-Gordon equations):

\begin{cases}P^{\mu}W_{\mu}^{(12)}(P)=0\\
\left(\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}+\left(\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\right)W_{\mu}^{(12)}=0\end{cases}\,,

(3.217)

\left(\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}+\left(\frac{\Delta y}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\right)\phi_{i}^{(12)}(P)=0\,.

(3.218)

The
L_{1}
constraint is subtle for
i=y
(the direction along which we have displaced the
D(p)
-branes).
It is not difficult to see that

L_{1}\phi_{y}^{(12)}(P)\alpha_{-1}^{y}\ket{0,P}=\phi_{y}^{(12)}(\alpha_{0}^{y}\alpha_{1}^{y})\alpha_{-1}^{y}\ket{0,P}\,\sim(\Delta y)\,\phi_{y}^{(12)}(P)\ket{0,P}\neq 0.

(3.219)

Therefore if
\Delta y\neq 0
the field
\phi_{y}^{(12)}(P)
is not physical and thus disappears from the spectrum.

Everything we said so far for the
(12)
string (stretched between the
D(p)_{1}
and the
D(p)_{2}
-brane) holds in the same way for the
(21)
string (stretched between the
D(p)_{2}
and the
D(p)_{1}
-brane).

The generic field in such a
D(p)-D(p)
system can be represented as a
2\times 2
matrix: on the diagonal we insert the strings having their endpoints both on the same
D(p)
-brane (namely the
(11)
and the
(22)
strings), while off the diagonal we locate the strings stretched between the two branes (namely the
(12)
and the
(21)
strings). The scalar fields’ matrix is (for
\Delta y\neq 0
)

\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{i\neq y}^{(11)}&\phi_{i\neq y}^{(12)}\\
\\
\phi_{i\neq y}^{(21)}&\phi_{i\neq y}^{(22)}\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{y}^{(11)}&0\\
\\
0&\phi_{y}^{(22)}\end{pmatrix}\,,\quad(\Delta y\neq 0)\,.

(3.220)

Notice that there are not the
\phi_{y}^{(12)}
and
\phi_{y}^{(21)}
fluctuations as they cannot be physical when
\Delta y\neq 0
.
The vector fields’ matrix is

\begin{pmatrix}A_{\mu}^{(11)}&W_{\mu}^{(12)}\\
W_{\mu}^{(21)}&A_{\mu}^{(22)}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(3.221)

These matrices are called
Chan-Paton matrices
.
Focusing on the latter, we can recall eq. (
3.216
) and notice that, when the two
D(p)
-branes are coincident (namely
\Delta y=0
), all the vector fields are massless.
This means that

\underbrace{U(1)\times U(1)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$A_{\mu}$ massless}\\
\text{$W_{\mu}$ massive}\end{subarray}}\quad\xrightarrow{\,\,\Delta y=0\,\,}\quad\!\!\!\underbrace{U(2)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$A_{\mu}$ massless}\\
\text{$W_{\mu}$ massless}\end{subarray}}\,.

(3.222)

Notice that in the
\Delta y\to 0
limit the number of degrees of freedom is conserved: at finite separation we have two massive vector fields (
d-1
degrees of freedom) which at vanishing separation become two massless vector fields (
d-2
degrees of freedom). However in the same limit the two scalars
\phi_{y}^{12}
and
\phi_{y}^{21}
become physical as the
L_{1}
constraint is now identically satisfied. Therefore a Higgs-like mechanism takes place by separating the two
D
-branes: the gauge symmetry
U(2)
is broken to
U(1)\times U(1)
and two Goldstone modes (
\phi_{y}^{12}
and
\phi_{y}^{21}
) are ‘eaten’ by the broken gauge fields that acquire mass. This is all very nice and consistent!

If we have
M
coincident
D(p)
-branes, then we get
M^{2}
massless gauge bosons giving rise to a non-abelian
U(M)
gauge theory. This is not fully justified from the spectrum, but we will verify later that these massless vectors will indeed interact precisely as in a non-abelian Yang-Mills theory.

3.3
BRST Quantization of the String

It is now time to introduce the BRST formalism. We will follow the steps as in section
2.2
and we start by defining the Polyakov path integral as

Z=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}{\mathcal{G}}}\int\mathcal{D}X^{\mu}\mathcal{D}h_{\alpha\beta}e^{iS[X,h]}\,,

(3.223)

where
S[h,X]
is the already introduced Polyakov action

S[h,X]=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\mathrm{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\,\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\,.

(3.224)

To simplify out the volume of the gauge group we must fix the gauge symmetries, which are generated by the following two infinitesimal transformations:

1.

Diffeomorphisms:
\delta_{D}X^{\mu}=\xi^{\rho}\partial_{\rho}X^{\mu};\quad\delta_{D}h_{\alpha\beta}=\nabla_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}+\nabla_{\beta}\xi_{\alpha},

2.

Weyl transformations:
\delta_{\mathrm{W}}h_{\alpha\beta}=\omega h_{\alpha\beta},

where we have defined the infinitesimal group parameters
\xi^{\rho}(\sigma,\tau),\ \omega(\sigma,\tau)
, and the covariant derivative

\nabla_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}-\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}\xi_{\gamma}\,,

(3.225)

with the Christoffel symbols

\Gamma^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{2}h^{\gamma\sigma}\left(\partial_{\alpha}h_{\sigma\beta}+\partial_{\beta}h_{\sigma\alpha}-\partial_{\sigma}h_{\alpha\beta}\right)\,.

(3.226)

The idea to fix these gauge redundancies is to fix the metric to a fiducial value. To do this we will assume that any metric
h
can be obtained by making a gauge transformation to the fiducial metric
\hat{h}
. I.e. we will assume that

h=\hat{h}^{g},

for some gauge transformation
g
. In particular the fiducial value
h=\hat{h}
corresponds to
g=1
.
This essentially means that we consider
all
degrees of freedom of the metric to be pure gauge. To be precise, this is true if the worldsheet has the topology of a sphere (or a disk). But on a generic surface this is true only locally and indeed we will see that there are in general
metric moduli
which cannot be changed by diffeomorphisms. When this happens the functional integration over the metric will not completely cancel against the volume of the gauge group but it will leave out a
finite dimensional
integral on the metric moduli. While this is a major ingredient for the construction of strings’ amplitudes, it is not going to affect the construction of the BRST charge and the spectrum so we will postpone this (however important) subtlety for later on.
That said, our gauge fixing condition will be represented by the functional condition

F(h)=h-\hat{h}=0\,\,\rightarrow\,\,h=\hat{h}\,.

(3.227)

We can now write the identity

1=\int\mathcal{D}h\,\delta(h-\hat{h})=\int\mathcal{D}\hat{h}^{g}\,\delta(\hat{h}^{g}-\hat{h})=\int\mathcal{D}g\,\delta(\hat{h}^{g}-\hat{h})\mathrm{det}\frac{\delta\hat{h}^{g}}{\delta g}\bigg|_{g=1}\,,

(3.228)

where
g
represents the gauge group element chosen to parametrize the gauge orbit of
\hat{h}^{g}
so that
\hat{h}^{1}=\hat{h}
.
Using this resolution of the identity in
3.223
we can factorize the gauge volume and obtain the gauge fixed Polyakov path integral

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}{\mathcal{G}}}\int\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}h\mathcal{D}g\,\delta(\hat{h}^{g}-\hat{h})\mathrm{det}\frac{\delta\hat{h}^{g}}{\delta g}\bigg|_{g=1}e^{iS[X,h]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}{\mathcal{G}}}\int\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}\hat{h}^{g}\mathcal{D}g\,\delta(\hat{h}^{g}-\hat{h})\mathrm{det}\frac{\delta\hat{h}^{g}}{\delta g}\bigg|_{g=1}e^{iS[X,\hat{h}^{g}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}{\mathcal{G}}}\int\mathcal{D}X^{g}\mathcal{D}\hat{h}^{g}\mathcal{D}g\,\delta(\hat{h}^{g}-\hat{h})\mathrm{det}\frac{\delta\hat{h}^{g}}{\delta g}\bigg|_{g=1}e^{iS[X^{g},\hat{h}^{g}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}{\mathcal{G}}}\int\mathcal{D}X^{g}\mathcal{D}\hat{h}^{g}\mathcal{D}g\,\delta(\hat{h}^{g}-\hat{h})\mathrm{det}\frac{\delta\hat{h}^{g}}{\delta g}\bigg|_{g=1}e^{iS[X,\hat{h}]}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}{\mathcal{G}}}\int\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}g\,\,\,\mathrm{det}\frac{\delta\hat{h}^{g}}{\delta g}\bigg|_{g=1}e^{iS[X,\hat{h}]}

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}X\,\,\mathrm{det}\frac{\delta\hat{h}^{g}}{\delta g}\bigg|_{g=1}e^{iS[X,\hat{h}]}\,,

(3.229)

where in the second line we have written
h
as
\hat{h}^{g}
, in the third line we have changed the dummy integration variable
X
to
X^{g}
, in the fourth line we have used the gauge invariance of the action, in the fifth line we have used the gauge invariance of the measure
\mathcal{D}X
and we have eliminated the delta function. Finally in the last line we have simplified the volume of the gauge group with
\int\mathcal{D}g
thanks to the gauge invariance of the determinant.

By formally rewriting the determinant as a Berezin integral (Fadeev-Popov trick) we obtain the FP action

\mathrm{det}\frac{\delta h^{g}}{\delta g}\bigg|_{g=1}=\int\mathcal{D}b_{\alpha\beta}\mathcal{D}\lambda\mathcal{D}c^{\rho}\,\mathrm{exp}\left[\frac{1}{4\pi}\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{-\hat{h}}\,b_{\alpha\beta}\frac{\delta h^{\alpha\beta}}{\delta g^{x}}\bigg|_{g=1}c^{x}\right]\,,

(3.230)

where we have defined
c^{x}=(\lambda,c^{\rho})
with
\lambda
and
c^{\rho}
respectively defined as the ghost fields related to the Weyl and the diffeos generators.
It is usual to explicitly write the variation of the metric as

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{tot}}h_{\alpha\beta}=\delta_{\mathrm{D}}h_{\alpha\beta}+\delta_{\mathrm{W}}h_{\alpha\beta}=\omega h_{\alpha\beta}+\nabla_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}+\nabla_{\beta}\xi_{\alpha}\,=\Omega h_{\alpha\beta}+2(P_{1}\xi)_{\alpha\beta},

(3.231)

where we have defined

\Omega\equiv\omega+\nabla\cdot\xi

(3.232)

and

(P_{1}\xi)_{\alpha\beta}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta}+\nabla_{\beta}\xi_{\alpha}-h_{\alpha\beta}\nabla\cdot\xi\right),

(3.233)

which is a map from vector fields (one index up) to traceless quadratic differentials (two lower symmetric indices with vanishing trace).
With these definitions we can easily write down the ghost lagrangian by substituting the gauge parameters
(\omega,\,\xi^{\alpha})
with the corresponding Weyl and Diff ghosts
(\lambda,\,c^{\alpha})
in
\delta h^{\alpha\beta}
and then contracting with the antighost
b_{\alpha\beta}
:

\displaystyle\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{-\hat{h}}\,b_{\alpha\beta}\frac{\delta h^{\alpha\beta}}{\delta g^{x}}\bigg|_{g=1}c^{x}

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{-\hat{h}}\,b_{\alpha\beta}\left[\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\nabla\cdot c\right)+(2P_{1}c)^{\alpha\beta}\right]\,.

(3.234)

Using this result we can integrate out the ghost
\lambda
in the path integral which imposes that
b_{\alpha\beta}\hat{h}^{\alpha\beta}=0
:
