nearby it, since this is the only contribution to the contour integral.
Such an information is contained in the Operator Product Expansion (OPE), which is the topic we will discuss next.

4.1.4
Operator product expansion

Let us take two fields
\phi_{i}(z_{i})
and
\phi_{j}(z_{j})
. Then it is possible to write their product as a series expansion of an infinite number of local fields:

\phi_{i}(z_{i})\phi_{j}(z_{j})=\sum_{k}{C_{ij}}^{k}(z_{i}-z_{j})\,\phi_{k}(z_{j})\,,

(4.48)

where
{C_{ij}}^{k}(z_{1}-z_{2})
are the so-called
structure functions
. This series is commonly called
operator product expansion
(OPE).

If we consider fields having a definite weight, their OPE can be further specified as

\phi_{i}^{(h_{i})}(z_{i})\phi_{j}^{(h_{j})}(z_{j})=\sum_{k}{C_{ij}}^{k}\phi_{k}^{(h_{k})}(z_{j})\frac{1}{(z_{i}-z_{j})^{h_{i}+h_{j}-h_{k}}}\,,

(4.49)

where
{C_{ij}}^{k}
are now called
structure constants
. The OPE can be performed in any QFT, but in a CFT this power series expansion has
infinite
radius of convergence, because a finite radius would result in a scale, which would break conformal invariance.

We now want to determine the OPE of a primary field
\phi^{(h)}(z)
with the stress-energy tensor
T
. We can parametrize our ignorance by writing

T(\tilde{z})\phi^{(h)}(z)=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{[T\phi]_{k}(z)}{(\tilde{z}-z)^{k}}+\text{regular terms $(k<1)$}\,,

(4.50)

where
[T\phi]_{k}(z)
are unknown operators. Recalling eq. (
4.33
), we have that

\delta_{\epsilon}\phi(z)=-\oint_{z}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\tilde{z})T(\tilde{z})\phi(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm must}}{{=}}-\left(\epsilon\partial\phi(z)+h(\partial\epsilon)\phi(z)\right).

(4.51)

Using (
4.50
) and evaluating the residues we can easily see that

•

\epsilon(\partial\phi)
fixes the single pole:

k=1\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ [T\phi]_{1}(z)=\partial\phi(z)\,;

(4.52)

•

h(\partial\epsilon)\phi
fixes the double pole:

k=2\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ [T\phi]_{2}(z)=h\phi(z)\,;

(4.53)

•

then we have no higher poles:

k>2\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ [T\phi]_{k>2}(z)=0\,.

(4.54)

Therefore the final result is

T(\tilde{z})\phi^{(h)}(z)=\frac{h\phi(z)}{(\tilde{z}-z)^{2}}+\frac{\partial\phi(z)}{(\tilde{z}-z)}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.55)

This OPE can be seen as an alternative definition of a primary field of weight
h
.

We now want to evaluate the OPE
T(z_{1})T(z_{2})
. We shall recall eqs. (
4.38
) and (
4.46
) and then determine the
TT
OPE in such a way as to enforce

\displaystyle[\delta_{\epsilon_{1}},\delta_{\epsilon_{2}}]\phi(0)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\epsilon_{1}}\delta_{\epsilon_{2}}\phi(0)-\delta_{\epsilon_{2}}\delta_{\epsilon_{1}}\phi(0)

\displaystyle=[T_{\epsilon_{1}},[T_{\epsilon_{2}},\phi(0)]]-[T_{\epsilon_{2}},[T_{\epsilon_{1}},\phi(0)]]

\displaystyle=\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\oint_{z_{2}}\frac{dz_{1}}{2\pi i}\epsilon_{1}(z_{1})\epsilon_{2}(z_{2})T(z_{1})T(z_{2})\phi(0)

(4.56)

\displaystyle=\delta_{\epsilon_{1}\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\partial}\epsilon_{2}}\phi(0)

\displaystyle=-\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\left(\epsilon_{1}\partial\epsilon_{2}(z_{2})-\epsilon_{2}\partial\epsilon_{1}(z_{2})\right)T(z_{2})\phi(0)

(the third step is graphically represented in fig.
4.4
).

Figure 4.4:
Representation of the operation
\left(\delta_{\epsilon_{1}}\delta_{\epsilon_{2}}-\delta_{\epsilon_{2}}\delta_{\epsilon_{1}}\right)\phi(0)
inside equation (
4.56
).

We can then parametrize our ignorance by writing the generic OPE

T(z_{1})T(z_{2})=\sum_{k\geq 1}\frac{[TT]_{k}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{k}}+\text{regular terms $(k<1)$}\,,

(4.57)

that has to reproduce eq. (
4.56
). Evaluating the contour integral around
z_{2}
in the third line of eq. (
4.56
) we get

\sum_{k\geq 1}\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\epsilon_{2}(z_{2})\frac{\partial^{k-1}\epsilon_{1}(z_{2})}{(k-1)!}[TT]_{k}(z_{2})\phi(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm must}}{{=}}-\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\left(\epsilon_{1}\partial\epsilon_{2}(z_{2})-\epsilon_{2}\partial\epsilon_{1}(z_{2})\right)T(z_{2})\phi(0)\,.

(4.58)

We then proceed as follows.

•

At
k=1
we must have a weight three operator, which is naturally
\partial T

[TT]_{1}=\alpha\partial T\,.

(4.59)

Now we integrate by part in the contour integral

\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\epsilon_{2}\epsilon_{1}\alpha\partial T(z_{2})\phi(0)\overset{\text{IBP}}{=}-\alpha\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\partial(\epsilon_{2}\epsilon_{1})T(z_{2})\phi(0)\,,

(4.60)

where there are obviously no boundary terms, since we are integrating on a cycle.

•

At
k=2
we must have a weight two operator and the obvious choice is
T
itself

[TT]_{2}=\beta T\,.

(4.61)

This gives

\beta\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\epsilon_{2}\partial(\epsilon_{1})T(z_{2})\phi(0)\,.

(4.62)

•

At
k=3
we would have a weight 1 operator, but there is no such operator in the game. Therefore we set

[TT]_{3}=0\,.

(4.63)

•

At
k=4
the possible operator should have vanishing weight. It can be the identity operator which, just like
T
, is present in any CFT. It can appear with a generic coefficient that we cannot fix unless we focus on a concrete CFT:

[TT]_{4}=\text{constant}\equiv\frac{c}{2}\,,

(4.64)

The contribution of this term is vanishing

\frac{c}{2}\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\,\frac{1}{6}\epsilon_{2}\partial^{3}\epsilon_{1}(z_{2})\,\phi(0)=0\,,

(4.65)

because
\epsilon(z)
is holomorphic at the origin. Therefore this quartic pole in the
TT
OPE is compatible with
[\delta_{\epsilon_{1}},\delta_{\epsilon_{2}}]=\delta_{\epsilon_{1}\overset{\leftrightarrow}{\partial}\epsilon_{2}}
for any value of the
central charge

c
.

After a proper fixing of
\alpha
and
\beta
, we get the final result, which is

T(z_{1})T(z_{2})=\frac{c}{2}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{4}}+\frac{2T(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial T(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.66)

Exercise 4.1.4

Show that the correct fixing produces
\alpha=1
and
\beta=2
, as written in (
4.66
).

4.1.5
Virasoro algebra

As said above (see eq. (
4.44
)), the stress-energy tensor decomposes in terms of
Virasoro operators

L_{n}
which can be written as

L_{n}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n+1}T(z)\,.

(4.67)

Their algebra is easily obtained by using the
TT
OPE in the contour integral

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\oint_{0}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\oint_{\tilde{z}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\tilde{z}^{m+1}z^{n+1}T(\tilde{z})T(z)

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,

(4.68)

which is the Virasoro algebra.

Exercise 4.1.5

Prove eq. (
4.68
).

If we now consider a primary field (with integer weigth
h
)
\phi^{(h)}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{n}z^{-n-h}
, we can evaluate

\left[L_{n},\phi^{(h)}(z)\right]=z^{n}\left(z\partial+(n+1)h\right)\phi^{(n)}(z)\,,

(4.69)

\left[L_{n},\phi_{m}\right]=\left(n(h-1)-m\right)\phi_{n+m}\,.

(4.70)

Exercise 4.1.6

Prove eq. (
4.69
) and eq. (
4.70
).

Moreover we have that

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}T(z)

\displaystyle=-\left[T_{\epsilon},T(z)\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{c}{12}\partial^{3}\epsilon(z)-2(\partial\epsilon)T(z)-\epsilon\partial T(z)\,.

(4.71)

Due to the anomalous term
\frac{c}{12}\partial^{3}\epsilon(z)
, the stress-energy tensor
T
is not a primary field.

Exercise 4.1.7

Prove eq. (
4.71
).

4.1.6
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
subgroup

There are infinitesimal transformations
\epsilon_{*}(z)
such that
\partial^{3}\epsilon_{*}(z)=0
, namely

\epsilon_{*}(z)=\alpha+\beta z+\gamma z^{2}\,.

(4.72)

These infinitesimal transformation are generated by
L_{-1},L_{0},L_{1}
, i.e. by
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
:

\displaystyle L_{-1}\,\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\,\ -\frac{d}{dz}\qquad\text{(generates translations);}

(4.73a)

\displaystyle L_{0}\,\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\,\ -z\frac{d}{dz}\quad\,\,\,\text{(generates dilatations);}

(4.73b)

\displaystyle L_{1}\,\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\,\ -z^{2}\frac{d}{dz}\quad\,\text{(generates special conformal transformations).}

(4.73c)

These transformations can be made finite by exponentiating the genereators

\displaystyle e^{-AL_{-1}}:\,\,

\displaystyle z\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ z+A\,;

(4.74a)

\displaystyle e^{-BL_{0}}:\,\,

\displaystyle z\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ e^{B}z\,;

(4.74b)

\displaystyle e^{-CL_{1}}:\,\,

\displaystyle z\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \frac{z}{1+Cz}\,.

(4.74c)

Their composition generates the generic transformation
f
of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
:

f(z)=\frac{Az+B}{Cz+D}\,.

(4.75)

Moreover it is useful to notice that a special conformal transformation can be obtained by composing an inversion (
\mathcal{I}
) with a translation (
\mathcal{T}
) and then with an inversion again (namely
\mathcal{I}\circ\mathcal{T}\circ\mathcal{I}
):

z\,\ \overset{\mathcal{I}}{\longrightarrow}\,\ \frac{1}{z}\,\ \overset{\mathcal{T}}{\longrightarrow}\,\ \frac{1}{z}+C\,\ \overset{\mathcal{I}}{\longrightarrow}\,\ \frac{1}{\frac{1}{z}+C}=\frac{z}{1+Cz}\,.

(4.76)

When a field
\phi(z)
is primary under such a
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
transformation, it is called
quasi-primary
. Hence
T
is a quasi-primary field.

4.1.7
Hilbert space

The vacuum of our CFT
\ket{0}
is universally defined in such a way that

\lim_{z\to 0}T(z)\ket{0}=\textrm{well defined}\,.

(4.77)

Decomposing the stress-energy tensor in terms of
L_{n}
operators we have

\lim_{z\to 0}T(z)\ket{0}=\lim_{z\to 0}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}L_{n}z^{-n-2}\ket{0}=\textrm{well defined}\,.

(4.78)

In order to be well-defined in
z\to 0
, singular terms should be absent. This statement fixes the annihilating operators as follows:

L_{n}\ket{0}=0\qquad\forall n\geq-1\,.

(4.79)

Since
L_{-n}=L_{n}^{\dagger}
, we have that

\displaystyle\bra{0}L_{n}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\forall n\leq 1\,.

(4.80a)

This vacuum
\ket{0}
is therefore annihilated from the left and from the right by
(L_{-1},L_{0},L_{1})
:

\displaystyle L_{-1},L_{0},L_{1}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(4.81a)

\displaystyle\bra{0}L_{-1},L_{0},L_{1}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(4.81b)

This means that
\ket{0}
is the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum.

Given the vacuum, the rest of the Hilbert space can be built by acting on
\ket{0}
with fields placed in
z=0
, namely
\phi(0)\ket{0}
.
If
\phi
is a primary field
\phi^{(h)}
of integer weigth
h\in\mathbb{Z}
we have that the limit

\lim_{z\to 0}\phi^{(h)}(z)\ket{0}=\lim_{z\to 0}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{n}z^{-n-h}\ket{0}

(4.82)

must be well defined. This implies

\displaystyle\phi_{n}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\forall n\geq-h+1\,,

(4.83a)

\displaystyle\bra{0}\phi_{n}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\forall n\leq h-1\,.

(4.83b)

4.1.7.1
State-operator correspondence

Given a local operator
\phi(z)
(i.e. a field on the worldsheet), it can be evaluated in
z=0
and then applied to the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum
\ket{0}
, thus identifying the state
\ket{\phi}=\phi(0)\ket{0}
. This is commonly called
state-operator correspondence
:

\phi(z)\,\ \longleftrightarrow\,\ \ket{\phi}=\phi(0)\ket{0}\,.

(4.84)

If we then consider a primary field
\phi^{(h)}(z)
, we can write its state-operator correspondence as

\phi^{(h)}(z)\,\ \longleftrightarrow\,\ \ket{\phi^{(h)}}=\phi^{(h)}(0)\ket{0}=\phi_{-h}\ket{0}\,.

(4.85)

We can also notice that the
n
-th derivative of a primary field
\phi^{(h)}(z)
increases its weight of
n
:

\displaystyle\phi^{(h)}(0)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\phi_{-h}\ket{0}\,,

(4.86a)

\displaystyle\partial\phi^{(h)}(0)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\phi_{-h-1}\ket{0}\,,

(4.86b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}\phi^{(h)}(0)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\phi_{-h-2}\ket{0}\,,

(4.86c)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}\partial^{n}\phi^{(h)}(0)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\phi_{-h-n}\ket{0}\,.

(4.86d)

Moreover it is useful to remember that

L_{0}\phi_{n}\ket{0}=-n\phi_{n}\ket{0}\,.

(4.87)

Finally, we can take a field
\phi
inserted at
z=0
and translate it to a generic
z
through the adjoint action of the linear operator that generates translations (see eq. (
4.74a
)):

\phi(z)=e^{zL_{-1}}\phi(0)e^{-zL_{-1}}\,.

(4.88)

We therefore get

\displaystyle e^{zL_{-1}}\ket{\phi}

\displaystyle=e^{zL_{-1}}\phi(0)\ket{0}=e^{zL_{-1}}\phi(0)e^{-zL_{-1}}e^{zL_{-1}}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\phi(z)e^{zL_{-1}}\ket{0}=\phi(z)\ket{0}\,,

(4.89)

where we have used that the vacuum is annihilated by
L_{-1}
. This gives a concrete way to extract the operator
\phi(z)
from the state
\ket{\phi}
and complete the proof of the operator-state correspondence.

4.1.7.2
BPZ conjugation

The BPZ conjugation is a map such that
\text{BPZ}:\,\ket{\cdot}\longrightarrow\bra{\cdot}
. We are used to the hermitian conjugation, but the BPZ conjugation is slightly different, since it works as follows:

\text{BPZ}:\,\phi^{(h)}(z)\,\longrightarrow\,\mathcal{I}\circ\phi^{(h)}(z)\,,

(4.90)

recalling that
f\circ\phi^{(h)}(z)=\phi^{{}^{\prime}(h)}(z)=\left[f^{\prime}(z)\right]^{h}\phi^{(h)}\left(f(z)\right)
and where we have introduced the inversion map

\mathcal{I}(z)=-\frac{1}{z}\,,

(4.91)

which maps
0
to
\infty
and viceversa.
Therefore we have that

\text{BPZ}\left(\,\ket{\phi}\,\right)=\bra{0}\mathcal{I}\circ\phi(0)=\bra{0}\phi\left(-\frac{1}{z}\right)\frac{1}{z^{2h}}\Big|_{z=0}\,.

(4.92)

this allows us to take a primary field
\phi^{(h)}
and write

\ket{\phi^{(h)}}=\phi_{-h}\ket{0}\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ \text{BPZ}\left(\,\bra{\phi^{(h)}}\,\right)=\bra{0}\phi_{h}(-1)^{2h}

(4.93)

and also

\text{BPZ}\left(\phi_{n}\right)=(-1)^{n-h}\phi_{-n}\,.

(4.94)

Exercise 4.1.8

Prove eq. (
4.93
) and eq. (
4.94
).

Given these definitions, we can also introduce the BPZ scalar product:

\langle\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\rangle=\innerproduct{\phi_1}{\phi_2}=\langle\phi_{1}(\infty)\phi_{2}(0)\rangle=\langle\mathcal{I}\circ\phi_{1}(0)\phi_{2}(0)\rangle\,.

(4.95)

As a final remark, the simultaneous presence of BPZ and hermitian conjugation defines a reality condition:

\displaystyle(\,\ket{\phi}\,)^{\dagger}=\bra{\phi}=\text{BPZ}(\,\ket{\phi}\,)

\displaystyle\,\ \Longleftrightarrow\,\ \text{$\ket{\phi}$ is real,}

(4.96a)

\displaystyle(\,\ket{\phi}\,)^{\dagger}=-\bra{\phi}=-\text{BPZ}(\,\ket{\phi}\,)

\displaystyle\,\ \Longleftrightarrow\,\ \text{$\ket{\phi}$ is imaginary.}

(4.96b)

Some examples are

•

\alpha_{-1}\ket{0}=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\partial X(0)\ket{0}
is real;

•

c_{1}\ket{0}
is real;

•

c_{0}\ket{0}
is imaginary.

4.1.7.3
Active conformal transformation

Let us recall the action of a conformal transformation
f(z)
over operators (graphically represented in fig.
4.5
)

f\circ\phi^{(h)}(z)=\left[f^{\prime}(z)\right]^{h}\phi^{(h)}\left(f(z)\right)\,.

(4.97)

Figure 4.5:
Conformal transformation, under the action of the map
f
, of a piece of worldsheet
\Sigma
, where a field
\phi^{(h)}(z)
is defined.

We can write it (thanks to the state-operator correspondence) as a linear transformation acting on the states of the Hilbert space.
Thus, let us express the adjoint action of the transformation on the field
\phi(z)
as

f\circ\phi(z)=U_{f}\phi(z)U_{f}^{-1}\,,

(4.98)

where we used the definition

U_{f}=e^{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}v_{n}L_{-n}}\,,

(4.99)

where

v(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}v_{n}z^{n+1}

(4.100)

is the vector field (that we previously called
\epsilon(z)
) generating the finite transformation

e^{v(z)\partial_{z}}z=f(z)\,.

(4.101)

4.1.8
Primary states and descendants

A primary state
\phi^{(h)}(z)
has the properties

\displaystyle L_{0}\ket{\phi^{(h)}}

\displaystyle=h\ket{\phi^{(h)}}\,,

(4.102a)

\displaystyle L_{n>0}\ket{\phi^{(h)}}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(4.102b)

For
h=1
these are the OCQ Virasoro constraints. We thus see that physical states are primaries of
h=1
.

Exercise 4.1.9

Prove eq. (
4.102a
) and eq. (
4.102b
).

This is like the Cartan decomposition of an algebra: given the Cartan generators (maximal set of mutually commuting generators), we can define primary states that are annihilated by half of the remaining operators (in this case lowering operators). Moreover, the action of the remaining raising operators generates the
descendant states
from the primary states, thus creating a complete irreducible representation.

In our case we only have one Cartan generator, namely
L_{0}
, and primary fields are the states with minimal weight in the representation. The lowering (or annihilation) operators are
L_{n>0}
, while the raising (or creation) operators are
L_{n<0}
. In this case the descendants are obtained by acting with Virasoro raising operators on a primary state:

L_{-k_{1}}\dots L_{-k_{n-1}}L_{-k_{n}}\ket{\phi^{(h)}}\quad k_{i}>0\,\,\forall i\,.

(4.103)

We then have that

L_{0}\left(L_{-k_{1}}\dots L_{-k_{n}}\ket{\phi^{(h)}}\right)=\left(h+\sum_{i}k_{i}\right)\left(L_{-k_{1}}\dots L_{-k_{n}}\ket{\phi^{(h)}}\right)\,.

(4.104)

We thus see that for every primary state we can build an infinite tower of descendants with increasing conformal weights. This infinite tower is called Verma module.

We can rephrase the terminology we have used to classify states in the OCQ, which is now reinterpreted as the CFT of the
X^{\mu}(z,\bar{z})
fields.

•

A physical state is a primary state of conformal weight 1.

•

A spurious state is a descendant of some primary.

•

A null state is a descendant that is also a primary. In generic CFT’s we are particularly interested in null states of any given possible conformal weight and not just weight=1.

The study of null states is a very important subject in CFT and it opens the door to the so-called
minimal models
. These are CFT’s characterized by having only a finite number of primary fields in their spectrum (once the spectrum is quotiented-out by the mull states). They are strongly interacting two-dimensional QFT’s that can be fully solved by simply exploiting the power of conformal symmetry. Although this is a very important subject in CFT, it is not needed in this first exposition of string theory and so we direct the interested student to the classical yellow book
Conformal Field Theory
by Di Francesco, Mathieu and Senechal.

4.1.9
Correlation functions

Let us now consider the
n
-point correlation function

\langle\phi_{1}(z_{1})\dots\phi_{n}(z_{n})\rangle=\bra{0}R\left[\phi_{1}(z_{1})\dots\phi_{n}(z_{n})\right]\ket{0}\,,

(4.105)

which is the same as a Green function of
n
fields in quantum field theory (from now on the radial ordering
R[\dots]
will not be explicitly written anymore).

Recalling the fact that, given a
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
transformation
f
,
\ket{0}
is
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant (i.e.
\bra{0}U_{f}^{-1}=\bra{0}
and
U_{f}\ket{0}=\ket{0}
), we can easily see that every possible correlation function is invariant under the action of such a conformal transformation
f\in SL(2,\mathbb{C})
:

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{1}(z_{1})\dots\phi_{n}(z_{n})\rangle

\displaystyle=\bra{0}\phi_{1}(z_{1})\dots\phi_{n}(z_{n})\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\bra{0}U_{f}^{-1}U_{f}\phi_{1}(z_{1})U_{f}^{-1}U_{f}\dots U_{f}^{-1}U_{f}\phi_{n}(z_{n})U_{f}^{-1}U_{f}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\bra{0}U_{f}\phi_{1}(z_{1})U_{f}^{-1}U_{f}\dots U_{f}^{-1}U_{f}\phi_{n}(z_{n})U_{f}^{-1}\ket{0}

\displaystyle=\bra{0}f\circ\phi_{1}(z_{1})\dots f\circ\phi_{n}(z_{n})\ket{0}\,.

(4.106)

We may now focus on the simple cases of 1, 2 and 3-point functions.

4.1.9.1
1-point functions

Let us consider a primary state
\phi^{(h)}(z)
and evaluate

\langle\phi^{(h)}(z)\rangle=\langle\phi^{(h)}(0)\rangle=\bra{0}\ket{\phi^{(h)}}\,,

(4.107)

where the first step has been performed thanks to translational invariance of correlation functions (see eq. (
4.106
)), while the second step is the application of the state-operator correspondence.
If we now insert an
L_{0}
operator, obtaining
\bra{0}L_{0}\ket{\phi^{(h)}}
, if we let it act on the left it gives 0, while on the right it gives
h\bra{0}\ket{\phi^{(h)}}
. Therefore we can write

\langle\phi^{(h)}(z)\rangle=A\delta_{h,\,0}\,,

(4.108)

where
A
is a number. In generic unitary CFT’s there won’t be dimension zero operators except the identity, however, as we will see, in the ghost conformal field theory (which is not unitary), we will have a weight zero operator with non vanishing one-point function.

4.1.9.2
2-point functions

Let us now consider the 2-point correlation function
\langle\phi_{i}(z_{i})\phi_{j}(z_{j})\rangle
. Let us write the conformal transformation
f
such that
z_{i}\to\Lambda\to\infty
and
z_{j}\to 0
, namely

f(\xi)=\frac{\Lambda}{(z_{i}-z_{j})}(\xi-z_{j})\,.

(4.109)

Therefore we have that

\displaystyle\langle f\circ\phi_{i}(z_{i})f\circ\phi_{j}(z_{j})\rangle

\displaystyle\,\,\,=\left(\frac{\Lambda}{(z_{i}-z_{j})}\right)^{h_{i}+h_{j}}\langle\phi_{i}(\Lambda)\phi_{j}(0)\rangle

\displaystyle\,\,\,=\left(\frac{\Lambda}{(z_{i}-z_{j})}\right)^{h_{i}+h_{j}}\underbrace{\langle\phi_{i}(\Lambda)\phi_{j}(0)\rangle\cdot\frac{\Lambda^{-2h_{i}}}{\Lambda^{-2h_{i}}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{for $\Lambda\to\infty$ it is the}\\[0.5pt]
\text{BPZ scalar product}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\overset{\Lambda\to\infty}{=}\bra{\phi_i}\ket{\phi_j}\left(\frac{1}{(z_{i}-z_{j})}\right)^{h_{i}+h_{j}}\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\Lambda^{h_{j}-h_{i}}\,.

(4.110)

Therefore, since this correlation function has to be finite, the only possibility is that
h_{i}=h_{j}
, which implies that
\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}\Lambda^{h_{j}-h_{i}}=1
.
We can then write the correct result as

\langle\phi_{i}(z_{i})\phi_{j}(z_{j})\rangle=\frac{G_{ij}}{(z_{i}-z_{j})^{2h}}\delta_{h_{i},\,h_{j}}\,,

(4.111)

where

G_{ij}=\bra{\phi_i}\ket{\phi_j}\,.

(4.112)

4.1.9.3
3-point functions

Let us finally consider the 3-point correlation function
\langle\phi_{i}^{(h_{i})}(z_{i})\phi_{j}^{(h_{j})}(z_{j})\phi_{k}^{(h_{k})}(z_{k})\rangle
. Let us write the conformal transformation
f
such that
z_{i}\to\infty
,
z_{j}\to 1
and
z_{k}\to 0
, namely

f(\xi)=\frac{(z_{j}-z_{i})}{(z_{j}-z_{k})}\frac{(\xi-z_{k})}{(\xi-z_{i})}\,,

(4.113)

which is completely fixed.
One can show that

\langle\phi_{i}^{(h_{i})}(z_{i})\phi_{j}^{(h_{j})}(z_{j})\phi_{k}^{(h_{k})}(z_{k})\rangle=\frac{C_{ijk}}{(z_{i}-z_{j})^{h_{i}+h_{j}-h_{k}}(z_{j}-z_{k})^{-h_{i}+h_{j}+h_{k}}(z_{i}-z_{k})^{h_{i}-h_{j}+h_{k}}}\,,

(4.114)

where

C_{ijk}=\bra{\phi_i}\phi_{j}(1)\ket{\phi_k}=\langle\mathcal{I}\circ\phi_{i}(0)\phi_{j}(1)\phi_{k}(0)\rangle\,.

(4.115)

Exercise 4.1.10

Prove eq. (
4.114
).

Higher point correlation functions are also constrained by
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
but not completely fixed.

4.2
Closed String CFT

As we saw in the previous chapter, the worldsheet of a closed string is a cylindrical surface embedded in the targepoint.
We can therefore study the physics of the closed string by a proper two-dimensional CFT built on the WS (of coordinate
w
), which can be mapped on the complex plane through the function
z=e^{w}
(see fig.
4.1
). We can split the matter and the ghost sectors and study the matter
X
-CFT and the ghost
(b,c)
-CFT separately.

4.2.1
Matter
X
-CFT

4.2.1.1
Fields and OPEs

The scalar field
X^{\mu}
is made of
D
free bosons living on the worldsheet and its indices
\mu=0,1,\dots,D-1
are global Lorentz indices. Its action on the complex plane is given by (recall eq. (
4.10
))

S_{\text{matter}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}z\,\partial X(z,\bar{z})\cdot\bar{\partial}X(z,\bar{z})\,,

(4.116)

where, as usual, we defined
\partial=\partial_{z}
and
\bar{\partial}=\partial_{\bar{z}}
.

Its EOM (being in the closed string case, recall eq. (
3.56
)) are

\partial\bar{\partial}X^{\mu}=0\,.

(4.117)

Therefore in these complex coordinates we can write

X^{\mu}(z,\bar{z})=X^{\mu}(z)+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mu}(\bar{z})\,,

(4.118)

with

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{0}^{\mu}+c^{\mu}}{2}-i\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}P^{\mu}\log z+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha^{\mu}_{n}z^{-n}\,,

(4.119a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mu}(\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{0}^{\mu}-c^{\mu}}{2}-i\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}P^{\mu}\log\bar{z}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha^{\mu}_{n}\bar{z}^{-n}\,,

(4.119b)

where, as usual,
X^{\mu}(z)
is the holomorphic part of
X^{\mu}(z,\bar{z})
, while
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mu}(\bar{z})
is its anti-holomorphic part. In the center of mass momentum term we now have a logarithmic dependence on
z
because
w=\log z
.

Given these fields, we then define the
current
operator (recall eq. (
4.28a
))

\displaystyle j^{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\partial X^{\mu}(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n}^{\mu}z^{-n-1}\,,

(4.120a)

\displaystyle\bar{j}^{\mu}(\bar{z})

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bar{\partial}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muX\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\mu}(\bar{z})=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}\bar{z}^{-n-1}\,.

(4.120b)

As we will see, it is a primary field of weight 1.

If we focus on its holomorphic part (namely on
j^{\mu}(z)
) along a specific direction (i.e. omitting the Lorentz index), we can evaluate the 2-point function:

\displaystyle\langle j(z_{1})j(z_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}z_{1}^{-n-1}z_{2}^{-m-1}\bra{0}\alpha_{n}\alpha_{m}\ket{0}=\sum_{n\geq 0}nz_{1}^{-n-1}z_{2}^{n-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{z_{1}z_{2}}\sum_{n\geq 0}n\left(\frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}\right)^{n}=\frac{1}{z_{1}z_{2}}\frac{\frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}}{\left(1-\frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\,,

(4.121)

where we choosed
|z_{1}|>|z_{2}|
for the radial ordering and recalled that for the closed string
\alpha_{0}=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}/2}P
.

If we then consider
D
free bosons (one for each direction of the target space), we get

\langle j^{\mu}(z_{1})j^{\nu}(z_{2})\rangle=\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\,.

(4.122)

Let us now evaluate the OPE
j(z_{1})j(z_{2})
using the oscillator algebra. We can consider

\alpha_{n}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n}j(z)

(4.123)

and

\left[\alpha_{n},\alpha_{m}\right]=n\delta_{n+m,\,0}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz_{1}}{2\pi i}\oint_{z_{1}}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}z_{1}^{n}z_{2}^{m}j(z_{1})j(z_{2})\,.

(4.124)

We can parametrize the short-distance behaviour as

j(z_{1})j(z_{2})=\frac{A}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{B}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.125)

It can be shown that, in order to find the oscillator algebra (
4.124
), we have to fix
A=1
and
B=0
.
Therefore the final result is

j(z_{1})j(z_{2})=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.126)

Exercise 4.2.1

Prove that we have to fix
A=1
and
B=0
inside (
4.125
) in order to find the oscillator algebra (
4.124
).

We shall now introduce a useful notation. Given the OPE
A(z_{1})B(z_{2})
, we can divide it into its singular part (also called
contraction
), denoted as

\underbracket{A(z_{1})B(z_{2})}\,,

(4.127)

and its regular part, denoted as

:\mathrel{A(z_{1})B(z_{2})}:\,.

(4.128)

This
:\mathrel{\,\cdots\,}:
is called
normal-ordered product
and, on the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum, it is the same as the oscillator normal ordering we have introduced in previous chapters.

From (
4.126
) we have that

\underbracket{j(z_{1})j(z_{2})}=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}

(4.129)

and therefore

\displaystyle\underbracket{\partial X(z_{1})\partial X(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\,,

(4.130a)

\displaystyle\underbracket{\partial X(z_{1})X(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\,,

(4.130b)

\displaystyle\underbracket{X(z_{1})X(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\log(z_1-z_2)\,,

(4.130c)

We observe that the scalar field
X^{\mu}
has a logarithmic OPE, which is not what we found for primary fields. This means that
X^{\mu}
is not a primary field but, on the other hand, a logarithmic field of weight zero.

The matter stress-energy tensor is given by

T^{\text{(m)}}(z)=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}:\mathrel{\partial X\partial X}:(z)=\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{jj}:(z)\,,

(4.131)

where we defined

\displaystyle:\mathrel{AB}:(z_{1})

\displaystyle=\lim_{z_{2}\to z_{1}}\left(A(z_{1})B(z_{2})-\underbracket{A(z_{1})B(z_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle=\oint_{z_{1}}\frac{dz_{2}}{2\pi i}\frac{A(z_{1})B(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\,.

(4.132)

We may then evaluate the singular term of the OPE
T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{1})T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{2})
using Wick theorem, which tells us to take all possible contractions and then to normal-order the non-contracted fields; the multiplicity in front of every contraction comes from the fact that some contractions can be taken in multiple ways. The result is the following:

\displaystyle\underbracket{T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{1})T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(\underbracket{:\mathrel{jj}:(z_{1}):\mathrel{jj}:(z_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left(2\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+4\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}:\mathrel{j(z_{1})j(z_{2})}:\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{4}}+\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}:\mathrel{\Big(j(z_{2})+(z_{1}-z_{2})\partial j(z_{2})+\dots\Big)j(z_{2})}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{4}}+\frac{2T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{:\mathrel{(\partial j)j}:(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{4}}+\frac{2T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\,,

(4.133)

where, inside the normal ordering, we expanded
j(z_{1})
around
z=z_{2}
using a Taylor expansion up to
\mathcal{O}\left((z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}\right)
(further terms would have given non singular contributions).

Moreover, recalling eq. (
4.66
), we see that this calculation has fixed the central charge to be

c=1\,.

(4.134)

The final OPE is hence

T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{1})T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{4}}+\frac{2T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.135)

We can also evaluate

\displaystyle\underbracket{T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{1})j(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\underbracket{:\mathrel{jj}:(z_{1})j(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}2j(z_{1})\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{j(z_{2})+(z_{1}-z_{2})\partial j(z_{2})+\dots}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{j(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial j(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\,.

(4.136)

The final OPE between
T^{\text{(m)}}
and
j
is hence

T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{1})j(z_{2})=\frac{j(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial j(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.137)

This means that
j
is a primary field of weight 1 (recalling eq. (
4.55
) as a definition of a primary field).

If we now want to create, starting from the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum, a definite-momentum vacuum
\ket{0,P}
, we have to introduce the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{P}(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\,\,:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\,\,:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}}:(z):\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X_{R}}}:(\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}_{P}(z)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{P}(\bar{z})\,.

(4.138)

It is factorized into its holomorphic and anti-holomorphic component. The normal ordering of a plane wave
:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z)
has to be understood as the power series

:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(iP)^{n}}{n!}:\mathrel{X^{n}}:(z)\,,

(4.139)

where

:\mathrel{X^{n}}:(z)=X^{n}-\underbracket{\overbrace{XX\cdots X}^{n}}\,.

(4.140)

We can now evaluate

\displaystyle\underbracket{j(z_{1})X(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\underbracket{\partial X(z_{1})X(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\left(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\,,

(4.141)

then

\displaystyle\underbracket{j(z_{1}):\mathrel{X^{n}}:(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=n\underbracket{j(z_{1})X(z_{2})}:\mathrel{X^{n-1}}:(z_{2})

\displaystyle=-in\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}:\mathrel{X^{n-1}}:(z_{2})\,,

(4.142)

and finally

\displaystyle\underbracket{j(z_{1}):\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(iP)^{n}}{n!}\left(-in\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\right):\mathrel{X^{n-1}}:(z_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{P\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(iP)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}:\mathrel{X^{n-1}}:(z_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{P\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})\,.

(4.143)

To summarize

\displaystyle j(z_{1})X(z_{2})

\displaystyle=-i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}+\text{(regular terms)}\,,

(4.144a)

\displaystyle j(z_{1}):\mathrel{X^{n}}:(z_{2})

\displaystyle=-in\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\frac{1}{z_{1}-z_{2}}:\mathrel{X^{n-1}}:(z_{2})+\text{(regular terms)}\,,

(4.144b)

\displaystyle j(z_{1}):\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{P\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.144c)

Via the operator/state correspondence we can write

\ket{0,P}=\,\,:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})\Big|_{z=\bar{z}=0}\ket{0}\,,

(4.145)

which is the same as what we did previously

\ket{0,P}=e^{iP\cdot\hat{X}_{0}}\ket{0}\,,

(4.146)

since our free boson
X(z,\bar{z})
is

X(z,\bar{z})=\hat{X}_{0}+i\hat{P}\log|z|^{2}+\text{ oscillators}\,.

(4.147)

Finally we have that

T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{1}):\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})=\frac{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.148)

Therefore the plane wave
:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z)
is a primary field of weight
\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}
.

Exercise 4.2.2

Prove eq. (
4.148
).

4.2.1.2
Vertex operators

Through the state-operator correspondence, we define a
vertex operator
(VO) as the wolrdsheet field operator associated to a physical state. For closed strings, a vertex operator is a primary field of weight
(1,1)
.
Let’s now proceed to analyze, in this new language of CFT, the first physical states.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N=0

At the level
N=0
in the matter sector we have the tachyon, whose vertex operator is given by

t(P):\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})\,,

(4.149)

which has to satisfy the weight one condition

h=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}=1

(4.150)

in order to be a weight 1 field.
On the other hand, speaking about the primariness, the plane wave is always a primary field and hence no further condition on the momentum
P
is needed.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N=1

At the level
N=1
in the matter sector we have the massless state

G_{\mu\nu}(P)\alpha_{-1}^{\mu}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{\nu}\ket{0,P}\,.

(4.151)

The corresponding vertex operator is

G_{\mu\nu}(P):\mathrel{j^{\mu}\bar{j}^{\nu}e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})\,,

(4.152)

its scaling dimension is
(1,1)
if

\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}=0\,,

(4.153)

since
j^{\mu}
and
\bar{j}^{\nu}
already give the correct
(1,1)
weight. However, due to the normal ordering, it is not guaranteed that it is a primary. To ascertain this we compute the OPE with
T(z)
.
Restricting to the holomorphic sector, we have that

\displaystyle T^{\text{(m)}}(z_{1}):\mathrel{j^{\mu}e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})G_{\mu\nu}(P)=

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}P^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{3}}:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\frac{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial_{z_{2}}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\right):\mathrel{j^{\mu}e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z_{2})G_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\qquad+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.154)

So we get a primary if the coefficient of the cubic pole vanishes. Altogether we recover the physicality conditiions

\begin{cases}\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+1=1\,\ \Longleftrightarrow\,\ P^{2}=0\\
P^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=0\end{cases}\,.

(4.155)

If we look at the anti-holomorphic part, we get the additional physical condition

G_{\mu\nu}P^{\nu}=0\,.

(4.156)

These are the same physical conditions we found in (
3.128
) and (
3.133
).

Exercise 4.2.3

Prove eq. (
4.154
).

If we think a little about it (comparing what we found now with (
3.128
)), we realize that the first term inside (
4.154
) corresponds to the
L_{1}
constraint, as it is expected from our general discussion.

4.2.1.3
Correlation functions

Let us now consider correlation functions of currents.
By Wick theorem we easily get that

\displaystyle\langle j^{\mu}(z_{1})\rangle

\displaystyle=0\,,

(4.157a)

\displaystyle\langle j^{\mu}(z_{1})j^{\nu}(z_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\,,

(4.157b)

\displaystyle\langle j^{\mu}(z_{1})j^{\nu}(z_{2})j^{\rho}(z_{3})\rangle

\displaystyle=0\,,

(4.157c)

\displaystyle\langle j^{\mu}(z_{1})j^{\nu}(z_{2})j^{\rho}(z_{3})j^{\sigma}(z_{4})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{\mu\nu}\eta^{\rho\sigma}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}(z_{3}-z_{4})^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\eta^{\mu\rho}\eta^{\nu\sigma}}{(z_{1}-z_{3})^{2}(z_{2}-z_{4})^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\eta^{\mu\sigma}\eta^{\nu\rho}}{(z_{1}-z_{4})^{2}(z_{2}-z_{3})^{2}}\,.

(4.157d)

The 1-point function vanishes because
h(j)=1
(recall eq. (
4.108
)).
The 3-point function vanishes because, whichever contraction we try to take, we are always left with a vanishing 1-point function. In general the correlation function of an odd number of
j
’s is always vanishing.

Let us now consider correlation functions of plane waves.
Since we are allowed to split the VO into its holomorphic and anti-holomorphic parts, the correlation function factorizes

\langle\mathcal{V}_{P_{1}}(z_{1},\bar{z_{1}})\dots\mathcal{V}_{P_{n}}(z_{n},\bar{z_{n}})\rangle=\langle\mathcal{V}_{P_{1}}(z_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{P_{n}}(z_{n})\rangle\cdot\langle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{P_{1}}(\bar{z_{1}})\dots\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{P_{n}}(\bar{z_{n}})\rangle\,,

(4.158)

we can focus on the holomorphic sector and write

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{V}_{P_{1}}(z_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{P_{n}}(z_{n})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\prod_{k=1}^{n}:\mathrel{e^{iP_{k}\cdot X(z_{k})}}:\rangle

\displaystyle=\prod_{k<l}(z_{k}-z_{l})^{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}P_{k}\cdot P_{l}}\cdot\delta\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}P_{k}\right)\,.

(4.159)

This holds because

\displaystyle\underbracket{:\mathrel{e^{iP_{k}\cdot X}}:(z_{k}):\mathrel{e^{iP_{l}\cdot X}}:(z_{l})}

\displaystyle=e^{\,\,\,\underbracket{iP_{k}\cdot X(z_{k})iP_{l}\cdot X(z_{l})}}:\mathrel{e^{iP_{k}\cdot X}(z_{k})e^{iP_{l}\cdot X}(z_{l})}:

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}P_{k}\cdot P_{l}\log(z_k-z_l)}:\mathrel{e^{iP_{k}\cdot X}(z_{k})e^{iP_{l}\cdot X}(z_{l})}:

\displaystyle=(z_{k}-z_{l})^{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}P_{k}\cdot P_{l}}:\mathrel{e^{iP_{k}\cdot X}(z_{k})e^{iP_{l}\cdot X}(z_{l})}:\,.

(4.160)

The Dirac delta conserving the total momentum inside (
4.159
) comes from the fact that, given the conserved charge
j^{\mu}_{0}
inside
j^{\mu}(z)
, namely

j^{\mu}_{0}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j^{\mu}(z)=\alpha_{0}^{\mu}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}P^{\mu}\,,

(4.161)

we can insert it inside the correlation function (
4.159
). Since it annihilates the vacuum, we have that

\displaystyle\langle j_{0}\big(\mathcal{V}_{P_{1}}(z_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{P_{n}}(z_{n})\big)\rangle

\displaystyle=\bra{0}j_{0}\big(\mathcal{V}_{P_{1}}(z_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{P_{n}}(z_{n})\big)\ket{0}=0

\displaystyle=\langle\oint_{z_{1}\dots z_{n}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j^{\mu}(z)\big(\mathcal{V}_{P_{1}}(z_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{P_{n}}(z_{n})\big)\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}P_{k}\right)\left(\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\right)^{n}\langle\mathcal{V}_{P_{1}}(z_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{P_{n}}(z_{n})\rangle\,,

(4.162)

where we used

\displaystyle j_{0}^{\mu}\mathcal{V}_{P_{k}}(z_{k})

\displaystyle=\oint_{z_{k}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j^{\mu}(z)e^{iP_{k}\cdot X}(z_{k})

\displaystyle=\oint_{z_{k}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\underbracket{j^{\mu}(z)e^{iP_{k}\cdot X}(z_{k})}

\displaystyle=\oint_{z_{k}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\frac{P^{\mu}}{(z-z_{k})}\mathcal{V}_{P_{k}}(z_{k})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}P^{\mu}\mathcal{V}_{P_{k}}(z_{k})\,.

(4.163)

Therefore eq. (
4.162
) implies that

\sum_{k=1}^{n}P_{k}=0\,,

(4.164)

namely the total momentum is conserved inside the
n
-point correlation function.

4.2.2
Ghost
(b,c)
-CFT

4.2.2.1
Fields and OPEs

The ghost action in the complex plane is given by (recall eq. (
4.10
))

S_{\text{ghost}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}z\,\left(b\bar{\partial}c+\bar{b}\partial\bar{c}\right)\,.

(4.165)

As we said before (see eq. (
4.28b
)), the
b
ghost has weight
2
(holomorphic quadratic differential) and can be written as

\begin{cases}b(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}b_{n}z^{-n-2}\\[8.0pt]
b_{n}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n+1}b(z)\end{cases}\,,

(4.166)

while the
c
ghost has weight
-1
(holomorphic vector field) and can be written as (recall eq. (
4.28c
))

\begin{cases}c(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}z^{-n+1}\\[8.0pt]
c_{n}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}z^{n-2}c(z)\end{cases}\,.

(4.167)

The ghosts’ modes algebra reads as

\left[b_{n},c_{m}\right]=\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,

(4.168)

and the corresponding OPE is

b(z_{1})c(z_{2})=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.169)

The ghost stress-energy tensor is defined as

T^{\text{(gh)}}(z)=-\Big(2:\mathrel{b(\partial c)}:+:\mathrel{(\partial b)c}:\Big)(z)

(4.170)

and satisfies

\displaystyle T^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{1})b(z_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{2b(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial b(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,,

(4.171a)

\displaystyle T^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{1})c(z_{2})

\displaystyle=-\frac{c(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial c(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.171b)

Exercise 4.2.4

Prove eq. (
4.171a
) and eq. (
4.171b
).

Moreover we have that

\displaystyle\underbracket{b(z_{1})b(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(4.172a)

\displaystyle\underbracket{c(z_{1})c(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(4.172b)

All in all we have that

T^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{1})T^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{2})=\frac{-\frac{26}{2}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{4}}+\frac{2T^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial T^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.173)

Exercise 4.2.5

Prove eq. (
4.173
). (Pay attention to the fact that
b
and
c
are Grassmann odd!)

We then define the ghost current as

\displaystyle j^{\text{(gh)}}(z)

\displaystyle=-:\mathrel{bc}:(z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}j_{n}^{\text{(gh)}}z^{-n-1}\,,

(4.174)

which obviously has weight
h(j^{(gh)})=1
. Although its scaling dimension is 1, it is not a primary

T^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{1})j^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{2})=\frac{-3}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{3}}+\frac{j^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial j^{\text{(gh)}}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.175)

Finally, we define the ghost number operator as

N_{\text{(gh)}}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j^{\text{(gh)}}(z)\,,

(4.176)

which is such that

\displaystyle\left[N_{\text{(gh)}},c(z)\right]

\displaystyle=c(z)\,,

(4.177a)

\displaystyle\left[N_{\text{(gh)}},b(z)\right]

\displaystyle=-b(z)\,.

(4.177b)

Hence
b
has ghost number
gh=-1
and
c
has ghost number
gh=1
.

4.2.2.2
Ghosts primaries

If we evaluate the OPE with
T^{\text{(gh)}}
of the states

\displaystyle c(z)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle b(z)\,,

(4.178a)

\displaystyle c(\partial c)(z)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle b(\partial b)(z)\,,

(4.178b)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}c(\partial c)(\partial^{2}c)(z)\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}b(\partial b)(\partial^{2}b)(z)\,,

(4.178c)

we realize that they are all primary fields. In general in a CFT the
\partial
of a primary is not a primary because the
\partial
converts the double pole into a triple pole in the OPE with
T
. But in the ghost-CFT one can see that the terms producing the non-primary structure vanish since they are multiplied by
b^{2},\,(\partial b)^{2},\dots
(or, analogously, for
c^{2},\,(\partial c)^{2},\dots
), which are vanishing because of their grassmanality.

If we now recall that we have four
c
-ghost vacua, we can associate them with primary fields:

\displaystyle\ket{0}\quad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad\text{1}

\displaystyle(h

\displaystyle=0)\,,

(4.179a)

\displaystyle c_{1}\ket{0}\quad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad c(z)

\displaystyle(h

\displaystyle=-1)\,,

(4.179b)

\displaystyle c_{0}c_{1}\ket{0}\quad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad(\partial c)c(z)

\displaystyle(h

\displaystyle=-1)\,,

(4.179c)

\displaystyle c_{-1}c_{0}c_{1}\ket{0}\quad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{2}(\partial^{2}c)(\partial c)c(z)

\displaystyle(h

\displaystyle=0)\,.

(4.179d)

We have

\innerproduct{0}{\hat{0}}=\bra{0}c_{-1}c_{0}c_{1}\ket{0}=1\,\ \longleftrightarrow\,\ \frac{1}{2}\langle c(\partial c)(\partial^{2}c)(z)\rangle=1\,.

(4.180)

We hence found a non-vanishing 1-point function of a vanishing weight field which is not the identity.

4.2.2.3
Three-ghost correlation function

Given what we said above, we have for a three ghost correlation function the only contribution of a
c
-ghost comes from the first terms on its modes expansion:

c(z)=c_{-1}z^{2}+c_{0}z+c_{1}\,.

(4.181)

One can hence evaluate

\langle c(z_{1})c(z_{2})c(z_{3})\rangle=-\text{det}\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\
z_{1}&z_{2}&z_{3}\\[5.0pt]
z_{1}^{2}&z_{2}^{2}&z_{3}^{2}\end{pmatrix}=-(z_{1}-z_{2})(z_{2}-z_{3})(z_{1}-z_{3})\,.

(4.182)

Exercise 4.2.6

Prove eq. (
4.182
).

4.2.3
BRST and CFT

Let us first recall from BRST quantization the definition of the BRST charge

\displaystyle Q_{B}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{-n}L_{n}^{\text{(m)}}+\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{c_{-n}L_{n}^{\text{(gh)}}}:

\displaystyle=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\left(cT^{\text{(m)}}(z)+\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{cT^{\text{(gh)}}}:(z)\right)=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\widetilde{j}_{B}(z)\,.

(4.183)

We can think to the matter sector (m) as a generic CFT of central charge
c=26
, which cancels the ghost sector (gh) central charge.
Moreover, we wrote
\widetilde{j}_{B}(z)
because the true current is

j_{B}(z)=cT^{\text{(m)}}(z)+\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{cT^{\text{(gh)}}}:(z)+\frac{3}{2}\partial^{2}c(z)\,,

(4.184)

which is a weight 1 primary field, with respect to the total stress-energy tensor

T^{\text{(tot)}}(z)=T^{\text{(m)}}(z)+T^{\text{(gh)}}(z)\,.

(4.185)

The additional term
\frac{3}{2}\partial^{2}c(z)
is needed for the primariness of
j_{B}(z)
, but in the definition of
Q_{B}
it is irrelevant, being a total derivative which gives no contribution to the integral.

Given these definitions, one can show that

\left[Q_{B},X^{\mu}(z)\right]=\oint_{z}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}j_{B}(\tilde{z})X^{\mu}(z)=c\partial X^{\mu}(z)

(4.186)

and also

\left[Q_{B},c(z)\right]=c\partial c(z)

(4.187)

that, thanks to the state-operator correspondence, is equivalent to

Q_{B}c_{1}\ket{0}=c_{1}c_{0}\ket{0}\,.

(4.188)

Moreover one can show that

Q_{B}^{2}=0

(4.189)

whenever
c^{\text{m}}=-c^{\text{gh}}=26
, namely when the matter central charge cancels the ghost central charge.

We also have that

\left[Q_{B},j_{\text{gh}}(z)\right]=-j_{B}(z)

(4.190)

which, thanks to the state-operator correspondence, is equivalent to

\left[N_{\text{gh}},Q_{B}\right]=Q_{B}\,.

(4.191)

Therefore
j_{B}
generates
Q_{B}
as a zero mode, but
j_{B}
is also
Q_{B}
-exact. Its primitive is the ghost current
j_{\text{gh}}
. Notice that while
j_{\text{gh}}
is non primary,
j_{B}
is primary.

Exercise 4.2.7

Prove eq. (
4.186
).

Exercise 4.2.8

Prove eq. (
4.187
).

Exercise 4.2.9

Prove eq. (
4.189
). Use the fact that

Q_{B}^{2}=\oint_{0}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\oint_{\tilde{z}}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\underbracket{j_{B}(z)j_{B}(\tilde{z})}

(4.192)

and that

j_{B}(z)j_{B}(\tilde{z})=\dots+\frac{c^{\text{(m)}}-26}{12}\frac{1}{(z-\tilde{z})}\left(\partial^{3}c\cdot c\right)(\tilde{z})+\dots\,.

(4.193)

Exercise 4.2.10

Prove eq. (
4.190
).

We are now ready to analyze the cohomology. Using the state-operator correspondence we can write

\displaystyle\ket{0}\quad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad\text{1}

\displaystyle(gh

\displaystyle=0)\,,

(4.194a)

\displaystyle c_{1}\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}\ket{0}\quad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad c\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}(z)

\displaystyle(gh

\displaystyle=1)\,,

(4.194b)

\displaystyle c_{0}c_{1}\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}\ket{0}\quad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad c(\partial c)\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}(z)

\displaystyle(gh

\displaystyle=2)\,,

(4.194c)

\displaystyle c_{-1}c_{0}c_{1}\ket{0}\quad

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{1}{2}c(\partial c)(\partial^{2}c)(z)

\displaystyle(gh

\displaystyle=3)\,,

(4.194d)

where
\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}
is a physical matter operator of weight
h=1
.

One can also show that

\left[Q_{B},\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}(z)\right]=\partial\left(c\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}\right)(z)\,.

(4.195)

Exercise 4.2.11

Prove eq. (
4.195
).

This tells us that there are two ways for a state to be killed by
Q_{B}
:

1.

a local operator
c\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}(z)
;

2.

a non-local
integrated
operator
\int dz\,\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}(z)=\mathscr{V}^{\text{(m)}}
.

Then the physical condition (i.e. the BRST charge annihilates the physical state) can be expressed in two possible ways:

1.

\left[Q_{B},c\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}(z)\right]=0
;

2.

\left[Q_{B},\mathscr{V}^{\text{(m)}}\right]=\int dz\,\partial\left(c\mathcal{V}_{h=1}^{\text{(m)}}\right)(z)\overset{\text{Stokes}}{=}0\quad
(within boundary terms).

We now want to write the complete closed string vertex operator, taking both the holomorphic and the anti-holomorphic part and also making explicit the momentum operator

1.

The non-integrated VO is

c\bar{c}{\cal V}(z,\bar{z})=\int d^{d}P\,\,\Phi_{ij}(P):\mathrel{c\mathcal{V}_{i}\bar{c}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})\,,

(4.196)

where
\Phi_{ij}(P)
is the spacetime polarization and
{\cal V}_{i}
is a chiral field of weight
h_{i}
(and similarly for
\bar{\cal V}_{j}
). The polarization
\Phi_{ij}(P)
has to satisfy appropriate conditions to make
:{\cal V}_{i}\,e^{iP\cdot X}:
a primary.
It has
gh=(1,1)
and is an overall primary of weight
h=(0,0)
if

\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}/4+h_{i}-1=\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}/4+h_{j}-1=0\,.

(4.197)

2.

The integrated VO is instead

\int dzd\bar{z}\mathcal{V}(z,\bar{z})=\int dzd\bar{z}\int d^{d}P\,\,\Phi_{ij}(P):\mathrel{\mathcal{V}_{i}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})\,.

(4.198)

The worldsheet integration
\int dzd\bar{z}
effectively lowers the total weight to
h=(0,0)
and therefore we again have a conformal invariant operator, but this time a non-local one.

4.3
Open String CFT

4.3.1
Boundary conformal field theory

So far we considered a theory defined on a cylinder with coordinate
w
that we mapped to the complex plane through the conformal map
z=e^{w}
. Such a construction was suitable for closed strings. When we have to deal with open strings we have some differences: the worldsheet is a now strip and under the conformal transformation
z=e^{w}
it is mapped to the upper part of the complex plane (see fig.
4.6
). We will refer to it as the
upper half plane
(UHP).

Therefore, after the transformation, the CFT is defined on a surface having as a boundary the real axis, where
z=\bar{z}
. This is an example of a
boundary conformal field theory
, (BCFT).

The presence of a boundary breaks the full conformal group to the subgroup that leaves the boundary invariant. This means for a given
f
such that

\displaystyle z\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ f(z)\,,

(4.199a)

\displaystyle\bar{z}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \bar{f}(\bar{z})\,,

(4.199b)

when we are on the boundary
z=\bar{z}
we have to require that

f(z)=\bar{f}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}\,,

(4.200)

so that
f
is real

f(z)=\sum_{n}f_{n}z^{n}\quad\text{with }f_{n}=\bar{f}_{n}\,\ \Longleftrightarrow\,\ f_{n}\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(4.201)

Figure 4.6:
The worldsheet of an open string, after a Wick rotation of the time coordinate, can be studied as an infinite strip of height
\pi i
on the Euclidean plane of coordinate
w
. With the exponential function it can then be mapped to the upper half of the complex plane with coordinate
z
, where the (
t=0
) slice of the WS corresponds to the unit semi-circle
|z|=1
(marked in red): the past (
t<0
, marked in blue) lies inside such a semi-circle, while the future (
t>0
) fills the external region.

Conformal transformations are generated by the stress-energy tensor and, on the Riemann sphere, we had that
T(z)
generates the transformation
f(z)
, while
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{z})
generates
\bar{f}(\bar{z})
. On the UHP we have
f(z)=\bar{f}(\bar{z})
for
z=\bar{z}
, and therefore we have to require that

T(z)=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}\,.

(4.202)

If we then recall the usual expansion (
4.44
) of the stress-energy tensor in terms of Virasoro operators, namely

\displaystyle T(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}L_{n}z^{-n-2}\,,

(4.203a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{L}_{n}\bar{z}^{-n-2}\,,

(4.203b)

we realize that the condition (
4.202
) implies

L_{n}=\tilde{L}_{n}\,,

(4.204)

which means that we have only one Virasoro algebra.

Just like the Riemann sphere is preserved by the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
subgroup of projective transformations, analogously for the UHP we have the group
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
:

f(z)=\frac{Az+B}{Cz+D}\qquad\text{with }A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{R},\quad(AD-BC)\neq 0\,.

(4.205)

It is the largest possible group of transformations which sends the UHP to itself in a one-to-one way.

4.3.1.1
Bulk fields and doubling trick

Consider a generic field
\phi(z,\bar{z})
living in the bulk. If we apply to it an infinitesimal conformal transformation (recalling eq. (
4.46
)), we have that

\displaystyle\delta_{(\epsilon,\bar{\epsilon})}\phi(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=-\left(\left[T_{\epsilon},\phi(z,\bar{z})\right]+\left[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\bar{\epsilon}},\phi(z,\bar{z})\right]\right)

\displaystyle=-\oint_{z}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\tilde{z})T(\tilde{z})\phi(z,\bar{z})-\oint_{\bar{z}}\frac{d\bar{\tilde{z}}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\bar{\tilde{z}})\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{\tilde{z}})\phi(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=-\oint_{z}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\tilde{z})\underbracket{T(\tilde{z})\phi(z,\bar{z})}-\oint_{\bar{z}}\frac{d\bar{\tilde{z}}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\bar{\tilde{z}})\underbracket{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{\tilde{z}})\phi(z,\bar{z})}\,,

(4.206)

where the contours of anti-holomorphic coordinates are
clockwise
(while contours of holomorphic currents, as usual, counter-clockwise). This is the same formula we would get in absence of a boundary, except that now
\epsilon(z)=\bar{\epsilon}(\bar{z})
at
z=\bar{z}
and analogously for
T
.

In order to proceed in the calculation, we can use the so-called
doubling trick
: instead of considering the UHP whose points have a
(z,\bar{z})
dependence (see fig.
4.7
), we can consider the whole complex plane
\mathbb{C}
with a purely holomorphic dependence on
z
and
z^{*}
, being
z^{*}
the complex conjugate of
z
(see fig.
4.7
).

((a))

((b))

Figure 4.7:
On the left we have the UHP, whose points have a
(z,\bar{z})
dependence. On the right we have the whole complex plane, having a holomorphic dependence on
z
and
z^{*}
.

As shown in fig.
4.8
, this procedure “unfolds” the UHP, which has a double dependence on (
z
and
\bar{z}
) to the whole complex plane, through the boundary
z=\bar{z}
.

Figure 4.8:
The doubling trick.

With the doubling trick, everything becomes holomorphic (see fig.
4.9
).

Figure 4.9:
The doubling trick makes everything holomorphic.

Therefore we can carry on our calculation as follows:

\displaystyle\delta_{(\epsilon,\bar{\epsilon})}\phi(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=-\oint_{z}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\tilde{z})\underbracket{T(\tilde{z})\phi(z,\bar{z})}+\oint_{\bar{z}}\frac{d\bar{\tilde{z}}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\bar{\tilde{z}})\underbracket{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{\tilde{z}})\phi(z,\bar{z})}

\displaystyle=-\oint_{z}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\tilde{z})\underbracket{T(\tilde{z})\phi(z,z^{*})}-\oint_{z^{*}}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\tilde{z})\underbracket{T(\tilde{z})\phi(z,z^{*})}

\displaystyle=-\oint_{z,z^{*}}\frac{d\tilde{z}}{2\pi i}\epsilon(\tilde{z})T_{\mathbb{C}}(\tilde{z})\phi(z,z^{*})\,,

(4.207)

where in the last step we deformed the integration path as shown in fig.
4.10
.

Figure 4.10:
Deformation of the integration path performed in the last step of equation (
4.207
). The vertical axis of the complex plane is not shown for convenience.

We have also defined

T_{\mathbb{C}}(\tilde{z})=\begin{cases}T_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(\tilde{z})\quad\text{for }Im(\tilde{z})>0\,\text{ (above the boundary)}\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(\bar{\tilde{z}})\quad\text{for }Im(\tilde{z})<0\,\text{ (below the boundary)}\end{cases}\,.

(4.208)

This construction agrees with the boundary conditions (
4.202
). Notice also that the bulk field
\phi(z,\bar{z})
is reinterpreted as a
bilocal
holomorphic field
\phi(z,z^{*})
. We will see in the following explicit examples that this interpretation
depends on the boundary conditions
and that the same bulk field will in general map to different bilocal holomorphic fields depending on the boundary conditions (i.e. the kind of
D
-brane).

4.3.2
Matter
X
-BCFT

We are now ready to study the matter
X
-CFT.
The action of the
X
field on the complex plane is given by (recall eq. (
4.10
))

S_{\text{matter}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}z\,\partial X(z,\bar{z})\cdot\bar{\partial}X(z,\bar{z})\,,

(4.209)

where, as usual, we defined
\partial=\partial_{z}
and
\bar{\partial}=\partial_{\bar{z}}
.

Its EOM, being in the open string case, are (
3.162
).
Moreover, in these complex coordinates we can write

X^{\mu}(z,\bar{z})=X_{L}^{\mu}(z)+X_{R}^{\mu}(\bar{z})\,,

(4.210)

where the two chiral fields
X_{L}^{\mu}(z)
and
X_{R}^{\mu}(\bar{z})
are

\displaystyle X_{L}^{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{0}^{\mu}}{2}+c^{\mu}/2-i\alpha^{\prime}\frac{P^{\mu}}{2}\log z+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha^{\mu}_{n}z^{-n}\,,

(4.211a)

\displaystyle X_{R}^{\mu}(\bar{z})

\displaystyle=\frac{X_{0}^{\mu}}{2}-c^{\mu}/2-i\alpha^{\prime}\frac{P^{\mu}}{2}\log\bar{z}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha^{\mu}_{n}\bar{z}^{-n}\,,

(4.211b)

where the two fields have only half of the total momentum of the center of mass.
The stress-energy tensor of this theory is

T(z)=-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}:\mathrel{\partial X\cdot\partial X}:\,.

(4.212)

On the other hand, if we are interested in the current, we may recall that

\displaystyle\partial X^{\mu}(z,\bar{z})\,\,\text{primary of $h=(1,0)$}\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ \text{current $j^{\mu}(z)$}\,,

(4.213a)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}X^{\mu}(z,\bar{z})\,\,\text{primary of $h=(0,1)$}\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ \text{current $\bar{j}^{\mu}(\bar{z})$}\,.

(4.213b)

Depending on the BC, the current will glue in different ways on the boundary:

•

Neumann BC along the
\mu
direction:
j^{\mu}(z)=\bar{j}^{\mu}(\bar{z})
on the boundary
z=\bar{z}
;

•

Dirichlet BC along the
i
direction:
j^{i}(z)=-\bar{j}^{i}(\bar{z})
on the boundary
z=\bar{z}
.

Hence the boundary is labelled by its BC (N or D). More in general, we can write the
gluing condition
on the boundary for the current as

j^{\mu}(z)=\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(j)}\bar{j}^{\mu}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for $z=\bar{z}$}\,,

(4.214)

with

\Omega_{\text{A}}=\begin{cases}+1\,\text{ for $\text{A}=\text{Neumann}$}\\
-1\,\text{ for $\text{A}=\text{Dirichlet}$}\end{cases}\,,

(4.215)

which is called
gluing map
.

This reasoning applies in the same way to the
X
field. Remembering the decomposition (
4.210
) of
X^{\mu}(z,\bar{z})
into the chiral fields
X_{L}^{\mu}(z)
and
X_{R}^{\mu}(\bar{z})
, we have that

X_{L}^{\mu}(z)=\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(X)}X_{R}^{\mu}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for $z=\bar{z}$}\,,

(4.216)

where the gluing map is again (
4.215
). Thus, using the doubling trick the gluing condition becomes

\displaystyle X^{\text{(N)}}_{L}(z)

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ X(z)\,,\quad

\displaystyle X^{\text{(N)}}_{R}(\bar{z})

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ X(z^{*})\,,

(4.217a)

\displaystyle X^{\text{(D)}}_{L}(z)

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ X(z)\,,\quad

\displaystyle X^{\text{(D)}}_{R}(\bar{z})

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ -X(z^{*})\,.

(4.217b)

As said, we know that on the boundary the Virasoro algebra is preserved (thanks to the BC (
4.202
)). Moreover, in the matter
X
-BCFT there is also an additional symmetry, namely the oscillator algebra, encapsulated by the current algebra, which comes in two copies (one for
j^{\mu}
and one for
\bar{j}^{\mu}
):

\displaystyle\underbracket{j^{\mu}(z_{1})j_{\mu}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}\,\,\,\ \overset{\text{ osc }}{\longrightarrow}\,\,\,\ \left[\alpha_{n}^{\mu},\alpha_{m}^{\mu}\right]=n\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,

(4.218a)

\displaystyle\underbracket{\bar{j}^{\mu}(\bar{z}_{1})\bar{j}_{\mu}(\bar{z}_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})^{2}}\,\,\,\ \overset{\text{ osc }}{\longrightarrow}\,\,\,\ \left[\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{m}^{\mu}\right]=n\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,

(4.218b)

This symmetry is preserved by the gluing condition (
4.214
) on the boundary, for which we have (
4.215
)

\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(j)}=\pm 1\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\left(\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(j)}\right)^{2}=1\,.

(4.219)

This result is consistent with the gluing of the stress-energy tensor:

\begin{cases}T=\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{j\cdot j}:\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{\bar{j}\cdot\bar{j}}:\end{cases}\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ \left(\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(j)}\right)^{2}=1\,.

(4.220)

Using the doubling trick, we can define (analogously to (
4.208
))

j^{\mu}_{\mathbb{C}}(z)=\begin{cases}j^{\mu}_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(z)\qquad\quad\text{for }Im(z)>0\,\text{ (above the boundary)}\\[5.0pt]
\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(j)}\bar{j}^{\mu}_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(\bar{z})\,\,\quad\text{for }Im(z)<0\,\text{ (below the boundary)}\end{cases}\,,

(4.221)

which is continuous at the boundary.

In a similar way, starting from the chiral fields
\mathcal{V}^{(h)}_{i,\,\text{\tiny UHP}}(z)
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(\bar{h})}_{i,\,\text{\tiny UHP}}(\bar{z})
, we can define

\mathcal{V}^{(h)}_{i,\,\mathbb{C}}(z)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{V}^{(h)}_{i,\,\text{\tiny UHP}}(z)\qquad\quad\text{for }Im(z)>0\,\text{ (above the boundary)}\\[5.0pt]
\left(\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(\mathcal{V})}\right)^{\,j}_{i}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(\bar{h})}_{j,\,\text{\tiny UHP}}(\bar{z})\,\quad\text{for }Im(z)<0\,\text{ (under the boundary)}\end{cases}\,,

(4.222)

where the gluing map is now a matrix
\Omega
which should preserve the V-V OPE, since we considered the general case where
\mathcal{V}^{(h)}_{i,\,\text{\tiny UHP}}(z)
is a family of operators.
If we now consider a bulk field

\phi(z,\bar{z})=\phi_{ij}\mathcal{V}^{i}(z)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{j}(\bar{z})\,,

(4.223)

in the presence of a boundary it will become

\phi(z,z^{*})=\phi_{ij}\left(\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(\mathcal{V})}\right)^{\,j}_{k}\mathcal{V}^{i}(z)\mathcal{V}^{k}(z^{*})\,.

(4.224)

For example we can consider the local (non-holomorphic) operator

\Phi(z,\bar{z})=j(z)\cdot\bar{j}(\bar{z})\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \alpha_{-1}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}\ket{0}

(4.225)

defining a closed string state. If we place it on the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
using the doubling trick, it will become a bi-local (completely holomorphic) operator that, depending on the BC, can be written as

\displaystyle\Phi^{\text{(N)}}(z,z^{*})

\displaystyle=j(z)\Omega_{\text{N}}^{(j)}j(z^{*})=j(z)j(z^{*})\,,

(4.226a)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\text{(D)}}(z,z^{*})

\displaystyle=j(z)\Omega_{\text{D}}^{(j)}j(z^{*})=-j(z)j(z^{*})\,.

(4.226b)

This is the mechanism that enables closed strings to distinguish different
D(p)
-branes (see exercise
4.3.2.1
).

4.3.2.1
Correlation functions of bulk fields

We may first focus on the 1-point functions of a generic field
\phi^{(h,\bar{h})}(z,\bar{z})
. It does not vanish a priori because, due to the boundary, the theory has a scale, namely the distance
y=\frac{1}{2}|z-\bar{z}|
of the field from the boundary itself (see fig.
4.11
). We then have

\langle\phi^{(h,\bar{h})}(z,\bar{z})\rangle^{(a)}=\frac{C_{\phi}^{(a)}}{|z-\bar{z}|^{2h}}\,\delta_{h,\bar{h}}\,.

(4.227)

Figure 4.11:
A generic field
\phi^{(h,\bar{h})}(z,\bar{z})
at a distance
y=\frac{1}{2}|z-\bar{z}|
from the boundary.

Moving on towards 2-point function, let us then analyze in detail the bulk correlator of two
X
fields.

Recalling eq. (
4.210
) and (
4.130c
), we can write

\displaystyle\langle X(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\cdot X(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle X_{L}(z_{1})\cdot X_{L}(z_{2})\rangle+\langle X_{R}(\bar{z_{1}})\cdot X_{R}(\bar{z_{2}})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\left(\log(z_1-z_2)+\log(\bar{z_1}-\bar{z_2})\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right)\,.

(4.228)

In the last step we had to merge the left and right contributions in order to get a single-valued function (and not a multi-valued one, which would be non-local).

We can then consider the same correlation function but on the UHP having Neumann BC. Using the doubling trick and recalling eq. (
4.217
), we can write

\displaystyle\langle X(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\cdot X(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}^{\text{(N)}}

\displaystyle=\langle\Big(X(z_{1})+X(z_{1}^{*})\Big)\cdot\Big(X(z_{2})+X(z_{2}^{*})\Big)\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle X(z_{1})\cdot X(z_{2})\rangle+\langle X(z_{1})\cdot X(z_{2}^{*})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad\,+\langle X(z_{1}^{*})\cdot X(z_{2})\rangle+\langle X(z_{1}^{*})\cdot X(z_{2}^{*})\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\log\left(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right)-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\Big(\log(z_1-z_2^*)+\log(z_1^*-z_2)\Big)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\Big(\log\left(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right)+\log\left(|z_{1}-\bar{z}_{2}|^{2}\right)\Big)\,.

(4.229)

On the other hand, in the Dirichlet case we can write

\langle X(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\cdot X(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}^{\text{(D)}}=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\Big(\log\left(|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{2}\right)-\log\left(|z_{1}-\bar{z}_{2}|^{2}\right)\Big)\,.

(4.230)

Finally, we can consider the 1-point function. We have that

\displaystyle\langle\partial X\cdot\bar{\partial}X(z,\bar{z})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}^{\text{(N)}}

\displaystyle=+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\frac{1}{|z-\bar{z}|^{2}}\,,

(4.231a)

\displaystyle\langle\partial X\cdot\bar{\partial}X(z,\bar{z})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}^{\text{(D)}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\frac{1}{|z-\bar{z}|^{2}}\,,

(4.231b)

which is consistent with (
4.227
).

Exercise 4.3.1

Prove eq. (
4.231
).

4.3.2.2
Plane wave operators

Let us now discuss plane waves operators. We already saw (eq. (
4.138
)) that the bulk plane wave can be written as

:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})=\,\,:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}}:(z):\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X_{R}}}:(\bar{z})\,.

(4.232)

On the boundary
z=\bar{z}
, recalling eq. (
4.211
) and the gluing condition (
4.216
) for the
X
field, we realize that the plane wave becomes

•

Neumann:
:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})=e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}(z)e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}(z^{*})
;

•

Dirichlet:
:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})=e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}(z)e^{-iP\cdot X_{L}}(z^{*})
.

We can then study the 1-point function of such a plane wave as we change the BC. This roughly models the interaction of a closed string with a a
D(p)
-brane.
In the Neumann case we have that

\langle:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})\rangle^{\text{(N)}}_{\text{\tiny UHP}}=\langle e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}(z)e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}(z^{*})\rangle=0\,,

(4.233)

since the total momentum of the plane wave cannot be conserved (unless
P=0
).

On the other hand, in the Dirichlet case (recalling eq. (
4.159
)) we have that

\displaystyle\langle:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})\rangle^{\text{(D)}}_{\text{\tiny UHP}}

\displaystyle=\langle e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}(z)e^{-iP\cdot X_{L}}(\bar{z})\rangle

\displaystyle=(z-\bar{z})^{-\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}\delta(0)

\displaystyle\propto|z-\bar{z}|^{-\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}=e^{-\alpha^{\prime}\Delta P^{2}}\,,

(4.234)

where
\Delta=\log|z-\bar{z}|
. In the momentum space, the result we found is a gaussian of width
\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}\Delta}
. If we then perform a Fourier transform we obtain
e^{-X^{2}/(\alpha^{\prime}\Delta)}
, which is a gaussian in the coordinates space, centered in
X=0
and of width
\alpha^{\prime}\Delta
, namely having the width of the string. This suggests that the closed string ‘sees’ the
D(p)
-brane as a localized object at the string scale.

If we now want to consider the more general case where a
D(p)
-brane is centered in a generic
X_{0}
, we can recall the definition of
X_{DD}
(eq. (
3.181
)). Then, in the momentum space we have

\langle:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})\rangle^{\text{(D)},\,X_{0}}_{\text{\tiny UHP}}\propto e^{iP\cdot X_{0}}e^{-\alpha^{\prime}\Delta P^{2}}\,,

(4.235)

while in the coordinates space we have (see fig.
4.12
)

\int dPe^{iP\cdot X_{0}}e^{-\alpha^{\prime}\Delta P^{2}}\,\propto e^{-\frac{(X-X_{0})^{2}}{\alpha^{\prime}\Delta}}\,.

(4.236)

Figure 4.12:
A
D(p)
-brane is seen by closed strings as a localized gaussian in the coordinates space, centered in
X=X_{0}
and having width of order
\sqrt{\alpha}^{\prime}
.

4.3.2.3
Boundary fields

So far we discussed fields living in the bulk of the UHP, namely away from the boundary. But as a bulk field
\phi^{(h,\bar{h})}(z,\bar{z})
approaches the boundary (i.e.
z\to\bar{z}
), it generates a singularity, as we immediately notice from the 1-point function (
4.227
). Using the doubling trick we can write

\phi^{(h)}(z,\bar{z})=\sum_{i,j}\phi_{i,j}\mathcal{V}_{i}^{(h)}(z)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{j}^{(h)}(\bar{z})=\sum_{i,j}\phi_{i,j}\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{two_points_complex_plane.pdf}}\put(0.3065,0.53011){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\mathcal{V}_{i}^{(h)}(z)$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.26668,-0.09234){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\Omega_{j}^{k}\mathcal{V}_{k}^{(h)}(z^{*})$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{two_points_complex_plane.pdf}}\end{picture}}
}}\,.

(4.237)

Therefore

\mathcal{V}_{i}^{(h)}(z)\mathcal{V}_{k}^{(h)}(z^{*})\propto\sum_{p}(z-z^{*})^{k}\left[\mathcal{V}_{i}\mathcal{V}_{k}\right]_{p}(0)\,,

(4.238)

and hence, on the boundary, the two chiral operators
\mathcal{V}_{i}^{(h)}(z)
and
\mathcal{V}_{i}^{(h)}(z^{*})
collide. This tells us that a bulk field that lands on the boundary is the same as two chiral fields crashing on each other.

We thus need a new object, called
boundary field
, which is a field living on the real axis
z=\bar{z}
. It can be thought of as a purely chiral field laid down on the boundary from the bulk (see fig.
4.13
):

\phi^{(h)}_{\text{A}}(x)=\lim_{z\to x}\phi^{(h)}(z)

(4.239)

where A is the label that identifies the boundary condition and
x
is the coordinate on the real axis. In this way there is no anti-holomorphic counterpart and therefore no collision at the boundary.

Figure 4.13:
A boundary field is defined as a purely holomorphic field laid on the real axis
z=\bar{z}
.

Let us now define (see fig.
4.14
)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\parallel}\,,

(4.240a)

\displaystyle\partial_{y}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\perp}\,.

(4.240b)

Then we can write

\displaystyle\partial

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}-i\partial_{y}\,,

(4.241a)

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}+i\partial_{y}\,.

(4.241b)

Figure 4.14:
In the UHP we can call
x
the boundary coordinate and
y
the vertical bulk coordinate. Then we can define the basis vectors to be
\partial x
and
\partial y
.

We can now consider the free boson along the
\mu
direction with Neumann BC and recall the current gluing condition:

\partial X^{\mu}(z)=\bar{\partial}X^{\mu}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for $z=\bar{z}$.}

(4.242)

Using the notation (
4.241
), we write

\displaystyle(\partial-\bar{\partial})X^{\mu}(z)\Big|_{z=\bar{z}}

\displaystyle=-2i\partial_{y}X^{\mu}(z)

\displaystyle=0\,.

(4.243)

Therefore we can express the Neumann boundary condition as

\partial_{\perp}X^{\mu}(z)=0\,.

(4.244)

This is exactly what we first wrote in (
3.163
). Moreover, if we want to build the associated boundary field, we can write

\displaystyle\partial X^{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{\perp}+\partial_{\parallel}\right)X^{\mu}(z)\Big|_{z\to x}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\parallel}X^{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}X^{\mu}(x)\,.

(4.245)

On the other hand, the Dirichlet boundary condition for the current

\partial X^{\mu}(z)=-\bar{\partial}X^{\mu}(\bar{z})\Big|_{z=\bar{z}}

(4.246)

can be written as

\partial_{\parallel}X^{\mu}(z)=0\,,

(4.247)

and the associated boundary field is

\displaystyle\partial X^{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{\perp}+\partial_{\parallel}\right)X^{\mu}(z)\Big|_{z\to x}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\perp}X^{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}X^{\mu}(x)\,.

(4.248)

We hence obtained all the boundary fields related to the bosonic current
\partial X
.

Let us then consider the bulk plane wave

:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X}}:(z,\bar{z})=\,\,:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X_{L}}}:(z):\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X_{R}}}:(\bar{z})\,.

(4.249)

We can build a boundary plane wave only if we have Neumann BC, where the momentum is a conserved quantity
3
3

3

We will see in a while that it is not meaningless at all to take a chiral plane wave and put it on a boundary with Dirichlet boundary conditions: this will result in an insertion of a boundary operator that
changes
the value of the modulus of the Dirichlet condition. In general if we take a generic chiral field and put it on the boundary we get a
boundary condition changing operator/field
.

:\mathrel{e^{2iP\cdot X_{L}}}:(z)\Big|_{z\to x}=\,\,:\mathrel{e^{2iP\cdot X_{L}}}:(x)\,.

(4.250)

We may now recall (see eq. (
4.211
)) that the chiral field
X^{\mu}_{L}
has only half of the total momentum of the center of mass (since
X^{\mu}_{R}
holds the other half). Therefore, in order to have a state with momentum
P
on the boundary, we have to define

X^{\mu\,\text{(open)}}(x)=2X_{L}^{\mu}(z)\Big|_{z\to x}\,,

(4.251)

which is yet another occurrence of what we have already discussed in the oscillator formalism

\alpha_{0}^{\text{(open)}}=2\alpha_{0}^{\text{(closed)}}=\alpha_{0}^{\text{(closed)}}+\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{\text{(closed)}}\,.

(4.252)

Using eq. (
4.211
) we can then write

X^{\mu}_{\text{(open)}}(x)=X^{\mu}_{0}-i\alpha^{\prime}P^{\mu}\log(x)+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\alpha^{\mu}_{n}x^{-n}\,.

(4.253)

Given this definition we can finally write the boundary plane wave as

:\mathrel{e^{iP\cdot X_{\text{(open)}}}}:(x)=\,\,:\mathrel{e^{2iP\cdot X_{L}}}:(z)\Big|_{z\to x}\,,

(4.254)

whose conformal weight is

h=4\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}=\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}\,.

(4.255)

We are hence saying that

L_{0}^{\text{(open)}}=L_{0}^{\text{(closed, L sector)}}=\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}\,.

(4.256)

To sum up: we have learned that the chiral field
X_{L}^{\mu}
on its own is non-local, since (recall eq. (
4.130c
)) it gives rise to non single-valued correlation functions

\langle X_{L}(z_{1})\cdot X_{L}(z_{2})\rangle=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\log(z_1-z_2)\,.

(4.257)

However, we have two possibilities to construct a local operator.

•

We can merge together the L and R sectors, finding correlation functions whose behaviour is described by an absolute value, which is single-valued. This generates a closed string state, namely an holomorphic
+
anti-holomorphic bulk field.

•

We can put the chiral bulk field
X_{L}^{\mu}
on the boundary, where the real numbers structure generates a natural ordering which removes ambiguities. This generates an open string state, namely a boundary field.

If we then want to give a picture of the Hilbert space of a BCFT, we can divide it into two sectors:

•

bulk Hilbert space, whose states are given by bulk fields (inserted at the origin of the complex plane) acting on the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum:

\phi(z,\bar{z})\Big|_{z=\bar{z}=0}\ket{0}_{SL(2,\mathbb{C})}=\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{closed_phi_in_zero.pdf}}\put(0.52651,0.47306){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\phi(z,\bar{z})\big|_{z=\bar{z}=0}$\end{tabular}}}}\end{picture}}
}}\qquad;

(4.258)

•

boundary Hilbert space, whose states are given by boundary fields (applied at the origin of the UHP) acting on the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
-invariant vacuum:

\phi(x)\Big|_{x=0}\ket{0}_{SL(2,\mathbb{R})}=\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{open_phi_in_zero.pdf}}\put(0.46316,0.23225){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\phi(x)\big|_{x=0}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{open_phi_in_zero.pdf}}\end{picture}}
}}\,.

(4.259)

4.3.2.4
Correlation functions of boundary fields

Let us first consider the generic 1-point function of a boundary field, which is given by

\langle\phi^{(h)}(x)\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}=A\delta_{h,\,0}\,.

(4.260)

On the other hand, the generic 2-point function is given by

\langle\phi_{1}^{(h_{1})}(x_{1})\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(x_{2})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}=\frac{C}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2h_{1}}}\,\delta_{h1,\,h2}\,,

(4.261)

where
x_{1}>x_{2}
, otherwise the correlator has no meaning. The ordering on the boundary is indeed uniquely determined by the real numbers ordering (see fig.
4.15
), and boundary fields cannot overstep one another.

Figure 4.15:
The boundary ordering in the 2-point function case.

This means that
\langle\phi_{1}^{(h_{1})}(x_{1})\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(x_{2})\rangle
is different than
\langle\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(x_{1})\phi_{1}^{(h_{1})}(x_{2})\rangle
, although in some cases they can coincide.

Finally, the generic 3-point function is

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{1}^{(h_{1})}(x_{1})\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(x_{2})\phi_{3}^{(h_{3})}(x_{3})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}=

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{C_{123}}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}}(x_{2}-x_{3})^{-h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}}(x_{1}-x_{3})^{h_{1}-h_{2}+h_{3}}}\,.

(4.262)

We can indeed map

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x_{1}&\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \Lambda\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \infty\\
x_{2}&\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ 1\\
x_{3}&\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ 0\end{cases}

(4.263)

using the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
transformation
f
and then write

\displaystyle\langle\phi_{1}^{(h_{1})}(x_{1})\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(x_{2})\phi_{3}^{(h_{3})}(x_{3})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}=

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\,=\langle f\circ\phi_{1}^{(h_{1})}(x_{1})f\circ\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(x_{2})f\circ\phi_{3}^{(h_{3})}(x_{3})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}

\displaystyle\quad\,\,\,=\left[f^{\prime}(x_{1})\right]^{h_{1}}\left[f^{\prime}(x_{2})\right]^{h_{2}}\left[f^{\prime}(x_{3})\right]^{h_{3}}\langle\phi_{1}^{(h_{1})}(\Lambda)\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(1)\phi_{3}^{(h_{3})}(0)\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}\cdot\frac{\Lambda^{2h_{1}}}{\Lambda^{2h_{1}}}\\
