\,\end{subarray}}{=}\frac{1}{\Lambda^{2h_{1}}}\left[f^{\prime}(x_{1})\right]^{h_{1}}\left[f^{\prime}(x_{2})\right]^{h_{2}}\left[f^{\prime}(x_{3})\right]^{h_{3}}\langle\mathcal{I}\circ\phi^{(h_{1})}_{1}\left(-\frac{1}{\Lambda}\right)\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(1)\phi_{3}^{(h_{3})}(0)\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\Lambda\to\infty}{=}\frac{C_{123}}{(x_{1}-x_{2})^{h_{1}+h_{2}-h_{3}}(x_{2}-x_{3})^{-h_{1}+h_{2}+h_{3}}(x_{1}-x_{3})^{h_{1}-h_{2}+h_{3}}}\,,

(4.264)

where we defined

C_{123}=\langle\mathcal{I}\circ\phi^{(h_{1})}_{1}(-\frac{1}{\Lambda})\phi_{2}^{(h_{2})}(1)\phi_{3}^{(h_{3})}(0)\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}\,.

(4.265)

We can also evaluate the
n
-point function of plane waves:

\displaystyle\langle e^{iP_{1}\cdot X}(x_{1})\cdots e^{iP_{n}\cdot X}(x_{n})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}^{\text{(N)}}=

\displaystyle\quad=\langle e^{2iP_{1}\cdot X_{L}}(z_{1})\cdots e^{2iP_{n}\cdot X_{L}}(z_{n})\rangle\Big|_{z_{i}\to x_{i}}

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{k<l}e^{\langle 2iP_{k}\cdot X_{L}(z_{k})2iP_{l}\cdot X_{L}(z_{l})\rangle}\Big|_{z_{i}\to x_{i}}\cdot\delta\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}P_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{k<l}e^{-4P_{k}\cdot P_{l}\langle X_{L}(z_{k})X_{L}(z_{l})\rangle}\Big|_{z_{i}\to x_{i}}\cdot\delta\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}P_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{k<l}e^{4P_{k}\cdot P_{l}\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\log(z_k-z_l)}\Big|_{z_{i}\to x_{i}}\cdot\delta\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}P_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\quad=\prod_{k<l}(x_{k}-x_{l})e^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{k}\cdot P_{l}}\cdot\delta\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}P_{k}\right)\,.

(4.266)

Notice that thanks to the boundary ordering, given by
\prod_{k<l}
the quantities
(x_{k}-x_{l})e^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{k}\cdot P_{l}}
have no phase ambiguities.

4.3.2.5
Boundary fields and
D(p)
-branes

Given the
D(p)-D(p)
branes system shown in fig.
3.6
, we can write its Chan-Paton as

\begin{pmatrix}\ket{\text{AA}}&\ket{\text{AB}}\\[5.0pt]
\ket{\text{BA}}&\ket{\text{BB}}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(4.267)

where

\displaystyle\ket{\text{AA}}

\displaystyle=\phi^{\text{(AA)}}(x=0)\ket{0}=\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{BC_AA.pdf}}\put(0.3511,0.20734){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\phi^{\text{(AA)}}(0)$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{BC_AA.pdf}}\put(0.03667,0.17999){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}A\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.88844,0.1822){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}A\end{tabular}}}}\end{picture}}
}}\,,

\displaystyle\ket{\text{AB}}

\displaystyle=\phi^{\text{(AB)}}(x=0)\ket{0}=\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{BC_AB.pdf}}\put(0.3511,0.20734){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\phi^{\text{(AB)}}(0)$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{BC_AB.pdf}}\put(0.03667,0.17999){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}A\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.88844,0.1822){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}B\end{tabular}}}}\end{picture}}
}}\,,

\displaystyle\ket{\text{BA}}

\displaystyle=\phi^{\text{(BA)}}(x=0)\ket{0}=\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{BC_BA.pdf}}\put(0.3511,0.20734){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\phi^{\text{(BA)}}(0)$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{BC_BA.pdf}}\put(0.03667,0.17999){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}B\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.88844,0.1822){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}A\end{tabular}}}}\end{picture}}
}}\,,

\displaystyle\ket{\text{BB}}

\displaystyle=\phi^{\text{(BB)}}(x=0)\ket{0}=\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{BC_BB.pdf}}\put(0.3511,0.20734){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\phi^{\text{(BB)}}(0)$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{BC_BB.pdf}}\put(0.03667,0.17999){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}B\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.88844,0.1822){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}B\end{tabular}}}}\end{picture}}
}}\,.

These
\phi^{\text{(AB)}}
and
\phi^{\text{(BA)}}
are called
boundary condition changing operators
, since they change the BC. They obey the boundary operator algebra:

\phi_{i}^{\text{(AB)}}(x)\phi_{j}^{\text{(CD)}}(y)\sim\delta^{\text{BC}}\sum_{k}C_{ijk}^{\text{ABD}}\phi_{k}^{\text{(AD)}}(y)\,,

(4.269)

where the lower indices give the Virasoro representation of the considered field.
Notice that the product
\phi_{i}^{\text{(AB)}}(x)\phi_{j}^{\text{(CD)}}(y)
vanishes unless
\text{B}=\text{C}
(see fig.
4.16
). This is thus analogous to matrix multiplication.

Figure 4.16:
The boundary operator algebra.

Let us consider, for example, the field
\phi^{\text{(ND)}}(0)
, which changes the BC from Neumann to Dirichlet. If we have
\partial X(z)
in the bulk and we place it on the boundary, we realize that, if we place it before or after the boundary condition changing operator, it turns into a different boundary field (see fig.
4.17
).

Figure 4.17:
The action of the boundary changing field
\phi^{\text{(ND)}}(0)
on the operator
\partial X(z)
.

Let us now consider two parallel
D(p)
-branes separated along a common Dirichlet direction
y
at a distance
\Delta_{y}
, as shown in fig.
4.18
. Since the stretched strings have a BC that on the first
D(p)
-brane is given by
X_{0}=0
while on the second
D(p)
-brane is given by
X_{0}=\Delta_{y}
, there is a change of boundary condition. We can call
\Delta(x)
the operator responsible for that.

Figure 4.18:
Two parallel
D(p)
-branes separated along a common Dirichlet direction
y
at a distance
\Delta_{y}
, also shown in the BCFT language through the boundary changing operator
\Delta(x)
applied in the origin.

We shall then study the
N=0
level. Recalling eq. (
3.194
) and (
3.212
), we have that the Chan-Paton reads as

\begin{pmatrix}\ket{\phi^{11}}&\ket{\phi^{12}}\\[5.0pt]
\ket{\phi^{21}}&\ket{\phi^{22}}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(4.270)

where the tachyon starting and ending on the same
D(p)
-brane is

\ket{\phi^{11}}=t(P)e^{iP\cdot X}(0)\ket{0}=t(P)\ket{0,P}

(4.271)

(the same holds for
\ket{\phi^{22}}
), while the stretched tachyon is

\ket{\phi^{12}}=t(P)e^{iP\cdot X}\Delta(0)\ket{0}=t(P)\ket{0,P}^{1,2}

(4.272)

(the same holds for
\ket{\phi^{21}}
). The field
\Delta(x)
inserted in the origin is the boundary condition changing operator, which shifts the Dirichlet modulus from 0 (first
D(p)
-brane) to
\Delta_{y}
(second
D(p)
-brane).
It can be written as a plane wave in the Dirichlet direction
y
, along which the two branes are separated:

\Delta(x)=e^{i\frac{\Delta_{y}}{\pi\alpha^{\prime}}X_{L}^{y}}(x)\,.

(4.273)

Moreover we have that

T(z)\Delta(x)=\left(\frac{\Delta_{y}}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\frac{\Delta(x)}{(z-x)^{2}}+\frac{\partial\Delta(x)}{(z-x)}+\text{(regular terms)}\,.

(4.274)

The stretched tachyon conformal weight is now
h=\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}+\left(\frac{\Delta_{y}}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}=1
, which means

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=\left(\frac{\Delta_{y}}{2\pi\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}-1
, exactly as we found previously using oscillators.

4.3.3
Ghost
(b,c)
-BCFT and BRST

We can finally briefly build the boundary CFT in the ghost sector. Recalling that
[Q_{B},b(z)]=T(z)
and remembering the gluing condition (
4.202
) for the stress-energy tensor, we can write the gluing condition for the
b
-ghost as

b(z)=\bar{b}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}\,.

(4.275)

Moreover, since we have (see eq. (
4.169
)) that

\displaystyle b(z_{1})c(z_{2})=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,,

(4.276a)

\displaystyle\bar{b}(\bar{z}_{1})\bar{c}(\bar{z}_{2})=\frac{1}{(\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,,

(4.276b)

we can write the gluing condition for the
c
-ghost as

c(z)=\bar{c}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}\,.

(4.277)

The BRST charge is thus

Q_{B}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}c_{n}L_{-n}^{\text{(m)}}+\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{c_{n}L_{-n}^{\text{(gh)}}}:\,,

(4.278)

and

Q_{B}=\bar{Q}_{B}\,.

(4.279)

4.3.4
Open string physical states

Following the usual logic, open string physical states are elements of the gh=1 open string cohomology, which can be written as

c{\cal V}(x)\,,

(4.280)

where
{\cal V}
is a matter boundary primary of weight one. This is the
non integrated
vertex operator. Taking advantage of the fact that
[Q,{\cal V}(x)]=\partial(c{\cal V})(x)
, we can also construct the integrated vertex operator as

\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\cal V}(x)\,,

(4.281)

which, just like the closed string case, is BRST invariant up to possible boundary terms which are vanishing in the case of generic amplitudes.

4.4
Open-Closed Correlation Functions

We now want to give a general idea of which are the possible string interactions.
The three fundamental interactions are the following.

•

CCC: it is the product of two
3
-point functions (holomorphic
\times
anti-holomorphic) and is conformally equivalent (
\sim
) to the Riemann sphere with
3
punctures (i.e. insertions of closed string vertex operators), which can also be seen as the the complex plane with
3
fields inserted.

Figure 4.19:
CCC correlation function.

•

OOO: it is an holomorphic 3-point function and is conformally equivalent to the disk with
3
punctures on the boundary, which can also be seen as the UHP with
3
fields inserted on the real axes.

Figure 4.20:
OOO correlation function.

•

CO: it is an holomorphic 3-point function, where C is bilocal (holomorphic
+
anti-holomorphic) and is conformally equivalent to the disk with a puncture in the bulk and a puncture on the boundary, which can also be seen as the UHP with one field inserted in the bulk and one on the real axes.

Figure 4.21:
CO correlation function.

Given these fundamental ingredients, we can build the following four categories of correlation functions.

\bullet\,
Closed strings only

If we write the generic closed string operator as

\phi_{i}(z_{i},\bar{z_{i}})=\sum_{k_{i},l_{i}}\left(\Phi_{i}\right)_{k_{i}l_{i}}\mathcal{V}_{k_{i}}(z_{i})\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{l_{i}}(\bar{z_{i}})\,,

(4.282)

the generic purely bulk (no boundary) correlation function is given by (see fig.
4.22
)

\langle\phi_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\cdots\phi_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{k_{i},l_{i}}\left(\Phi_{i}\right)_{k_{i}l_{i}}\right)\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{k_{i}}(z_{i})\rangle\cdot\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\mathcal{V}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{l_{i}}(\bar{z_{i}})\rangle\,.

(4.283)

Figure 4.22:
A generic correlation function of
n
closed strings.

\bullet\,
Closed strings with
D(p)
-brane

If we use the doubling trick to write the generic closed operator as

\phi_{i}(z_{i},\bar{z_{i}})=\sum_{k_{i},l_{i}}\left(\Phi_{i}\right)_{k_{i}l_{i}}\Omega_{i}\mathcal{V}_{k_{i}}(z_{i})\mathcal{V}_{l_{i}}(z^{*}_{i})\,,

(4.284)

where
\Omega_{i}
is the gluing map, the generic purely bulk with boundary correlation function is given by (see fig.
4.23
)

\langle\phi_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\cdots\phi_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}^{\text{(A)}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{k_{i},l_{i}}\left(\Phi_{i}\right)_{k_{i}l_{i}}\Omega_{i}\right)\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{k_{i}}(z_{i})\mathcal{V}_{l_{i}}(z^{*}_{i})\rangle\,,

(4.285)

where A is the boundary condition. The correlation function, thanks to the doubling trick, is completely holomorphic, but it is now a
2n
-point function.

Figure 4.23:
A generic correlation function of
n
closed strings with a
D(p)
-brane.

\bullet\,
Open strings only

If we write the generic boundary field as

\psi_{i}(x_{i})=\Psi_{i}\mathcal{V}_{i}(x_{i})\,,

(4.286)

where
\Psi_{i}
is a matrix and
\mathcal{V}_{i}(x_{i})
is a boundary vertex operator, the generic purely boundary (no bulk) correlation function is given by (see fig.
4.24
)

\langle\psi_{1}(x_{1})\cdots\psi_{n}(x_{n})\rangle_{\text{\tiny UHP}}^{\text{(A)}}=\Tr{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Psi_i}\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}(x_{i})\rangle\,.

(4.287)

Figure 4.24:
A generic correlation function of
n
open strings.

\bullet\,
Open strings and closed strings

If we consider a process involving open strings and closed strings, the generic mixed (bulk with boundary) correlation function is given by (see fig.
4.25
)

\displaystyle\langle\psi_{1}(x_{1})\cdots\psi_{n}(x_{n})\phi_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\cdots\phi_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle=

\displaystyle\quad=\Tr{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Psi_i}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\sum_{k_{i},l_{i}}\left(\Phi_{i}\right)_{k_{i}l_{i}}\Omega_{i}\right)\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{i}(x_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{V}_{k_{i}}(z_{i})\mathcal{V}_{l_{i}}(z^{*}_{i})\rangle\,.

(4.288)

Figure 4.25:
A generic correlation function of
n
open strings and
m
closed strings.

Chapter 5
String Perturbation Theory

5.1
Closed Strings Amplitudes

We are now ready to compute simple string scattering amplitudes. At first it is necessary to fix the string background that typically will force us to work in a BCFT due to the presence of
D
-branes.

We can start with the simplest case, namely no
D
-branes in the background, just closed strings. Scattering amplitudes are
\mathbb{C}
-numbers giving the probability of transition from an
\ket{\mathrm{in}}
state to an
\ket{\mathrm{out}}
state (see fig.
5.1
).

Figure 5.1:
Closed strings scattering.

Since string theory is constructed from a
2d
field theory with Diff
\times
Weyl gauge symmetry, its scattering amplitudes will be naturally associated to correlation functions of gauge invariant operators in the
2d
theory. The correlation functions will be evaluated using the Polyakov path integral, with insertions of gauge invariant operators. What gauge invariant insertions can produce the string physical states? We already know the answer from the previous chapter, where we saw that the vertex operators
\mathcal{V}
are gauge invariant by construction and they represent the physical string states. Then the typical
n
-strings amplitude will have the form

\mathcal{A}\left(1,\dots,n\right)=\int\frac{\mathcal{D}h_{\alpha\beta}\mathcal{D}X^{\mu}}{\mathrm{Vol}\left(\mathcal{G}\right)}\,\mathscr{V}_{1}\dots\mathscr{V}_{n}\,e^{-S[h,x]}\,.

(5.1)

Notice that we are now using an Euclidean path integral in two dimensions, because we have Wick rotated the worldsheet theory to have a standard CFT description. The most natural choice for these
\mathcal{V}_{i}
falls back to the integrated vertex operators which in covariant language (before fixing conformal gauge) can be written as

\mathscr{V}_{i}=\int d^{2}\sigma\,\sqrt{h}\,\mathcal{V}_{i}(\sigma)\,,

(5.2)

which are diff-invariant a priori being integrated over the entire worldsheet. As we will see shortly, we will have to sharpen this intuition.

A key aspect in all of this discussion is represented by the metric integral
\mathcal{D}h
. Previously, when we were only interested in the BRST structure of the free string, we were implicilty working on a worldsheet with the topology of a sphere. On a more general ground we have now to understand the integral over metrics to also include all possible two-dimensional topologies. This is indeed the prescription of the path integral: to some over all possible paths (in this case surfaces) connecting the initial and the final configurations, described by the vertex operators
\mathscr{V}_{i}
. It is therefore more appropriate to explicitly write

\int\mathcal{D}h_{\alpha\beta}=\sum_{g\in\mathrm{Top}}\int\mathcal{D}h^{(g)}_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(5.3)

where
h^{(g)}_{\alpha\beta}
is the generic metric (to be integrated over) on the worldsheet with topology
g
. To fully grasp the meaning of this summation we can now use a powerful result of low dimensional topology, the
classification theorem of closed surfaces
, which states that the topology of all orientable surfaces without boundary is classified by the genus
g
, i.e. the number of “handles” (see fig.
5.2
).

Figure 5.2:
The surfaces classification in term of the genus
g
.

In other words, a closed surface without boundary and genus
g
is homeomorphic to a connected sum (see fig.
5.3
) of
g
-torii. If
g=0
, the surface is homeomorphic to a sphere.

Figure 5.3:
The connected sum of two surfaces
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
is a new surface
S_{1}\#S_{2}
obtained by deleting a disk from
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
and gluing together the remaining boundary circles.

To better account these multiple worldsheet topologies, let us remind that on any
d
-dimensional Riemannian manifold it is possible to add to the action a purely metric term, namely the (euclidean) Einstein-Hilbert action:

S_{EH}=\frac{1}{2^{d}\pi}\int d^{d}x\,\sqrt{h}\,R^{(d)}\,.

(5.4)

Here the metric can be interpreted as a dynamical local field with a number of massless propagating degrees of freedom (gravitons) equal to

\#\mathrm{d.o.f.}=\frac{d(d-3)}{2}\quad d\geq 3\,.

(5.5)

It is clear then that for
d\leq 3
we do not get any local degrees of freedom. In particular in
d=2
the above action becomes a topological invariant. The Gauss-Bonnet theorem states that for a closed surface without boundary we have

S_{EH}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int d^{2}z\,\sqrt{h}\,R^{(2)}=\chi\,,

(5.6)

where
\chi
is the
Euler characteristic
of the surface. Given a polyhedron we have

\chi=\mathrm{Vertices}-\mathrm{Edges}+\mathrm{Faces}.

(5.7)

Therefore for a generic surface
S
the Euler number can be computed considering any polyhedron homeomorphic to the surface
S
. It is possible to prove that this computation yields

\chi=2-2g\,,

(5.8)

with
g
the genus of S. See fig.
5.4

for the example of the torus and an homeomorphic polyhedron.

Figure 5.4:
The torus and an homeomorphic polyhedron.

Now, being
S_{EH}
a topological term, it is also Diff
\times
Weyl invariant and we can freely add it to the Polyakov action
1
1

1

There is a more physical reason to do such a thing and we will explore it later.
:

S_{T}[h,X]=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha}\int d^{2}z\,\sqrt{-h}\,h^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X_{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\mu}+\frac{\lambda}{4\pi}\underbrace{\int d^{2}z\,\sqrt{-h}R^{(2)}}_{=8\pi(1-g)}\,.

(5.9)

This addition has an important effect in the path integral, since

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{g}e^{-2\lambda(1-g)}\int\frac{\mathcal{D}h^{(g)}_{\alpha\beta}\mathcal{D}X^{\mu}}{\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{G})}\,e^{-S_{\mathrm{Pol}}[h,X]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{g}(e^{\lambda})^{2(g-1)}\int\frac{\mathcal{D}h^{(g)}_{\alpha\beta}\mathcal{D}X^{\mu}}{\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{G})}\,e^{-S_{\mathrm{Pol}}[h,X]}\,.

(5.10)

Indeed we see that the summation over all the topologies is transforming into a sort of loop expansion (see fig.
5.5
) in terms of the
string coupling constant

g_{s}=e^{\lambda},

with a loop counting parameter represented by genus of the Riemann surface. Notice that at this level it seems that this new constant
\lambda
is yet a new free parameter of string theory (together with
\alpha^{\prime}
) . On the contrary we will later see that
\lambda
is nothing but the expectation value of the dilaton field
\phi
and that the string dynamics is also fixing its own coupling constant.

Figure 5.5:
The loop expansion.

What happens now if we insert the vertex operators to compute the
n
-point amplitude
\mathcal{A}(1,...,n)
? Each vertex insertion appears as a puncture on the worldsheet, which therefore becomes a punctured Riemann surface. This changes the topology so we expect to see a change in the Euler characteristic.

To understand if this intuition is correct we take as an example a simple two-point function on a sphere(
g=0
). Intuitively we can think of the two punctures as two semi-infinite tubes. We can further imagine that these two infinite tubes meet and join at infinity thus forming a degenerate handle (see fig.
5.6
) as this does not change the curvature.

Figure 5.6:
A sphere with two punctures has the same Euler number of a sphere with an infinite handle.

In this configuration the Euler characteristics shifts from
\chi=2
to
\chi=0
.
This allows for an easy generalization: for any Riemann surface with characteristic
\chi_{0}
, the insertion of
2n
punctures will be the same (as far as
\chi
is concerned) as the insertion of
n
infinite handles. Therefore the punctured Riemann surface has
\chi=\chi_{0}-2n
. This implies that for any vertex operator the Euler characteristic is reduced by 1 and we get the general formula

\chi(\Sigma_{g,n})=2(1-g)-n

(5.11)

for a surface
\Sigma_{g,n}
with genus
g
and
n
punctures.

The same result can be obtained in a more geometrical way noticing that a puncture
z^{*}
can be interpreted as a thin hyperbolic spike centered in the puncture and extending to infinity (see fig.
5.7
) which has the geometric effect of shifting the Ricci scalar by a negative delta-function term so that

\chi_{0}\,\longrightarrow\,\chi=\chi_{0}-\int d^{2}z\,\sqrt{-h}\delta^{(2)}(z-z^{*})=\chi_{0}-1\,,

(5.12)

which coincides with the previous result.

Figure 5.7:
A sphere with two punctures is homeomorphic to a sphere with two infinite spikes centered in the punctures.

The physical effect of what we just said is that any
n
point function is additionally suppressed by a factor of
g_{s}^{n}
with respect the vacuum bubble (0-point amplitude):

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(1,...,n)

\displaystyle=g_{s}^{n}\sum_{g=0}^{\infty}g_{s}^{2(g-1)}\int\frac{\mathcal{D}h^{g}\mathcal{D}X}{\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{G})}\,\mathscr{V}_{1}\dots\mathscr{V}_{n}\,e^{-S_{\mathrm{Pol}}[h,X]}

\displaystyle=g_{s}^{n}\sum_{g=0}^{\infty}g_{s}^{2(g-1)}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathscr{V}_{1}\dots\mathscr{V}_{n}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}^{(g)}\,.

(5.13)

This is the most general form of closed string scattering amplitude: a correlation function of gauge invariant operators in a
2d
quantum gravity!

5.1.1
Tree level amplitudes

Let’s now analyze the first order of the perturbative series, genus zero. This is the tree-level contribution. The
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathscr{V}_{1}\dots\mathscr{V}_{n}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}^{(0)}
is now an integrated correlation function on the complex plane, which we have studied in detail in the previous chapter

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathscr{V}_{1}\dots\mathscr{V}_{n}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathcal{D}h^{g}\mathcal{D}X}{\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{G})}\int d^{2}z_{1}\sqrt{h}\dots d^{2}z_{n}\sqrt{h}\,\mathcal{V}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}\,e^{-S_{\mathrm{Pol}}[h,X]}

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}c\mathcal{D}b\,d^{2}z_{1}\dots d^{2}z_{n}\,\mathcal{V}_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n}),e^{-S[X,b,c]}

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}z_{1}\dots d^{2}z_{n}\,\underbrace{\langle\mathcal{V}_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle}_{\text{CFT correlator on $\mathbb{C}$}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{0}\,,

(5.14)

where in the second step we gauge-fixed the metric to
h_{\alpha\beta}=\delta_{\alpha\beta}
.

Now we must be careful because we are integrating over a CFT correlator which is invariant under the action of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
. This means that inside
\langle\mathcal{V}_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle
the integral over
n
variables will necessarily give a divergent result, proportional to the infinite volume of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
. On the other hand it seems that we can just put this amplitude to zero because
\langle\mathcal{V}_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle
is the correlator of a
\mathrm{ghost}\otimes\mathrm{matter}
CFT, and because the
\mathcal{V}_{i}
are all in the matter sector, in the ghost sector we are left with

\langle 0|1|0\rangle=\innerproduct{0}{0}=0\,.

(5.15)

We know indeed that the non vanishing correlator is not
\langle 1\rangle
, but instead
\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial^{2}c\,\partial c\,c\rangle=\langle c_{-1}\,c_{0}\,c_{1}\rangle=1
and analogously for the anti-holomorphic sector. We are then getting a sort of undetermined product

\mathcal{A}_{0}=0\,\times\,\infty\,,

so it is clear that we must be very careful with this computation.

Let us start to better understand where the limit
\mathcal{A}_{0}\rightarrow 0
is coming from by analyzing explicitly the ghost part of the path integral:

\int\mathcal{D}b\mathcal{D}\bar{b}\mathcal{D}c\mathcal{D}\bar{c}\,(1)\,e^{-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int d^{2}z(b\bar{\partial}c+\bar{b}\partial\bar{c})}\,.

(5.16)

At first we notice that the action in the exponential does not depend on the zero modes of
c
and
\bar{c}
, namely the solutions of the equations
\partial\bar{c}=0,\,\bar{\partial}c=0
which extend globally on the complex plane. Focusing on
c
it is obvious that the zero modes do not depend on the anti-holomorphic coordinate and in general can be written as a series expansion around 0:

c^{(0)}(z)=\sum_{n\leq 1}c_{n}z^{-n+1}\,,

(5.17)

where we imposed
c_{n>1}=0
to assure regularity in 0. However this is not the end of the story, because for this solution to extend correctly over all the complex plane we must also make sure that the solution is regualar at infinity. To do so we perform a coordinate change
z\rightarrow-1/z
so that the zero mode transforms as

\mathcal{I}\circ c^{(0)}(z)=\left(\frac{1}{z^{2}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{n\leq 1}c_{n}(-1)^{n}z^{n-1}=\sum_{n\leq 1}c_{n}(-1)^{n}z^{n+1}\,,

(5.18)

which is regular at
z=0
only if
c_{n<-1}=0
. We are thus left with a simple polynomial solution:

c^{(0)}(z)=c_{1}+c_{0}z+c_{-1}z^{2}\,,

(5.19)

with
c^{(0)}
parameterized by just the three zero modes coefficient
(c_{1},c_{0},c_{-1})
. By the fact that this solution extends globally on
\mathbb{C}
, every other possible field
c
can then be rewritten in the form
c(z)=c^{(0)}(z)+C(z)
with
c^{(0)}
an arbitrary zero mode, and the integration measure takes the form
\mathcal{D}c=\mathcal{D}C\mathcal{D}c^{(0)}=\mathcal{D}C\,dc_{-1}dc_{0}dc_{1}
. The total path integral measure then generally factorizes as
\mathcal{D}c\mathcal{D}\bar{c}=\mathcal{D}C\mathcal{D}\bar{C}\,|dc_{-1}dc_{0}dc_{1}|^{2}
and the origin of the indeterminate value of the amplitude
\mathcal{A}_{0}
becomes clear

\mathcal{A}_{0}=\underbrace{\int d^{2}z_{1}\dots d^{2}z_{n}}_{=\infty}\underbrace{\int\prod_{i=0,\pm 1}dc_{i}d\bar{c}_{i}}_{=0}\underbrace{\langle\mathcal{V}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}\rangle^{\prime}}_{\text{no zero modes}}\,,

(5.20)

since the Berezin integral in
dc_{i}
is identically zero given that
\langle\mathcal{V}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}\rangle^{\prime}
is independent of
c_{i}
.

On these grounds it is clear then that the easiest way to make this integral finite is by changing the vertex operators inside the correlator. Indeed we know that there are two types of vertex operators, the integrated and the non-integrated vertex operators, which are given by
c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}(z,\bar{z})
, and are BRST invariant. Hence if we consider in the amplitude the insertions of three non-integrated operators the ambiguity will be solved if we write

\mathcal{A}_{0}=\int d^{2}z_{4}\dots d^{2}z_{n}\,\langle c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\dots c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}_{3}(z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})\,\mathcal{V}_{4}(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle\,.

(5.21)

The
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
gauge invariance is now fixed because the three non-integrated vertex operators are fixing three points on the sphere. Moreover the three ghost insertions saturate the zero mode integrals giving a non-vanishing result. In particular, thanks to the splitting of the CFT into ghost and matter sectors, we can separately compute the ghost correlator and obtain

\displaystyle\langle c\bar{c}(z_{1})c\bar{c}(z_{2})c\bar{c}(z_{3})\rangle=\underbrace{\bra{0}c_{-1}\bar{c}_{-1}c_{0}\bar{c}_{0}c_{1}\bar{c}_{1}\ket{0}}_{=1}\begin{vmatrix}1&1&1\\
z_{1}&z_{2}&z_{3}\\
z_{1}^{2}&z_{2}^{2}&z_{3}^{2}\end{vmatrix}^{2}=|z_{12}z_{13}z_{23}|^{2}\,,

(5.22)

where we used (
4.182
) and we have fixed the zero mode convention for a non vanishing inner product as above.

It is interesting to notice that the same result can be obtained by treating the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
invariance in the starting
\mathcal{A}_{0}
as a gauge invariance to fix using the Faddeev-Popov method. As a matter of fact, let us consider the initial
\mathcal{A}_{0}
normalized by the volume of
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
:

\mathcal{A}_{0}=\int\frac{d^{2}z_{1}\dots d^{2}z_{n}}{\mathrm{Vol}(SL(2,\mathbb{C}))}\,\langle\mathcal{V}_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle\,.

(5.23)

We can apply the Faddeev-Popov procedure by fixing three points
z_{i}=\hat{z}_{i}
,
\bar{z}_{i}=\bar{\hat{z}}_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
. The gauge fixing functions will then be
f_{i}(z_{i})=z_{i}-\hat{z}_{i},\,\bar{f}_{i}(z_{i})=\bar{z}_{i}-\bar{\hat{z}}_{i}
. Therefore, given the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
action

\delta_{\epsilon}z=\epsilon_{1}+\epsilon_{2}z+\epsilon_{3}z^{2}\,,\quad\delta_{\epsilon}\bar{z}=\bar{\epsilon}_{1}+\bar{\epsilon}_{2}\bar{z}+\bar{\epsilon}_{3}\bar{z}^{2}\,,

(5.24)

the usual resolution of the identity takes the form

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\int|d\epsilon_{1}d\epsilon_{2}d\epsilon_{3}|^{2}|\delta(\epsilon_{1})\delta(\epsilon_{2})\delta(\epsilon_{3})|^{2}=\int[d\epsilon]\prod_{i=1}^{3}|\delta(z_{i}-\hat{z}_{i})|^{2}\,\left|\frac{\delta z_{i}}{\delta\epsilon_{j}}\right|

\displaystyle=\int[d\epsilon]\prod_{i=1}^{3}|\delta(z_{i}-\hat{z}_{i})|^{2}\,\begin{vmatrix}1&1&1\\
\hat{z}_{1}&\hat{z}_{2}&\hat{z}_{3}\\
{\hat{z}}_{1}^{2}&{\hat{z}}_{2}^{2}&{\hat{z}}_{3}^{2}\end{vmatrix}^{2}=\int[d\epsilon]\prod_{i=1}^{3}|\delta(z_{i}-\hat{z}_{i})|^{2}|z_{12}z_{13}z_{23}|^{2}

(5.25)

so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{0}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\left(\int[d\epsilon]\frac{1}{\mathrm{Vol}(SL(2,\mathbb{C}))}\right)}_{=1}\int d^{2}z_{1}\dots d^{2}z_{n}\prod_{i=1}^{3}|\delta(z_{i}-\hat{z}_{i})|^{2}|z_{12}z_{13}z_{23}|^{2}\langle\mathcal{V}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}z_{4}\dots d^{2}z_{n}\,|\hat{z}_{12}\hat{z}_{13}\hat{z}_{23}|^{2}\langle\mathcal{V}_{1}(\hat{z}_{1},\bar{\hat{z}}_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}_{3}(\hat{z}_{3},\bar{\hat{z}}_{3})\mathcal{V}_{4}(z_{4},\bar{z}_{4})\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}(z_{n},\bar{z}_{n})\rangle\,,

(5.26)

which is exactly the result obtained above. The ghost correlator can then be exaclty identified as the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
FP determinant.

Looking at the obtained result, however, it superficially seems that it depends on the position of the three points
\hat{z}_{i},\,i=1,\dots,3
, which clearly would be inconsistent. We will now prove that this dependence is just apparent. Let us indeed suppose to take two different points
\hat{z}_{1},\hat{w}_{1}
and two amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\prime}
computed respectively in
\hat{z}_{1}
and
\hat{w}_{1}
. Then we can consider the difference of the two amplitudes and notice that this difference has again the form of an amplitude, but containing the difference of the unintegrated vertex operator evaluated at the two points:

\mathcal{A}_{0}-\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\prime}\to\left[c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}(\hat{z}_{1},\bar{\hat{z}}_{1})-c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}(\hat{w}_{1},\bar{\hat{w}}_{1})\right].

(5.27)

By definition of vertex operator we can write

c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}(\hat{z}_{1},\bar{\hat{z}}_{1})-c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}(\hat{w}_{1},\bar{\hat{w}}_{1})=\sum_{ij}\Phi_{ij}\left[c\mathcal{V}_{i}(\hat{z}_{1})\bar{c}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(\bar{\hat{z}}_{1})-c\mathcal{V}_{i}(\hat{w}_{1})\bar{c}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(\bar{\hat{w}}_{1})\right]\,.

(5.28)

Using then eq. (
4.195
) we obtain:

\displaystyle c\mathcal{V}_{i}(\hat{z})

\displaystyle=c\mathcal{V}_{i}(\hat{w})-\int_{\hat{z}}^{\hat{w}}d\xi\left[Q,\mathcal{V}_{i}(\xi)\right]\,,

(5.29a)

\displaystyle\bar{c}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}(\bar{\hat{z}})

\displaystyle=\bar{c}\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}(\hat{w})-\int_{\bar{\hat{z}}}^{\bar{\hat{w}}}d\bar{\xi}\left[\bar{Q},\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{i}(\bar{\xi})\right]\,,

(5.29b)

which plugged back into (
5.28
) allows to write

\displaystyle\left[c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}(\hat{z}_{1},\bar{\hat{z}}_{1})-c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}(\hat{w}_{1},\bar{\hat{w}}_{1})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}\Phi_{ij}

\displaystyle\Bigg\{-\left[Q,\int_{\hat{z}}^{\hat{w}}d\xi\,\mathcal{V}_{i}(\xi)\right]\bar{c}\,\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(\bar{\hat{w}})-c\,\mathcal{V}_{i}(\bar{w})\left[\bar{Q},\int_{\bar{\hat{z}}}^{\bar{\hat{w}}}d\bar{\xi}\,\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(\bar{\xi})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\,+\left[Q\bar{Q},\int_{\hat{z}}^{\hat{w}}d\xi\int_{\bar{\hat{z}}}^{\bar{\hat{w}}}d\bar{\xi}\,\mathcal{V}_{i}(\xi)\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{j}(\bar{\xi})\right]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}

\displaystyle\left\{\left[Q,A_{ij}\right]+\left[\bar{Q},B_{ij}\right]+\left[Q\bar{Q},C_{ij}\right]\right\}\,,

(5.30)

which is BRST-exact. Plugging this back in the complete form of
\mathcal{A}_{0}-\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\prime}
we get

\mathcal{A}_{0}-\mathcal{A}_{0}^{\prime}=\int d^{2}z_{4}\dots d^{2}z_{n}\,\langle\left[Q_{B},\dots\right]\mathcal{V}_{2}(\hat{z}_{2},\bar{\hat{z}}_{2})\mathcal{V}_{3}(\hat{z}_{3},\bar{\hat{z}}_{3})\mathcal{V}_{4}\dots\mathcal{V}_{n}\rangle=0\,,

(5.31)

because

\displaystyle Q_{B}c\bar{c}\mathcal{V}(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=0

(5.32)

\displaystyle Q_{B}\int dzd\bar{z}\mathcal{V}(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=0\quad\textrm{(up to boundary terms)}.

(5.33)

For generic external momenta the possible contributions from boundary terms are vanishing and so everything is ok. Repeating the same steps for
z_{2}
and
z_{3}
it is immediate to see that the amplitude does not depend on any of the three coordinates.

5.1.2
Example: Shapiro–Virasoro amplitude

Let’s start with the simpler three-tachyons closed string amplitude. In this case we have to fix all of the three vertex operators and there is no integration:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})=

\displaystyle g_{s}\,t_{1}(p_{1})t_{2}(p_{2})t_{3}(p_{3})\left\langle c\bar{c}e^{ip_{1}\cdot X}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\,c\bar{c}e^{ip_{2}\cdot X}(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\,c\bar{c}e^{ip_{3}\cdot X}(z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{s}\,t_{1}(p_{1})t_{2}(p_{2})t_{3}(p_{3})\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3})\,.

(5.34)

Notice that this is a 3-point function of weight-zero primary bulk fields. Therefore it is a constant and the constant is given by the momentum conserving delta function.
The first non trivial amplitude is the 4-tachyons scattering. In this case we have to fix 3 vertex operators and we have to integrate the 4th over the whole complex plane:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})

\displaystyle=g_{s}^{2}\,t_{1}(p_{1})t_{2}(p_{2})t_{3}(p_{3})t_{4}(p_{4})\times

\displaystyle\quad\times\int d^{2}z\,\left\langle c\bar{c}e^{ip_{1}\cdot X}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\,c\bar{c}e^{ip_{2}\cdot X}(z_{2},\bar{z}_{2})\,c\bar{c}e^{ip_{3}\cdot X}(z_{3},\bar{z}_{3})e^{ip_{4}\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\right\rangle\,.

(5.35)

It is common to choose the fixed positions as
z_{1}=\Lambda\to\infty
,
z_{2}=1
and
z_{3}=0
. The ghost correlator is given by

\left\langle c\bar{c}(\Lambda,\bar{\Lambda})\,c\bar{c}(1,\bar{1})\,c\bar{c}(0,\bar{0})\right\rangle=|(\Lambda-1)\Lambda|^{2}\sim|\Lambda|^{4}\,,

(5.36)

while, using (
4.159
), the matter four-point function gives

\displaystyle\left\langle e^{ip_{1}\cdot X}(\Lambda,\bar{\Lambda})\,e^{ip_{2}\cdot X}(1,\bar{1})\,e^{ip_{3}\cdot X}(0,\bar{0})e^{ip_{4}\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\right\rangle=

\displaystyle\quad=|\Lambda-1|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{1}\cdot p_{2}}\,|\Lambda|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{1}\cdot p_{3}}\,|\Lambda-z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{1}\cdot p_{4}}\,|1-z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{2}\cdot p_{4}}\,|z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{3}\cdot p_{4}}\delta(P)

\displaystyle\quad\sim|\Lambda|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{1}\cdot(p_{2}+p_{3}+p_{4})}|1-z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{2}\cdot p_{4}}\,|z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{3}\cdot p_{4}}\delta(P)

\displaystyle\quad=|\Lambda|^{-\alpha^{\prime}p_{1}\cdot p_{1}}|1-z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{2}\cdot p_{4}}\,|z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{3}\cdot p_{4}}=|\Lambda|^{-4}|1-z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{2}\cdot p_{4}}\,|z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{3}\cdot p_{4}}\delta(P)\,,

(5.37)

where we have used momentum conservation
P\equiv p_{1}+p_{2}+p_{3}+p_{3}=0
and the mass-shell condition
\alpha^{\prime}p_{i}^{2}=4
.
Putting together matter and ghost, the
\Lambda
-dependence cancels and we are left with the total amplitude

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=

\displaystyle g_{s}^{2}\,\prod_{i=1}^{4}t_{i}(p_{i})\delta(P)\int d^{2}z\,|1-z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{2}\cdot p_{4}}\,|z|^{\alpha^{\prime}p_{3}\cdot p_{4}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{s}^{2}\,\prod_{i=1}^{4}t_{i}(p_{i})\delta(P)\int d^{2}z\,|1-z|^{-\alpha^{\prime}\frac{t}{2}-4}\,|z|^{-\alpha^{\prime}\frac{s}{2}-4}\,,

(5.38)

where we have defined the Mandelstam invariants

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\equiv-(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}=-(p_{3}+p_{4})^{2}\,,

(5.39)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\equiv-(p_{1}+p_{3})^{2}=-(p_{2}+p_{4})^{2}\,,

(5.40)

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle\equiv-(p_{1}+p_{4})^{2}=-(p_{3}+p_{2})^{2}\,,

(5.41)

which in the present case obey

s+t+u=4\times\left(-\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)\,.

(5.42)

We can now use the relevant integration formula
2
2

2

See, for example, Tong’s lecture notes for a detailed proof.

\int\,d^{2}z\,|z|^{2(a-1)}\,|1-z|^{2(b-1)}=2\pi\frac{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\Gamma(1-a-b)}{\Gamma(1-a)\Gamma(1-b)\Gamma(a+b)}\,,

(5.43)

to finally write the
Shapiro-Virasoro
amplitude

\mathcal{A}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})=2\pi g_{s}^{2}\prod_{i=1}^{4}t_{i}(p_{i})\delta(P)\frac{\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}s}{4}-1\right)\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}t}{4}-1\right)\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}u}{4}-1\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}s}{4}+2\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}t}{4}+2\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}u}{4}+2\right)}\,.

(5.44)

This is an example of an amplitude exhibiting
channel duality
, which means invariance under exchange
s\leftrightarrow t\leftrightarrow u
. In a usual field theory this would be achieved only after explicitly adding the three channel contributions (just think of a scalar
\phi^{3}
theory, for example). Here instead the amplitude already contains the three channels, without having to explicitly add them, as we would do with QFT Feynman diagrams. This should not be a surprise, this is indeed a
string
amplitude. For generic values of
t
(and hence of
u
) we can have a look at the
s
-poles of the amplitude. This should give us an indication of the mass of the particles that are exchanged in the
s
-channel. The analysis is easy: we have just to look at the poles of
\Gamma\left(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}s}{4}-1\right)
which, as it is well-known, appear when the argument of the
\Gamma
is a non positive integer. This happens precisely when

\alpha^{\prime}s=\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=4(n-1)\,,\quad n=0,1,2,3,\cdots\,,

(5.45)

which is in fact the full mass spectrum of the closed bosonic string! This is why the dual amplitude cannot come from a usual field theory: it describes the scattering of four scalar (tachyonic) particles that interact by exchanging an infinite tower of massive particles. This amplitude could only be reproduced by a field theory with an
infinite
number of fields!

5.2
Amplitudes involving open strings

Adding open strings in the game means that the worldsheet is a now a manifold with a boundary and the Gauss-Bonnet theorem is modified to

\lambda\chi=\lambda\left(\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{M}d^{2}z\,\sqrt{h}R^{(2)}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial M}ds\,k\right)\,,

(5.46)

where
k
is the geodesic curvature of the boundary
\partial M
, defined as

k=\frac{1}{2}t^{\alpha}t^{\beta}\nabla_{\alpha}n_{\beta}\,,

(5.47)

where
t
and
n
are the tangent and normal (pointing outwards the surface) unit vector to
\partial M
respectively and
\nabla
is the covariant derivative. As an example we can consider a disk with flat euclidean metric, whose boundary has radius
R
and it is parameterized as

\gamma(\theta)=R(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\,,\quad t(\theta)=(-\sin\theta,\cos\theta)\,,\quad n(\theta)=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\,,

(5.48)

so that

k=\frac{1}{R}\,.

(5.49)

Exercise 5.2.1

Prove eq. (
5.49
).

Using this we can calculate the Euler characteristic of the disk as

\chi=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial M}ds\,k=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\partial M}\frac{ds}{R}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\theta}{2\pi}=1\,,

(5.50)

since in this case the bulk term in
\chi
does not contribute. If we do the same computation for an
annulus
we find this time two cancelling contributions (because the normal vectors of the two boundaries of the annulus are oppositely oriented). In general for a worldsheet of genus
g
and
b
boundaries we have

\chi=2(1-g)-b\,.

(5.51)

Moreover, if we also consider
n_{o}
open and
n_{c}
closed string insertions we have an additional change in the value of
\chi
which in the most general case becomes

\chi=2(1-g)-b-n_{c}-\frac{n_{o}}{2}\,.

(5.52)

The
-1/2
factor in front of the open string number
n_{o}
comes from a similar reasoning as the one followed to fix to
-1
the coefficient in front
n_{c}
. Let us indeed suppose to add to a boundary two new boundary punctures. These two can be thought as two infinite spikes coming out from the boundary and fusing at infinity creating a new inner boundary (see fig.
5.8
).

Figure 5.8:
A disk with two punctures on the boundary is homeomorphic to a disk with one more (inner) boundary.

This means that adding 2 boundary punctures changes the value of
\chi
as the insertion of a new boundary in the worldsheet, namely
\Delta\chi=-1
. Because two punctures change the value of
\chi
by
-1
, a single puncture will contribute by a factor of
-1/2
.

Putting all of the above informations together we can then write a complete open-closed string scattering amplitude:

\mathcal{A}(n_{c},n_{o})=g_{s}^{n_{c}+\frac{n_{o}}{2}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}g_{s}^{2(g-1)}\sum_{b=1}^{\infty}g_{s}^{b}\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathcal{V}^{c}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}^{c}_{n_{c}}\mathcal{V}^{o}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}^{o}_{n_{o}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}_{g,b}\,,

(5.53)

where as usual
g_{s}=g_{\rm closed}=e^{\lambda}
. The summation over topologies must now also take care of the presence of boundaries, so it also runs over
b
. We can also notice that for every open string in the amplitude there is an extra factor of
g_{s}^{1/2}
which means that the open string coupling
g_{\rm open}
can be seen as the square root of the closed string coupling:

g_{\rm open}=\sqrt{g_{\rm closed}}\,.

(5.54)

The term
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathcal{V}^{c}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}^{c}_{n_{c}}\mathcal{V}^{o}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}^{o}_{n_{o}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}_{g,b}
denotes a correlation function of gauge invariant operators corresponding to closed and open string punctures, computed on a
2d
manifold with genus
g
and
b
boundaries.

The general form of an open-closed amplitude on a Riemann surface with
b
boundaries is combinatorially rather cumbersome: indeed the path integral prescribes to sum over all possible ways of distributing
n_{o}
open string punctures on the available
b
boundaries and, on every boundary, to integrate over all possible positions on the boundary, paying attention to the path-ordering prescription. Every boundary will then give rise to a trace in the Chan-Paton matrices associated to the open string insertions. In this introductory lectures we will only be interested in tree-level processes, associated with a disk with boundary and bulk punctures which is conformally equivalent to the (punctured) UHP. We will thus consider

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathcal{V}^{c}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}^{c}_{n_{c}}\mathcal{V}^{o}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}^{o}_{n_{o}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}_{g=0,b=1}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}h}{\mathrm{Vol}(\mathcal{G})}\,e^{-S_{\mathrm{Pol}}[X,h]}

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx_{1}\,\dots\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx_{n_{o}}\Tr\mathcal{P}[\mathcal{V}^{o}_{1}(x_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}^{o}_{n_{o}}(x_{n_{o}})]

\displaystyle\quad\times\int_{\rm UHP}d^{2}z_{1}\,\dots\int_{\rm UHP}d^{2}z_{n_{c}}\mathcal{V}^{c}_{1}(z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}^{c}_{n_{c}}(z_{n_{c}},\bar{z}_{n_{c}})\,.

(5.55)

As already discussed, because the boundary is
1
-dimensional we need to take care of the operator ordering. That is why we have introduced the path ordering operator
\mathcal{P}
defined as

\mathcal{P}\left[\dots\mathcal{V}_{i}(x_{i})\dots\mathcal{V}_{j}(x_{j})\dots\right]=\begin{cases}\left[\dots\mathcal{V}_{i}(x_{i})\dots\mathcal{V}_{j}(x_{j})\dots\right]&x_{i}>x_{j}\\[5.0pt]
(-1)^{\epsilon}\left[\dots\mathcal{V}_{j}(x_{j})\dots\mathcal{V}_{i}(x_{i})\dots\right]&x_{j}<x_{i}\end{cases}\,,

(5.56)

where the
(-1)^{\epsilon}
takes care of the grassmannality of the operators.

5.3
Tree level open string amplitudes

Let us now gauge fix Diff
\times
Weyl and focus on a pure open string scattering at tree level

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathcal{V}^{o}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}^{o}_{n_{o}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}_{g=0,b=1}=

\displaystyle\Tr\int\mathcal{D}X\mathcal{D}c\mathcal{D}b\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx_{1}\dots dx_{n_{o}}\,\mathcal{P}\left[\mathcal{V}^{o}_{1}(x_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}^{o}_{n_{o}}(x_{n_{o}})\right]e^{-S[X,b,c]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Tr\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx_{1}\dots dx_{n_{o}}\,\mathcal{P}\left\langle\mathcal{V}^{o}_{1}(x_{1})\dots\mathcal{V}^{o}_{n_{o}}(x_{n_{o}})\right\rangle_{\rm UHP},

(5.57)

where in the last line we have explicitly written the matter+ghost path integral as a BCFT correlator. Just as in the closed string case this expression is formally
0\times\infty
, the zero coming from the unsaturated
c
zero modes and the infinity coming from the overcounting proportional to the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
volume.
To gauge fix this redundancy we need again to introduce three non-integrated operators with an explicit dependence on the boundary

\int dx_{i}\mathcal{V}^{o}_{i}(x_{i})\to c\mathcal{V}^{o}_{i}(x_{i}),\quad i=1,2,3\,.

(5.58)

However, when fixing these three open strings we have to sum over the two configurations which are not cyclically equivalent, for example
(1,2,3)+(1,3,2)
3
3

3

In the gauge unfixed form, with only integrated open string vertex operators, this sum would have been implicitly included in the triple integration
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\,dx_{1}\,dx_{2}\,dx_{3}
.
.
Thus the tree level amplitude takes the form

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\mathcal{V}^{o}_{1}\dots\mathcal{V}^{o}_{n_{o}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}_{g=0,b=1}

\displaystyle=\Tr\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx_{4}\dots dx_{n_{o}}\,\mathcal{P}\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(x_{1})c\mathcal{V}_{2}(x_{2})c\mathcal{V}_{3}(x_{3})\mathcal{V}(x_{4})\dots\mathcal{V}(x_{n_{o}})\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+\Tr\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx_{4}\dots dx_{n_{o}}\,\mathcal{P}\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(x_{1})c\mathcal{V}_{3}(x_{2})c\mathcal{V}_{2}(x_{3})\mathcal{V}(x_{4})\dots\mathcal{V}(x_{n_{o}})\rangle.

(5.59)

Clearly, just as in the closed string case, the amplitude does not depend on the choice of
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})
. Moreover it does also not depend on which vertex operators we decide to fix.

5.3.1
Three tachyons amplitude

At first we consider the simplest example possible: the scattering of three open string tachyons in a
D(25)
-brane background (only Neumann condition) at tree level (
g=0
,
b=1
). The three tachyons will have momenta
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
that by construction must satisfy

P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3}=0,\quad\alpha^{\prime}P_{i}^{2}=1,\quad i=1,\dots,3\,,

(5.60)

and will be created by the simplest matter vertex operator, namely

\mathcal{V}_{i}(x)=t(P_{i}):\mathrel{e^{iP_{i}\cdot X}}:(x)\,.

(5.61)

The amplitude then takes the form

\mathcal{A}(1,2,3)=g_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}{\Big(}\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(x_{1})c\mathcal{V}_{2}(x_{2})c\mathcal{V}_{3}(x_{3})\rangle+\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(x_{1})c\mathcal{V}_{3}(x_{2})c\mathcal{V}_{2}(x_{3})\rangle{\Big)}\,,

(5.62)

where we have summed over the two possible non-equivalent orderings on the boundary of the disk (see fig.
5.9
).

Figure 5.9:
The two possible boundary orderings for three operators.

The computation is easy because we know that

\displaystyle\langle c(x_{1})c(x_{2})c(x_{3})\rangle=x_{12}x_{13}x_{23}\,,

(5.63)

\displaystyle\langle e^{iP_{1}\cdot X}(x_{1})e^{iP_{2}\cdot X}(x_{2})e^{iP_{3}\cdot X}(x_{3})\rangle=x_{12}^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}\cdot P_{2}}x_{13}^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}\cdot P_{3}}x_{23}^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{3}}\,,

(5.64)

where we used the definition
x_{ij}=x_{i}-x_{j}
.
Then squaring
P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3}=0
we get

P_{1}^{2}+P_{2}^{2}+2P_{1}\cdot P_{2}=P_{3}^{2}\,\,\Longrightarrow\,\,P_{1}\cdot P_{2}=-\frac{1}{2\alpha^{\prime}}=P_{i}\cdot P_{j}\quad\text{for}\quad i\neq j

(5.65)

so that the three matter vertices produce a contribution equal to
(x_{12}x_{13}x_{23})^{-1}
that perfectly cancels with the ghost contribution. This gives a result which is independent of the choice of the three points on the boundary of the disk:

\mathcal{A}(1,2,3)=2g_{s}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,t(P_{1})t(P_{2})t(P_{3})\delta(P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3})\,,

(5.66)

where the other ordering (giving the same result) has been included.

5.3.2
Veneziano amplitude

A more complex case is represented by the tree-level
(g=0,b=1)
four-open-string-tachyon amplitude. This amplitude is also known as the
Veneziano Amplitude
. Explicitly we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(1,\dots,4)

\displaystyle=g_{s}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dx\,\mathcal{P}\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(x_{1})c\mathcal{V}_{2}(x_{2})c\mathcal{V}_{3}(x_{3})\mathcal{V}_{4}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle\quad+g_{s}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dx\,\mathcal{P}\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(x_{1})c\mathcal{V}_{3}(x_{3})c\mathcal{V}_{2}(x_{2})\mathcal{V}_{4}(x)\rangle\,.

(5.67)

We start to compute the first of the two integrals. The second will just give the same result in this simple case. Using the
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
invariance to fix arbitrarily
x_{1}=\Lambda\rightarrow\infty,\,x_{2}=1,\,x_{3}=0
the correlators give the following results:

\displaystyle\langle c(\Lambda)c(1)c(0)\rangle

\displaystyle=\Lambda(\Lambda-1)\,,

(5.68)

\displaystyle\langle e^{iP_{1}\cdot X}(\Lambda)e^{iP_{2}\cdot X}(1)e^{iP_{3}\cdot X}(0)e^{iP_{4}\cdot X}(x)\rangle

\displaystyle=(\Lambda-1)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}\cdot P_{2}}\Lambda^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}\cdot P_{3}}(\Lambda-x)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}\cdot P_{4}}\times

\displaystyle\quad\times|x|^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}|1-x|^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}\,.

(5.69)

Notice that the absolute value is a clever shortcut to right a result which is valid for all possible positions of
x
with respect to
0,1
. Since
\Lambda\to\infty
, we take
x<\Lambda
without loosing generality.
When
\Lambda\rightarrow\infty
the first three terms of the matter correlator reduce to

\Lambda^{2\alpha^{\prime}(P_{2}+P_{3}+P_{4})\cdot P_{1}}=\Lambda^{-2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}^{2}}=\Lambda^{-2}\,,

(5.70)

that we have simplified using the on-shell condition
\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}_{i}=1,\,i=1,\dots,4
and the momentum conservation
P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3}+P_{4}=0
. Putting everything together we get

\displaystyle\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(\Lambda)c\mathcal{V}_{2}(1)c\mathcal{V}_{3}(0)\mathcal{V}_{4}(x)\rangle{\Bigg|}_{\Lambda\to\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Lambda(\Lambda-1)}{\Lambda^{2}}{\Bigg|}_{\Lambda\to\infty}|x|^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}|1-x|^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}\ \

\displaystyle=|x|^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}|1-x|^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}\,.

(5.71)

Now we have to integrate this form over the real line, which splits into three different parts:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}dx|x|^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}|1-x|^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}\,,

(5.72)

with

\displaystyle I_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}dx\,x^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}(1-x)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}\,,

(5.73a)

\displaystyle I_{2}=\int_{1}^{\infty}dx\,x^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}(x-1)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}\,,

(5.73b)

\displaystyle I_{3}=\int_{-\infty}^{0}dx\,(-x)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}(1-x)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}\,.

(5.73c)

The second and third integrals can be nicely recast in a form similar to
I_{1}
by changing the integration variable and using momentum conservation plus on-shell condition. For
I_{2}
we choose
x=1/y
,
dx=-dy/y^{2}
so that

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{1}^{0}\frac{dy}{y^{2}}y^{-2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}\left(\frac{1-y}{y}\right)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}dy\,y^{-2-2\alpha^{\prime}(P_{2}+P_{3})\cdot P_{4}}(1-y)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}dx\,x^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}\cdot P_{4}}(1-y)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}\,.

(5.74)

For
I_{3}
on the other hand we define
x=y/(y-1),\,dx=\frac{dy}{(1-y)^{2}}
so that

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=-\int_{1}^{0}\frac{dy}{(1-y)^{2}}\left(\frac{y}{1-y}\right)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}{(1-y)^{-2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}\cdot P_{4}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}dy\,y^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}(1-y)^{-2-2\alpha^{\prime}P_{4}\cdot(P_{3}+P_{2})}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}dx\,x^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}\cdot P_{4}}(1-x)^{2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}\cdot P_{4}}\,.

(5.75)

If we consider the Mandelstam variables:

\displaystyle s=-(P_{1}+P_{2})^{2},\quad t=-(P_{1}+P_{3})^{2},\quad u=-(P_{1}+P_{4})^{2}\,,

(5.76)

with

\displaystyle s+t+u=4\times\left(-\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right),\quad\alpha^{\prime}P_{i}\cdot P_{j}=-2-\alpha^{\prime}s_{ij}\,,

(5.77)

where

\displaystyle s_{12}=s,\quad s_{13}=t,\quad s_{14}=u\,,

(5.78)

it is obvious that the final amplitude can then be written as

\mathcal{A}(1,\dots,4)=2g_{o}^{2}\delta(P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3}+P_{4})\left[I(s,t)+I(t,u)+I(u,s)\right]\,,

(5.79)

where we have introduced the open string coupling
g_{o}^{2}=g_{s}
and the integral function

I(a,b)=\int_{0}^{1}dx\,x^{-2-\alpha^{\prime}a}(1-x)^{-2-\alpha^{\prime}b}=B(-1-\alpha^{\prime}a,\,-1-\alpha^{\prime}b)\,,

(5.80)

with
B
the Euler beta function. The properties of the Beta function are now important to understand the phenomenology.

1.

The property
B(x,y)=B(y,x)
is another example of
channel duality
which is a distinctive feature of strings amplitudes and which is rather unusual for a QFT. In a typical QFT with a finite number of interacting fields one has to add to the
s
channel contribution explicit contributions from the
t
and
u
channels. This is not the case in string theory: the channel duality says that, for example, in the
s
-channel interpretation of
I(s,t)
we don’t have to add the
t
channel since it is already included in the infinite sum of the
s
channel poles. Channel duality was postulated in the prehistory of string theory as a typical property of interactions of hadron resonances organizing themselves into Regge trajectories. In 1968 Veneziano smartly wrote down his amplitude as the simplest elementary function having explicit channel duality, without having an underlying understanding of what kind of theory could give rise to this amplitude. Then a lot of fun came by analyzing the pole structure of that amplitude and this resulted in the birth of String Theory.

2.

Because the scattering is at tree level, we can represent its
s
-channel with the diagram in fig.
5.10
.

t
t
t
t
P_{1}
P_{2}
\sqrt{-s}
P_{3}
P_{4}

Figure 5.10:
The
s
-channel diagram of the Veneziano amplitude.

By looking at the
s
simple poles in the amplitudes we can read-off the mass of the exchanged particles inside the inner propagator. We can then study the singularities of
I(s,t)
writing it in terms of Gamma functions:

I(s,t)=\frac{\Gamma(-\alpha^{\prime}s-1)\Gamma(-\alpha^{\prime}t-1)}{\Gamma(-\alpha^{\prime}(s+t)-2)}\,.

(5.81)

Since near the poles of the
\Gamma
we have

\Gamma(z)=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\frac{1}{z+n}\quad\text{for}\ z\sim-n\in-\mathbb{N}\,,

(5.82)

it is clear that
I(s,t)
has poles for
\alpha^{\prime}s=n-1
exaclty in correspondence of particles of mass
m^{2}=\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}(n-1)
which is precisely the tower of masses of the open string spectrum. This means that the amplitude is correctly describing the interaction of the external states with an infinite tower of massive particles.

3.

We can now expand
I(s,t)
around the
s
poles to get

\displaystyle I(s,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\frac{1}{n-1-\alpha^{\prime}s}\frac{\Gamma(-1-\alpha^{\prime}t)}{\Gamma(-1-n-\alpha^{\prime}t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\frac{1}{n-1-\alpha^{\prime}s}(\alpha^{\prime}t+1)\dots(\alpha^{\prime}t+n+1)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\overset{\makebox[0.0pt]{\mbox{\tiny$t\rightarrow\infty$}}}{\sim}}\ \frac{(\alpha^{\prime}t)^{n}}{s+m_{n}^{2}}\,,

(5.83)

with
\alpha^{\prime}m_{n}=(n-1)
.
Notice that in the numerator we get a contribution
t^{n}
which gives us an information over the spin of the exchanged particle. Indeed for a scattering between scalars due to the exchange of a particle of spin
J
the amplitude is of the form

\mathcal{A}\sim\frac{t^{J}}{s+m^{2}},\quad s\sim-m^{2}\,,

(5.84)

because a typical cubic vertex between two scalars and a spin
J
field (represented by a polarization tensor with
J
fully symmetric Lorentz indices) necessarily brings a power
\sim p^{J}
from the
J
derivatives which have to contract with the polarization in a coupling that, without further specification, will have a rough form
\sim\psi^{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{J}}[\phi\overleftrightarrow{\partial}_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{J}}\phi]
. Therefore a quartic amplitude obtained by 2 cubic vertices connected by a propagator will necessarily have a numerator proportional to
\sim p^{2J}\sim t^{J}
(for large
t
and close to the pole
s\sim-m^{2}
).
This is clearly consistent with what we know from the string spectrum, where at level
n
we find particles with spin
J=n
and lower. The particles with maximal spin
J=n
inside the string spectrum formula gives rise to the mass-spin relation

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=J-1\,,

(5.85)

which is the equation of the first
Regge trajectory
.

At the time of this discovery this was one of the reasons to believe that string theory could describe hadron interactions. Today we know that the fundamental theory behind hadron resonances is instead QCD, a more traditional (but not so traditional at that time!) Yang-Mills theory. At low energy QCD becomes strongly coupled and confines (a physical phenomenon whose theoretical mechanism is still rather mysterious) and quarks get bound with antiquarks by flux tubes which can be well-described by an effective string. Therefore a Yang-Mills Theory like QCD can have, in its strong-coupling phase, a description in terms of effective strings! This is yet another astonishing fact about strings which is at the hearth of the celebrated
AdS/CFT
correspondence.

5.3.3
Three gluons scattering

Let us now consider a background with
N
coincident
D(25)
branes and a three-gluons amplitude. The matter boundary vertex operator is

\mathcal{V}(x)=\mathbb{A}_{\mu}(P):\mathrel{j^{\mu}\,e^{iP\cdot X}}:(x)\quad\text{with}\quad P^{2}=0

(5.86)

and
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}(P)
an
N\times N
matrix such that
P_{\mu}\mathbb{A}^{\mu}=0
. Because we are dealing with a tree level
(g=0,b=1)
three point function the amplitude will be of the same form of (
5.62
), with an overall coupling given by
g_{c}^{2g-2+b-n_{c}/2}=g_{c}^{-2+1-3/2}=g_{c}^{1/2}=g_{o}
:

\mathcal{A}=g_{o}\underbrace{\Tr\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(x_{1})c\mathcal{V}_{2}(x_{2})c\mathcal{V}_{3}(x_{3})\rangle}_{\mathcal{A}_{123}}+g_{o}\underbrace{\Tr\langle c\mathcal{V}_{1}(x_{1})c\mathcal{V}_{3}(x_{2})c\mathcal{V}_{2}(x_{3})\rangle}_{\mathcal{A}_{132}}

(5.87)

where the trace is now done on the
U(N)
indices of
\mathbb{A}_{\mu}(P)
. Focusing now on the first term and choosing again
x_{1}=\Lambda\rightarrow\infty,\ x_{2}=1,\ x_{3}=0
we are left with the following correlator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{123}

\displaystyle=\Tr\langle c\mathbb{A}_{\mu}(P_{1}):\mathrel{j^{\mu}e^{iP_{1}\cdot X}}:(\Lambda)\,c\mathbb{A}_{\nu}(P_{2}):\mathrel{j^{\nu}e^{iP_{2}\cdot X}}:(1)\,c\mathbb{A}_{\rho}(P_{3}):\mathrel{j^{\rho}e^{iP_{3}\cdot X}}:(0)\rangle=

\displaystyle=\Lambda(\Lambda-1)\Tr\left[\mathbb{A}_{\mu}(P_{1})\mathbb{A}_{\nu}(P_{2})\mathbb{A}_{\rho}(P_{3})\right]\times

(5.88)

\displaystyle\times\langle:\mathrel{j^{\mu}e^{iP_{1}\cdot X}}:(\Lambda):\mathrel{j^{\nu}e^{iP_{2}\cdot X}}:(1):\mathrel{j^{\rho}e^{iP_{3}\cdot X}}:(0)\rangle\,.

Now the computation of the matter correlator results to be a little bit more complicated because we have many ways to contract the operators together. Using the properties of the currents and the exponential after a tedious but straightforward calculation we are left with

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{123}

\displaystyle=\Tr[\mathbb{A}_{\mu}^{(1)}\mathbb{A}_{\nu}^{(2)}\mathbb{A}_{\rho}^{(3)}]\left(P_{1}^{\rho}\eta^{\mu\nu}+P_{2}^{\mu}\eta^{\rho\nu}+P_{3}^{\nu}\eta^{\rho\mu}+2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}^{\rho}P_{2}^{\mu}P_{3}^{\nu}\right)\delta(P)\,,

(5.89a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{132}

\displaystyle=\Tr[\mathbb{A}_{\mu}^{(1)}\mathbb{A}_{\rho}^{(3)}\mathbb{A}_{\nu}^{(2)}]\underbrace{\left(P_{1}^{\nu}\eta^{\mu\rho}+P_{3}^{\mu}\eta^{\nu\rho}+P_{2}^{\rho}\eta^{\mu\nu}+2\alpha^{\prime}P_{1}^{\nu}P_{3}^{\mu}P_{2}^{\rho}\right)}_{t_{132}^{\mu\rho\nu}}\delta(P)\,,

(5.89b)

where
P=P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{3}
,
\mathbb{A}^{(i)}=\mathbb{A}^{(i)}(P_{i})
. Now we can put them together and rewrite the full amplitude as

\mathcal{A}=g_{o}\delta(P)\Tr\left[\mathbb{A}_{\mu}^{(1)}\mathbb{A}_{\rho}^{(3)}\mathbb{A}_{\nu}^{(2)}\right](t_{123}^{\mu\nu\rho}+t_{132}^{\mu\rho\nu})+g_{o}\delta(P)t_{123}^{\mu\nu\rho}\Tr\left[\mathbb{A}_{\mu}^{(1)}[\mathbb{A}_{\nu}^{(2)},\mathbb{A}_{\rho}^{(3)}]\right]\,,

(5.90)

where we simply added and subtracted
\delta(P)\Tr[\mathbb{A}_{\mu}^{(1)}\mathbb{A}_{\rho}^{(3)}\mathbb{A}_{\nu}^{(2)}]t^{\mu\nu\rho}_{123}
. Then we can explicitly compute

t_{123}^{\mu\nu\rho}+t_{132}^{\mu\rho\nu}=-P_{1}^{\mu}\left(\eta^{\nu\rho}-2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}^{\nu}P_{2}^{\rho}\right)-P_{2}^{\nu}\left(\eta^{\mu\rho}-2\alpha^{\prime}P_{3}^{\mu}P_{2}^{\rho}\right)-P_{3}^{\rho}\left(\eta^{\mu\nu}-2\alpha^{\prime}P_{2}^{\mu}P_{3}^{\nu}\right)=0

(5.91)

because
P_{i}^{\mu}\mathbb{A}_{\mu}(P_{i})
. So only the commutator term survives and the amplitude takes the form

\mathcal{A}=g_{o}\,\delta(P)t_{123}^{\mu\nu\rho}\Tr\left[\mathbb{A}_{\mu}^{(1)}[\mathbb{A}_{\nu}^{(2)},\mathbb{A}_{\rho}^{(3)}]\right]\,.

(5.92)

This result is very important. Just looking at the spectrum on
N
coincident D-branes we were finding
N^{2}
gauge bosons, but we didn’t have any information about their interactions. Now we know that they interact as in a
U(N)
Yang-Mills theory! In particular for
N=1
we notice that the amplitude goes to zero because
[\mathbb{A}^{(1)},\mathbb{A}^{(2)}]=0
.
Looking at
\mathcal{A}
we can try to write down an effective action that reproduces it. Knowing the free equation of motion derived from the spectrum and looking at the terms in
\mathcal{A}
which are linear in the momenta, we can write the effective lagrangian that reproduce them as

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\supset-\frac{1}{4}\Tr[\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}]^{2}-\frac{1}{2}g_{o}\Tr[\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}[A^{\mu},A^{\nu}]]\,

(5.93)

which are exactly two of the three terms appearing in the Yang-Mills Lagrangian. To see the remaining term, the commutator squared
\Tr\left[[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}][A^{\mu},A^{\nu}]\right]
, we should compute the 4-gluon scattering. This can be done, although not in these introductory lectures, and indeed, after subtracting the contribution from two previously found cubic vertices connected by the gluon propagator, one can reproduce the full YM lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\supset-\frac{1}{4}\Tr[F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}]\,,

(5.94)

where
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}+ig_{\mathrm{YM}}[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}]
and where we identify
g_{\mathrm{YM}}=g_{o}
.

On top of this, inside
\mathcal{A}
we also have a new term
\propto\alpha^{\prime}\,P_{1}P_{2}P_{3}
which is reproduced by a term in the action of the form
\alpha^{\prime}\,\Tr[\partial_{[\mu}A_{\nu]}\partial^{[\nu}A^{\rho]}\partial_{[\rho}A_{\sigma]}]\eta^{\sigma\mu}
. This term is clearly non-renormalizable and IR irrelevant and is an example of an
\alpha^{\prime}
-correction. If we compute the string amplitudes up to 6-points, we would explicitly verify that this term contributes to a completely new non-renormalizable gauge invariant term in the effective Yang-Mills Lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\supset-\frac{1}{4}\Tr[F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+\alpha^{\prime}{F_{\mu}}^{\nu}{F_{\nu}}^{\rho}{F_{\rho}}^{\mu}]+O({\alpha^{\prime}}^{2})\,.

(5.95)

This is an example of how string theory modifies well-known QFT’s like Yang-Mills by adding irrelevant deformations controlled by the string scale. This is how the effective action is trying to tell us that what we are studying is not just a theory of gluons, but it is in fact a (open in this case)
string theory
.

5.4
Strings in Curved Background

In this section we would like to understand how the full Einstein equations are predicted by String Theory.
To do so, let us consider the
non-linear sigma model

which we get by taking the Polyakov action and substituting the flat metric
\eta_{\mu\nu}
with a generic metric
G_{\mu\nu}(X)
on the target space
M
:

S[X]=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}z\,\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}(X)\,.

(5.96)

If we suppose

G_{\mu\nu}(X)=\eta_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}(X)

(5.97)

with infinitesimal
h_{\mu\nu}
, then

\displaystyle S_{\eta+h}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}z\,\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}z\,\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}(X)

\displaystyle=S_{\eta}+\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}z\,V_{h}(z,\bar{z})\,,

(5.98)

where we defined the vertex operator of the graviton as

\mathcal{V}_{h}(z,\bar{z})=\int dp\,h_{\mu\nu}(p)\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}e^{ip\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\,,

(5.99)

using the Fourier transform

h_{\mu\nu}(X)=\int dp\,e^{ip\cdot X}h_{\mu\nu}(p)\,.

(5.100)

Therefore an infinitesimal deformation of the background metric is obtained by deforming the original world-sheet action with the integrated vertex operator of the graviton.

Classically the insertion of
\mathcal{V}_{h}
satisfies conformal invariance.
From the quantum point of view, however,
\mathcal{V}_{h}
has to be a primary of weight
(1,1)
, which is true if (see eq. (
4.155
))

\displaystyle\begin{cases}P^{2}=0\\
P_{\mu}h^{\mu\nu}=h^{\mu\nu}P_{\nu}=0\end{cases}\,,

(5.101)

which imply the linearized Einstein equations.
Hence, when we slightly modify the flat metric, the theory preserves conformal invariance (and thus remains consistent as a string theory) if the fluctuation satisfies the linearized Einstein equations.

To go further, the full path integral is deformed as follows:

\displaystyle Z=\int\mathcal{D}X\,e^{-S[X]-\int d^{2}z\,\mathcal{V}_{h}(z,\bar{z})}=\int\mathcal{D}X\,e^{-S[X]}e^{-\int d^{2}z\,\mathcal{V}_{h}(z,\bar{z})}

(5.102)

with

e^{-\int d^{2}z\,\mathcal{V}_{h}(z,\bar{z})}=1-\int d^{2}z\,\mathcal{V}_{h}(z,\bar{z})-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{2}z^{\prime}\int d^{2}z\,\mathcal{V}_{h}(z,\bar{z})\mathcal{V}_{h}^{\prime}(z^{\prime},\bar{z}^{\prime})+\dots

(5.103)

which is an insertion of a sort of coherent state of gravitons.
In this approach, however, it is not easy to regulate the collisions of the graviton vertex operators, to end up with renormalized operators. This is in principle possible and it would give non-linear conditions on
h_{\mu\nu}
and its space-time derivatives to ensure that the renormalized operators remain conformal. If we could perform this
conformal perturbation theory
to every order we would then get the full space-time equations for
h_{\mu\nu}
and we could compare them with Einstein equation. However we don’t know how to do this in generality. Let us thus follow an alternative route and reconsider the initial sigma-model (
5.96
). If we expand
G_{\mu\nu}
nearby a
X_{0}\in M
we get

G_{\mu\nu}(X)=G_{\mu\nu}(X_{0})+G_{\mu\nu,\rho}(X_{0})Y^{\rho}+G_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}(X_{0})Y^{\rho}Y^{\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(Y^{3})

(5.104)

with
Y=X-X_{0}
. Then the action reads

S[Y]=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}z\,\left(\partial Y^{\mu}\bar{\partial}Y^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}(X_{0})+\partial Y^{\mu}\bar{\partial}Y^{\nu}\cdot Y^{\rho}G_{\mu\nu,\rho}(X_{0})+\dots\right)\,,

(5.105)

where the first contribution of the sum is the kinetic term, while the others are infinitely many interactions with
G_{\mu\nu,\rho},\dots
serving as coupling constants.

Before the deformations this 2
d
-QFT was conformal. Now we are deforming it with an
\infty
number of interactions that in the quantum theory will be renormalized. The theory preserves its conformal symmetry if the coupling constants are unchanged under the RG flow (and thus if their
\beta
functions vanish). Since in string theory conformal symmetry is a gauge symmetry and cannot be broken, we are only interested in the fixed points of the RG flow.

To simplify this problem, let us choose a system of geodesic coordinates, which can be further specialized to Riemann normal coordinates:

G_{\mu\nu}(X)=\underbrace{G_{\mu\nu}(X_{0})}_{\eta_{\mu\nu}}-\frac{1}{3}R_{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}(X-X_{0})^{\rho}(X-X_{0})^{\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(X^{3})\,.

(5.106)

In a two-dimensional context it is usually convenient to use dimensionless fields, namely
[Y]=\varnothing
. But since
[X]=L
we take

X^{\mu}=X_{0}^{\mu}+\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}Y^{\mu}\,.

(5.107)

Thus

G_{\mu\nu}(X)=G_{\mu\nu}(X_{0})-\frac{1}{3}\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}Y^{\rho}Y^{\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(X^{3})\,.

(5.108)

Then the actions reads

S[Y]=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}z\,\alpha^{\prime}\partial Y^{\mu}\bar{\partial}Y^{\nu}\left(\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{3}\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}Y^{\rho}Y^{\sigma}+\dots\right)\,.

(5.109)

This 2
d
-QFT has the following Feynman rules:

\mu
\nu
k

\displaystyle\,\xrightarrow{\text{FT}}\,\,\frac{\eta_{\mu\nu}}{k^{2}}\,,

(5.110)

\mu
\nu
\rho
\sigma
k_{1}
k_{2}
k_{3}
k_{4}

\displaystyle\,\,\xrightarrow{\text{FT}}\,\,\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{3}R_{\mu\rho\nu\sigma}(k_{1}\cdot k_{2})\,,

(5.111)

where the dot
\cdot
is the scalar product in 2 dimensions.

In order to do perturbation theory we have to ask for a small coupling constant:

\alpha^{\prime}R\ll 1\,,

(5.112)

which means that the spacetime is curved just a little with respect to the string scale, i.e. the string is so small that sees the spacetime as if it was flat.

Let us now evaluate the self energy of the scalar
Y
:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\propto\,(k_{1}\cdot k_{2})\,\delta(k_{1}+k_{2})\,{R_{\mu\rho\nu}}^{\rho}\int d^{2}q\,\frac{1}{q^{2}}\,.

(5.113)

In order to regulate the logarithmic divergence
\int^{\infty}dq/q
we can use dimensional regularization:

\int d^{2}q\,\frac{1}{q^{2}}\to\mu^{-\epsilon}\int\frac{d^{2+\epsilon}q}{q^{2}}\sim\mu^{-\epsilon}\int^{\infty}dq\,\,q^{\epsilon-1}\sim\mu^{-\epsilon}\frac{1}{\epsilon},

(5.114)

where we have introduced the renormalization (mass) scale
\mu
to keep the dimensions of the amplitude fixed
4
4

4

We hope the reader won’t confuse the renormalization scale
\mu
with the Lorentz index
μ
.
.
This implies

{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{}}{}{{}}

{}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{}}{}{{}}

{{}}{}{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{}}{{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}
}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -31.5 0 C -33.09 -17.39 -20.34 -32.72 -2.95 -34.32}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{}{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{

{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -50 -34.45 L -2.95 -34.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.95 -34.45 L 50.75 -34.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}{{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\propto\,\mu^{-\epsilon}\frac{R_{\mu\nu}}{\epsilon}(k_{1}\cdot k_{2})\delta(k_{1}+k_{2})\,.

(5.115)

We can now renormalize the action, absorbing the divergence into a counterterm as follows:

\displaystyle S_{\text{ren}}

\displaystyle=S_{\text{bare}}+\int d^{2}z\,\partial Y^{\mu}\bar{\partial}Y^{\nu}\left(-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{3}\right)\mu^{-\epsilon}\frac{R_{\mu\nu}}{\epsilon}=\int d^{2}z\,\partial Y^{\mu}\bar{\partial}Y^{\nu}G^{\text{ren}}_{\mu\nu}\,,

(5.116)

with

G^{\text{ren}}_{\mu\nu}=G^{\text{bare}}_{\mu\nu}-\mu^{-\epsilon}\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{3}R_{\mu\nu}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\,.

(5.117)

The divergence we have found means that there is a nonvanishing
\beta
function which is simply the coefficient in front of the divergence
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
:

\beta_{\mu\nu}=\mu\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu}G^{\text{ren}}_{\mu\nu}{\Bigg|}_{\epsilon\to 0}\propto\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\nu}\,,

(5.118)

namely the
\beta
function is the spacetime Ricci tensor. Then if we impose the condition

\beta_{\mu\nu}=0

(5.119)

we get

R_{\mu\nu}=0\,,

(5.120)

which are the vacuum Einstein equations. Therefore we have established that, at one-loop order in the world-sheet QFT, conformal invariance implies the Einstein equations!

If we go on with the Riemann normal coordinates expansion, we get

G_{\mu\nu}(X)=G_{\mu\nu}(X_{0})-\frac{1}{3}\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}Y^{\rho}Y^{\sigma}+\frac{8}{180}(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}R_{\mu\alpha\beta\lambda}R_{\nu\gamma\delta}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\lambda}Y^{\alpha}Y^{\beta}Y^{\gamma}Y^{\delta}+\mathcal{O}(Y^{5})\,.

(5.121)

At
{\alpha^{\prime}}^{2}
there is a two-loop self-energy. If we evaluate it we can get the following
\beta
function:

\beta_{\mu\nu}\propto\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\nu}+\sharp(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}R_{\mu\rho}R_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\rho}\,.

(5.122)

Then if we set
\beta=0
we find the first
\alpha^{\prime}
correction to the Einstein equations.

It is instructive to consider what we have just seen from the point of view of Weyl invariance. Using dimensional regularisation we can write

S[X]=\int d^{2+\epsilon}z\,\sqrt{\gamma}\gamma^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}_{b}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}(X)\,,

(5.123)

where
\gamma_{ab}
is the worldsheet metric and the indices
a,b=1,2
are the worldsheet indices.
In the conformal gauge

\displaystyle\gamma^{ab}

\displaystyle=e^{+\phi}\delta^{ab}\,,

(5.124a)

\displaystyle\gamma_{ab}

\displaystyle=e^{-\phi}\delta_{ab}\,,

(5.124b)

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\text{det}\left(\gamma_{ab}\right)=e^{\Tr{\log{\gamma_{ab}}}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\phi\Tr{\delta_{ab}}}=e^{-\phi(2+2\epsilon)}\,,

(5.125)

\sqrt{\gamma}\gamma^{ab}=e^{-\epsilon\phi}\delta^{ab}=(1-\epsilon\phi)\delta^{ab}\,.

(5.126)

Therefore

S[X]=\int d^{2+\epsilon}z\,(1-\epsilon\phi)\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}(X)\,.

(5.127)

If we evaluate the loop contribution and renormalize
G_{\mu\nu}
, we find

\displaystyle S[X]

\displaystyle=\int d^{2+\epsilon}z\,(1-\epsilon\phi)\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}\left(G_{\mu\nu}-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{3\epsilon}R_{\mu\nu}\right)

\displaystyle\!\!\overset{\epsilon\to 0}{=}\int d^{2}z\,\left(\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}+\alpha^{\prime}\phi R_{\mu\nu}\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{3\epsilon}R_{\mu\nu}\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}\right)\,.

(5.128)

We can then see that the
\phi
field does not decouple anymore. This results in an anomaly in the Weyl invariance. Indeed

\delta_{w}S=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\gamma^{ab}}\delta_{w}\gamma^{ab}=T_{ab}(-w\gamma^{ab})=-wT_{a}^{a}\,,

(5.129)

which implies

\delta_{w}S=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad T_{a}^{a}=0\,.

(5.130)

In particular for the action we are considering we have

\displaystyle T^{ab}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\gamma^{ab}}=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\left(e^{-\phi}\delta_{ab}\right)}=\frac{\delta S}{\delta\left((1-\phi)\delta_{ab}\right)}=-\frac{\delta S}{\delta\phi}\delta^{ab}

\displaystyle=-\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\nu}\partial X^{\mu}\cdot\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}\delta^{ab}\,,

(5.131)

\displaystyle\delta_{w}S

\displaystyle=-wT_{a}^{a}=\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\nu}\partial X^{\mu}\cdot\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}w\delta^{a}_{a}

\displaystyle\propto w\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\nu}\partial X^{\mu}\cdot\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}\,.

(5.132)

This means that

T_{a}^{a}=-\alpha^{\prime}R_{\mu\nu}\partial X^{\mu}\cdot\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}

(5.133)

and hence

T_{a}^{a}=0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad R_{\mu\nu}=0\,.

(5.134)

In other words, Weyl symmetry is non-anomalous if and only if the Einstein equations hold.

Everything we discussed in this section came from the fact that we coupled the graviton to the worldsheet. But in the closed string spectrum at level
N=1
we found not only the graviton
G_{\mu\nu}
, but also the Kalb-Ramond
B_{\mu\nu}
and the dilaton
\Phi
.

As we saw, inserting
G_{\mu\nu}
in the non-linear sigma model we get Einstein’s equations; if we then couple
B_{\mu\nu}
and
\Phi
as well, we get a generalized sigma model:

\displaystyle S[X]=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}\sigma\,\Big[\sqrt{\gamma}\gamma^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}_{b}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}(X)+i\epsilon^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}_{b}X^{\nu}B_{\mu\nu}(X)+\alpha^{\prime}R^{(2)}\Phi(X)\Big]\,.

(5.135)

The first term is the graviton coupling we discussed above. The second term is the Kalb-Ramond coupling: being a differential form it is topological, and hence it does not couple to the WS metric, but to the volume form
\epsilon^{ab}
. Finally, the third term is the dilaton coupling: being a scalar it has no indices and thus couples to
R^{(2)}
, which has no index and is invariant under diffeomorphisms in
d=2
just like the other objects inside
S
. The reason why it couples to the 2-dimensional Ricci scalar (and not for example to the identity, which is the 2d cosmological constant) is (classical) Weyl invariance.

Evaluating the
\beta
functions one finds the classical result

\displaystyle\beta^{G}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{\prime}\left(R_{\mu\nu}+\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\Phi-\frac{1}{4}H_{\mu\rho\sigma}H_{\nu}^{\,\,\,\rho\sigma}\right)+\mathcal{O}((\alpha^{\prime})^{2})\,,

(5.136a)

\displaystyle\beta^{B}_{\mu\nu}
