This is the
critical dimension
of the superstring (for the bosonic string, it was
d=26
).

Finally, we can recall that in the bosonic string the
TT
algebra was interpreted as a constraint algebra. The missing EOM for the metric, namely
T^{\text{(m)}}=0
, was the classical constraint. Then BRST quantization added to each constraint a ghost (with an additional term, due to the non-abelian nature of the constraints), hence defining the current

j_{\text{B}}(z)=cT^{\text{(m)}}(z)+\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{cT^{\text{(gh)}}}:(z)\,.

(6.45)

This led to the definition of the BRST charge

Q_{\text{B}}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j_{\text{B}}(z)\,,

(6.46)

which is nilpotent iff the total central charge of the algebra vanishes:

Q_{\text{B}}^{2}=0\,\ \Longleftrightarrow\,\ c^{\text{(m)}}+c^{\text{(gh)}}=0\,.

(6.47)

Analogously, in the superstring case we have two sets of classical constraints, given by

\displaystyle T^{\text{(m)}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.48a)

\displaystyle G^{\text{(m)}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.48b)

where the first equation is again the missing EOM for the metric, while the second one (as we will later argue) is the missing EOM for the superpartner of the metric (the so-called gravitino).
If we then associate the
(b,c)
-system to
T^{\text{(m)}}
and the
(\beta,\gamma)
-system to
G^{\text{(m)}}
, the BRST current is constructed as

j_{\text{B}}(z)=cT^{\text{(m)}}(z)+\gamma G^{\text{(m)}}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\left(:\mathrel{cT^{\text{(gh)}}}:(z)+:\mathrel{\gamma G^{\text{(gh)}}}:(z)\right)

(6.49)

and the BRST charge is, as usual, the integral of such a current:

Q_{\text{B}}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j_{\text{B}}(z)\,.

(6.50)

Again we have that

Q_{\text{B}}^{2}=0\,\ \Longleftrightarrow\,\ c^{\text{(m)}}+c^{\text{(gh)}}=0\,,

(6.51)

which is satisfied at the critical dimension
d=10
.

Moreover, one can show that

\displaystyle\left[Q_{\text{B}},b(z)\right]

\displaystyle=T^{\text{(tot)}}(z)=T^{\text{(m)}}(z)+T^{\text{(gh)}}(z)\,,

(6.52a)

\displaystyle\left[Q_{\text{B}},\beta(z)\right]

\displaystyle=G^{\text{(tot)}}(z)=G^{\text{(m)}}(z)+G^{\text{(gh)}}(z)\,.

(6.52b)

Exercise 6.1.5

Prove eqs. (
6.52
).

In conclusion, all the fields of our superconformal theory are related by WS supersymmetry

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}\,\,\left(h=\frac{1}{2}\right)\,\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad X^{\mu}\,\,\left(h=0\right)\,,

(6.53a)

\displaystyle\gamma\,\,\left(h=-\frac{1}{2}\right)\quad\longleftrightarrow\quad c\,\,\left(h=-1\right)\,,

(6.53b)

\displaystyle\beta\,\,\left(h=\frac{3}{2}\right)\qquad\!\!\longleftrightarrow\quad b\,\,\left(h=2\right)\,.

(6.53c)

6.2
Closed Superstring

6.2.1
Neveu-Schwarz and Ramond sectors

Let us now consider a closed superstring. The worldsheet spinors we introduced (that we will generically denote as
\chi
, i.e.
\chi=\psi,\beta,\gamma
) can be periodic or anti-periodic along the
\sigma
direction:

•

periodic:
\chi(\sigma+2\pi)=+\chi(\sigma)
;

•

anti-periodic:
\chi(\sigma+2\pi)=-\chi(\sigma)
.

These two conditions are both possible, because the density given by spinor bilinears remains the same. On the other hand, the anti-periodic condition would not be acceptable in the bosonic
X
case, since it would break Lorentz invariance.
3
3

3

However this possibility is still considered in string theory in the construction of
orbifolds
.

If we now write as usual
w=t+i\sigma
, we can define two sectors:

\chi(w+2\pi i)=\begin{cases}+\chi(w)\quad\text{Ramond (R) sector}\\
-\chi(w)\quad\text{Neveu-Schwarz (NS) sector}\end{cases}\,.

(6.54)

Analogously, since the supercurrent is a fermion, we can write

G(w+2\pi i)=\begin{cases}+G(w)\quad\text{(R)}\\
-G(w)\quad\text{(NS)}\end{cases}\,.

(6.55)

If we then map the WS cylinder on the complex plane via the exponential map
z=e^{w}
, recalling that the conformal weight of
\psi
is
\frac{1}{2}
we can write

\psi(z)(dz)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\psi(w)(dw)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(6.56)

and hence

\displaystyle\psi(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{dz}{dw}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi\big(w(z)\big)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{d(e^{w})}{dw}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\psi\big(\log(z)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\psi\big(\log(z)\big)\,.

(6.57)

Therefore we can write the oscillator expansion of the
\psi
field in its two possible sectors:

\begin{cases}\psi_{\text{NS}}^{\mu}(z)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\psi_{r}^{\mu}z^{-r-\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.0pt]
\psi_{\text{R}}^{\mu}(z)\,\,\;\,\!\!=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\psi_{n}^{\mu}z^{-n-\frac{1}{2}}\end{cases}\,.

(6.58)

In the NS sector there are no branch cuts on
\mathbb{C}
(see fig.
6.1
), since for
r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
the function
z^{-r-\frac{1}{2}}
is meromorphic (because
-r-\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
).
In the R sector, on the other hand, there is a branch cut from
0
to
\infty
(see fig.
6.1
) because for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the function
z^{-n-\frac{1}{2}}
is double-valued (since
-n-\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
).

The presence of the branch cut can be understood as an insertion of a peculiar field in the origin of the complex plane, which changes the nature of the
SL(2,C)
vacuum. This field
S(0)
is called
spin field
and its contraction with a fermion
\psi
behaves as a square root:

\underbracket{\psi(z_{1})S(z_{2})}=\frac{(\cdots)}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}}\,.

(6.59)

NS
1

((a))

R

((b))

Figure 6.1:
The fermion
\psi(z)
in the NS and R cases. In the NS plane there are no branch cuts and in the origin there is the identity field, corresponding to the
SL(2,C)
vacuum. On the other hand, in the R case there is a branch cut (marked in red) due to the spin field inserted in the origin, which defines a new vacuum, the R vacuum.

The OPE of two fermions
\psi
remains the same in both sectors:

\psi(z_{1})\psi(z_{2})=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,,

(6.60)

but it is realized as two different oscillator algebras

\displaystyle\text{NS)}\quad\left[\psi_{r}^{\mu},\psi_{s}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{r+s,\,0}\,,\,\,\quad\text{with }r,s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\,,

(6.61a)

\displaystyle\text{R)}\quad\left[\psi_{n}^{\mu},\psi_{m}^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,\quad\text{with }n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(6.61b)

The same can be said for
(\beta,\gamma)
ghosts. We have that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\beta_{\text{NS}}(z)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\beta_{r}z^{-r-\frac{3}{2}}\\[5.0pt]
\beta_{\text{R}}(z)\,\,\;\!=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\beta_{n}z^{-n-\frac{3}{2}}\end{cases}\,,

(6.62a)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\gamma_{\text{NS}}(z)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\gamma_{r}z^{-r+\frac{1}{2}}\\[5.0pt]
\gamma_{\text{R}}(z)\,\,\;\!=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\gamma_{n}z^{-n+\frac{1}{2}}\end{cases}\,.

(6.62b)

which implies

\displaystyle\text{NS)}\quad\left[\beta_{r},\gamma_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=\delta_{r+s,\,0}\,,\,\,\quad\text{with }r,s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\,,

(6.63a)

\displaystyle\text{R)}\quad\left[\beta_{n},\gamma_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,\quad\text{with }n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(6.63b)

Analogously, for the supercurrent we can write

\begin{cases}G_{\text{NS}}(z)=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}G_{r}z^{-r-\frac{3}{2}}\\[5.0pt]
G_{\text{R}}(z)\,\,\;\!=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}G_{n}z^{-n-\frac{3}{2}}\end{cases}\,,

(6.64)

which implies

\displaystyle\text{NS)}\quad\left[G_{r},G_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=2L_{r+s}+\frac{c}{12}(4r^{2}-1)\delta_{r+s,\,0}\,,\,\,\,\,\,\quad\text{with }r,s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\,,

(6.65a)

\displaystyle\text{R)}\quad\left[G_{n},G_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=2L_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}(4n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,\quad\text{with }n,m\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(6.65b)

Therefore the superconformal algebra can be written in terms of oscillators as

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,\,0}\,,

(6.66a)

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},G_{r}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{n-2r}{2}G_{n+r}\,,

(6.66b)

\displaystyle\text{NS)}\quad\left[G_{r},G_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=2L_{r+s}+\frac{c}{12}(4r^{2}-1)\delta_{r+s,\,0}\,,\quad\text{with }r,s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\,,

(6.66c)

\displaystyle\text{R)}\quad\left[G_{r},G_{s}\right]

\displaystyle=2L_{r+s}+\frac{c}{12}(4r^{2}-1)\delta_{r+s,\,0}\,,\quad\text{with }r,s\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(6.66d)

where eq. (
6.66b
) comes from the fact that
G
is a primary field.

This algebra is generated by two SCFTs:

•

matter
(X;\psi)
-SCFT with central charge
c^{\text{(m)}}=\frac{3}{2}d
;

•

ghost
(b,c\,;\beta,\gamma)
-SCFT with central charge
c^{\text{(gh)}}=-15
.

As already said above, the total SCFT has
c^{\text{(tot)}}=c^{\text{(m)}}+c^{\text{(gh)}}=0
when
d=10
.

6.2.2
Neveu-Schwarz and Ramond vacua

Let us then built the vacua of the theory.
We will use the following notation

\cdot

\alpha_{n}^{\mu}
: oscillators in the
X
-CFT;

\cdot

\psi_{r}^{\mu}
: oscillators in the
\psi_{\text{NS}}
-CFT;

\cdot

\psi_{n}^{\mu}
: oscillators in the
\psi_{\text{R}}
-CFT.

We then have two possibilities.

•

In the NS sector the definite-momentum vacuum
\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}
is such that

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}^{\mu}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{for }n\geq 1\,,

(6.67a)

\displaystyle\psi_{r}^{\mu}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{for }r\geq\frac{1}{2}\,.

(6.67b)

•

In the R sector the definite-momentum vacuum
\ket{0,P}_{\text{R}}
is such that

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}^{\mu}\ket{0,P}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{for }n\geq 1\,,

(6.68a)

\displaystyle\psi_{n}^{\mu}\ket{0,P}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{for }n\geq 1\,.

(6.68b)

Let us now recall that
\left[\psi_{n}^{\mu},\psi_{m}^{\nu}\right]=\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{n+m,\,0}
. This implies that for the zero-modes we have

\left[\psi_{0}^{\mu},\psi_{0}^{\nu}\right]=\eta^{\mu\nu}\,.

(6.69)

Therefore, just like
b_{0}
and
c_{0}
, the zero-mode
\psi_{0}^{\mu}
is neither a creation nor an annihilation operator.

Since we have many of these zero-modes, the Ramond vacuum is degenerate. The degeneracy is labelled by a label
\alpha
. Then we can write

\psi_{0}^{\mu}\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}=(\psi^{\mu})_{\alpha\beta}\ket{\beta,P}_{\text{R}}\,.

(6.70)

If we now define

\Gamma^{\mu}=\sqrt{2}\psi_{0}^{\mu}\,,

(6.71)

from eq. (
6.69
) we recognize the Clifford algebra in
d
dimensions:

\left[\Gamma^{\mu},\Gamma^{\nu}\right]=2\eta^{\mu\nu}\,,

(6.72)

where obviously the square brackets stand for an anti-commutator, since we are dealing with fermionic objects (recall that we are using the graded commutator convention). This means that the Ramond vacuum is the space where gamma matrices (of dimension
d
) act, namely the spinor space in
d
dimensions (see appendix
A
). Therefore, if we work in the critical dimension
d=10
, the
\psi_{\text{R}}
sector Ramond vacuum
\ket{\alpha}_{\text{R}}
is a
10
-dimensional spinor of
SO(1,9)
, and it can be decomposed in two irreps as

\ket{\alpha}_{\text{R}}=\underbrace{\ket{\alpha}}_{16_{c}}\oplus\underbrace{\ket{\dot{\alpha}}}_{16_{s}}\,,

(6.73)

where
16_{c}
is the positive chirality
16
-dimensional irrep, while
16_{s}
is the negative chirality
16
-dimensional irrep. These two irreps are defined by the action of the chirality matrix
\Gamma
as

\displaystyle\Gamma\ket{\alpha}=+\ket{\alpha}\,,

(6.74a)

\displaystyle\Gamma\ket{\dot{\alpha}}=-\ket{\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(6.74b)

Given what we said so far, recalling that the momentum is given by the definite-momentum vacuum in the
X
sector, we can write the total matter vacuum in the NS sector as

\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}^{\text{\tiny matter}}=\underbrace{\ket{0,P}}_{\text{$X$ sector}}\otimes\underbrace{\ket{0}_{\text{NS}}}_{\text{$\psi$ sector}}=\ket{0,P}\otimes\ket{0}\,,

(6.75)

where we wrote
\ket{0}_{\text{NS}}=\ket{0}
since, at the matter level, the NS vacuum in the
\psi
sector is precisely the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum.

On the other hand, the total vacuum in the R sector can be written as

\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}^{\text{\tiny matter}}=\underbrace{\ket{0,P}}_{\text{$X$ sector}}\otimes\underbrace{\ket{\alpha}_{\text{R}}}_{\text{$\psi$ sector}}=\ket{0,P}\otimes\bigg(\ket{\alpha}\oplus\ket{\dot{\alpha}}\bigg)\,.

(6.76)

As we anticipated, the R vacuum is obtained by inserting a spin field to the (NS)
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum:

|\overset{(\,\cdot\,)}{\alpha}\,\rangle=S_{\overset{(\,\cdot\,)}{\alpha}}(0)\ket{0}\,.

(6.77)

Therefore the Ramond vacuum is in a sense an excited state of the Neveu-Schwarz vacuum. This is analogous to what we found previously in the
X
-BCFT for the vacuum of open strings satisfying mixed Neumann-Dirichlet boundary conditions.

6.2.3
Old covariant quantization

6.2.3.1
Constraints

We are now able to write every possible state in the holomorphic matter sector (for the anti-holomorphic sector everything works in the same way).
The most generic state is made up of a polarization tensor,
X
oscillators (bosonic sector) and
\psi
oscillators (fermionic sector) acting on the definite momentum vacuum.

In the NS sector we have

G_{\left\{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{k}\right\}\left\{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}\right\}}(P)\left(\alpha_{-n_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}\dots\alpha_{-n_{k}}^{\mu_{k}}\right)\left(\psi_{-r_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}\dots\psi_{-r_{q}}^{\nu_{q}}\right)\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,,

(6.78)

which is the most generic Lorentz representation for an integer-spin field, namely for a spacetime boson. Indeed
\left\{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{k}\right\}
and
\left\{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}\right\}
are vectorial Lorentz indices.

On the other hand, in the R sector we have

\chi_{\alpha\left\{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{k}\right\}\left\{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}\right\}}(P)\left(\alpha_{-n_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}\dots\alpha_{-n_{k}}^{\mu_{k}}\right)\left(\psi_{-r_{1}}^{\nu_{1}}\dots\psi_{-r_{q}}^{\nu_{q}}\right)\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}\,,

(6.79)

which is the most generic Lorentz representation for a half-integer-spin field, namely for a spacetime fermion. Indeed
\alpha
is a spinorial index in
d=10
, while
\left\{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{k}\right\}
and
\left\{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{q}\right\}
are again vectorial Lorentz indices. This means that we finally found fermions in string theory. But everything is yet to be set on-shell, we still have to impose the physical constraints.

Let us first look at the old covariant quantization. For
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
, the constraints are

•

in the NS sector:

\displaystyle\left(L_{0}-a_{\text{NS}}\right)\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{NS}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.80a)

\displaystyle L_{n>0}\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{NS}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.80b)

\displaystyle G_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{NS}}

\displaystyle=0\,;

(6.80c)

•

in the R sector:

\displaystyle\left(L_{0}-a_{\text{R}}\right)\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.81a)

\displaystyle L_{n>0}\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.81b)

\displaystyle G_{n\geq 0}\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6.81c)

If we then incorporate the ghost contribution, from BRST (or lightcone) quantization we have that (no proof at this stage)

a_{\text{NS}}=\frac{1}{2}\,,\qquad a_{\text{R}}=\frac{5}{8}\,.

(6.82)

In order to understand the origin of these values of the normal ordering constant
a
, we may recall that, in the bosonic string,
a
was the conformal weight of the ghost vacuum
c_{1}|0\rangle
:

\displaystyle L_{0}^{\text{(tot)}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}+L_{0}^{\text{(gh)}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}-a

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}-1

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6.83)

Now, coming back to the superstring, it can be shown that the matter
+
ghost NS vacuum is related to the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum as

c_{1}\ket{0}_{\text{NS}}=c\mathcal{V}_{\frac{1}{2}}^{(\beta,\gamma)}(0)\ket{0}\,,

(6.84)

where
\mathcal{V}_{1/2}^{(\beta,\gamma)}
is a primary operator of weight
1/2
in the
(\beta,\gamma)
-SCFT, which can be written as
\mathcal{V}_{1/2}^{(\beta,\gamma)}=e^{-\varphi}
(see
6.5.1
for more details on this topic).
It is hence clear that
a_{\text{NS}}=1/2
is the weight of the NS vacuum, since the total weight of
c\mathcal{V}_{1/2}^{(\beta,\gamma)}
is
1-1/2=-1/2
.
Therefore in the NS sector we have that

\displaystyle L_{0}^{\text{(tot)}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}+L_{0}^{\text{(gh)}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}-a_{\text{NS}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}-\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6.85)

On the other hand, in the R sector the matter
+
ghost vacuum can be written as

c_{1}|\overset{(\,\cdot\,)}{\alpha}\,\rangle_{\text{R}}=S_{\overset{(\,\cdot\,)}{\alpha}}c\mathcal{V}_{\frac{3}{8}}^{(\beta,\gamma)}(0)\ket{0}\,,

(6.86)

where
\mathcal{V}_{3/8}^{(\beta,\gamma)}
is a primary operator of weight
3/8
in the
(\beta,\gamma)
-SCFT, which can be written as
\mathcal{V}_{3/8}^{(\beta,\gamma)}=e^{-\varphi/2}
(this field
\varphi
is the same we encountered in the NS case and it will be explained in
6.5.1
).
Therefore in the R sector we have that

\displaystyle L_{0}^{\text{(tot)}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}+L_{0}^{\text{(gh)}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}-a_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}+\left(-1+\frac{3}{8}\right)

\displaystyle=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}-\frac{5}{8}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6.87)

Given what we said so far, we can write

\displaystyle\text{NS)}\quad L_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(2r-n):\mathrel{\psi_{n-r}\cdot\psi_{r}}:\,,

(6.88a)

\displaystyle\text{R)}\quad L_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(2k-n):\mathrel{\psi_{n-k}\cdot\psi_{k}}:+\frac{d}{16}\delta_{n,\,0}\,.

(6.88b)

We can notice that the R sector has a shift equal to the spin field weight, namely of
d/16
(which in
d=10
is exactly
5/8
; see
6.5.1
for more details). It shifts the vacuum from the NS sector to the R sector.

Moreover we have that the supercurrent is defined as

G_{r}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{n}\cdot\psi_{n-r}\,,

(6.89)

where
r\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2
in the NS case, while
r\in\mathbb{Z}
in the R case.

6.2.3.2
Spectrum

Let us now write

L_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{0})^{2}+N\,,

(6.90)

where
\alpha_{0}^{\mu}=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}P^{\mu}
is the
X
-CFT zero-mode (in the closed string case, which is the one we are studying) and where
N
is the level operator, defined in
d=10
as

\displaystyle\text{NS)}\quad N_{\text{NS}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(2r):\mathrel{\psi_{-r}\cdot\psi_{r}}:\,,

(6.91a)

\displaystyle\text{R)}\,\,\quad N_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{-k}\cdot\alpha_{k}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}(2k):\mathrel{\psi_{-k}\cdot\psi_{k}}:+\frac{5}{8}\,.

(6.91b)

We can then analyze the first levels of the spectrum in the NS sector.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N_{\text{NS}}=0

At the level
N_{\text{NS}}=0
we have the tachyon

t(P)\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,,

(6.92)

which is lighter then the closed bosonic string tachyon because

\displaystyle L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}-a_{\text{NS}}=L_{0}^{\text{(m)}}-\frac{1}{2}=0\,\,\

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\,\,\ \frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}=\frac{1}{2}=a_{\text{NS}}\,\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\,\ m^{2}=-\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

(6.93)

\blacksquare\,
Level
N_{\text{NS}}=\frac{1}{2}

At the level
N_{\text{NS}}=1/2
the generic state is

\xi_{\mu}(P)\psi^{\mu}_{\frac{1}{2}}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,,

(6.94)

which is a massless vector:

m^{2}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-a_{\text{NS}}\right)=0\,.

(6.95)

If we look at the supercurrent, we have that

G_{\frac{1}{2}}=\sum_{n}\alpha_{n}\cdot\psi_{\frac{1}{2}-n}\,,

(6.96)

that for
n=0
becomes
\alpha_{0}\cdot\psi_{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Then the constraint (
6.80c
) is

G_{\frac{1}{2}}=0\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ \alpha_{0}\cdot\xi=0\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ P\cdot\xi=0\,,

(6.97)

where (as we usually do for
L_{n}
) we used the same notation for the operator
G_{1/2}
and its eigenvalue on the physical state. If we take
a_{NS}=1/2
we find a transverse massless vector which falls in vector representation of the little group
SO(8)
, which we denote
8_{V}
.
If we look at higher levels, we find massive states.

Let us now analyze the first level of the spectrum in the R sector.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N_{\text{R}}=0

At the level
N_{\text{R}}=0
the generic state is

u_{\alpha}(P)\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}+v_{\dot{\alpha}}(P)\ket{\dot{\alpha},P}_{\text{R}}\,,

(6.98)

where
u_{\alpha}(P)
is a left spinor in
d=10
, while
v_{\dot{\alpha}}(P)
is a right spinor in
d=10
. We are hence treating separately the two chirality irreps.

If we look at the supercurrent, we have that

G_{0}=\sum_{n}\alpha_{n}\cdot\psi_{-n}\,,

(6.99)

on the R vacuum only
n=0
gives contribution

G_{0}=\alpha_{0}\cdot\psi_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}P\cdot\Gamma\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}{2}\not{P}\,,

(6.100)

where
\not{P}
is the Dirac kinetic operator of spacetime fermions.

The constraint (
6.80c
) reads as (recalling that
(\not{P})^{2}=P^{2}
)

\displaystyle\not{P}u(P)

\displaystyle=0\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ m^{2}=0\,,

(6.101a)

\displaystyle\not{P}v(P)

\displaystyle=0\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ m^{2}=0\,.

(6.101b)

We hence found the Dirac equation. As usual this equation reduces by half the spinorial d.o.f. Therefore the two states
u_{\alpha}(P)\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}
and
v_{\dot{\alpha}}(P)\ket{\dot{\alpha},P}_{\text{R}}
fall into massless
SO(8)
spinorial representations of dimension
2^{8/2}=16=8+8
:

SO(8)\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \underbrace{8_{C}}_{\text{left}}\oplus\underbrace{8_{S}}_{\text{right}}\,.

(6.102)

If we look at higher levels, we find massive states with half-integer spin.

What we found in the holomorphic sector is summed up in table
6.1
. We notice that the massless part of the spectrum is organized in representations of
SO(8)
.

NS

R

N_{\text{NS}}=0
:

tachyon

N_{\text{R}}=0
:

8_{C}\oplus 8_{S}
massless

N_{\text{NS}}=\frac{1}{2}
:

8_{V}
massless

Table 6.1:
The first spectrum levels of the holomorphic sector of the closed superstring.

6.2.4
Lightcone quantization

As with the bosonic string, we can handle directly all the physical states by going to the lightcone gauge.

6.2.4.1
Constraints

In the bosonic string we fixed the conformal transformations by writing

X_{L}^{+}(z)=\frac{X_{0}^{+}}{2}-i\alpha^{\prime}P^{+}\log z\quad\text{(no oscillators)}\,,

(6.103)

and hence

j^{+}(z)=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\partial X^{+}(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}P^{+}\frac{1}{z}\,.

(6.104)

In the superstring, however, we also have the
\psi
sector and superconformal transformations. If we write in the lightcone direction
+
the superconformal transformation

\delta^{\text{sc}}_{\eta}\psi^{+}(z)=-i\eta j^{+}(z)\,,

(6.105)

we can choose to fix

\psi^{+}(z)=0\,.

(6.106)

Therefore, in the superstring case, the lightcone gauge is

\begin{cases}X_{L}^{+}(z)=\frac{X_{0}^{+}}{2}-i\alpha^{\prime}P^{+}\log z\\[5.0pt]
\psi^{+}(z)\,\;\!=0\end{cases}\,.

(6.107)

At the classical level, we can implement the constraints
L_{n>0}\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{NS}}=0
and
G_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{NS}}=0
in the NS sector (recall eqs. (
6.80
)), or
L_{n>0}\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{R}}=0
and
G_{n\geq 0}\ket{\text{phys}}_{\text{R}}=0
in the R sector (recall eqs. (
6.81
)). For the time being, we ignored the
L_{0}
constraint. We then find that oscillators in the
-
direction can be expressed in terms of transverse oscillators as

\begin{cases}\partial X_{L}^{-}(z)=\frac{z}{2P^{+}}\left(\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}\partial X^{j}\partial X_{j}+i\psi^{j}\partial\psi_{j}\right)\\[5.0pt]
\,\,\psi^{-}(z)\,\,\,=\frac{2z}{\alpha^{\prime}P^{+}}\psi^{j}\partial X_{L,\,j}\end{cases}\,,

(6.108)

where the index
j
denotes transverse directions. Since we now have transverse oscillators only, the Hilbert space will be made up of states of the type

\displaystyle\text{NS})

\displaystyle\quad\left(\alpha_{-n_{1}}^{i_{1}}\dots\alpha_{-n_{k}}^{i_{k}}\right)\left(\psi_{-r_{1}}^{j_{1}}\dots\psi_{-r_{q}}^{j_{q}}\right)\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,,

(6.109a)

\displaystyle\text{R})

\displaystyle\quad\left(\alpha_{-n_{1}}^{i_{1}}\dots\alpha_{-n_{k}}^{i_{k}}\right)\left(\psi_{-r_{1}}^{j_{1}}\dots\psi_{-r_{q}}^{j_{q}}\right)|\overset{(\,\cdot\,)}{\alpha},P\,\rangle_{\text{R}}\,,

(6.109b)

where, as said above,
\ket{\alpha}
is the
8_{C}
spinorial representation of
SO(8)
, while
\ket{\dot{\alpha}}
is the
8_{S}
spinorial representation.

If we now implement the lightcone
L_{0}
constraint, we have that

\displaystyle\text{NS})

\displaystyle\quad L_{0}^{\text{(lc)}}-a_{\text{NS}}^{\text{(lc)}}=0\,,

(6.110a)

\displaystyle\text{R})

\displaystyle\quad L_{0}^{\text{(lc)}}-a_{\text{R}}^{\text{(lc)}}=0\,.

(6.110b)

6.2.4.2
Lightcone NS spectrum

Let us now analyze the lightcone NS spectrum up to the massless level.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N_{\text{NS}}^{\text{(lc)}}=0

At the level
N_{\text{NS}}^{\text{(lc)}}=0
we find the tachyon

t(P)\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,,

(6.111)

having

m^{2}=-a_{\text{NS}}^{\text{(lc)}}\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

(6.112)

\blacksquare\,
Level
N_{\text{NS}}^{\text{(lc)}}=\frac{1}{2}

At the level
N_{\text{NS}}^{\text{(lc)}}=1/2
the generic state is

\xi_{i}(P)\psi^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,,

(6.113)

where, again, the index
i
denotes transverse directions only.
The mass-shell is

\frac{\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}}{4}=\frac{1}{2}-a_{\text{NS}}^{\text{(lc)}}\,.

(6.114)

Such a state is a massless vector in the
8_{V}
representation of the Lorentz group
SO(8)
, since it has
d-2
d.o.f. (which are the transverse directions). Therefore it has to be massless, which implies

a_{\text{NS}}^{\text{(lc)}}=\frac{1}{2}\,,

(6.115)

which is the same result we found before (see eq. (
6.82
)).

If we try to directly normal order
L_{0}
we have that

\displaystyle L_{0}^{\text{(lc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+N_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{-k}^{i}\alpha_{k,\,i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}r\psi_{-r}^{i}\psi_{r,\,i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\left(\alpha_{-k}^{i}\alpha_{k,\,i}+\frac{1}{2}k(d-2)\right)+\sum_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}\left(r\psi_{-r}^{i}\psi_{r,\,i}-\frac{1}{2}r(d-2)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+\hat{N}_{\perp}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}(d-2)\sum_{k\geq 1}k-\frac{1}{2}(d-2)\sum_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}r}_{\text{infinite zero point energies}}\,,

(6.116)

where the second step has been performed just like we did in eq. (
3.361
), with the only difference that in the
\psi
case we had to take into account its fermionic nature with a proper minus sign. Moreover, we denoted as
\hat{N}_{\perp}
the level operator in the transverse directions.

In analogy to the renormalization we performed for the bosonic string, we can write more in general that

\sum_{k\geq\nu}k\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \sum_{k\geq\nu}ke^{-\epsilon k}=\frac{e^{\epsilon\nu}(\nu+e^{\epsilon(1-\nu)})}{(1-e^{\epsilon})^{2}}\overset{\epsilon\to 0}{=}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{12}(1-6\nu+6\nu^{2})+O(\epsilon)\,.

(6.117)

This infinite sum can be also understood as a non convergent representation of the Hurwitz zeta-function

\zeta(s,\nu)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+\nu)^{-s}\,,\qquad\zeta(-1,\nu)=-\frac{1}{12}(1-6\nu+6\nu^{2})\,.

(6.118)

In the
X
-CFT case we have
\nu=1
, while in the
\psi
-CFT case we have
\nu=1/2
. If we now renormalize the action inserting a proper counterterm that absorbs the
1/\epsilon^{2}
divergence (as we did in (
3.364
)), we can write the renormalized zero point energies as

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 1}k=-\frac{1}{12}\,,\qquad\sum_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}r=\frac{1}{24}\,.

(6.119a)

Therefore

L_{0}^{\text{(lc)}}=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+\hat{N}_{\perp}-\frac{d-2}{16}\,,

(6.120)

and the constraint
L_{0}^{\text{(lc)}}-a^{\text{(lc)}}_{\text{NS}}=0
imposes

a^{\text{(lc)}}_{\text{NS}}=\frac{d-2}{16}=\frac{1}{2}\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ d=10\,.

(6.121)

6.2.4.3
Lightcone R spectrum

Let us now analyze the lightcone R spectrum up to the massless level.

\blacksquare\,
Level
N_{\text{R}}^{\text{(lc)}}=0

At the level
N_{\text{R}}^{\text{(lc)}}=0
the generic state is the massless
8_{C}+8_{S}
:

u_{\alpha}(P)\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}+v_{\dot{\alpha}}(P)\ket{\dot{\alpha},P}_{\text{R}}\,.

(6.122)

We can write

\displaystyle L_{0}^{\text{(lc)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha_{-k}^{i}\alpha_{k,\,i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}n\psi_{-n}^{i}\psi_{n,\,i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{4}+\hat{N}_{\perp}+\cancel{\frac{1}{2}(d-2)\sum_{k\geq 1}k}-\cancel{\frac{1}{2}(d-2)\sum_{n\geq 1}n}\,.

(6.123)

We notice that zero point energies cancel each other.

The constraint
L_{0}^{\text{(lc)}}-a^{\text{(lc)}}_{\text{R}}=0
imposes

a^{\text{(lc)}}_{\text{R}}=0\,.

(6.124)

Notice that this is a different value from the one we adopted in the closed superstring OCQ (see eq. (
6.82
)).
4
4

4

The reason is that the lightcone value is computing the full conformal weight of the R vacuum state in the matter-ghost theory,
\sim c\,e^{-\varphi/2}S_{\alpha}\ket{0}_{SL(2,\mathbb{C})}.

6.2.5
The tachyon and the problem with modular invariance

Considering what we said so far for the holomorphic closed superstring spectrum, we can apply it to the anti-holomorphic sector too in order to find the full closed-string spectrum. The result is shown in table
6.2
.

\text{NS}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{NS}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\text{R}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{NS}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\text{NS}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\text{R}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

L_{0}-a=0

\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muL\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{0}-a=0

m^{2}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N_{\text{NS}}-\frac{1}{2}\right)

N_{\text{NS}}=\bar{N}_{\text{NS}}

m^{2}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}N_{\text{R}}

N_{\text{R}}=\bar{N}_{\text{NS}}-\frac{1}{2}

m^{2}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{N}_{\text{R}}

\bar{N}_{\text{R}}=N_{\text{NS}}-\frac{1}{2}

m^{2}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\bar{N}_{\text{R}}

N_{\text{R}}=\bar{N}_{\text{R}}

N=0

tachyon:

t(P)\ket{0,P}

m^{2}=-\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}

N_{NS}=\frac{1}{2}

N_{R}=0

massless boson:

\underbrace{8_{V}}_{\text{bos}}\otimes\underbrace{8_{V}}_{\text{bos}}

m^{2}=0

massless fermion:

\underbrace{(8_{C}\oplus 8_{S})}_{\text{ferm}}\otimes\underbrace{8_{V}}_{\text{bos}}

m^{2}=0

massless fermion:

\underbrace{8_{V}}_{\text{bos}}\otimes\underbrace{(8_{C}\oplus 8_{S})}_{\text{ferm}}

m^{2}=0

massless boson:

\underbrace{(8_{C}\oplus 8_{S})}_{\text{ferm}}\otimes\underbrace{(8_{C}\oplus 8_{S})}_{\text{ferm}}

m^{2}=0

Table 6.2:
The full closed superstring spectrum up to the massless level.

We notice that the annoying tachyon is still there. But this is not the real problem.
It is not obvious that the theory is consistent at one-loop. In particular the one-loop partition function must be modular invariant, and this is a constraint on the states inside the spectrum. The full one-loop path integral in the matter-ghost sector is subject to an analogous lightcone reduction as in the bosonic string and, in full analogy to the bosonic string, it is given by

Z_{\text{1-loop}}=\int_{\cal F}\frac{d\tau d\bar{\tau}}{\left({\rm Im}(\tau)\right)^{2}}\,\text{Tr}_{\perp}\left[(-1)^{\mathbb{F}}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right]\,,

(6.125)

where, since now we have both spacetime bosons and fermions circulating in the loop, we put a
(-1)^{\mathbb{F}}
where
\mathbb{F}
is the
space-time
fermion number (not to be confused with the
worldsheet
fermion number, which we will introduce later), to account for the standard minus sign for spacetime fermions circulating in the loop.
The measure
d^{2}\tau/({\rm Im}\tau)^{2}
is already modular invariant and contains as usual the volume of the CKG and the momentum integral in the lightcone directions
d^{2}p^{\pm}
. The remaining trace is over the transverse
c=8+4

(X,\psi)
SCFT.

L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}=\left(L_{0}^{X}-\frac{8}{24}\right)+\left(L_{0}^{\psi}-\frac{4}{24}\right)\,.

(6.126)

Specializing on the NS or R sector for the 8 transverse
\psi
’s

\displaystyle\text{NS})

\displaystyle\quad{L_{0}^{\psi}}_{\text{NS}}=N_{\text{NS}}

(6.127a)

\displaystyle\text{R})

\displaystyle\quad{L_{0}^{\psi}}_{\text{R}}=N_{\text{R}}+\frac{8}{16}=N_{\text{R}}+\frac{1}{2}\,,

(6.127b)

where we have considered that in the
R
sector all of the states are build on the
R
vacua
\ket{a}_{R}
which obey

L_{0}\ket{a}_{R}=\frac{1}{2}\ket{a}_{R}.

(6.128)

Let’s then define

\displaystyle f(\tau,\bar{\tau})=

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\perp}\left[(-1)^{\mathbb{F}}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right]

(6.129)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{X}\left[q^{L_{0}-\frac{1}{3}}\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{1}{3}}\right]\left(\text{Tr}_{\rm NS}\left[q^{N-\frac{1}{6}}\right]-\text{Tr}_{\rm R}\left[q^{N+\frac{1}{3}}\right]\right)\left(\text{Tr}_{\overline{\rm NS}}\left[\bar{q}^{\bar{N}-\frac{1}{6}}\right]-\text{Tr}_{\overline{\rm R}}\left[\bar{q}^{\bar{N}+\frac{1}{3}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\rm Im}\tau}\,\eta(\tau)\,\eta(\bar{\tau})}\right)^{8}\left(\text{Tr}_{\rm NS}\left[q^{N-\frac{1}{6}}\right]-\text{Tr}_{\rm R}\left[q^{N+\frac{1}{3}}\right]\right)\left(\text{Tr}_{\overline{\rm NS}}\left[\bar{q}^{\bar{N}-\frac{1}{6}}\right]-\text{Tr}_{\overline{\rm R}}\left[\bar{q}^{\bar{N}+\frac{1}{3}}\right]\right)

where we have calculated the trace over 8 free bosons
X^{i}
, recalling the Dedekind
\eta
function

\displaystyle\eta(\tau)=q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}{(1-q^{n})}\,,\qquad q=e^{2\pi i\tau}\,.

(6.130)

It is easy to compute the fermionic traces. We start with

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\rm NS}\left[q^{N-\frac{1}{6}}\right]=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{1}{6}}\left(\prod_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}(1+q^{r})\right)^{8}=\left(\frac{\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})\left(1+q^{n-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}}{\eta(\tau)}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{4}}\,,

(6.131)

where we have defined the first of the
\theta
-functions,
\theta_{3}(\tau)
. Continuing with the
R
sector we have

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\rm R}\left[q^{N+\frac{1}{3}}\right]=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{2}}\left(\prod_{n\geq 0}(1+q^{n})\right)^{8}=\left(\frac{q^{\frac{1}{8}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})\left(1+q^{n}\right)^{2}(1+q^{0})^{2}}{\eta(\tau)}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{2}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{4}},

(6.132)

where
\theta_{2}(\tau)
has been defined.

To understand the behaviour of these two traces under modular transformations we need to complete the picture with the remaining two
\theta
functions
\theta_{4}
and
\theta_{1}
. These two rely on the definition of the
worldsheet
fermion number
F
, which is defined as follows

\displaystyle\text{NS})

\displaystyle\quad(-1)^{F}=-e^{i\pi\sum_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}\psi_{-r}^{i}\psi_{r}^{i}}\,,

(6.133a)

\displaystyle\text{R})

\displaystyle\quad(-1)^{F}=e^{i\pi\sum_{n\geq 0}\psi_{-n}^{i}\psi_{n}^{i}}\to\Gamma_{9}\,e^{i\pi\sum_{n\geq 1}\psi_{-n}^{i}\psi_{n}^{i}}\,.

(6.133b)

Few explanations: the reason for the minus sign in the NS sector is that
|0\rangle_{NS}
secretly contains a fermionic insertion
\delta(\gamma)=e^{-\phi}
from the ghost
(\beta,\gamma)
system, over the bosonic
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
vacuum
|0\rangle_{NS}=e^{-\phi}(0)\ket{0}_{SL(2,\mathbb{C})}
5
5

5

As you can see, some residual super-ghost contamination remains even in the lightcone, see section
6.5.1
for details.
. In the Ramond sector instead we have isolated the zero modes and we have recognized that they are fully captured by the chirality matrix
\Gamma_{9}
: this is clear since every zero-mode fermionic oscillator is a gamma matrix and we can span the whole zero-mode sector by acting on the conventional vacuum
\ket{+\frac 12,+\frac 12,+\frac 12,+\frac 12}
with all possible gamma matrices, each time changing the worldsheet fermion number by one.
Let’s see some relevant example, starting from the holomorphic NS vacuum

(-1)^{F}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}=-\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,.

(6.134)

So, as anticipated, the (lightcone) NS vacuum is considered to be fermionic. On the first excited state we have

(-1)^{F}\psi_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{i}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}=+\psi_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{i}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,.

(6.135)

On the other hand, in the R sector we have that

\displaystyle(-1)^{F}\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=+\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}\,,

(6.136a)

\displaystyle(-1)^{F}\ket{\dot{\alpha},P}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=-\ket{\dot{\alpha},P}_{\text{R}}\,.

(6.136b)

With this understanding, we can now compute

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\rm NS}\left[(-1)^{F}q^{N-\frac{1}{6}}\right]=

\displaystyle-q^{-\frac{1}{6}}\left(\prod_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}(1-q^{r})\right)^{8}=-\left(\frac{\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})\left(1-q^{n-\frac{1}{2}}\right)^{2}}{\eta(\tau)}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\frac{\theta_{4}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{4}}

(6.137)

and

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\rm R}\left[(-1)^{F}q^{N+\frac{1}{3}}\right]=

\displaystyle q^{-\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{2}}\left(\prod_{n\geq 0}(1-q^{n})\right)^{8}=\left(\frac{q^{\frac{1}{8}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n})\left(1+q^{n}\right)^{2}(1-q^{0})^{2}}{\eta(\tau)}\right)^{4}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{1}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{4}}\equiv 0\,.

(6.138)

This last trace
6
6

6

The four traces we have computed correspond to the possible periodicities that we can have for a fermion around the euclidean time circle and the space circle (which gives the already discussed R, NS degeneracy). Assigning a given periodicity around all possible independent cycles of a Riemann surface means to give a
spin structure
. On the torus we have a total of four different spin structures and each of them is giving a trace.
is indeed identically zero because of the cancellation
(1-q^{0})^{2}=(1-1)^{2}
in the zero mode sector:

\theta_{1}(\tau)\equiv 0\,.

(6.139)

Now we are ready to discuss the modular invariance. The modular properties of the
\theta
functions (together with the
\eta
) are given by

\displaystyle\theta_{2}(\tau+1)=

\displaystyle\,e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}}\,\theta_{2}(\tau)\,,

(6.140a)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(\tau+1)=

\displaystyle\,\theta_{4}(\tau)\,,

(6.140b)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}(\tau+1)=

\displaystyle\,\theta_{3}(\tau)\,,

(6.140c)

\displaystyle\eta(\tau+1)=

\displaystyle\,e^{i\frac{\pi}{12}}\,\eta(\tau)\,,

(6.140d)

and

\displaystyle\theta_{2}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=

\displaystyle\,(-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\theta_{4}(\tau)\,,

(6.141a)

\displaystyle\theta_{3}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=

\displaystyle\,(-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\theta_{3}(\tau)\,,

(6.141b)

\displaystyle\theta_{4}\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=

\displaystyle\,(-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\theta_{2}(\tau)\,,

(6.141c)

\displaystyle\eta\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=

\displaystyle\,(-i\tau)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\eta(\tau)\,.

(6.141d)

Using these properties it is immediate to see that the full R-NS partition function of the
c=4

\psi
sector

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\psi}\left[(-1)^{\mathbb{F}}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right]=\left|\frac{\theta_{3}(\tau)^{4}-\theta_{2}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{4}}\right|^{2}

(6.142)

is not modular invariant!
Luckily this is not a dead-end and there is a way-out, the
GSO
- projection (Gliozzi, Sherk, Olive).

6.3
GSO Projection

Given the operator
(-1)^{F}
in the NS and R sector, we can build the projectors

\mathcal{P}_{\pm}^{\text{(R,NS)}}=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(1\pm(-1)^{F_{\text{(R,NS)}}}\bigg)\,.

(6.143)

Notice that in the R sector the two
\pm
choices simply change the selected chirality of the spacetime fermions at every level.
Using these projectors in the holomorphic and anti-holomorphic sector of the
\psi
CFT it has been understood (and you can easily check) that there are four possible truncations of the spectrum which are modular invariant.

•

Type IIA or Type IIB.

These are two projections that are done separately on each holomorphic sector

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{IIA}\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(NS)}}+\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(R)}}\right)\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(NS)}}+\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(R)}}\right)}\,,

(6.144)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{IIB}\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(NS)}}+\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(R)}}\right)\overline{\left(\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(NS)}}+\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(R)}}\right)}\,.

(6.145)

They give rise to the same partition function
7
7

7

This is because
\theta_{1}(\tau)\equiv 0
and it is an accident of the fact that we are computing a vacuum amplitude with no vertex-operator insertions. The generic Type IIA-B loop amplitudes are of course different as they describe interactions of two different set of states, as we will see shortly.

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\psi}\left[\mathcal{P}_{IIA}(-1)^{\mathbb{F}}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}_{\psi}\left[\mathcal{P}_{IIB}(-1)^{\mathbb{F}}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right]

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\theta_{3}(\tau)^{4}-\theta_{4}(\tau)^{4}-\theta_{2}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{4}}\right|^{2},

(6.146)

which is easily shown to be modular invariant.
There is one important fact that we have to know about this partition function: it is
identically vanishing!
This is because of Jacobi’s
aequatio identica satis abstrusa
(friendly referred to as Jacobi’s abstruse identity):

\displaystyle\theta_{3}(\tau)^{4}-\theta_{4}(\tau)^{4}-\theta_{2}(\tau)^{4}=0\,.

(6.147)

The physical meaning of this is easy to grasp: we are considering a partition function
that computes the number of bosonic states minus the fermionic states. Since we find zero it means that we have a perfect pairing of bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom. This is the sign of
spacetime Supersymmetry
. Another related thing, as we will shortly see, is that the NS-NS tachyon state has been projected out. These are thus the first examples of stable string theory vacua. Collecting also the
X
sector (which is already modular invariant by itself, as we have seen in the bosonic string) we finally have

\displaystyle Z_{\text{1-loop}}^{IIA,B}=

\displaystyle\int_{\cal F}\frac{d\tau d\bar{\tau}}{\left({\rm Im}(\tau)\right)^{2}}\,\text{Tr}_{\perp}\left[\mathcal{P}_{IIA,B}(-1)^{\mathbb{F}}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{1}{2}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{1}{2}}\right]\,,

(6.148)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\cal F}\frac{d\tau d\bar{\tau}}{\left({\rm Im}(\tau)\right)^{2}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\rm Im}\tau}\,\eta(\tau)\,\eta(\bar{\tau})}\right)^{8}\left|\frac{\theta_{3}(\tau)^{4}-\theta_{4}(\tau)^{4}-\theta_{2}(\tau)^{4}}{\eta(\tau)^{4}}\right|^{2}=0\,.

Since this loop amplitude is computing the space-time vacuum energy due to the formation of bubbles, this is saying that there is no space-time cosmological constant that is generated by vacuum fluctuations. This is again due to the precise microscopic cancellations implied by space-time supersymmetry.

•

Type 0A or Type 0B.

The other two possibilities are given by the following projections

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{0A}\equiv

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(NS)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(NS)}}}+\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(NS)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(NS)}}}+\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(R)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(R)}}}+\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(R)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(R)}}}\,,

(6.149)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{0B}\equiv

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(NS)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(NS)}}}+\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(NS)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(NS)}}}+\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(R)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{+}^{\text{(R)}}}+\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(R)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(R)}}}\,.

(6.150)

One thing is immediately noticed: in this truncation there are only space-time bosons and fermions have been completely projected out. Second, the tachyon is still there since it is selected by
\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(NS)}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{-}^{\text{(NS)}}}
.
The two partition functions are again identical (because of
\theta_{1}=0
)

\displaystyle\text{Tr}_{\psi}\left[\mathcal{P}_{0A}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}_{\psi}\left[\mathcal{P}_{0B}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{|\theta_{3}(\tau)|^{8}+|\theta_{4}(\tau)|^{8}+|\theta_{2}(\tau)|^{8}}{|\eta(\tau)|^{8}}

(6.151)

and the full loop vacuum amplitude is

\displaystyle Z_{\text{1-loop}}^{0A,B}=

\displaystyle\int_{\cal F}\frac{d\tau d\bar{\tau}}{\left({\rm Im}(\tau)\right)^{2}}\,\text{Tr}_{\perp}\left[\mathcal{P}_{0A,B}\,q^{L_{0}-\frac{1}{2}}\,\bar{q}^{\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{1}{2}}\right]\,,

(6.152)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\cal F}\frac{d\tau d\bar{\tau}}{\left({\rm Im}(\tau)\right)^{2}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\rm Im}\tau}\,\eta(\tau)\,\eta(\bar{\tau})}\right)^{8}\frac{|\theta_{3}(\tau)|^{8}+|\theta_{4}(\tau)|^{8}+|\theta_{2}(\tau)|^{8}}{|\eta(\tau)|^{8}}\,.

Both Type 0A and 0B theories are qualitatively similar to the bosonic string: no fermions and an unstable vacuum signaled by the closed string tachyon. In the sequel we will not consider them anymore.

6.4
Type II Superstring

6.4.1
Closed string

Let us keep studying the Type II closed superstring.
Given what we said about the GSO projection, we now write
\text{GSO}^{\pm}
to identify the GSO projection in the holomorphic sector and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{GSO}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\pm}
to identify the GSO projection in the anti-holomorphic sector.
Hence we have that the choice of the Ramond GSO projection generates two inequivalent theories:

•

type IIA, which corresponds to the choice
\big(\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{R}},\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{GSO}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{-}_{\text{R}}\big)
;

•

type IIB, which corresponds to the choice
\big(\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{R}},\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{GSO}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{+}_{\text{R}}\big)
.

Obviously, they both are
\big(\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{NS}},\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{GSO}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{+}_{\text{NS}}\big)
in the NS sector, so that the
\text{NS}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{NS}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
tachyon is removed. We are now going to explicitly see that these two theories have an equal number of bosonic and fermionic d.o.f. at every mass level, which is an explicit signal of spacetime supersymmetry.

6.4.1.1
Closed type IIA

If we now analyze the closed type IIA spectrum up to the massless level in the lightcone quantization, we find what follows.

\bullet\,

\text{NS}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{NS}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector

The generic polarization of this sector is a Lorentz representation with two
8_{V}
indices which can be split up into its symmetric part (s), anti-symmetric part (a) and trace:

t^{ij}=\delta^{ij}\Phi+a^{[ij]}+s^{(ij)}\,.

(6.153)

This can be written as

8_{V}\otimes 8_{V}=1\oplus 28_{a}\oplus 35_{s}\,,

(6.154)

where

\cdot

the singlet (1) is the dilaton (spin 0);

\cdot

the anti-symmetric part (
28_{a}
) is the Kalb-Ramond field (spin 1);

\cdot

the symmetric part (
35_{s}
) is the graviton (spin 2).

\bullet\,

\text{NS}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector

The generic polarization of this sector has a vector index
\mu
and a spinorial index
\dot{\alpha}
, namely
\chi^{i}_{\dot{\alpha}}
.
This can be written as

8_{V}\otimes 8_{S}=8_{C}\oplus 56_{S}\,,

(6.155)

where

\cdot

the
8_{C}
is the dilatino (spin
1/2
, left in spacetime), which is the
\Gamma
-trace of
\chi^{i}_{\dot{\alpha}}
:

\eta_{\beta}=\Gamma^{i}_{\beta\dot{\alpha}}\,\chi^{i}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,;

(6.156)

\cdot

the
56_{S}
is the gravitino (spin
3/2
, right in spacetime):

\psi^{i}_{\dot{\beta}}=\chi^{i}_{\dot{\beta}}-\frac{1}{D-2}\Gamma^{i}_{\dot{\beta}\alpha}\eta_{\alpha}.

(6.157)

\bullet\,

\text{R}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{NS}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector

The generic polarization of this sector has a vector index
\mu
and a spinorial index
\alpha
, namely
\chi^{i}_{\alpha}
.
This means

8_{C}\otimes 8_{V}=8_{S}\oplus 56_{C}\,,

(6.158)

where

\cdot

the
8_{S}
is the dilatino (spin
1/2
, right in spacetime), which is the
\Gamma
-trace of
\chi^{i}_{\alpha}
:

\eta_{\dot{\beta}}=\Gamma^{i}_{\dot{\beta}\alpha}\,\chi^{i}_{\alpha}\,;

(6.159)

\cdot

the
56_{C}
is the gravitino (spin
3/2
, left in spacetime):

\psi^{i}_{\beta}=\chi^{i}_{\beta}-\frac{1}{D-2}\Gamma^{i}_{\beta\dot{\alpha}}\eta_{\dot{\alpha}}.

(6.160)

\bullet\,

\text{R}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector

The generic polarization of this sector has two spinorial indices
\alpha,\dot{\alpha}
, namely
H_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}
. It can be expanded in
\Gamma
matrices as follows:

H_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=C_{i}\Gamma_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{i}+C_{ijk}\Gamma_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{ijk}\,,

(6.161)

where we used the definition

\Gamma^{i_{1}\dots i_{n}}=\frac{1}{n!}\Gamma^{[i_{1}}\cdots\Gamma^{i_{n}]}\,.

(6.162)

The reason why, inside the decomposition (
6.161
), we did not go further in the differential forms order will be clarified in
6.4.1.3
.
In representation notation, this expansion can be written as

8_{C}\otimes 8_{S}=8_{V}\oplus 56_{a}\,,

(6.163)

where

\cdot

the
8_{V}
is the massless vector
C_{\mu}
(spin
1
), which is a 1-form whose associated field strength is
F^{(2)}=dC^{(1)}
, namely

F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{[\mu}C_{\nu]}\,;

(6.164)

\cdot

the anti-symmetric
56_{a}
is the
3
-form
C_{\mu\nu\rho}
whose associated field strength is
F^{(4)}=dC^{(3)}
, namely

F_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}=\partial_{[\mu}C_{\nu\rho\sigma]}\,.

(6.165)

We used the notation
C^{(p)}=C_{i_{1}\dots i_{p}}dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{i_{p}}
.
These differential forms are called
Ramond-Ramond forms
and we will investigate them more in
6.4.1.3
.

Everything we said so far about
SO(8)
representations is deeply related to its
triality
property
8
8

8

The
SO(8)
group has a triality property, which means that its vectorial representation and its two spinorial representations, having the same dimension, are isomorphic.
We can write

\displaystyle 8_{i}\otimes 8_{i}

\displaystyle=1\oplus 28\oplus 35\,,\quad\text{with }i=V,C,S\,,

(6.166a)

\displaystyle 8_{i}\otimes 8_{j}

\displaystyle=8_{k}\oplus 56\,,\quad\qquad\!\;\!\!\text{with }i\neq j\neq k\,.

(6.166b)

Such a property is evocative of the explicit
\mathbb{Z}_{3}
-symmetry of the Dynkin diagram of the
SO(8)
algebra (see fig.
6.2
).

.

Figure 6.2:
The
SO(8)
algebra Dynkin diagram.

The massless content of the type IIA closed superstring is summarized in table
6.3
.

Closed Type IIA

Bosons

Fermions

\Phi

dilaton

spin 0

\eta_{\alpha}

dilatino (left)

spin
1/2

B_{ij}

Kalb-Ramond

spin 1

\eta_{\dot{\alpha}}

dilatino (right)

spin
1/2

G_{ij}

graviton

spin 2

\psi^{i}_{\dot{\beta}}

gravitino (left)

spin
3/2

C_{i}

1-form

\psi^{i}_{{\beta}}

gravitino (right)

spin
3/2

C_{ijk}

3-form

Table 6.3:
The massless spectrum of the type IIA closed superstring.

It is the same spectrum we find in type IIA supergravity (SUGRA), a theory invariant under local supersimmetry which leads to general coordinate invariance (since, as we saw in eq. (
6.34
),
(\text{SUSY})^{2}=\text{translation}
).

Its action is

\displaystyle S_{\text{IIA}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2K_{10}^{2}}\int d^{10}x\,\sqrt{-G}\left\{e^{-2\Phi}\left(R+4\left(\partial\Phi\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|H_{(3)}|^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}|F_{(2)}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|F_{(4)}|^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4K_{10}^{2}}\int B_{(2)}\wedge dC_{(3)}\wedge dC_{(3)}

\displaystyle\quad+(\text{fermions couplings})

\displaystyle\quad+(\alpha^{\prime}\text{ corrections})\,,

(6.167)

where

-

K_{10}
is the Newton constant in
10
dimensions, which is related to the basic string parameters as
K_{10}=8\pi^{7/2}{\alpha^{\prime}}^{2}g_{s}
;

-

\int d^{10}x\,\sqrt{-G}\,e^{-2\phi}R
is the Einstein-Hilbert action;

-

4\left(\partial\Phi\right)^{2}
is the kinetic term for the dilaton;

-

H_{(3)}=dB_{(2)}
is the kinetic term of the Kalb-Ramond field;

-

F_{(2)}=dC_{(1)}
and
F_{(4)}=dC_{(3)}-dB_{(2)}\wedge C_{(1)}
are the kinetic terms for the RR fields;

-

\int B_{(2)}\wedge dC_{(3)}\wedge dC_{(3)}
is a topological term, independent of the metric.

6.4.1.2
Closed type IIB

If we now look at the closed type IIB spectrum up to the massless level in the lightcone quantization, we realize that, mutatis mutandis, is quite similar to the type IIA spectrum.
It is summarized in table
6.4
.

Closed Type IIB

Bosons

Fermions

\Phi

dilaton

spin 0

\eta_{\dot{\alpha}}

dilatino (right)

spin
1/2

B_{ij}

Kalb-Ramond

spin 1

\eta^{\prime}_{\dot{\alpha}}

dilatino (right)

spin
1/2

G_{ij}

graviton

spin 2

\psi^{i}_{\beta}

gravitino (left)

spin
3/2

C

0-form

spin 0

\psi^{\prime i}_{\beta}

gravitino (left)

spin
3/2

C_{ij}

2-form

C_{ijkl}

Table 6.4:
The massless spectrum of the type IIB closed superstring.

It is the same spectrum we find in type IIB supergravity, whose action is

\displaystyle S_{\text{IIB}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2K_{10}^{2}}\int d^{10}x\,\sqrt{-G}\Bigg\{e^{-2\Phi}\left(R+4\left(\partial\Phi\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|H_{(3)}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,-\frac{1}{2}|F_{(1)}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|F_{(3)}|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|F_{(5)}|^{2}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4K_{10}^{2}}\int C_{(4)}\wedge H_{(3)}\wedge F_{(3)}

\displaystyle\quad+(\text{fermions couplings})

\displaystyle\quad+(\alpha^{\prime}\text{ corrections})\,,

(6.168)

where everything has an analogous meaning to what we previously said, and where

\displaystyle F_{(1)}

\displaystyle=dC\,,

(6.169a)

\displaystyle F_{(3)}

\displaystyle=dC_{(2)}-CdB_{(2)}\,,

(6.169b)

\displaystyle F_{(5)}

\displaystyle=dC_{(4)}-\frac{1}{2}dC_{(2)}\wedge B_{(2)}+\frac{1}{2}B_{(2)}\wedge dC_{(2)}\,.

(6.169c)

6.4.1.3
Ramond-Ramond potentials

Let us now consider the type IIA case. In the
\text{R}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector the generic state can be expanded in differential forms as

H_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}=C_{i}\Gamma_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{i}+C_{i_{1}\dots i_{3}}\Gamma_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{i_{1}\dots i_{3}}+C_{i_{1}\dots i_{5}}\Gamma_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{i_{1}\dots i_{5}}+C_{i_{1}\dots i_{7}}\Gamma_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}^{i_{1}\dots i_{7}}\,.

(6.170)

However, thanks to the
\Gamma
duality property

\Gamma^{i_{1}\dots i_{n}}\propto\varepsilon^{i_{1}\dots i_{n}j_{1}\dots j_{8-n}}\Gamma_{j_{1}\dots j_{8-n}}\,,

(6.171)

these differential forms are not independent with respect to each other. Indeed we have that

\displaystyle C_{i_{1}\dots i_{7}}\propto\varepsilon_{i_{1}\dots i_{7}}^{\hskip 16.38895pt\!\!j}C_{j}

\displaystyle\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ C_{(7)}=\star_{8}C_{(1)}\,,

(6.172a)

\displaystyle C_{i_{1}\dots i_{3}}\propto\varepsilon_{i_{1}\dots i_{3}}^{\hskip 16.38895pt\!\!j_{1}\dots j_{5}}C_{j_{1}\dots j_{5}}

\displaystyle\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ C_{(3)}=\star_{8}C_{(5)}\,,

(6.172b)

where
\star_{8}
is the Hodge duality in 8 dimensions. We call
Ramond-Ramond potentials
the two independents forms
C_{(1)}
and
C_{(3)}
. The other two,
C_{(5)}
and
C_{(7)}
are not independent and therefore they are not needed in the expansion of the bispinor
H_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}
. This is the reason why we did not write them in eq. (
6.161
).

Let’s now extend this reasoning from 8 to 10 dimensions. Let’s start recalling the form of the Ramond vacua as a spinor-values spin-field acting on the conformal vacuum

\displaystyle\text{R})

\displaystyle\quad S_{\alpha}(0)\ket{0}\qquad\text{with }\alpha\in 16_{C}\,,

(6.173a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu)

\displaystyle\quad S_{\dot{\alpha}}(0)\ket{0}\qquad\text{with }\dot{\alpha}\in 16_{S}\,.

(6.173b)

The gamma matrices are now
32\times 32
. Moreover, we define
\mathcal{C}
as the charge conjugation matrix, which realizes a similarity transformation in the Clifford algebra:

\mathcal{C}^{-1}\left(\Gamma^{\mu}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}=\Gamma^{\mu}\,.

(6.174)

The spinors
S_{\alpha}
that are involved in the VO defining the Ramond states satisfy the reality condition

S_{\alpha}^{\dagger}\Gamma^{0}=S_{\alpha}^{T}\mathcal{C}\,.

(6.175)

This means that they are Majorana spinors and hence, while performing the transformation
\ket{S_{\alpha}}\,\longrightarrow\,\bra{S_{\alpha}}
, we do not have to worry about taking the hermitian or the BPZ conjugation.

Let us now write the vertex operator

F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}(P)\bigg(\left(S(z)\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}(\bar{z})\bigg)\,.

(6.176)

If we recall the action of the chirality matrix (in
D=10
)
\Gamma^{11}
on the spin fields of Type IIA

\displaystyle(\Gamma^{11}S)_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=+S_{\alpha}\,,

(6.177a)

\displaystyle(\Gamma^{11}\tilde{S})_{\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{S}_{\dot{\alpha}}\,,

(6.177b)

the fermionic bilinear can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\left(S\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}

\displaystyle=\left(S\right)^{T}\left(\Gamma^{11}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}\,.

(6.178)

Now, using
\left\{\Gamma^{11},\Gamma^{\mu}\right\}=0
and
\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{11}+\left(\Gamma^{11}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}=0
, we can write

\displaystyle\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}

\displaystyle=\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\left(\Gamma^{11}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\Gamma^{11}\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+1}\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\left(-\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p}\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}\,.

(6.179)

Therefore
p
has to be even. This means that in type IIA a
p
-form
F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}
can be coupled to the two holomorphic and anti-holomorphic spin fields of opposite chirlaity only if
p=2n
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. On the other hand, in lightcone quantization RR potentials had an odd number of vectorial indices. This strongly suggests that
F
is the field strenght of the
C
gauge potentials we have enxountered in the lightcone.

To establish this, let us now consider, in the
\text{R}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector, the state

\ket{\text{state}}=F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}\,e^{iP\cdot X}(0,0)\ket{0}\,.

(6.180)

The two super-current constraint give two Dirac operators in the two holomorphic sides

\displaystyle G_{0}\ket{\text{state}}

\displaystyle=0\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ P^{\mu}\psi_{0\,\mu}=0\,,

(6.181a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muG\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{0}\ket{\text{state}}

\displaystyle=0\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ P^{\mu}\bar{\psi}_{0\,\mu}=0\,,

(6.181b)

namely

\displaystyle G_{0}\ket{\text{state}}

\displaystyle=P_{\mu}F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\left(\Gamma^{\mu}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}(0,0)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=P_{\mu}F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}(0,0)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=P_{\mu}F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}\left(S_{\alpha}\right)^{T}\mathcal{C}\big(\Gamma_{\mu}^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}+p\delta_{\mu}^{[\mu_{1}}\Gamma^{\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{p}]}\big)\tilde{S}_{\dot{\beta}}(0,0)\ket{0}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.182)

and similarly for
\overline{G}_{0}
.
Therefore the constraints produce

•

the Bianchi identities:

P_{[\mu}F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}]}=0\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ dF^{(p)}=0\,;

(6.183)

•

the generalized propagating Maxwell equations:

P_{\mu}F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}^{\mu}=0\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ d\star_{10}F^{(p)}=0\,.

(6.184)

Given a field strength
F^{(p)}
, locally there exists a potential
C^{(p-1)}
such that
F^{(p)}=dC^{(p-1)}
. This is the reason why we will call them “Ramond-Ramond potentials”.

In the lightcone we have
C^{(1)}
,
C^{(3)}
and their Hodge-duals
C^{(7)}
,
C^{(5)}
. In
D=10
they become R-R potentials that we still call
C^{(1)}
,
C^{(3)}
and
C^{(7)}
,
C^{(5)}
, with their associated field strength
F^{(2)}
,
F^{(4)}
and
F^{(8)}
,
F^{(6)}
. In this 10-dimensional case we use the field strengths, which are gauge invariants. In the lightcone case, on the other hand, we used the potentials
C^{(p-1)}
since there we had no gauge symmetry.
Moreover

\displaystyle F^{(6)}

\displaystyle=\star_{10}F^{(4)}\,,

(6.185a)

\displaystyle F^{(8)}

\displaystyle=\star_{10}F^{(2)}\,,

(6.185b)

where we used the general definition of the Hodge duality in
d
dimensions:

\big(\star_{10}F^{(p)}\big)_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{d-p}}=\sqrt{-g}\,\varepsilon_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{d-p}\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{p}}F^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{p}}\,.

(6.186)

This is clearly not a topological operation: it can only be performed on Riemaniann manifolds, namely on metric-equipped manifolds.

The kinetic term for the field strength is given by
F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}F^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}
(just like we used
F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}
in electromagnetism). If we couple it to gravity, we get

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\int d^{d}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,F_{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}F^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}

\displaystyle=\int d^{d}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,\left|F^{(p)}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{M}_{d}}F^{(p)}\wedge\star F^{(p)}\,,

(6.187)

where
\mathcal{M}_{d}
is a
d
-dimensional manifold.

6.4.1.4
Electric-Magnetic duality

In order to understand which is the physical role of Hodge duality, we can look at the electromagnetic tensor in
d=4
:

F_{\mu\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}0&E_{1}&E_{2}&E_{3}\\
-E_{1}&0&B_{3}&-B_{2}\\
-E_{2}&-B_{3}&0&B_{1}\\
-E_{3}&B_{2}&-B_{1}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(6.188)

Its Hodge-dual is

(\star F)_{\mu\nu}=\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}=\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}0&B_{1}&B_{2}&B_{3}\\
-B_{1}&0&-E_{3}&E_{2}\\
-B_{2}&E_{3}&0&E_{1}\\
-B_{3}&-E_{2}&E_{1}&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(6.189)

Therefore the action of the Hodge duality is to swap

\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\displaystyle E\hfil

\displaystyle\,\longrightarrow\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle B\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle B\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle B\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle B\hfil$\crcr}}}\,,

\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\displaystyle B\hfil

\displaystyle\,\longrightarrow\,-\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle E\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle E\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle E\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle E\hfil$\crcr}}}\,.

Moreover we can write

•

Maxwell equations:

\partial_{\mu}F^{\mu\nu}=0\,;

(6.191)

•

Bianchi identities:

\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=0\,.

(6.192)

There is an explicit symmetry between these two equations.
If we then introduce an electromagnetic source, Maxwell equations become inhomogeneous and the duality is lost unless we assume the existence of a magnetic current:

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}F^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=j^{\nu}_{\text{electr.}}\,,

(6.193a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}\widetilde{F}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=j^{\nu}_{\text{magn.}}\,.

(6.193b)

These equations are invariant under

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\,\longleftrightarrow\,\widetilde{F}=\star F\,,

(6.194a)

\displaystyle j_{\text{electr.}}

\displaystyle\,\longleftrightarrow\,j_{\text{magn.}}\,.

(6.194b)

Let us now suppose we have a magnetic monopole. We may recall that for a common electric charge
q_{e}
the Gauss theorem says that

where

. Therefore, for a magnetic charge

Let us then suppose we have an electric charge
q_{e}
moving around in the background generated by the magnetic charge
q_{m}
. The minimal coupling between

and the gauge field
A^{(1)}
(with

) is given by

\int_{\gamma}A^{(1)}=\int d\tau\,A_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\,,

(6.197)

where
\tau
is the parameter that parametrizes

’s trajectory
\gamma
.
