e^{iq_{e}\int_{\gamma}A^{(1)}}\Psi_{q_{e}}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})\,.

(6.198)

Moreover, referring to fig.
6.3
, we have that

\gamma=\partial D_{1}=-\partial D_{2}\,.

(6.199)

What we just said (together with the assumption that, being far from the magnetic monopole, we can locally write
dA^{(1)}=F^{(2)}
) allows us to compute the following:

\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}A^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial D_{1}}A^{(1)}=\int_{D_{1}}F^{(2)}\,,

(6.200a)

\displaystyle\int_{\gamma}A^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\partial D_{2}}A^{(1)}=-\int_{D_{2}}F^{(2)}\,,

(6.200b)

and since
D_{1}+D_{2}=\mathcal{S}_{2}
, this all implies that

-\int_{D_{2}}F^{(2)}=-\int_{\mathcal{S}_{2}}F^{(2)}+\int_{D_{1}}F^{(2)}\,.

(6.201)

Figure 6.3:
The electric charge
q_{e}
travelling in a closed loop
\gamma
around the magnetic charge
q_{m}
, radiating flux lines.

We thus found an ambiguity in the phase. In order to remove this problem we have to impose

e^{-iq_{e}\int_{S^{2}}F^{(2)}}=e^{-iq_{e}q_{m}}=1\,,

(6.202)

where we have used eq. (
6.196
). Therefore we obtain

q_{e}q_{m}=2\pi n\,,\quad\text{with }n\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(6.203)

which is called
Dirac quantization condition
. The smallest electric and magnetic charges that satisfy such a condition (i.e. for
n=1
) are

\hat{q}_{e}\hat{q}_{m}=2\pi\,.

(6.204)

6.4.1.5
Electric and magnetic branes

We saw that a 1-form
A^{(1)}
naturally couples to a worldline, namely to a particle (of charge
q
), as follows:

q\int_{\gamma}A^{(1)}=q\int d\tau\,A_{\mu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\,.

(6.205)

Analogously, a 2-form
B^{(2)}
naturally couples to a worldsheet, namely to a string. This is the case of the Kalb-Ramond form:

\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}B^{(2)}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int_{\text{WS}}d^{2}\sigma\,B_{\mu\nu}\partial X^{\mu}\bar{\partial}X^{\nu}\,.

(6.206)

This story generalizes for RR fields

•

In type IIA theory the R-R potentials are

–

C^{(1)}
, which couples to an object having a 1-dimensional worldvolume and an electric charge given by the flux of the R-R field, namely to a 0-brane (i.e. a particle);

–

its Hodge-dual
C^{(7)}
, which couples to 6-brane;

–

C^{(3)}
, which couples to a 2-brane;

–

its Hodge-dual
C^{(5)}
, which couples to 4-brane.

•

In type IIB theory the R-R potentials are

–

C^{(0)}
, which couples to a (-1)-brane;

–

its Hodge-dual
C^{(8)}
, which couples to 7-brane;

–

C^{(2)}
, which couples to a 1-brane;

–

its Hodge-dual
C^{(6)}
, which couples to 5-brane;

–

C^{(4)}
, which couples to a 3-brane and which is self-dual.

Polchinski realized that these are precisely the open string
D(p)
-branes!
We could then say that the
D(p)
-brane boundary conditions of the
(X;\psi)
-SCFT corresponds to R-R field sources. This means that, in superstring theory,
D(p)
-branes not only have mass and energy, but also a charge, called
R-R charge
.

A
D(p)
-brane can be described through a boundary state
\ket{D(p)}
defined as

\innerproduct{D(p)}{\mathcal{V}}=\langle\mathcal{V}(0,0)\rangle^{\text{$D(p)$ BC}}_{\text{disk}}\,,

(6.207)

where
\mathcal{V}
is a generic matter vertex operator.
The boundary state of
\text{BC}=a
defined as

\innerproduct{B_a}{\mathcal{V}}=\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{disk_VO.pdf}}\put(0.62492,0.63479){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$a$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{disk_VO.pdf}}\put(0.43668,0.30548){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\mathcal{V}$\end{tabular}}}}\end{picture}}
}}=\langle\mathcal{V}(0,\bar{0})\rangle^{(a)}_{\text{disk}}

(6.208)

is an object analogous to what we introduced in the bosonic string to discuss the Cardy condition (see eq. (
5.212
)). The reason can be understood as follows:

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left\{e^{-2\pi tH}\right\}

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Cardy}}{=}\bra{B_a}e^{-2\frac{\pi}{t}(H+\bar{H})}\ket{B_a}=\bra{B_a}\text{1}e^{-2\frac{\pi}{t}(H+\bar{H})}\text{1}\ket{B_a}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,=\sum_{\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V}^{\prime}}\innerproduct{B_a}{\mathcal{V}}\bra{\mathcal{V}}e^{-2\frac{\pi}{t}(H+\bar{H})}\ket{\mathcal{V}'}\innerproduct{\mathcal{V}'}{B_a}

\displaystyle\,\,\,\,=\sum_{\mathcal{V}}\left|\innerproduct{\mathcal{V}}{B_a}\right|^{2}e^{-\frac{\pi}{t}(h_{\mathcal{V}}+h_{\mathcal{V}})}\,,

(6.209)

where
h_{\mathcal{V}}
is the weight of
\mathcal{V}
and where we used a basis where
H
(and hence
L_{0}
) is diagonal, which implies
\ket{\mathcal{V}}=\ket{\mathcal{V}'}
. We decomposed the propagator in all its possible channels:

\text{Tr}\left\{e^{-2\pi tH}\right\}=\sum_{\mathcal{V}}\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{two_disk_VO.pdf}}\put(0.21472,0.00183){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\ket{B_a}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.25506,0.45125){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\mathcal{V}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{two_disk_VO.pdf}}\put(0.62823,0.0){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\ket{B_a}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.68575,0.44619){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\mathcal{V}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.3224,0.17255){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$e^{-\frac{\pi}{t}(h_{\mathcal{V}}+h_{\mathcal{V}})}$\end{tabular}}}}\end{picture}}
}}\,.

(6.210)

We hence realize that
\innerproduct{B_a}{\mathcal{V}}
represents the emission of an asymptotic state
\bra{\mathcal{V}}
from the disk of
\text{BC}=a
(see eq. (
6.208
)).

If we evaluate the R-R potential on a disk having
D(p)
BC we find

\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{disk_VO_Dp.pdf}}\put(0.4815,0.25457){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\mathcal{V}_{RR}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(-0.20674,0.1426){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$D(p)$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{disk_VO_Dp.pdf}}\end{picture}}
}}\sim\mu_{p}\,,

(6.211)

which is the R-R charge of a
D(p)
-brane.

On the other hand, for the graviton
G_{\mu\nu}
(whose vertex operator is written as
\mathcal{V}_{G}
) we have that

\vbox{\hbox{
{ \begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{disk_VO_Dp_graviton.pdf}}\put(0.4815,0.25457){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$\mathcal{V}_{G}$\end{tabular}}}}\put(-0.20674,0.1426){\pic@makebox@{\makebox}{}(0.0,0.0)[lt]{\smash{\begin{tabular}[t]{l}$D(p)$\end{tabular}}}}\put(0.0,0.0){\includegraphics[width,page]{disk_VO_Dp_graviton.pdf}}\end{picture}}
}}\sim T_{p}\,,

(6.212)

which is the
D(p)
-brane tension, namely its mass per volume unit.

This tells us that

\bra{D(p)}=T_{p}\bra{\text{NS}\,\overbar{\text{NS}}}+\mu_{p}\bra{\text{R}\,\overbar{\text{R}}}\,,

(6.213)

where
\bra{\text{NS}\,\overbar{\text{NS}}}
is a state in the
\text{NS}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{NS}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector, while
\bra{\text{R}\,\overbar{\text{R}}}
is a state in the
\text{R}\,\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector.

From a spacetime point of view, the R-R charge of a
D(p)
-brane is obtained by straightorward generalization of Gauss theorem

\mu_{p}=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{8-p}}\star F^{(p+2)}=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{8-p}}{F}^{(8-p)}\,,

(6.214)

being
\mathcal{S}_{8-p}
an
(8-p)
-sphere surrounding the
D(p)
-brane.

All in all we get the following structure:

\displaystyle F^{(p+2)}\,\,\quad\qquad\xleftrightarrow{\text{Hodge duality}}\,\,\quad\qquad{F}^{(8-p)}

\displaystyle d\Bigg\uparrow\phantom{d}\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!d\Bigg\uparrow\phantom{d}

\displaystyle C^{(p+1)}\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\!\!\!\!\!C^{(7-p)}

\displaystyle\downarrow\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\downarrow

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\text{couples to a $D(p)$-brane}\\
\text{which is an extended}\\
\text{object with electric charge}\end{subarray}\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\begin{subarray}{c}\text{couples to a $D(6-p)$-brane}\\
\text{which is an extended}\\
\text{object with magnetic charge}\end{subarray}

\displaystyle\mu_{p}=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{8-p}}{F}^{(8-p)}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\mu}_{6-p}=\int_{\mathcal{S}_{p+2}}F^{(p+2)}

Polchinski explicitly showed that, if one evaluates separately
\mu_{p}
and
{\mu}_{6-p}
, they are such that (in appropriate units)

\mu_{p}\mu_{6-p}=2\pi\,.

(6.215)

Therefore
D(p)
-branes have the smallest electric and magnetic charge allowed by Dirac quantization condition: they are the fundamental quanta of RR-charge.

In type IIA the fundamental charged objects are therefore

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\text{$D(0)$-brane: }&\bra{D(0)}=T_{0}\bra{\text{NS}\,\overbar{\text{NS}}}+\mu_{0}\bra{\text{R}\,\overbar{\text{R}}}\\
\text{anti-$D(0)$-brane: }&\bra{\overline{D(0)}}=T_{0}\bra{\text{NS}\,\overbar{\text{NS}}}-\mu_{0}\bra{\text{R}\,\overbar{\text{R}}}\end{cases}

(6.216a)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\text{$D(2)$-brane: }&\bra{D(2)}=T_{2}\bra{\text{NS}\,\overbar{\text{NS}}}+\mu_{2}\bra{\text{R}\,\overbar{\text{R}}}\\
\text{anti-$D(2)$-brane: }&\bra{\overline{D(2)}}=T_{2}\bra{\text{NS}\,\overbar{\text{NS}}}-\mu_{2}\bra{\text{R}\,\overbar{\text{R}}}\end{cases}

(6.216b)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\vdots\,.

If we build a
D-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
system with a
D
-brane and a
\bar{D}
-brane on top of each other, their boundary states get summed together and the
\text{R}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector cancels out:

\bra{D(p)-\overbar{D(p)}}=2T_{p}\bra{\text{NS}\,\overbar{\text{NS}}}\,.

(6.217)

means that the
D-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
system has a vanishing R-R charge. As we will see, this system is unstable, just like an electron-positron pair.

We can also build bosonic-like
D(p)
-branes considering the cases where the corresponding R-R potential is not present, for example in Type IIA:

\bra{D(1)}=T_{1}\bra{\text{NS}\,\overbar{\text{NS}}}\,.

(6.218)

These
D
-branes are called
non-BPS
and are unstable objects, just like bosonic
D
-branes.
In conclusion, type IIA has R-R charged
D(p)
-branes for
p
even and un-charged (bosonic-like)
D(p)
-branes for
p
odd.

On the other hand, type IIB has R-R charged
D(p)
-branes for
p
odd and un-charged (bosonic-like)
D(p)
-branes for
p
even.

6.4.2
D
-branes and open superstrings

Let us now talk about open strings, whose physics depend on the
D(p)
-branes system we are considering.
As we already know, in the open case we have a single holomorphic sector, which splits into NS and R. We thus have to build a boundary SCFT where the gluing condition will be

\displaystyle T(z)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{z})\,,

(6.219a)

\displaystyle\text{NS})\quad G(z)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muG\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{z})\,,

(6.219b)

\displaystyle\text{R})\quad G(z)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muG\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}<0\\
-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muG\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}>0\\
\end{cases}\,.

(6.219c)

The gluing condition of the supercurrent in the R case comes from the fact that in
z=\bar{z}=0
there is a spin field which generates a branch cut on the real axis from 0 to
\infty
.

The doubling trick for the stress-energy tensor reads as

T_{\mathbb{C}}=\begin{cases}T_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(z)\quad\text{for }Im(z)>0\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muT\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(z^{*})\!\quad\text{for }Im(z)<0\end{cases}\,.

(6.220)

We now define a label in order to distinguish between NS and R:

\epsilon=\begin{cases}+1\quad\text{for NS}\\
-1\quad\text{for R}\end{cases}\,.

(6.221)

Hence we can write the doubling trick for the supercurrent as

G_{\mathbb{C}}^{(\epsilon)}(z)=\begin{cases}G_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(z)\,\,\,\qquad\text{for }Im(z)>0\\
\begin{cases}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muG\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(z^{*})\,\,\quad\text{for }Im(z)>0\text{, }Re(z)<0\\
\epsilon\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muG\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\text{\tiny UHP}}(z^{*})\quad\text{for }Im(z)>0\text{, }Re(z)>0\end{cases}\end{cases}\,.

(6.222)

After a closed path around the origin, we have that

G_{\mathbb{C}}^{(\epsilon)}(ze^{2i\pi})=\epsilon G_{\mathbb{C}}^{(\epsilon)}(z)\,,

(6.223)

since in the R case there is a branch cut from
0
to
\infty
on the real axis.

The bosonic current
j^{\mu}(z)
has the usual gluing condition

j^{\mu}(z)=\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(j)}\bar{j}^{\mu}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for $z=\bar{z}$}\,,

(6.224)

with

\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(j)}=\begin{cases}+1\,\text{ for $\text{A}=\text{Neumann}$}\\
-1\,\text{ for $\text{A}=\text{Dirichlet}$}\end{cases}\,.

(6.225)

If we then recall that

\delta_{\text{SUSY}}^{(\epsilon)}\psi^{\mu\,(\epsilon)}(z)=j^{\mu}(z)\,,

(6.226)

we have that the bosonic gluing condition for
j^{\mu}(z)
immediately induces the fermionic gluing condition for
\psi^{\mu}(z)
, which are

\psi^{\mu\,(\epsilon)}(z)=\begin{cases}\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(\psi)}\bar{\psi}^{\mu\,(\epsilon)}(\bar{z})\,\,\;\!\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}<0\\[5.0pt]
\epsilon\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(\psi)}\bar{\psi}^{\mu\,(\epsilon)}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}>0\\
\end{cases}\,,

(6.227)

where the gluing map
\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(\psi)}
is the same as
\Omega_{\text{A}}^{(j)}
.

A
D(p)
-brane is a
(X;\psi)
-BCFT where

•

(X^{\mu};\psi^{\mu})
have
\Omega=+1
(Neumann BC) for
\mu=0,1,\dots,p
;

•

(X^{i};\psi^{i})
have
\Omega=-1
(Dirichlet BC) for
i=p+1,\dots,9
.

These
D(p)
-branes live in a 10-dimensional spacetime codified by closed superstring of type IIA or type IIB.

We now want to see what is the effect of GSO projection on the open strings sector.
Given a type IIA/B background, let us consider two boundary states
\ket{D(p_1)}
and
\ket{D(p_2)}
in the directions transverse to the light-cone. We then use the Cardy condition and write

\bra{D(p_1)}e^{-\frac{\pi}{t}(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{12})}\ket{D(p_2)}=\text{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{12}}\left\{e^{-2\pi t(L_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}\right\}\,,

(6.228)

where
\mathcal{H}_{12}
is the Hilbert space of the strings stretched between the
D(p_{1})
-brane and the
D(p_{2})
-brane, transverse to the light-cone. In the following we separately study the cases where
p_{1}=p_{2}=p
.

6.4.2.1
The charged
D(p)-D(p)
system

Let us consider the
D(p)-D(p)
branes system, where the two
D(p)
-branes both have the same non-zero R-R charge (hence
p=2n
in type IIA and
p=2n+1
in type IIB). The open strings

\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{11}&\phi_{12}\\
\phi_{21}&\phi_{22}\end{pmatrix}

(6.229)

can have both NS and R sectors.
From the Cardy condition it follows that

•

in the NS sector the
\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{NS}}
projection leaves the
8_{V}
representation and throws away the tachyon:

•

in the R sector the
\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{R}}
projection leaves the
8_{C}
representation, while the
\text{GSO}^{-}_{\text{R}}
projection leaves the
8_{S}
representation (this is the
SO(8)
content, which should be appropriately decomposed following the breaking pattern induced by the
Dp
boundary conditions).

The double possibility we have in the R sector (namely
\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{R}}
or
\text{GSO}^{-}_{\text{R}}
) corresponds to the fact that we can have positive R-R charge (
D(p)-D(p)
system) or negative R-R charge (
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
system).
These GSO projections will be denoted as
\text{GSO}^{\pm}(+)
, where the superscript refers to the R sector, while the parenthesis refers to the NS sector.
The GSO we perform on the open strings are hence

\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{11}&\phi_{12}\\
\phi_{21}&\phi_{22}\end{pmatrix}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \begin{pmatrix}\text{GSO}^{\pm}(+)&\text{GSO}^{\pm}(+)\\
\text{GSO}^{\pm}(+)&\text{GSO}^{\pm}(+)\end{pmatrix}\,.

(6.230)

6.4.2.2
BPS condition

Let us now look at the annulus partition function. We have already seen that in the bulk the torus partition is identically vanishing which is the sign that there is space-time supersymmetry.
On the boundary, namely for the open string, let us consider a charged
D(p)-D(p)
system, where the two
D(p)
-branes are parallel to each other and separated along a Dirichlet direction
y
, at a distance
\Delta y
. Computing the one-loop vacuum amplitude, one finds

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(2t)^{2}}\text{Tr}^{\perp}\left[(-1)^{\mathbb{F}}\frac{1}{2}\Big(1+(-1)^{F}\Big)e^{-2\pi t(L_{0}-\frac{1}{2})}\right]=

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(2t)^{2}}\,\left(\frac{1}{2t}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\,e^{-\frac{t(\Delta y)^{2}}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\eta(it)}\right)^{8}\,\left(\frac{{\theta_{3}}^{4}-{\theta_{4}}^{4}-{\theta_{2}}^{4}}{\eta^{4}}\right)\!(it)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\left(\frac{\pi}{s}\right)^{\frac{d_{\perp}}{2}}\,e^{-\frac{(\Delta y)^{2}}{2\alpha^{\prime}s}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\eta\!\left(\frac{is}{\pi}\right)}\right)^{8}\,\left(\frac{{\theta_{3}}^{4}-{\theta_{4}}^{4}-{\theta_{2}}^{4}}{\eta^{4}}\right)\!\left(\frac{is}{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\bra{D(p)}e^{-s(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{12})}\ket{D(p)}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(6.231)

where we have set
t=\frac{\pi}{s}
and used the modular properties for the
\theta
and
\eta
functions ((
6.140a
), (
6.141a
)). This quantity is identically vanishing which means that the open string states stretched between the two identical
D
-branes have a perfectly balanced boson-fermion degeneracy. However in the
s
variable this has another complementary interpretation. We recall indeed from the bosonic string that the large
s
behaviour of the integrand is sensible to the massless closed string states that are exchanged between the two
D
-branes.
A power series expansion in
s\to\infty
can isolate the contribution from the exchange of massless states:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\eta\!\left(\frac{is}{\pi}\right)}\right)^{8}\,\left(\frac{[{\theta_{3}}^{4}-{\theta_{4}}^{4}]_{\rm NS\,NS}-[{\theta_{2}}^{4}]_{\rm R\,R}}{\eta^{4}}\right)\!\left(\frac{is}{\pi}\right)\underset{s\to\infty}{\sim}(16_{\rm NS\,NS}-16_{\rm R\,R})+(0)e^{-s}+\cdots.

(6.232)

Considering only the massless exchange, in complete analogy with the bosonic string, we find again the Green function
G(\Delta y)\sim 1/|\Delta y|^{d_{\perp}-2}
:

\Big(\underbrace{\overbrace{16}^{\text{NS}}-\overbrace{16}^{\text{R}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\,0\\
\text{matching of bosonic}\\
\text{and fermionic d.o.f.}\end{subarray}}\Big)G(\Delta y)=0\,,

(6.233)

Therefore two charged
D(p)
-branes at a distance
\Delta y
are attracted by a force coming from the
\text{NS}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{NS}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector (due to the dilaton and the graviton) which is balanced by an equal and opposite force coming from the
\text{R}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector:

\underbrace{\text{tension}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{produces gravitational attraction}\\
\text{between the two $D(p)$-branes}\end{subarray}}=\underbrace{\text{R-R charge}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{produces electric repulsion}\\
\text{between the two $D(p)$-branes}\end{subarray}}\,.

(6.234)

Hence the two
D(p)
-branes do not interact. In supersimmetry, this is called
BPS condition
.

6.4.2.3
\mathcal{N}=4
Super Yang-Mills

Let us now consider a stack of
N

D(3)
-branes in type IIB theory, where they are R-R charged (and have a field-strength
F^{(5)}
). Given this setting, we have 4 Neumann directions
\mu=0,1,2,3
and 6 Dirichlet directions
i=4,\dots,10
: therefore these BC break
SO(1,9)
into
SO(1,3)\otimes SO(6)
.

In order to throw away the tachyon, the GSO projection for open strings will be the following:

\displaystyle\text{Neumann }X^{\mu},\psi^{\mu}

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \begin{cases}\text{NS})\quad\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{NS}}\\
\text{R})\quad\,\,\,\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{R}}\end{cases}\,;

\displaystyle\text{Dirichlet }X^{i},\psi^{i}

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \begin{cases}\text{NS})\quad\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{NS}}\\
\text{R})\quad\,\,\,\text{GSO}^{+}_{\text{R}}\end{cases}\,.

The first state in the NS sector is

A_{\mu}(P)\psi_{\frac{1}{2}}^{\mu}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}+\lambda_{i}(P)\psi_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\mu}\ket{0,P}_{\text{NS}}\,,

(6.235)

where
A
and
\lambda
are
N\times N
matrices, since we have a stack of
N

D(3)
-branes.
The physical constraints are

G_{\frac{1}{2}}\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ P\cdot A=0\,,\qquad L_{0}\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ P^{2}=0\,.

(6.236)

Therefore we find
N^{2}
gluons (in the adjoint representation of
U(N)
) and
6N^{2}
transverse scalars (in the adjoint representation of
U(N)
).

On the other hand, the first state in the R sector in
d=10
, without considering the GSO projection, is

\chi_{\alpha}(P)\ket{\alpha,P}_{\text{R}}+\chi_{\dot{\alpha}}(P)\ket{\dot{\alpha},P}_{\text{R}}\,,

(6.237)

where
\alpha\in 16_{C}
and
\dot{\alpha}\in 16_{S}
. When we then perform the GSO projection, the
16_{S}
is removed and we are left with a single chiral spinor in
d=10
. In order to understand how
\alpha
decomposes in
SO(1,9)
broken into
SO(1,3)\otimes SO(6)
, we write

\alpha=\left(\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2}\right)\,,

(6.238)

and since we are dealing with a left spinor there is an even number of
-1/2
. Therefore we can decompose
\alpha
as

\alpha=\bigg(\underbrace{\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2}}_{a,\,\dot{a}},\,\underbrace{\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2}}_{I,\,\bar{I}}\bigg)\,,

(6.239)

which gives rise to the two following cases:

•

\alpha=\left(a,I\right)
, where
a
is a Weyl spinor of
SO(1,3)
with positive chirality and
I
is a Weyl spinor of
SO(6)
with positive chirality;

•

\alpha=\left(\dot{a},\bar{I}\right)
, where
\dot{a}
is a Weyl spinor of
SO(1,3)
with negative chirality and
\bar{I}
is a Weyl spinor of
SO(6)
with negative chirality.

The two possible representations
I=4_{C}
and
\bar{I}=4_{S}
of
SO(6)
can be seen as the fundamental
4
and the anti-fundamental
\bar{4}
representations of
SU(4)
, since the Dynkin diagram of their algebras are topologically equivalent (see fig.
6.4
).

((a))

((b))

Figure 6.4:
The
SO(6)
and the
SU(4)
algebra Dynkin diagram are topologically equivalent.

Therefore
\chi_{\alpha}
in
d=10
can be written as
(\chi_{\alpha}^{I},\,\chi_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\bar{I}})
, which are massless fermions in the fundamental and the anti-fundamental representations of
SU(4)
. They are called
gluinos
(or
gauginos
).

What we found is that in the case of
N

D(3)
-branes in type IIB there is an effective theory for the fields

\displaystyle A_{\mu}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{vectors (spin $1$)}\,,

\displaystyle\chi_{\alpha}^{I},\,\chi_{\dot{\alpha}}^{\bar{I}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{gauginos (spin $1/2$)}\,,

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{scalars (spin $0$)}\,,

which form the vector multiplet for the
\mathcal{N}=4
super Yang-Mills theory. The related action is

\displaystyle S^{\text{\tiny SYM}}_{\mathcal{N}=4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{g_{s}}\int d^{4}x\,\text{Tr}\bigg\{F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+D_{\mu}\lambda^{i}D^{\mu}\lambda_{i}+\left[\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}\right]\left[\lambda^{i},\lambda^{j}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\chi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{\bar{I}}\not{D}\chi^{I}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\chi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{I}\left[\chi^{\bar{I}},\lambda_{i}\right]\gamma_{i}^{I,\bar{I}}\bigg\}+\left(\alpha^{\prime}\text{ corrections}\right)\,,

(6.240)

where the term
\left[\lambda_{i},\lambda_{j}\right]\left[\lambda^{i},\lambda^{j}\right]
shows a quartic potential structure for the
\lambda
field, while the
\gamma
coefficients are the gamma-matrices of
SO(6)
. Every term of this action can be obtained by computing open strings amplitudes in the
N

D(3)
-branes system, as we sketched in the bosonic string example.

Since there is no dimensional parameter inside
S^{\text{\tiny SYM}}_{\mathcal{N}=4}
, there is a conformal invariance at the classical level.
Moreover, the
\beta
function of this theory vanishes, which means that it also has a superconformal invariance at the quantum level.

6.4.2.4
The
D(p)-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
system

Let us consider a
D(p)-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
branes system, whose total R-R charge is zero. This time the closed string exchange amplitude (or equivalently the one-loop bubble of the stretched string) will be

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\bra{\overline{D}(p)}e^{-s(L_{0}+\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{12})}\ket{D(p)}=

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\left(\frac{\pi}{s}\right)^{\frac{d_{\perp}}{2}}\,e^{-\frac{(\Delta y)^{2}}{2\alpha^{\prime}s}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\eta\!\left(\frac{is}{\pi}\right)}\right)^{8}\,\left(\frac{{\theta_{3}}^{4}-{\theta_{4}}^{4}+{\theta_{2}}^{4}}{\eta^{4}}\right)\!\left(\frac{is}{\pi}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{(2t)^{2}}\,\left(\frac{1}{2t}\right)^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\,e^{-\frac{t(\Delta y)^{2}}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\eta(it)}\right)^{8}\,\left(\frac{{\theta_{3}}^{4}+{\theta_{4}}^{4}-{\theta_{2}}^{4}}{\eta^{4}}\right)\!(it)\,.

(6.241)

The plus sign in front of
\theta_{2}
is there because this is the contribution from the exchange of RR fields. Since the the two RR charges are opposite now this contribution is opposite from the
D-D
case. After passing to the open string variable
t=\pi/s
and using the modular transformation we have that under
t\to 1/t

\theta_{2}/\eta\leftrightarrow\theta_{4}/\eta.

Therefore this time we find that the GSO projection for the open string is
opposite
from the one we used in the DD system.
In total, from the Cardy condition, this time we find a different GSO projection in the off-diagonal sector, describing open strings stretching from the brane and the anti-brane

\begin{pmatrix}\phi_{11}&\phi_{12}\\
\phi_{21}&\phi_{22}\end{pmatrix}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \begin{pmatrix}\text{GSO}^{+}(+)&\text{GSO}^{+}(-)\\
\text{GSO}^{-}(-)&\text{GSO}^{-}(+)\end{pmatrix}\,.

(6.242)

Therefore we have a complex open string tachyon in the off-diagonal sector.

From a closed string point of view, we have already discussed that we have

|{D(p)-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu}\rangle=2T_{p}\ket{\text{NS}-\overbar{\text{NS}}}\,,

(6.243)

and we see that the total R-R charge is zero. On the other hand, from an open string point of view we see a tachyon, hence an unstable vacuum.
Therefore we have two complementary informations:

\text{vanishing R-R charge}\,\ \Longleftrightarrow\,\ \text{unstable vacuum}\,.

This essentially means that, since the
D(p)-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
is not charged, the system is unstable and it will decay. Such a process can be codified by the dynamics of the open string tachyon
t
. It can be shown using string field theory that its effective potential
V(t,\bar{t})
(where
t\in\mathbb{C}
, since the Chan-Paton is
\begin{pmatrix}0&t\\
\bar{t}&0\end{pmatrix}
) has a Mexican-hat shape (see fig.
6.5
).

Figure 6.5:
The tachyon ‘mexican hat’ potential for a
D
-
\bar{D}
system. The tip of the potential, located in
t=0
, is the unstable vacuum. The red circle
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(t)}
is the manifold of the minima, where
V(t)=0
. We hence have a 1-parameter degeneration of the stable vacuum.

Let us now write the tachyon as
t=t(x^{\mu})
(where
x^{\mu}
are the worldvolume coordinates of the
D(p)-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
system, i.e.
\mu=0,1,\dots,p
). Consider the tachyon equation of motion derived from the effective potential

\square t-V^{\prime}(t)=0\,.

(6.244)

This equation has several interesting solutions. The simplest is

t=0,

(6.245)

which corresponds to the initial unstable system.
Then we have

t=t_{*}e^{i\theta},

(6.246)

where
V^{\prime}(t_{*})=0
, minimum of the potential.
This is a one-parameter family of solutions which describe a constant field configuration. This is a stable vacuum where the
D
-
\bar{D}
system has dissolved into pure closed string radiation, leaving empty space-time, just like an electron and a positron decay by emitting photons. This is a curious vacuum state for the effective open string description, since there are no more
D
-branes in this vacuum, yet the open string field theory exists. This configuration is called
Tachyon Vacuum
. A detailed analysis using string field theory shows that the energy difference
V(0)-V(t_{*}e^{i\theta})
precisely accounts for the total mass of the initial unstable
D-\bar{D}
system.

It is also interesting to mention the so-called
vortex solution
depending only on two space-like coordinates
x^{1},x^{2}\in\left\{x^{0},x^{1},\dots,x^{p}\right\}
, that is to consider
t(x^{1},x^{2})=t(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern . The gauge fields on the D-branes also play a crucial role, but we will not consider them in our sketchy argument

Figure 6.6:
On the left, we have the
(x_{1},x_{2})
plane where the vortex solution
t(x^{1},x^{2})
lives: the perturbative vacuum is in the origin, while the point at infinity is the non-perturbative vacuum (since
t
acquires vev). On the right, we have the
t
plane: the red circle is the minima manifold where
V(t)=0
, namely the 1-parameter degeneration of the stable vacuum, while the origin of the two axis corresponds to the unstable minimum (see figure
6.5
).

These vortex solutions are identified by a topological charge, called
winding number
. Its meaning can be grasped as follows.
Since the vortex solution has to be normalizable (i.e. the vortex has a finite energy),
t(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern has to be in the minimum of the potential. If

is the radius of the minima manifold (see fig.

), and if we write

, what we just said reads as

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})=\lim_{|x|\to\infty}t\left(|x|e^{i\theta_{x}}\right)=t_{*}e^{i\theta_{t}}\,,

(6.247)

where
\theta_{x}
is related to the manifold
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(x)}
of the configurations for
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}\to\infty
in the
(x^{1},x^{2})
plane, while
\theta_{t}
is linked to the manifold
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(t)}
of the stable minima in the
t
plane.

Thus the generic vortex solution is associated to a map

m:\ \mathcal{S}_{1}^{(x)}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \mathcal{S}_{1}^{(t)}\,,

(6.248)

that is classified by the winding number
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, which counts how many times
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(x)}
winds around
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{(t)}
. If the winding is performed clockwise, the winding number is positive, and negative otherwise.
This is a topological charge, since it cannot be changed by continuous deformations of the map
m
.

As a consequence of all of this, the vortex solution is labelled by the winding number:
t_{n}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})
. The space profile will describe a concentration of mass-energy near
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}\sim 0
while for large

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle x\hfil

the solution will be relaxed at the tachyon vacuum where there are no
D
-branes. This solution is therefore describing a charged object which has a world-volume with two dimensions less than the original
D-\bar{D}
system.

Ashoke Sen conjectured that the vortex solution
t_{n}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})
represents
n
coincident
D(p-2)
-branes located at
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}=0
. Indeed the
D(p)-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
system has no R-R charge for
F^{(p+2)}
, whereas a stack of
n

D(p-2)
-branes has a R-R charge of
n
given by
F^{(p)}
and, according to the conjecture, it is exactly the winding number of the tachyonic vortex. A quantitative prediction of this conjecture is that the vortex energy per volume unit is equal to the tension of
n

D(p-2)
-branes. This speculation has been numerically proven in string field theory, but a full analytic solution is still lacking.

6.5
Bosonization and Picture Charge (More advanced)

6.5.1
Bosonization

6.5.1.1
Spin field

Let’s consider type II superstring theory.
As we said before, we define the Ramond vacuum as a spin field acting on the
SL(2,\mathbb{C})
-invariant vacuum:

\displaystyle\ket{\alpha}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=S_{\alpha}(0)\ket{0}\,,

(6.249a)

\displaystyle\ket{\dot{\alpha}}_{\text{R}}

\displaystyle=S_{\dot{\alpha}}(0)\ket{0}\,.

(6.249b)

Let us now recall the contractions for the fermions
\psi^{\pm}_{I}(z)
we defined in (
A.4
):

\displaystyle\underbracket{\psi^{+}_{I}(z_{1})\psi^{-}_{J}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{IJ}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\,,

(6.250)

\displaystyle\underbracket{\psi^{\pm}_{I}(z_{1})\psi^{\pm}_{J}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6.251)

We may now consider a logarithmic free chiral boson
H_{I}(z)
, such that

\underbracket{H_{I}(z_{1})H_{J}(z_{2})}=-\delta_{IJ}\log(z_1-z_2)\,,

(6.252)

with a stress-energy tensor given by

T_{H}(z)=\frac{1}{2}:\mathrel{\partial H\cdot\partial H}:(z)\,.

(6.253)

We can employ it to build the plane waves
e^{\pm iH_{I}}(z)
, which have the following contractions:

\displaystyle\underbracket{e^{+iH_{I}}(z_{1})e^{-iH_{J}}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta_{IJ}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\,,

(6.254a)

\displaystyle\underbracket{e^{\pm iH_{I}}(z_{1})e^{\pm iH_{J}}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=0\,.

(6.254b)

We notice that the contractions of these plane waves are the same as the contractions of the fermionic field
\psi^{\pm}_{I}(z)
.
We can thus perform a field redefinition called
bosonization
:

\psi^{\pm}_{I}(z)=e^{\pm iH_{I}}(z)\,.

(6.255)

This name comes from the fact that we wrote a fermion as a plane wave of a boson.

We can then use this technique to bosonize the spin field as follows:

S_{\alpha}(z)=e^{i\sum_{I}s_{I}H_{I}}(z)\,,

(6.256)

where
s_{I}
is the so-called spinor weight (recall that we are in
d=10
):

s_{I}=\left\{\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2},\,\pm\frac{1}{2}\right\}\,.

(6.257)

According to the definition (
A.15
), the spinor weight of the left spin field
S_{\alpha}
will have an even number of
-1/2
, whereas the spinor weight of the right spin field
S_{\dot{\alpha}}
will have an odd number of
-1/2
.

In
d=10
the spin field is a product of 5 elementary plane-waves
e^{\pm\frac{i}{2}H_{I}}
, one for each independent 2-dimensional plane. The weight of
e^{\pm\frac{i}{2}H_{I}}
can be easily computed to be
1/8
. This means that

h\left(S_{\alpha}\right)=h\left(S_{\dot{\alpha}}\right)=\frac{5}{8}\,.

(6.258)

This is the reason why in the definition (
6.86
) of the Ramond matter+ghost vacuum we took a ghost part
c\mathcal{V}_{3/8}^{(\beta,\gamma)}
having overall ghost weight
-5/8
: this indeed combines with the matter weight of the spin field, leaving a weight zero physical state. Now we are going to describe the
\beta\gamma
operator
\mathcal{V}_{3/8}^{(\beta,\gamma)}
.

6.5.1.2
Ghost
(\beta,\gamma)
system

The
\beta
and
\gamma
ghosts are (spinorial) bosons and to bosonize them (since they are already bosonic) we need two decoupled CFTs:

(\eta,\xi)\oplus\varphi\,,

(6.259)

where
\eta
and
\xi
are fermions, while
\varphi
is a boson. With these ingredients we can redefine
\beta
and
\gamma
as

\displaystyle\beta(z)

\displaystyle=e^{-\varphi}\partial\xi(z)\,,

(6.260a)

\displaystyle\gamma(z)

\displaystyle=\eta e^{\varphi}(z)\,.

(6.260b)

The
(\eta,\xi)
system (which is kind of a
(b,c)
system) is such that

h(\eta)=1\,,\qquad h(\xi)=0\,,

(6.261)

and

\underbracket{\xi(z_{1})\eta(z_{2})}=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\,.

(6.262)

The stress-energy tensor of this
(\eta,\xi)
-CFT is

T^{(\eta,\xi)}(z)=\,\,:\mathrel{(\partial\eta)\xi}:(z)-\partial(:\mathrel{\eta\xi}:)(z)\,,

(6.263)

and its central charge turns out to be
c=-2
. Moreover, the normalization is

\bra{0}\xi_{0}\ket{0}=1\,.

(6.264)

On the other hand,
\varphi
is a chiral twisted free boson with logarithmic OPE.

\underbracket{\varphi(z_{1})\varphi(z_{2})}=-\log(z_1-z_2)\,.

(6.265)

The stress-energy tensor of this
\varphi
-CFT is

T^{\varphi}(z)=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial\varphi)(\partial\varphi)-\partial^{2}\varphi\,,

(6.266)

where
\partial^{2}\varphi=\partial[\partial\varphi]
is the twist term (here is where the name “twisted boson” comes from).
The central charge turns out to be
c=+13
.

Therefore the overall
\{(\eta,\xi)\oplus\varphi\}
-CFT has a
c=-2+13=+11
central charge, which is the same as the
(\beta,\gamma)
system.

We can evaluate

h(e^{q\varphi})=-\frac{q}{2}(q+2)\,,

(6.267)

which means that
h=0
if and only if

e^{q\varphi}=\begin{cases}\text{1}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ q=0\\
e^{-2\varphi}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ q=-2\end{cases}\,,

(6.268)

with

\langle\text{1}\rangle=0\,,\qquad\langle e^{-2\varphi}\rangle=1\neq 0\,.

(6.269)

This fact is called
anomalous momentum conservation
, and can be explained as follows. If we define the charge

Q_{\varphi}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}(-\partial\varphi)=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j_{\varphi}(z)\,,

(6.270)

we have that

\left[Q_{\varphi},e^{q\varphi}\right]=qe^{q\varphi}

(6.271)

and

T_{\varphi}(z_{1})j_{\varphi}(z_{2})=-\frac{2}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{3}}+\frac{j_{\varphi}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+\frac{\partial j_{\varphi}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}+\text{(regular terms)}\,,

(6.272)

which means that the current
j_{\varphi}
is not a primary. This has the following effect on BPZ conjugation:

Q_{\varphi}\ket{0}=0\,\ \xrightarrow{\,\,\,\mathcal{I}(z)=-\frac{1}{z}\,\,\,}\,\ \bra{0}\left(Q_{\varphi}+2\right)=0\,,

(6.273)

which implies that

\langle\prod_{i}e^{q_{i}\varphi}(z_{1})\rangle\neq 0\,\ \Longleftrightarrow\,\ \sum_{i}q_{i}=-2\,.

(6.274)

Thanks to this construction, we can finally write the full expression of the NS and R vacua, namely

\ket{0}_{\text{NS}}=e^{-\varphi}(0)\ket{0}\,,\qquad|\overset{(\,\cdot\,)}{\alpha}\,\rangle_{\text{R}}=S_{\overset{(\,\cdot\,)}{\alpha}}e^{-\frac{\varphi}{2}}(0)\ket{0}\,,

(6.275)

which are essentially what we wrote in (
6.84
) and (
6.86
). The NS vacuum has weight 1/2 while the R vacuum has weight 1. After dressing them with a
c
-ghost we find that the vacua are respectively
-1/2=-a_{NS}
and
0=-a_{R}
, which precisely match the lightcone zero point energies, as they should.

6.5.2
Picture charge and large Hilbert space

Combining the two CFTs arising from the bosonization of the
(\beta,\gamma)
system, we can define the current

j_{p}(z)=\,\,:\mathrel{\xi\eta}:(z)-\partial\varphi(z)=\,\,:\mathrel{\xi\eta}:(z)+j_{\varphi}(z)\,,

(6.276)

the related conserved charge is called
picture charge

p
.
Its values for the fields we are dealing with are written in table
6.5

Field

p

\beta

0

\gamma

0

\eta

-1

\xi

1

e^{-q\varphi}

-q

\ket{0}_{\text{NS}}

-1

\ket{\alpha}_{\text{R}}

-\frac{1}{2}

Table 6.5:
The value of the picture charge
p
for the main fields we are interested in.

Now we have three anomalous currents which have to be conserved in correlators. The first is the
(b,c)
ghost current which fixes the 3 c zero modes (
4.180
). Then we have anomalous momentum conservation (
6.269
) and finally we have the
\xi,\eta
current which fixes a
\xi
. In total the non-vanishing 1-point function is

\langle\xi e^{-2\varphi}\frac{1}{2}(\partial^{2}c)(\partial c)c(z)\rangle_{\text{\tiny LHS}}^{\text{\tiny chiral}}=1\,.

(6.277)

It has
p=-1
and
gh=3
.
The label LHS says that the correlator is computed in the so-called
large Hilbert space
. In order to understand what does this mean, we can go back to the field redefinitions (
6.260a
) and (
6.260b
) of
\beta
and
\gamma
. We notice that they do not use the full Hilbert space of the
\{(\eta,\xi)\oplus\varphi\}
-CFT, because the dependence on
\xi
is all inside
\partial\xi
(in
\beta
), where the zero-mode
\xi_{0}
is excluded. Indeed, since
\xi
is a weight-zero primary, we can write

\xi(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi_{n}z^{-n}=\xi_{0}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\xi_{n}z^{-n}

(6.278)

and realize that inside
\partial\xi
the zero-mode
\xi_{0}
is not present.

We then have to distinguish between

•

the small Hilbert space (SHS) where the
(\beta,\gamma)
system lives and where the zero-mode
\xi_{0}
is not present;

•

the large Hilbert space (LHS) where the whole
\{(\eta,\xi)\oplus\varphi\}
system lives and where the zero-mode
\xi_{0}
is present.

The LHS is twice as big as the SHS because
\forall\ket{\text{state}}\in\text{SHS}
the LHS contains both
\ket{\text{state}}
and
\xi_{0}\ket{\text{state}}
.

String amplitudes and observables will live inside the SHS, since they are build with the
(\beta,\gamma)
system. Hence, starting from the LHS, it is useful to know how to come back to the physical SHS, and this is simply realized as follows:

\langle\dots\rangle_{\text{\tiny SHS}}=\langle\xi_{0}(\dots)\rangle_{\text{\tiny LHS}}\,.

(6.279)

Moreover, in the SHS the correlator (
6.277
) reads as

\langle e^{-2\varphi}\frac{1}{2}(\partial^{2}c)(\partial c)c(z)\rangle_{\text{\tiny SHS}}^{\text{\tiny chiral}}=1\,.

(6.280)

It has
p=-2
and
gh=3
.

Let us now study the structure of the LHS a bit more in detail.
We can start by considering the zero-modes

\xi_{0}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{z}\xi(z)\,,\qquad\eta_{0}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\eta(z)\,,

(6.281)

which obey

\left[\eta_{0},\xi_{0}\right]=1\,.

(6.282)

Since in the SHS there is no dependence on
\xi_{0}
, we have that a generic vector in SHS will be annihilated by

\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi_{0}}=\eta_{0}=0\,.

(6.283)

Hence we can write

\text{SHS}=\mathcal{H}_{S}=\ker(\eta_{0})\,.

(6.284)

If we now also recall the BRST charge

Q_{B}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j_{\text{B}}(z)\,,

(6.285)

we realize that in the LHS there are two nilpotent operators

Q_{B}^{2}=0\,,\qquad\eta_{0}^{2}=0\,,

(6.286)

which (anti-)commute with each other:

[Q_{B},\eta_{0}]=0\,.

(6.287)

Moreover, both
Q_{B}
and
\eta_{0}
have empty cohomology
9
9

9

Given a nilpotent odd operator
d
, if there exists another odd operator
h
such that
[d,h]=1
then, for every
d
-closed state
\psi
, (
d\psi=0
) we have that
\psi=d(h(\psi))
, so every closed state is exact and
d
has no cohomology.
:

\left[\eta_{0},\xi(z)\right]=1\,,\qquad\left[Q_{B},-c\xi\partial\xi e^{-2\varphi}(z)\right]=1\,.

(6.288)

The operator
\xi(z)
is called
contracting-homotopy operator
for
\eta
, while
-c\xi\partial\xi e^{-2\varphi}(z)
is the contracting-homotopy operator for
Q_{B}
. Notice in particular that the contracting homotopy operator for
Q_{B}
explicitly contains
\xi
, which only exists in the LHS. We thus learned that

•

in the LHS neither
Q_{B}
nor
\eta_{0}
have cohomology since they both have a contracting-homotopy operator;

•

in the SHS
\eta_{0}=0
(since we are inside its kernel) and
\xi_{0}
is not present:
Q_{B}
has no contracting-homotopy operator and has a non-empty cohomology.

The two contracting homotopy operators we have found allow to construct two interesting new operators (by interchanging the role of
Q
and
\eta
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(z)

\displaystyle=\left[Q_{B},\xi(z)\right]\,,

(6.289a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(z)

\displaystyle=\left[\eta_{0},-c\xi\partial\xi e^{-2\varphi}(z)\right]=c\partial\xi e^{-2\varphi}(z)\,.

(6.289b)

It is immediate to see that

\begin{cases}\left[Q_{B},\mathcal{X}(z)\right]=0\\
\left[\eta_{0},\mathcal{X}(z)\right]=0\end{cases}\,,\qquad\begin{cases}\left[Q_{B},\mathcal{Y}(z)\right]=0\\
\left[\eta_{0},\mathcal{Y}(z)\right]=0\end{cases}\,,

(6.290)

and so
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\text{SHS}
.

Moreover

•

\mathcal{X}
has picture
p=+1
and hence is called
picture raising operator
;

•

\mathcal{Y}
has picture
p=-1
and hence is called
picture lowering operator
.

These two operators are one the inverse of the other in the OPE sense:

\lim_{z\to 0}\mathcal{X}(z)\mathcal{Y}(0)=1\,.

(6.291)

We now define the zero-mode of
\mathcal{X}
as

\mathcal{X}_{0}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}\frac{1}{z}\mathcal{X}\,.

(6.292)

In this way, given a physical state
\phi_{p}
of picture
p
(which, being physical, satisfies
Q_{B}\phi_{p}=0
), we can raise its picture with
\mathcal{X}_{0}
and the resulting state will still be physical:

\mathcal{X}_{0}\phi_{p}=\phi_{p+1}\,,\qquad Q_{B}\phi_{p+1}=0\,.

(6.293)

This means that, for a given physical field, we can build an infinite chain of fields that have a different picture charge and yet are still physical:

\dots\,\ \overset{\mathcal{X}}{\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\rightleftarrows}}\,\ \phi_{p-1}\,\ \overset{\mathcal{X}}{\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\rightleftarrows}}\,\ \phi_{p}\,\ \overset{\mathcal{X}}{\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\rightleftarrows}}\,\ \phi_{p+1}\,\ \overset{\mathcal{X}}{\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\rightleftarrows}}\,\ \dots\,.

(6.294)

We therefore learn that in superstring theory every physical state is infinitely degenerate because we can take it in any picture we want.
While evaluating superstring (tree-level) amplitudes we will have to end up with a total picture of
p=-2
(recall eq. (
6.280
)), and this will be the criterion to be employed in order to choose at which picture we want to take our fields.

On top of this, we have the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
degeneracy due to the two possible vertex operators we can use, namely integrated or non-integrated.

6.5.3
GSO projection and bosonization

We now want to use what we learned about bosonization to better understand the GSO projection.

First of all, it is useful to recall the field redefinitions of the fermion
\psi
and of the
\beta
and
\gamma
ghosts:

\displaystyle\psi^{\pm}_{I}(z)

\displaystyle=e^{\pm iH_{I}}(z)\,,

(6.295a)

\displaystyle\beta(z)

\displaystyle=e^{-\varphi}\partial\xi(z)\,,

(6.295b)

\displaystyle\gamma(z)

\displaystyle=\eta e^{\varphi}(z)\,.

(6.295c)

We can then write a generic state in the NS or in the R sector as a plane wave with generalized momenta
v_{I},\,s_{I}
(related to the matter
\psi
part, where
I=1,\dots,5
since we are in
d=10
) and
v_{6},\,s_{6}
(related to the ghost
(\beta,\gamma)
part):

\displaystyle\ket{\text{NS state}}

\displaystyle=e^{iv_{I}H_{I}+v_{6}\varphi}\,,\quad\text{with }v_{I},\,v_{6}\in\mathbb{Z}\,,

(6.296a)

\displaystyle\ket{\text{R state}}

\displaystyle=e^{is_{I}H_{I}+s_{6}\varphi}\,,\quad\text{with }s_{I},\,s_{6}\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}\,.

(6.296b)

Since, obviously,

\displaystyle\text{integer}+\text{integer}

\displaystyle=\text{integer}\,,

(6.297a)

\displaystyle\text{integer}+\text{half-integer}

\displaystyle=\text{half-integer}\,,

(6.297b)

\displaystyle\text{half-integer}+\text{half-integer}

\displaystyle=\text{integer}\,,

(6.297c)

we have the general OPEs

\displaystyle\ket{\text{NS state}}\cdot\ket{\text{NS state}}

\displaystyle\approx\ket{\text{NS state}}\,,

(6.298a)

\displaystyle\ket{\text{NS state}}\cdot\ket{\text{R state}}

\displaystyle\approx\ket{\text{R state}}\,,

(6.298b)

\displaystyle\ket{\text{R state}}\cdot\ket{\text{R state}}

\displaystyle\approx\ket{\text{NS state}}\,.

(6.298c)

Using GSO projection we can then split the NS sector and the R sector as follows:

NS

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \begin{cases}\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{+}\quad\text{where $\sum_{I}v_{I}+v_{6}$ is even}\\
\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{-}\quad\text{where $\sum_{I}v_{I}+v_{6}$ is odd}\end{cases}\,,

(6.299a)

R

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \begin{cases}\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{+}\quad\text{where $\sum_{I}s_{I}+s_{6}$ is even}\\
\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{-}\quad\text{where $\sum_{I}s_{I}+s_{6}$ is odd}\end{cases}\,.

(6.299b)

We immediately see that the
\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{+}
sector is closed under OPEs, which means that

\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{+}\cdot\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{+}\approx\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{+}\,.

(6.300)

On the other hand, in the R sector we can fix
s_{6}
(which is essentially the picture charge, related to the bosonized ghosts) and the difference between even
\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{+}
and odd
\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{-}
reduces to the chirality of the corresponding spacetime fermion
\ket{\{s_{I}\}}
.

The algebra of these generalized plane waves is

\underbracket{e^{ip_{I}H_{I}+p_{6}\varphi}(z_{1})e^{iq_{I}H_{I}+q_{6}\varphi}(z_{2})}=(z_{1}-z_{2})^{-p_{I}q_{I}+p_{6}q_{6}}e^{i(p_{I}+q_{I})H_{I}+(p_{6}+q_{6})\varphi}(z_{2})\,.

(6.301)

If we want to ask for locality in the chiral sector (namely the absence of branch cuts) we have to take

-p_{I}q_{I}+p_{6}q_{6}\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(6.302)

But we have that

\displaystyle\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{-}(z_{1})\cdot\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{\pm}(z_{2})

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{z_{1}-z_{2}}}\,,

(6.303a)

\displaystyle\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{-}(z_{1})\cdot\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{+}(z_{2})

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{z_{1}-z_{2}}}\,.

(6.303b)

Therefore the minimal consistent sectors where fermions are still in the spectrum (as we would like it to be, since this is the reason we introduced supersimmetry for) are the two following possibilities:

\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{+}\oplus\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{+}\,,\qquad\text{GSO}_{\text{NS}}^{+}\oplus\text{GSO}_{\text{R}}^{-}\,.

(6.304)

This is the truncation giving rise to Type II A-B and to spacetime SUSY.

6.5.4
D(p)
-branes and supersymmetry

Let us consider type IIA theory. Since we have two spin fields of opposite chirality, we can build the two following fermionic supercharges:

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle=S_{\alpha}e^{-\frac{\varphi}{2}}(z)\,,

(6.305a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\dot{\alpha}}(z)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muS\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\dot{\alpha}}e^{-\frac{\bar{\varphi}}{2}}(\bar{z})\,.

(6.305b)

Using the contractions

\displaystyle\underbracket{S_{\alpha}(z_{1})S_{\beta}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{(\Gamma_{\mu})_{\alpha\beta}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{\frac{3}{4}}}\psi^{\mu}(z_{2})\,,

(6.306a)

\displaystyle\underbracket{S_{\alpha}(z_{1})S_{\dot{\beta}}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{C_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{\frac{5}{4}}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Gamma_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}:\mathrel{\psi^{\mu}\psi^{\nu}}:(z_{2})\,,

(6.306b)

\displaystyle\underbracket{e^{-\frac{\varphi}{2}}(z_{1})e^{-\frac{\varphi}{2}}(z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{\frac{1}{4}}}e^{-\varphi}(z_{2})\,,

(6.306c)

we can evaluate

\underbracket{Q_{\alpha}(z_{1})Q_{\beta}(z_{2})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{(\Gamma_{\mu})_{\alpha\beta}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\psi^{\mu}e^{-\varphi}(z_{2})\,,

(6.307)

where the
\varphi
term comes from
e^{-\varphi/2}e^{-\varphi/2}=e^{-\varphi}
, and hence the total picture charge of the result is
-1/2-1/2=-1
. We would have expected something like
Q_{\alpha}(z_{1})Q_{\beta}(z_{2})\approx\Gamma_{\mu}j^{\mu}
, but instead of the momentum generator
j^{\mu}
we have found its representative at picture (-1), namely
\psi^{\mu}e^{-\varphi}
:

\mathcal{X}_{0}(\psi^{\mu}e^{-\varphi})=j^{\mu}\,.

(6.308)

We thus learn one important point of the RNS string:
Space time supersymmetry is only realized up to picture changing.

On the other hand, if we want to consider two supercharges of different chirality in the same holomorphic sector we get

\underbracket{Q_{\alpha}(z_{1})Q_{\dot{\beta}}(z_{2})}=\frac{C_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}e^{-\varphi}(z_{2})+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Gamma_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}:\mathrel{\psi^{\mu}\psi^{\nu}}:(z_{2})\,.

(6.309)

It has branch cuts, but we got rid of this bad situation through GSO projection, that forbids the combination
Q_{\alpha}(z_{1})Q_{\dot{\beta}}(z_{2})
.

In general, we have

•

type IIA, with
Q_{\alpha}
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\dot{\beta}}
;

•

type IIB, with
Q_{\alpha}
and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\beta}
.

If we now want to study
D(p)
-branes, we have to give a set of gluing conditions. For the bosonic currents we already know how to proceed, but the question now is whether it is possible or not to consistently glue the two chiral parts of the supercharge
Q
at the boundary.

If we choose type IIB theory, we can write a plausible gluing condition

Q_{\alpha}(z)=\mathscr{P}_{\alpha}^{\,\,\beta}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{\beta}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}

(6.310)

and try to find the gluing map
\mathscr{P}_{\alpha}^{\,\,\beta}
.

If we take
M=(\{\mu\},\{i\})
(where
\{\mu\}
are the Neumann indices and
\{i\}
are the Dirichlet indices), we can write the OPE
Q_{\alpha}(z_{1})Q_{\beta}(z_{2})
. The left-hand side is simply given by (
6.307
):

\underbracket{Q_{\alpha}(z_{1})Q_{\beta}(z_{2})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{(\Gamma_{M})_{\alpha\beta}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\psi^{M}e^{-\varphi}(z_{2})\,.

(6.311)

The right-hand side can be written using the gluing condition (
6.310
) and reads as

\underbracket{\mathscr{P}_{\alpha}^{\,\,\gamma}\bar{Q}_{\gamma}(\bar{z}_{1})\mathscr{P}_{\beta}^{\,\,\delta}\bar{Q}_{\delta}(\bar{z}_{2})}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{(\Gamma_{M})_{\alpha\beta}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\Omega_{\,\,\,\,\,\,N}^{(\psi)\,M}\bar{\psi}^{N}e^{-\bar{\varphi}}\,.

(6.312)

We wrote the usual gluing map (
6.225
) as a matrix:

\Omega_{N}^{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{\nu}^{\mu}&0\\
0&-\delta_{j}^{i}\end{pmatrix}\,\ \longleftrightarrow\,\ \Omega_{A}=\begin{cases}+1\,\text{ for $\text{A}=\text{Neumann}$}\\
-1\,\text{ for $\text{A}=\text{Dirichlet}$}\end{cases}\,.

(6.313)

In this notation, the usual bosonic and fermionic gluing conditions read as

\begin{cases}j^{M}(z)=\Omega_{N}^{M}\bar{j}^{N}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for $z=\bar{z}$}\\[5.0pt]
\psi^{M}(z)=\Omega_{N}^{M}\bar{\psi}^{N}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for $z=\bar{z}$}\end{cases}\,.

(6.314)

Therefore, if we want the left-hand side and the right-hand side to be compatible, we have to impose

\mathscr{P}_{\alpha}^{\,\,\gamma}\mathscr{P}_{\beta}^{\,\,\delta}(\Gamma_{N})_{\gamma\delta}=(\Gamma_{M})_{\alpha\beta}\Omega_{M}^{N}\,,

(6.315)

which can be written as

\mathscr{P}\Gamma_{N}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\mathscr{P}^{T}=\Omega_{N}^{M}\Gamma_{M}\mathcal{C}^{-1}\,,

(6.316)

where
T
denotes transposition.

It can then be shown that, for a given
D(p)
-brane, the relation (
6.316
) is solved by the gluing map

\mathscr{P}_{p}^{\pm}=\pm\prod_{i=p+2}^{9}(\Gamma_{i}\Gamma)\,,

(6.317)

being
\Gamma
the chirality matrix. Thus we find two solutions. However in type IIB (which is the setting we chose)
\mathscr{P}
must have indices of the same chirality, namely
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}_{\alpha}^{\,\,\beta}
. Since every
\Gamma
matrix changes the chirality, this implies that
p
must be odd. Hence we found a consistent gluing condition for the supercharge only for
D(2p-1)
-branes.

On the other hand, if we are in type IIA we have that the gluing condition is

Q_{\alpha}(z)=\mathscr{P}_{\alpha}^{\,\,\dot{\beta}}Q_{\dot{\beta}}(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}

(6.318)

and the gluing map has indices of opposite chirality (i.e.
\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}_{\alpha}^{\,\,\dot{\beta}}
). This implies that
p
inside
\mathscr{P}_{p}^{\pm}
is even and hence we have a consistent gluing condition for the supercharge only for
D(2p)
-branes.

What we just found teaches us that in type IIA or type IIB we have charged
D(p)
-branes preserving half of the spacetime SUSY.
The general gluing condition for the supercharge is

Q(z)=\mathscr{P}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu(\bar{z})\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}

(6.319)

with

\mathscr{P}_{p}^{\pm}=\pm\prod_{i=p+2}^{9}(\Gamma_{i}\Gamma)\,.

(6.320)

The
\pm
inside
\mathscr{P}_{p}^{\pm}
distinguishes between
D(p)
-branes and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-branes. This fact gives us a geometrical intuition which can be grasped us follows.

Let us recall that the difference between
D(p)
-branes and
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-branes is the sign of the R-R charge. Its value is related to the integration on the worldvolume
\mathcal{M}_{p+1}
of the
p
-dimensional
D(p)
-brane of the corresponding R-R potential
C^{(p+1)}
. But since
C^{(p+1)}
is a volume-form for the manifold
\mathcal{M}_{p+1}
, it defines an orientation:

\int_{\mathcal{M}_{p+1}}C^{(p+1)}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \begin{cases}>0\,\ \Rightarrow\,\ \text{positive orientation}\,\ \Rightarrow\,\ \text{$D(p)$-brane}\\[5.0pt]
<0\,\ \Rightarrow\,\ \text{negative orientation}\,\ \Rightarrow\,\ \text{$\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu$-brane}\end{cases}\,.

Hence a
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-brane is just a
D(p)
-brane with opposite orientation, and this is consistent with the
\pm
ambiguity inside
\mathscr{P}_{p}^{\pm}
.

We can then talk about preserved SUSY. We have that

•

a
D(p)
-brane preserves
Q+\mathscr{P}_{p}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
;

•

a
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-brane preserves
Q-\mathscr{P}_{p}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
.

They are both
1/2
BPS because they preserve only half of the supersimmetry.

If we have many
D(p)
-branes (or many
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
-branes), SUSY is still preserved. On the other hand, a
D(p)-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muD(p)\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
branes system breaks SUSY completely (in fact the system is unstable with a tachyon, with consequences discussed in
6.4.2.2
).

Finally, let us consider a
D(p)-D(q)
branes system. Here there is a certain number of Neumann-Dirichlet (ND) directions, along which a twisting in the boundary conditions occurs. From what we have just seen we have that

•

the
D(p)
-brane preserves
Q+\mathscr{P}_{p}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
;

•

the
D(q)
-brane preserves
Q+\mathscr{P}_{q}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
, which can be written as

\displaystyle Q+\mathscr{P}_{q}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu

\displaystyle=Q+\left(\mathscr{P}_{p}\mathscr{P}_{p}^{-1}\right)\mathscr{P}_{q}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=Q+\mathscr{P}_{p}\left(\prod_{i\in\text{ND}}\Gamma_{i}\right)\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\,.

(6.321)

Therefore the preserved supersymmetry must satisfy

\mathscr{P}_{p}^{-1}\mathscr{P}_{q}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu=\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu\,,

(6.322)

which requires that the matrix
\mathscr{P}_{p}^{-1}\mathscr{P}_{q}
has some eigenvalues equal to
1
.

Since
(\Gamma_{i}\Gamma_{i+1})^{2}=-1
, the matrix
\Gamma_{i}\Gamma_{i+1}
has eigenvalues
\pm i
, which is not good. We thus see that the only possibility to have the proper eigenvalues is to have a difference between the number of Neumann directions on the two branes that is
4
or a multiple of
4
, namely

p-q=4n\,,\quad\text{with }n\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(6.323)

These systems preserve
1/4
of SUSY and therefore are called
1/4
BPS.
Different combinations (for example the
D(p)-D(p+2)
system) break SUSY completely.

6.6
Effective Superstring Theories

There are 5 superstring theories in 10 dimensions, having space-time supersymmetry. We only studied the Type II A and Type IIB. There are however 3 more perturbative string theories that can be constructed. The list of the 5 superstring is as follows

•

Type IIA:

–

closed and open strings (
D
-branes) in
d=10
;

–

32
supercharges;

–

2
real Weyl spinors (left
+
right) in
d=10
.

•

Type IIB:

–

closed and open strings (
D
-branes) in
d=10
;

–

32
supercharges;

–

2
real Weyl spinors (left
+
left) in
d=10
.

•

Type I:

–

Unoriented closed and open strings in
d=10
;

–

16
supercharges;

–

32 space-filling D9-branes whose RR charge is annihilated by an orientifold making the string unoriented.

•

Heterotic
SO(32)
:

–

closed strings in
d=10
.

–

16
supercharges;

–

no D-branes

–

SO(32)
gauge symmetry realized through a self-dual compactification

•

Heterotic
E_{8}\times E_{8}
:

–

closed strings in
d=10
.

–

16
supercharges;

–

no D-branes

–

E_{8}\times E_{8}
gauge symmetry realized through a self-dual compactification

We can take a low energy limit of each one of these superstring theories. This will give rise to an effective theory (
+\alpha^{\prime}
corrections).

•

Type IIA
\xrightarrow{\,\,\text{low energy}\,\,}
Type IIA SUGRA:

–

16_{S}+16_{C}
SUSY;

–

effective action given by eq. (
6.167
);

–

related to Type IIB SUGRA by
T
-duality.

•

Type IIB
\xrightarrow{\,\,\text{low energy}\,\,}
Type IIB SUGRA:

–

16_{C}+16_{C}
SUSY;

–

effective action given by eq. (
6.168
);

–

related to Type IIA SUGRA by
T
-duality.

•

Type I
\xrightarrow{\,\,\text{low energy}\,\,}
Type I SUGRA:

–

16_{C}
SUSY;

–

coupled to
d=10
super Yang-Mills with
SO(32)
gauge group.

•

Heterotic
SO(32)

\xrightarrow{\,\,\text{low energy}\,\,}
Type I SUGRA:

–

16_{C}
SUSY;

–

coupled to
d=10
super Yang-Mills with
SO(32)
gauge group.

•

Heterotic
E_{8}\times E_{8}

\xrightarrow{\,\,\text{low energy}\,\,}
Type I SUGRA:

–

16_{C}
SUSY;

–

coupled to
d=10
super Yang-Mills with
E_{8}\times E_{8}
gauge group.

Moreover for type I supergravities we have that

g_{s}^{\text{heterotic}}=\frac{1}{g_{s}^{\text{type I}}}\,,

(6.324)

which means that the heterotic weak coupling is the same as the type I strong coupling. This is a duality between a perturbative regime and a non-perturbative regime. As we will see later on, this is called
S
-duality. This relation between the heterotic and the type I superstring will be part of the M-theory picture (see fig.
7.1
).

Chapter 7
Introduction to String Dualities

7.1
Circle compactification and
T
-Duality

7.1.1
Compactification on a circle

When we perform a
compactification
on a circle, it means that we shift from a
\mathbb{R}^{1,d}
spacetime to a
\mathcal{M}^{1,d}
spacetime defined as

\mathcal{M}^{1,d}=\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}\times\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}\,,

(7.1)

where
\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}
is a
circle of radius
R
.

If we call
y
the coordinate on the circle (and
x^{\mu}
the coordinates on
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
, with
\mu=0,1,\dots,d-1
), we have that the periodicity on
\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}
turns into the following condition:

y\sim y+2\pi R\,.

(7.2)

7.1.2
Compactification of a scalar

Let us consider, on the compactified spacetime
\mathcal{M}^{1,d}
, a scalar field
\phi(x^{\mu},y)
. If we choose it to be massless, it will satisfy the massless Klein-Gordon equation

\square\phi(x^{\mu},y)=0\,,

(7.3)

with
\square=\square_{x}+\partial_{y}^{2}
. We can Fourier expand the field in harmonics on the circle as

\phi(x^{\mu},y)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\phi_{n}(x^{\mu})e^{in\frac{y}{R}}\,,

(7.4)

and the reality of the field
\phi(x^{\mu},y)
turns into the condition

\phi^{*}_{-n}(x^{\mu})=\phi_{n}(x^{\mu})\,.

(7.5)

The EOMs are then

\square\phi(x^{\mu},y)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left(\square_{x}-\frac{n^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)\phi_{n}(x^{\mu})e^{in\frac{y}{R}}=0\,.

(7.6)

The
\phi_{n}(x^{\mu})
fields can be seen as
\infty
scalar fields in
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
whose EOMs are:

\left(\square_{x}-\frac{n^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)\phi_{n}(x^{\mu})=0\,.

(7.7)

Therefore they have a mass:

m_{n}^{2}=\frac{n^{2}}{R^{2}}\,.

(7.8)

We thus found that we can consider a massless scalar field in the compactified space
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}\times\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}
or, equivalently, an
infinite tower
of massive scalar fields (of increasing mass) living in
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
, which are called
Kaluza-Klein fields
.

7.1.3
Compactification of a gauge field
