The story becomes more interesting when we consider the fate of spacetime fields having a non-trivial tensor structure. Think for example of an abelian gauge field
A_{M}(x^{\mu},y)
. Upon compactification the vector index
M
will split into
(\mu,y)
and therefore the
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
observer will perceive a gauge field
A_{\mu}
with its KK tower, i.e.

\displaystyle A_{\mu}(x,y)=A_{\mu}^{0}(x)+\sum_{n\neq 0}A_{\mu}^{n}(x)e^{iny/R}\,,

(7.9)

plus a scalar
\phi\equiv A_{y}
with again its KK tower, namely

\displaystyle A_{y}(x,y)=A_{y}^{0}(x)+\sum_{n\neq 0}A_{y}^{n}(x)e^{iny/R}\,.

(7.10)

If we only focus on the KK zero mode
n=0
we can see that the electromagnetism in
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}\times S^{1}_{R}
becomes precisely electromagnetism in
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
plus the action for a scalar field
\phi(x)\equiv A^{0}_{y}(x)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\int d^{d}xdy\,F_{MN}F^{MN}=-\frac{1}{4}\int d^{d}x\left(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi+\textrm{KK fields}\right)\,.

(7.11)

It is not difficult to see that, by the same mechanism, a
p
-form field
A_{M_{1}\cdots M_{p}}
will give rise to a
p
-form field
A_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p}}
, together with a
(p-1)
form field
B_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p-1}}\equiv A_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{p-1},y}
.

7.1.4
Compactification of gravity

The circle compactification of General Relativity is even more interesting, although a little bit more technical. Without entering in the explicit computation let us give the main result. Upon circle compactification, we can express the total metric tensor
g_{MN}
in
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
language as

\displaystyle g_{MN}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{\mu\nu}-e^{2\phi}A_{\mu}A_{\nu}&e^{2\phi}A_{\mu}\\
e^{2\phi}A_{\mu}&e^{2\phi}\,.\end{pmatrix}

(7.12)

Then it is a classic result that, in the zero mode KK sector the Einsten-Hilbert action in
d+1
dimension becomes Einsten-Hilbert in
d
dimension, coupled to electromagnetism and a dilaton like field
\phi

\displaystyle\int d^{d}xdy\,R^{(d+1)}=\int d^{d}x\,\left(R^{(d)}-\frac{1}{4}e^{2\phi}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-2e^{-\phi}\square e^{\phi}+\textrm{KK fields}\right)\,.

(7.13)

7.1.5
Closed bosonic string

It is now time to see what happens when we compactify string theory on a circle.

7.1.5.1
Momentum and winding

Let us now consider the bosonic string theory. If we perform the compactification (
7.1
), the free boson gets split into

•

a free boson
X^{\mu}(w,\bar{w})
living in
\mathbb{R}^{1,d-1}
(with
\mu=0,1,\dots,d-1
);

•

a free boson
Y(w,\bar{w})
living on the circle
\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}
.

In order to implement the periodic constraint (
7.2
), we set

Y^{(R)}(w,\bar{w})=Y(w,\bar{w})+A\sigma\,,

(7.14)

where
Y
is the un-compactified free boson.
Then we fix
A
so that

\sigma\ \to\ \sigma+2\pi\quad\Longrightarrow\quad y\ \to\ y+2\pi R\omega\,,

(7.15)

where
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}
is called
winding
and counts how many times the string wraps around the circle
\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}
.
It’s immediate to see that we have to impose

A=R\omega\,.

(7.16)

Going to the usual complex coordinates on the complex plane

z=e^{w}\,,\qquad w=t+i\sigma\,,

(7.17)

and hence

Y(z,\bar{z})=Y_{0}-\frac{i}{2}\alpha^{\prime}\left(P_{y}+R\omega/\alpha^{\prime}\right)\log z-\frac{i}{2}\alpha^{\prime}\left(P_{y}-R\omega/\alpha^{\prime}\right)\log\bar{z}+\text{(oscillators)}\,.

(7.18)

The
R\omega
terms are the only parts that make this
Y
boson different from the usual free boson.
Moreover, the center of mass momentum
P_{y}
along the compactified direction has to be quantized as

P_{y}=\frac{n}{R}\,,\quad\text{with }n\in\mathbb{Z}\,.

(7.19)

Therefore the final solution is

Y(z,\bar{z})=Y_{0}-\frac{i}{2}\alpha^{\prime}\left(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)\log z-\frac{i}{2}\alpha^{\prime}\left(\frac{n}{R}-\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)\log\bar{z}+\text{(oscillators)}\,,

(7.20)

with

•

\frac{n}{R}
Kaluza-Klein momentum (with
n\in\mathbb{Z}
);

•

R\omega
winding (with
\omega\in\mathbb{Z}
).

It is convenient to split
Y(z,\bar{z})
into its left and right-moving parts:

Y(z,\bar{z})=Y_{L}(z)+Y_{R}(\bar{z})\,,

(7.21)

\begin{cases}Y_{L}(z)=\frac{Y_{0}-c}{2}-\frac{i}{2}\left(\alpha^{\prime}\frac{n}{R}+R\omega\right)\log z+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{\alpha^{y}_{m}}{m}z^{-m}\\[10.0pt]
Y_{R}(\bar{z})=\frac{Y_{0}+c}{2}-\frac{i}{2}\left(\alpha^{\prime}\frac{n}{R}-R\omega\right)\log\bar{z}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{\tilde{\alpha}^{y}_{m}}{m}\bar{z}^{-m}\end{cases}\,.

(7.22)

Then we consider the usual
U(1)
current as

\begin{cases}j(z)=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\partial Y_{L}(z)=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\alpha^{y}_{m}z^{-m-1}\,,\\[10.0pt]
\bar{j}(\bar{z})=i\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\bar{\partial}Y_{R}(\bar{z})=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\alpha}^{y}_{m}\bar{z}^{-m-1}\,,\end{cases}

(7.23)

and

\begin{cases}\alpha^{y}_{0}=\oint_{0}\frac{dz}{2\pi i}j(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\left(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)\equiv\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\,p_{L}\\[10.0pt]
\tilde{\alpha}^{y}_{0}=\oint_{0}\frac{d\bar{z}}{2\pi i}\bar{j}(\bar{z})=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\left(\frac{n}{R}-\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)\equiv\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\,p_{R}\end{cases}\,.

(7.24)

The compactification process does not affect the OPEs, but it only changes the definition of the zero-modes in the compactified direction.

We define momentum and winding operators as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}}\left(\alpha^{y}_{0}+\tilde{\alpha}^{y}_{0}\right)\,,

(7.25)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}}\left(\alpha^{y}_{0}-\tilde{\alpha}^{y}_{0}\right)\,,

(7.26)

where the winding has been normalized to have the same dimensions as the momentum.
The states that carry momentum are called momentum (or Kaluza-Klein) modes and are plane waves of the type

\ket{\frac{n}{R}}=e^{i\frac{n}{R}(Y_{L}+Y_{R})}(z,\bar{z})\ket{0}\Big|_{z=\bar{z}=0}\,,

(7.27)

with

\hat{\mathcal{P}}\ket{\frac{n}{R}}=\frac{n}{R}\ket{\frac{n}{R}}\,.

(7.28)

The states that carry winding are called winding modes and are “twisted” plane waves of the type

\ket{\frac{\omega R}{\alpha'}}=e^{i\frac{\omega R}{\alpha^{\prime}}(Y_{L}-Y_{R})}(z,\bar{z})\ket{0}\Big|_{z=\bar{z}=0}\,,

(7.29)

with

\hat{\mathcal{W}}\ket{\frac{\omega R}{\alpha'}}=\frac{\omega R}{\alpha^{\prime}}\ket{\frac{\omega R}{\alpha'}}\,.

(7.30)

It is like we had two position operators, i.e.
Y_{L}+Y_{R}
and its dual
Y_{L}-Y_{R}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Y(z,\bar{z})&=Y_{L}(z)+Y_{R}(\bar{z})\\
&=Y_{0}-i\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}\log z\bar{z}-i\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\hat{\mathcal{W}}\log\frac{z}{\bar{z}}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\left(\frac{\alpha^{y}_{m}}{m}z^{-m}+\frac{\tilde{\alpha}^{y}_{m}}{m}\bar{z}^{-m}\right)\\[10.0pt]
\widetilde{Y}(z,\bar{z})&=Y_{L}(z)-Y_{R}(\bar{zz})\\
&=C-i\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\hat{\mathcal{W}}\log z\bar{z}-i\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\hat{\mathcal{P}}\log\frac{z}{\bar{z}}+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\left(\frac{\alpha^{y}_{m}}{m}z^{-m}-\frac{\tilde{\alpha}^{y}_{m}}{m}\bar{z}^{-m}\right).\end{cases}

(7.31)

Let us now consider a generic state having both momentum and winding. Its vertex operator can be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{n,\omega}(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=e^{i\frac{n}{R}Y}(z,\bar{z})\cdot e^{i\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\widetilde{Y}}(z,\bar{z})

\displaystyle=e^{i\left(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)Y_{L}}(z)\cdot e^{i\left(\frac{n}{R}-\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)Y_{R}}(\bar{z})\,.

(7.32)

We can then evaluate

\displaystyle\left[L_{0},\mathcal{V}_{n,\omega}(z,\bar{z})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{4}\left(\frac{n}{R}+\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\mathcal{V}_{n,\omega}(z,\bar{z})\,,

(7.33a)

\displaystyle\left[\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muL\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{0},\mathcal{V}_{n,\omega}(z,\bar{z})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{4}\left(\frac{n}{R}-\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\mathcal{V}_{n,\omega}(z,\bar{z})\,.

(7.33b)

7.1.5.2
Spectrum

We just saw that the Hilbert space of a compactified free boson
Y
is the same as the non-compactified exception made for zero-modes, that carry momentum and winding.
The compactification radius
R
defines a
1
-parameter family of string backgrounds, since for every
R
we have a different
Y
-CFT.

If we then want to look at the spectrum of the whole
X+Y
free boson, we first have to study the Virasoro constraints, which are the following.

•

The mass-shell is

\displaystyle L_{0}+\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muL\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{0}^{2}+\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{2}\right)+N+\tilde{N}

\displaystyle=2

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\left(\frac{n}{R}\right)^{2}+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\left(\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2}\right)+N+\tilde{N}\,,

(7.34)

where
P
is the momentum along the non-compactified
\mu
directions. Using the dispersion relation in the uncompactified directions

\frac{\alpha^{\prime}P^{2}}{2}=-\frac{\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}}{2}

(7.35)

we get the mass-shell condition

m^{2}=\underbrace{\left(\frac{n}{R}\right)^{2}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Kaluza-Klein}\\
\text{momentum}\end{subarray}}+\,\,\,\,\,\underbrace{\left(\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2}}_{\text{winding}}\,\,\,+\,\,\,\,\,\underbrace{\frac{2(N+\tilde{N})}{\alpha^{\prime}}}_{\text{oscillators}}\,\,\,\,\,\,-\underbrace{\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{zero point}\\
\text{energy}\end{subarray}}\,,

(7.36)

with
n,\omega\in\mathbb{Z}
are momentum and the winding.

•

The level matching is

\displaystyle L_{0}-\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muL\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{0}^{2}-\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{2}\right)+N-\tilde{N}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left((\alpha_{0}^{y})^{2}-(\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{y})^{2}\right)+N-\tilde{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(4\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}\frac{n}{R}\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)+N-\tilde{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(2n\omega\right)+N-\tilde{N}\,,

(7.37)

since only compactified zero-modes give contribution to
\alpha_{0}^{2}-\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{2}
.
Therefore the constraint is

n\omega=\tilde{N}-N\,.

(7.38)

Notice that the simultaneous presence of winding and KK momentum in a state results in an unbalance of the oscillator level.

In the following we analyze the closed bosonic string spectrum at the massless level.

\blacksquare\,
Generic radius

For a generic radius
R
, in order to have a massless state we have to impose
n=\omega=0
(and hence in this case we will not find anything peculiar).
If we denote the global spacetime index as
M=(\mu,y)
, we can write the generic massless state as

G_{MN}(P)\alpha_{-1}^{M}\tilde{\alpha}_{-1}^{N}\ket{0,P}\,.

(7.39)

Since the effect of the compactification is

SO(1,d)\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \underbrace{SO(1,d-1)}_{\mu}\,,

(7.40)

we can split the polarization tensor as

G_{MN}\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \begin{cases}G_{\mu\nu}\\
G_{\mu y},\,G_{y\nu}\\
G_{yy}\end{cases}\,.

(7.41)

If we now recall from (
3.125
) that the field content of this state is made of a graviton
h
, a Kalb-Ramond
B
and a dilaton
\Phi
, we have that the compactification effect is the following:

\displaystyle h_{MN}

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ h_{\mu\nu},\,A_{\mu}=h_{\mu y}(\text{and }A_{\nu}=h_{y\nu}),\,\varphi=h_{yy}\,,

(7.42a)

\displaystyle B_{MN}

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ B_{\mu\nu},\,B_{\mu}=B_{\mu y}(\text{and }B_{\nu}=B_{y\nu})\,,

(7.42b)

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \Phi\,.

(7.42c)

Since
A_{\mu}
and
B_{\mu}
are gauge bosons, we find a gauge symmetry
U(1)\otimes U(1)
of the Yang-Mills type.

\blacksquare\,
Self-dual radius

If we look at the mass formula (
7.36
) we see that something special happens when

\frac{1}{R}=\frac{R}{\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

(7.43)

The radius that satisfies this equation is called
self-dual radius
:

R=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

(7.44)

It is the radius such that the compactification scale is the same as the string scale.

At the self-dual radius, the mass-shell becomes

\alpha^{\prime}m^{2}=n^{2}+\omega^{2}+2(N+\tilde{N})-4\,.

(7.45)

We find new massless states, the simplest of which are the following.

•

Scalar massless states with
N=\tilde{N}=0
and

\begin{cases}n=\pm 2,\,\,\omega=0\\
n=0,\,\,\omega=\pm 2\end{cases}\,.

(7.46)

They are scalars because there are no oscillators and thus there is no Lorentz index.

•

We can get extra massless vectors with

\begin{cases}n=\pm 1,\,\,\omega=\pm 1\\
n=\pm 1,\,\,\omega=\mp 1\end{cases}\,.

(7.47)

The level matching imposes

\begin{cases}1=N-\tilde{N}\\
-1=N-\tilde{N}\end{cases}\,\ \Longrightarrow\,\ \begin{cases}N=1,\,\,\tilde{N}=0\\
N=0,\,\,\tilde{N}=1\end{cases}\,,

(7.48)

So the total state will contain an unpaired oscillator.
The
Y
part of the states will be written as

\begin{cases}\ket{\pm 1,\,\pm 1}=e^{\pm\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}Y_{L}}(z)\ket{0}\Big|_{z=0}\\[15.0pt]
\ket{\pm 1,\,\mp 1}=e^{\pm\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}Y_{R}}(\bar{z})\ket{0}\Big|_{\bar{z}=0}\\
\end{cases}\,.

(7.49)

We hence have purely holomorphic or purely anti-holomorphic plane waves.

In total we can build the following massless vectors.

\cdot

In the holomorphic sector

\displaystyle A_{L,\,\mu}^{(0)}j^{\mu}\bar{j}^{y}e^{iP\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\,,

(7.50a)

\displaystyle A_{L,\,\mu}^{(+)}j^{\mu}e^{\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}Y_{R}}e^{iP\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\,,

(7.50b)

\displaystyle A_{L,\,\mu}^{(-)}j^{\mu}e^{\frac{-2i}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}Y_{R}}e^{iP\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\,.

(7.50c)

\cdot

In the anti-holomorphic sector

\displaystyle A_{R,\,\mu}^{(0)}j^{y}\bar{j}^{\mu}e^{iP\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\,,

(7.51a)

\displaystyle A_{R,\,\mu}^{(+)}e^{\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}Y_{L}}\bar{j}^{\mu}e^{iP\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\,,

(7.51b)

\displaystyle A_{R,\,\mu}^{(-)}e^{\frac{-2i}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}Y_{L}}\bar{j}^{\mu}e^{iP\cdot X}(z,\bar{z})\,.

(7.51c)

For generic values of the radius we only had (
7.50a
) and (
7.51a
), and the arising gauge symmetry was
U(1)\otimes U(1)
. Now that, at the self dual radius, we build
3+3
massless vectors, we have a gauge symmetry given by
SU(2)_{L}\otimes SU(2)_{R}
. This can be understood as follows. If we define

\displaystyle j^{0}

\displaystyle=j^{y}\,,

(7.52a)

\displaystyle j^{+}

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}Y_{L}}\,,

(7.52b)

\displaystyle j^{-}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}Y_{L}}\,,

(7.52c)

and

\displaystyle j^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(j^{+}+j^{-}\right)=\sqrt{2}\cos(2Y)\,,

(7.53a)

\displaystyle j^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(j^{+}-j^{-}\right)=\sqrt{2}\sin(2Y)\,,

(7.53b)

\displaystyle j^{3}

\displaystyle=j^{0}\,,

(7.53c)

we find the algebra

\underbracket{j^{a}(z_{1})j^{b}(z_{2})}=\frac{\delta^{ab}}{(z_{1}-z_{2})^{2}}+i{\varepsilon^{ab}}_{c}\frac{j^{c}(z_{2})}{(z_{1}-z_{2})}\,,

(7.54)

which has an additional term than the usual OPE (
4.126
). The structure constant of
SU(2)

{\varepsilon^{ab}}_{c}
appears. This is a non-abelian current algebra called
Kac-Moody algebra
. This mechanism of symmetry enhancement is the same that generates the non-abelian gauge symmetry in the heterotic string.

7.1.5.3
T
-duality

Let us look at the constraints that generate the spectrum

\begin{cases}m^{2}=\left(\frac{n}{R}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\right)^{2}+\frac{2(N+\tilde{N})}{\alpha^{\prime}}-\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\\
n\omega=N-\tilde{N}\end{cases}\,.

(7.55)

If we also consider the momentum modes
e^{i\frac{n}{R}Y}
and the winding modes
e^{i\frac{R\omega}{\alpha^{\prime}}\tilde{Y}}
, we realize that there is a symmetry between momentum and winding:

\begin{cases}R\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{R}\\
n\,\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \omega\\
Y\,\ \longrightarrow\,\ \tilde{Y}\end{cases}\,.

(7.56)

This is called
T
-duality
and its meaning is that the string cannot distinguish between a compactification on the circle
\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}
or on the circle
\mathcal{S}_{\alpha^{\prime}/R}^{1}
.

In QFT in order to understand which is the compactification radius it is sufficient to measure the masses of Kaluza-Klein modes (see eq. (
7.8
)), but strings also have winding modes and the theory cannot discriminate between the two.

It is also clear why we called “self-dual” the radius
R=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
: under
T
-duality it is mapped to itself.

As a final remark, we notice that in QFT we could, in principle, compactify on a circle of radius
R=0
. However, in string theory “small” compactifications of
R<\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
are dual to “bigger” ones of
R>\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Thus the inequivalent compactifications can be taken to be the ones having
R>\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}
, and the string is thus (again) protected from the quantities smaller than the string scale.

7.1.6
Open bosonic string and
D(p)
-branes

Under
T
-duality we have that

Y\,\to\,\tilde{Y}\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ \begin{cases}Y_{L}\,\to\,Y_{L}\\
Y_{R}\,\to\,-Y_{R}\end{cases}\,.

(7.57)

Let us then take a free compactified boson
Y
with boundary condition

j^{y}(z)=\Omega^{(j^{y})}\bar{j}(\bar{z})\,,\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}\,,

(7.58)

where the gluing map is the usual one (see eq. (
6.225
)).

Under
T
-duality the gluing condition turns into

j^{y}(z)=-\Omega^{(j^{y})}\bar{j}(\bar{z})\,,\quad\text{for }z=\bar{z}\,.

(7.59)

Therefore the effect of
T
-duality on the boundary conditions is

\text{Neumann}\,\ \xleftrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ \text{Dirichlet}\,.

(7.60)

This means that

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$D(p)$-brane wrapping around $S^{1}_{R}$}\\
\text{(i.{}e.{} $S^{1}_{R}$ with Neumann BC)}\end{subarray}

\displaystyle\ \ \xleftrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\ \ \begin{subarray}{c}\text{$D(p-1)$-brane transverse to $S^{1}_{\alpha^{\prime}/R}$}\end{subarray}\,,

\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$D(p)$-brane transverse to $S^{1}_{R}$}\\
\text{(i.{}e.{} $S^{1}_{R}$ with Dirichlet BC)}\end{subarray}

\displaystyle\ \ \xleftrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\ \ \begin{subarray}{c}\text{$D(p+1)$-brane wrapping around $S^{1}_{\alpha^{\prime}/R}$}\end{subarray}\,.

7.1.7
Superstring

Let us now consider, for example, a type IIA superstring theory. We may compactify
X^{y}=Y
(and hence
M=(\mu,y)
with
\mu=0,1,\dots,8
). Its supersymmetric partner is
\psi^{y}
, with

\begin{cases}\delta_{\text{SUSY}}\sqrt{2/\alpha^{\prime}}Y\,\;\!=\psi^{y}\\
\delta_{\text{SUSY}}\psi^{y}=j^{y}\end{cases}\,.

(7.61)

This implies that under
T
-duality we have that

Y_{R}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ -Y_{R}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\bar{\psi}^{y}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ -\bar{\psi}^{y}\,,

(7.62)

both in NS or R sector. In particular, in the R sector

\bar{\psi}_{0}^{y}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ -\bar{\psi}_{0}^{y}\,.

(7.63)

In the
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\text{R}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu
sector have considered the world-sheet fermion-counting operator

(-1)^{\bar{F}}=\bar{\Gamma}_{11}e^{-i\pi\sum_{n\geq 1}\bar{\psi}_{-n}\cdot\bar{\psi}_{n}}\,.

(7.64)

Therefore, remembering that
\psi_{0}^{\mu}\sim\Gamma^{\mu}
, we have

\bar{\Gamma}_{11}e^{-i\pi\sum_{n\geq 1}\bar{\psi}_{-n}\cdot\bar{\psi}_{n}}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ -\bar{\Gamma}_{11}(-1)^{-i\pi\sum_{n\geq 1}\bar{\psi}_{-n}\cdot\bar{\psi}_{n}}\,.

(7.65)

So the net effect of
T
-duality is to change the chirality chosen by the GSO projection of the anti-holomorphic R sector.
This means that, when compactified, Type-IIA and Type-IIB are, at the end of the day, the
same
theory:

\begin{subarray}{c}\text{type IIA}\\
\text{compactified on $S^{1}_{R}$}\end{subarray}\ \ \xleftrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\ \ \begin{subarray}{c}\text{type IIB}\\
\text{compactified on $S^{1}_{\alpha^{\prime}/R}$}\end{subarray}\,.

(7.66)

Let us now study how
T
-duality affects the R-R potentials. Since

{X_{R}}^{9}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ -{X_{R}}^{9}\,,

(7.67)

T
-duality is a parity operation on
{X_{R}}^{9}
(the anti-holomorphic sector).

Moreover, under parity spinors transform as

\tilde{S}_{\alpha}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ \left(\Gamma_{11}\Gamma^{9}\tilde{S}\right)_{\alpha}\,,

(7.68)

and therefore a fermionic bilinear transforms as

S^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{j}\tilde{S}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ \begin{cases}S^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{j}\Gamma^{9}\tilde{S}\quad\text{for }j\neq 9\\[5.0pt]
S^{T}\mathcal{C}\tilde{S}\qquad\quad\text{for }j=9\end{cases}\,.

(7.69)

Therefore we have that

F^{(p)}S^{T}\mathcal{C}\Gamma^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{9}}\tilde{S}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ \begin{cases}F^{(p-1)}\quad\text{for }9\in\{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{9}\}\\[5.0pt]
F^{(p+1)}\quad\text{for }9\notin\{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{9}\}\end{cases}\,.

(7.70)

This leads to

type IIA/B

type IIB/A

\displaystyle F^{(p+1)}\text{ (i.{}e.{} $F^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}9}$)}

\displaystyle\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ F^{(p)}\text{ (i.{}e.{} $F^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p}}$)}

\displaystyle F^{(p-1)}\text{ (i.{}e.{} $F^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p-1}}$)}

\displaystyle\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ F^{(p+1)}\text{ (i.{}e.{} $F^{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{p-1}9}$)}\,.

If we have a
D(2p)
-brane in type IIA:

\text{$D(2p)$-brane}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ \begin{cases}\text{$D(2p-1)$-brane}\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\text{if the $D(2p)$-brane was}\\
\text{wrapped around $\mathcal{S}^{1}$}\end{subarray}\\[10.0pt]
\text{$D(2p+1)$-brane}\quad\begin{subarray}{c}\text{if the $D(2p)$-brane was not}\\
\text{wrapped around $\mathcal{S}^{1}$}\end{subarray}\end{cases}\,.

(7.71)

Moreover it can be shown that
T
-duality transforms the dilaton as follows:

e^{-\Phi}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ e^{-\Phi^{\prime}}=e^{-\Phi}\frac{R}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\,,

(7.72)

and hence at the self-dual radius the dilaton is also self-dual.

For the string constant we thus have that

g_{s}=e^{\langle\Phi\rangle}\,\ \xrightarrow{\text{$T$-duality}}\,\ g_{s}^{\prime}=g_{s}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}{R}\,,

(7.73)

which tells us that the perturbation theory under
T
-duality stays the same, up to a constant factor (given by the compactification radius) that however does not change the topological series expansion. This means that
T
-duality is a perturbative duality, which maps perturbative amplitudes into perturbative amplitudes. As we will see this is the only string duality that is perturbative on both sides.

T
-duality can be extended to more complicated compactification geometries. For example a rather straightforward generalization is to consider toroidal compactifications. A much less trivial generalization is to consider compactifications of the ten dimensional superstrings on the so-called
Calabi-Yau manifolds
which are six dimensional, and thus give rise to EFT in four dimensions. Just as a circle of radius
R
is dual to a circle of radius
\alpha^{\prime}/R
, every Calabi-Yau has a dual and the generalized
T
-duality between the two compactifications is called
Mirror Simmetry
.

7.2
S
-Duality

The so-called
S
-duality relates the weak coupling regime (where there is a worldsheet description) and the strong coupling regime (where we cannot perform the perturbative topological series anymore, and thus we have no worldsheet description).

Let us start from the effective theory of type IIB superstring, i.e. with the type IIB SUGRA, whose action is (recall eq. (
6.168
))

\displaystyle S_{\text{IIB}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2K_{10}^{2}}\int d^{10}x\,\sqrt{-G}\left\{e^{-2\Phi}\left(R+4\left(\partial\Phi\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}|H_{(3)}|^{2}\right)+\dots\right\}\,.

(7.74)

Let us then perform the following field redefinition on the metric:

G_{MN}^{(\text{E})}=e^{-\frac{1}{2}\Phi}G_{MN}^{(\text{s})}\,,

(7.75)

where the E stands for “Einstein frame”, while the s stands for “string frame”.
Doing this we get

\displaystyle S_{\text{IIB}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2K_{10}^{2}}\int d^{10}x\,\sqrt{-G^{(E)}}\left\{R-\frac{\partial_{\mu}\tau\partial^{\mu}\bar{\tau}}{2(\text{Im}(\tau))^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\left|G_{(3)}\right|^{2}}{\text{Im}(\tau)}-\frac{1}{4}\left|F_{(5)}\right|\right\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{8iK_{10}^{2}}\int\frac{1}{\text{Im}(\tau)}G_{(4)}\wedge G_{(3)}\wedge\overline{G}_{(3)}\,,

(7.76)

where

•

\tau
is a spacetime scalar in
d=10
defined as

\tau=C_{(0)}+ie^{-\Phi}\,,

(7.77)

with
C_{(0)}
the R-R potential that couples to the
D(-1)
-brane and
\Phi
the NS-NS dilaton;

•

G_{(3)}
is defined as

G_{(3)}=F_{(3)}-ie^{-\Phi}H_{(3)}\,,

(7.78)

where
F_{(3)}=dC_{(2)}
is the R-R field-strength that couples to the
D(1)
-string, while
H_{(3)}=dB_{(2)}
is the Kalb-Ramond field-strength that couples to the
F(1)
-string (i.e. the fundamental string already mentioned above, see eq. (
3.154
));

•

F_{(5)}=dC_{(4)}
is the field-strength that couples to the
D(3)
-brane.

This effective action
S_{\text{IIB}}
is invariant under
SL(2,\mathbb{R})
transformations, i.e. maps of the type

\tau\quad\longrightarrow\quad\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\,.

(7.79)

This is a global field transformation which also comes with the following map:

\begin{pmatrix}C_{(2)}\\
B_{(2)}\end{pmatrix}\quad\longrightarrow\quad\begin{pmatrix}a\quad b\\
c\quad d\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C_{(2)}\\
B_{(2)}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(7.80)

If we select the specific map (which we will call
S
) such that

S:\quad\tau\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,-\frac{1}{\tau}

(7.81)

and if we choose a background where
C_{(0)}=0
, recalling that (see eq.
5.140
) and that
\tau=C_{(0)}+ie^{-\Phi}
, we can write

S:\quad g_{s}\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\frac{1}{g_{s}}\,.

(7.82)

We can now
conjecture
that this is a symmetry of the full Type IIB String Theory. The first step is to understand how the other string theory parameter (
\alpha^{\prime}
) is transformed by
S
-duality. This can be obtained by noting that under the S transformation we have

\displaystyle B_{2}\to

\displaystyle-C_{2}

(7.83)

\displaystyle C_{2}\to

\displaystyle\quad B_{2}.

(7.84)

Since
C_{2}
couples to the D1 brane in the same way as
B_{2}
couples to the fundamental F1 string, this strongly suggests that under S duality a
D(1)
-brane should turn into a F1-string.
Let us remember that the tension of a
D(p)
-brane is given by

T_{p}=\frac{1}{g_{s}}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\right)^{p}\left(\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{-\frac{p+1}{2}}\,.

(7.85)

We now demand that under
S
-duality the tension of the
D(1)
-brane becomes the tension of the fundamental string

T_{D(1)}=\frac{1}{2\pi g_{s}\alpha^{\prime}}\,\,\xleftrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447ptS\hskip 8.19447pt}\,\,\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\,,

(7.86)

This is realized if and only if

S:\quad\alpha^{\prime}\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,g_{s}\alpha^{\prime}\,.

(7.87)

Let us then see what happens to the other objects of IIB string theory

•

If we look at the
D(3)
-brane we find that it is self-dual:

D(3)\,\,\xleftrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447ptS\hskip 8.19447pt}\,\,D(3)

(7.88)

since

T_{D(3)}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}g_{s}(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}}\,\,\xleftrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447ptS\hskip 8.19447pt}\,\,\frac{g_{s}}{(2\pi)^{3}(g_{s}\alpha^{\prime})^{2}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{3}g_{s}(\alpha^{\prime})^{2}}=T_{D(3)}\,.

(7.89)

This is perfectly consistent with the fact the the 5-form is also self-dual

F_{(5)}\,\,\xleftrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447ptS\hskip 8.19447pt}\,\,F_{(5)}\,.

(7.90)

•

If we look at the
D(5)
-brane we have that it is exchanged with the
NS(5)
-brane
1
1

1

The
NS(5)
-brane is a gravity soliton, i.e. a non-perturbative object. To be more precise, its origin lies in a
(5+1)
-dimensional Kalb-Ramond-charged black brane solution of type II SUGRA (where such a black brane is the higher-dimensional analogue of a charged black hole). Since the Kalb-Ramond field and the graviton constituting this solution are in the NS-NS sector, we call it
NS(5)
-brane.
, namely

D(5)\,\,\xleftrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447ptS\hskip 8.19447pt}\,\,NS(5)\text{-brane}

(7.91)

since

T_{D(5)}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{5}g_{s}(\alpha^{\prime})^{3}}\,\,\xleftrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447ptS\hskip 8.19447pt}\,\,\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{5}g_{s}^{2}(\alpha^{\prime})^{3}}.

(7.92)

The
NS(5)
.brane is the magnetic dual of the fundamental string, just like the
D(5)
brane is the magnetic dual of the
D(1)
-brane. Therefore it makes perfect sense that under
S
-duality the
D
-string is sent to the
F
-string and the
D(5)
is sent to the
NS(5)
.

The
S
-duality conjecture is the answer to the question of how to characterize the strong coupling limit of type IIB superstring. Indeed, when the string coupling constant is not infinitesimal, string perturbation theory breaks down and we don’t know anymore how to compute amplitudes. The situation is similar to QCD where, when we go down in energy, the coupling constant grows and non-perturbative physics enter the game.
S
-duality is telling us something quite astonishing: at strong coupling the d.o.f. of string theory reshuffle and reorganize in such a way as to form the
same
theory at weak coupling, where perturbation theory is again valid!

7.3
M-Theory

Let us now talk about the strong coupling limit in type IIA superstring.
We may start considering a
D(0)
-brane, whose tension (and mass) is

T_{D(0)}=\frac{1}{g_{s}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\,.

(7.93)

The
D(0)
-branes are R-R charged (under
F_{(2)}
), they do not decay nor they interact with one another. Thus we can take a bound state of
n\in\mathbb{N}
coincident
D(0)
-branes, whose tension is

T_{nD(0)}=nT_{D(0)}\,.

(7.94)

The mass
2
of this object is

(m_{nD(0)})^{2}=\left(\frac{n}{g_{s}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\right)^{2}\,,

(7.95)

which resembles the Kaluza-Klein tower:

m_{n}^{2}=\left(\frac{n}{R}\right)^{2}\,.

(7.96)

We immediately see that (
7.96
) equals (
7.95
) for

R=g_{s}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\,.

(7.97)

This means that looking at the type IIA spectrum we find a Kaluza-Klein tower, which reveals the presence of a compactified dimension. This calls on the
d=11
SUGRA, whose action is

\displaystyle S_{d=11}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2K_{11}^{2}}\int d^{11}x\,\sqrt{-G}\left(R-\frac{1}{2}\left|dA_{(3)}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2K_{11}^{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{6}\right)\int A_{(3)}\wedge F_{(4)}\wedge F_{(4)}\,.

(7.98)

The type IIA SUGRA is the compactification of the
d=11
SUGRA on a circle:

\mathcal{M}^{11}=\mathbb{R}^{1,9}\times\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}\,.

(7.99)

We have that

K_{11}^{2}=2\pi RK_{10}^{2}

(7.100)

since

\displaystyle\int d^{d}X

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle\text{compactification: }\underbrace{\mathcal{M}^{d}}_{X}=\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{1,d-2}}_{x}\times\underbrace{\mathcal{S}_{R}^{1}}_{y}

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle\int d^{d}X=\int d^{d-1}x\int dy=2\pi R\int d^{d-1}x

(7.101)

and hence

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K_{d}^{2}}\int d^{d}X\,(\cdots)

\displaystyle\downarrow

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi R}{K_{d-1}^{2}}\int d^{d-1}x(\cdots)\,.

(7.102)

If we look at the metric in
d=11
we have that

\displaystyle G_{MN}\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\begin{cases}G_{\mu\nu}\,(\mu,\nu=0,1,\dots,9)&\text{$d=10$ metric in type IIA}\quad\text{(NS-NS)}\\
G_{\mu\,10}\to C_{\mu}&\text{vector in type IIA}\qquad\qquad\,\,\text{(R-R)}\\
G_{10\,10}\to e^{2\Phi}&\text{scalar in type IIA}\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\text{(NS-NS)}\end{cases}\,.

Moreover, we have a potential with 3 indices in
d=11
:

\displaystyle A_{MNP}\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\begin{cases}A_{\mu\nu\rho}\,(\mu,\nu,\rho=0,1,\dots,9)&\text{$d=10$ 3-form in type IIA}\quad\text{(R-R)}\\
A_{\mu\nu\,10}\to B_{\mu\nu}&\text{Kalb-Ramond in type IIA}\quad\text{(NS-NS)}\end{cases}\,.

What we are saying is that the strong coupling limit of the type IIA superstring is the UV-completion of the
d=11
SUGRA:

Weak Coupling

Strong Coupling

UV

Type IIA Superstring

M-theory?

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\qquad\alpha^{\prime}\to 0\Bigg\downarrow\phantom{\alpha^{\prime}\to 0}\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad?\Bigg\uparrow\phantom{?}

\displaystyle\text{IR}\quad\quad\,\,\text{type IIA SUGRA}\quad\,\,\,\xleftarrow[\,\text{compactification on }\mathcal{S}_{1}\,]{\hskip 8.19447pt}\quad\,\,\,\text{$d=11$ SUGRA}\,.

This M-theory is the hypothetical UV completion of the
d=11
SUGRA (whose coupling constant is the Plank mass in
d=11
, i.e.
m_{11}
), but not much is known about it.

The fact that there is a 3-form, which couples to something that has 2 dimensions, suggests the existence of a 2-dimensional object that we may call
M(2)
-brane. It is the fundamental object of M-theory and its tension should be naturally expressed as

T_{M(2)}=\frac{\left(m_{11}\right)^{3}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,.

(7.103)

It is possible to show that

\displaystyle K_{10}

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\pi\alpha^{\prime})^{2}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}g_{s}\,,

(7.104a)

\displaystyle K_{11}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\pi RK_{10}^{2}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\left(\frac{2\pi}{m_{11}}\right)^{2}\,.

(7.104b)

Thus we find that the 11-th dimension is compactified on a circle of radius

R_{11}=g_{s}\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}

(7.105)

and that the Plank mass in
d=11
is

m_{11}=g_{s}^{-\frac{1}{3}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}\,.

(7.106)

These are the two scales of M-theory, which are mapped into the two d.o.f. of string theory, i.e.
\alpha^{\prime}
and
g_{s}
:

\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{l_{11}^{3}}{R_{11}}\,,\qquad g_{s}=\left(\frac{R_{11}}{l_{11}}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\,,

(7.107)

where
l_{11}=m_{11}^{-1}
is the Plank length. If we increase the coupling
g_{s}
,
R_{11}
grows and the compactified dimension unfolds.

Therefore the
M(2)
-brane tension is

T_{M(2)}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{g_{s}}\frac{1}{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\frac{3}{2}}}=T_{D(2)}\,,

(7.108)

which means that the
M(2)
-brane is a
D(2)
-brane at strong coupling.

Moreover, in
d=11
with a circle-compactified dimension the
M(2)
-brane can wrap
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and for a
10
-dimensional observer it will look like a string of tension

T_{M(2)/\mathcal{S}_{1}}=2\pi RT_{M(2)}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\,,

(7.109)

which is the tension of the fundamental
F(1)
-string. Thus from M-theory we obtained the fundamental objects
D(2)
and
F(1)
, and also
D(0)
as the Kaluza-Klein modes of the circle compactification. However there are still
D(4)
,
D(6)
and
NS(5)
missing. We can recover them as follows.

The
M(2)
-brane is coupled to the
3
-form
A^{(3)}
. If we consider
F^{(4)}=dA^{(3)}
, we can take its Hodge dual and get

F^{(7)}=\star_{11}F^{(4)}=dA^{(6)}\,.

(7.110)

This
A^{(6)}
form couples to the “magnetic” counterpart of the
M(2)
-brane, i.e. the
M(5)
-brane, whose tension is naturally given by

T_{M(5)}=\frac{m_{11}^{6}}{(2\pi)^{5}}\,.

(7.111)

This expression is not just natural, but it is dictated by the Dirac quantization condition

\displaystyle T_{M(2)}T_{M(5)}=\frac{2\pi}{2K_{11}^{2}},

(7.112)

as one can easily check.
In string theory variables, the tension of the
M(5)
-branes becomes the tension of the
NS(5)
-brane

T_{M(5)}=\frac{m_{11}^{6}}{(2\pi)^{5}}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{5}}\frac{1}{g_{s}^{2}}\frac{1}{(\alpha^{\prime})^{3}}=T_{NS(5)}\,.

(7.113)

Moreover, if we wrap the
M(5)
-brane on the compactified dimension, we get the
D(4)
-brane:

T_{M(5)/\mathcal{S}_{1}}=2\pi RT_{M(5)}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{1}{g_{s}}\frac{1}{(\alpha^{\prime})^{\frac{5}{2}}}=T_{D(4)}\,.

(7.114)

Finally, the
D(6)
-brane can be recovered as a “Kaluza-Klein monopole”, the magnetic dual of the KK-momentum modes, which have been identified as the
D(0)
-branes .

All in all, the only parameter of M-theory is
m_{11}
, namely the Plank constant. The theory is therefore inherently strongly coupled and does not have a natural small parameter to do perturbation theory. When we compactify, the radius can be the small parameter and a perturbative window opens up. This perturbative window is in fact the Type IIA superstring. There are other ways to connect M-theory with the five superstring theories and this is summarized in figure
7.1
.

Figure 7.1:
M-theory represented as a (yet unknown) land where some patches are the known superstring theories (related to each other by the
T
and
S
dualities and the orientifold operation
\Omega
) and the
d=11
supergravity, which under a circle compactification reduces to type IIA superstring.

Appendix A
Spinors in
d
(even) Dimensions

Let us consider
d
directions, where
d
is even.
We can group them in pairs that identify mutually orthogonal planes:

(0,1),(2,3),\dots,(d-2,d-1)\,.

(A.1)

We can label these planes as follows:

I=0,1,\dots,\frac{d}{2}-1=(0,j)\qquad\text{with }j=1,\dots,\frac{d}{2}-1\,.

(A.2)

Consider now the Grassmann odd operators
\psi^{\mu}
(with
\mu=0,1,\dots,d-1
Lorentz index) which obey the Clifford algebra

[\psi^{\mu},\psi^{\nu}]=\eta^{\mu\nu},

(A.3)

where as usual
[\cdot\,,\cdot]
is the graded commutator.
We now define

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\pm}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\psi_{0}^{0}\pm\psi_{0}^{1}\right)\,,

(A.4a)

\displaystyle\psi_{j}^{\pm}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\psi_{0}^{2j}\pm i\psi_{0}^{2j+1}\right)\,.

(A.4b)

We then have that

\displaystyle\left[\psi_{I}^{+},\psi_{J}^{-}\right]

\displaystyle=\delta_{I,\,J}\,,

(A.5a)

\displaystyle\left[\psi_{I}^{\pm},\psi_{J}^{\pm}\right]

\displaystyle=0\,.

(A.5b)

Exercise A.0.1

Prove eqs. (
A.5
).

Therefore,
\forall I
we have that
\psi_{I}^{+},\psi_{I}^{-}
generates a two-dimensional space, namely

\displaystyle\psi^{+}\ket{+1/2}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(A.6a)

\displaystyle\psi^{-}\ket{+1/2}

\displaystyle=\ket{-1/2}\,,

(A.6b)

\displaystyle\psi^{-}\ket{-1/2}

\displaystyle=0\,,

(A.6c)

where the basis
\left\{\ket{+1/2},\ket{-1/2}\right\}
generates the two-dimensional space:
\ket{+1/2}
is the highest weight state and
\psi^{+}
is the raising operator, while
\ket{-1/2}
is the lowest weight state and
\psi^{-}
is the lowering operator.

Therefore, for a generic even value of
d
, the representation space of the Clifford algebra space has a vacuum of the type

\ket{0}=\underbrace{\ket{+1/2}\otimes\ket{+1/2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{+1/2}}_{\text{$\frac{d}{2}$ times}}=|\underbrace{+1/2,+1/2,\dots,+1/2}_{\text{$\frac{d}{2}$ times}}\,\rangle\,,

(A.7)

and the generic state is

\ket{\alpha}=\underbrace{\ket{\pm 1/2}\otimes\ket{\pm 1/2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\ket{\pm 1/2}}_{\text{$\frac{d}{2}$ times}}=|\underbrace{\pm 1/2,\pm 1/2,\dots,\pm 1/2}_{\text{$\frac{d}{2}$ times}}\,\rangle\,.

(A.8)

The label
\alpha
counts how many of these states one can build. In
d
(even) dimensions we have that

\alpha=1,\cdots,2^{\frac{d}{2}}.

(A.9)

The state
\ket{\alpha}
is thus a spinor in
d=2n
dimensions (for
n\in\mathbb{N}
), and its related spinor space will have dimension
2^{n}
.

Let us now consider (recalling eqs. (
A
))

\left[\psi_{0}^{+},\psi_{0}^{-}\right]=\psi_{0}^{+}\psi_{0}^{-}+\psi_{0}^{-}\psi_{0}^{+}=\delta_{0,\,0}=1\,.

(A.10)

Hence we can write

\displaystyle-\psi_{0}^{0}\psi_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\psi_{0}^{+}+\psi_{0}^{-}\right)\left(\psi_{0}^{+}-\psi_{0}^{-}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\psi_{0}^{-}\psi_{0}^{+}-\psi_{0}^{+}\psi_{0}^{-}\right)

\displaystyle=\psi_{0}^{+}\psi_{0}^{-}-\frac{1}{2}\,,

(A.11a)

\displaystyle-i\psi_{0}^{2j}\psi_{0}^{2j+1}

\displaystyle=\psi_{j}^{+}\psi_{j}^{-}-\frac{1}{2}\,.

(A.11b)

We then define

\chi_{I}=\begin{cases}-\psi_{0}^{0}\psi_{0}^{1}\quad\!\!\;\qquad\text{if $I=0$}\\
-i\psi_{0}^{2j}\psi_{0}^{2j+1}\quad\text{if $I=j$}\end{cases}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\chi_{I}=\psi_{I}^{+}\psi_{I}^{-}-\frac{1}{2}\,,

(A.12)

and we find that

\displaystyle\chi_{I}\ket{+1/2}

\displaystyle=\psi_{I}^{+}\psi_{I}^{-}-\frac{1}{2}\ket{+1/2}=\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\right)\ket{+1/2}=\frac{1}{2}\ket{+1/2}\,,

(A.13a)

\displaystyle\chi_{I}\ket{-1/2}

\displaystyle=\psi_{I}^{+}\psi_{I}^{-}-\frac{1}{2}\ket{-1/2}=\left(0-\frac{1}{2}\right)\ket{+1/2}=-\frac{1}{2}\ket{+1/2}\,.

(A.13b)

This makes clear why we used the notation
\left\{\ket{+1/2},\ket{-1/2}\right\}
for the basis vectors: we labelled them with their eigenvalues with respect to
\chi_{I}
.

We can finally define the chirality matrix (which in
d=4
is commonly called
\Gamma^{5}
) as

\Gamma=2^{n}\prod_{I=0}^{n-1}\chi_{I}=-(-i)^{n-1}\Gamma^{0}\Gamma^{1}\dots\Gamma^{(2n-2)}\Gamma^{(2n-1)}\,,

(A.14)

where
\Gamma^{\mu}=\sqrt{2}\psi^{\mu}
.
If we then define

\displaystyle\ket{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\ket{(\text{even \# of }-1/2)}\,,

(A.15a)

\displaystyle\ket{\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\ket{(\text{odd \# of }-1/2)}\,,

(A.15b)

the action of the chirality matrix
\Gamma
on the spinor states is

\displaystyle\Gamma\ket{\alpha}=+\ket{\alpha}\,,

(A.16a)

\displaystyle\Gamma\ket{\dot{\alpha}}=-\ket{\dot{\alpha}}\,.

(A.16b)

We will then say that
\ket{\alpha}
has a positive chirality, while
\ket{\dot{\alpha}}
has a negative chirality. They are two irrepses of
SO(1,d-1)
. Indeed the Lorentz group acts on spinors through the matrix

\Gamma^{\mu\nu}=\left[\Gamma^{\mu},\Gamma^{\nu}\right]\,,

(A.17)

and since

\left[\Gamma^{\mu\nu},\Gamma\right]=0

(A.18)

the two chirality representations transform independently of each other under the Lorentz group
SO(1,d-1)
.

Appendix B
Dedekind
\eta
function

During the computation of the closed string 1-loop vacuum bubble we have defined the Dedekind eta function:

\tilde{\eta}(\tau)=q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{l})\,,\qquad\text{with }q=e^{2\pi i\tau}\,,\,\,|q|<1\,.

(B.1)

We will now provide a simple proof of its modular property

\eta\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\eta(\tau)\,.

(B.2)

If we take the logarithm of
\eta(\tau)
and expand
\log(1-q^k)
in its McLaurin series, convergent in the domain of
\eta
, we get

\frac{1}{12}i\pi\tau-\log\eta(\tau)=-\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\log(1-q^l)=\sum_{k,l=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}q^{lk}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\frac{1}{q^{-k}-1}\,.

(B.3)

Doing the same computation for
\eta(-1/\tau)
and using (
B.2
) we also obtain

-\frac{i\pi}{12\tau}-\log\eta\left(-\frac{1}{\tau}\right)=-\frac{i\pi}{12\tau}-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\tau}{i}-\log\eta(\tau)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\frac{1}{e^{\frac{2\pi ik}{\tau}}-1}\,.

(B.4)

Then if we take the difference between (
B.3
) and (
B.4
), we see that to prove the modular property of
\eta
it suffices to check that

\frac{\pi i}{12}\left(\tau+\frac{1}{\tau}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\tau}{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k}\left[\frac{1}{e^{-2\pi ik\tau}-1}-\frac{1}{e^{2\pi ik/\tau}-1}\right]\,.

(B.5)

To do so we consider the function
g(z)=\cot(z)\cot(z/\tau)
and the following meromorphic family:

f_{\nu}(z)=\frac{1}{z}g(\nu z),\quad\nu=\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)\pi\,,\qquad(n=0,1,\dots)\,.

(B.6)

The function
f_{n}u(z)
has simple poles in
z_{k}=\pi k/\nu
and
z^{\tau}_{k}=\pm\pi k\tau/\nu
with residues

\mathrm{Res}_{z_{k}}(f_{\nu})=\frac{1}{\pi k}\cot\frac{\pi k}{\tau},\quad\mathrm{Res}_{z^{\tau}_{k}}(f_{\nu})=\frac{1}{\pi k}\cot\pi k\tau

(B.7)

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. In addition there is a third order pole at
z=0
with residue

\Res_{0}(f_{\nu})=-\frac{1}{3}\left(\tau+\frac{1}{\tau}\right)\,.

(B.8)

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the contour of the rhombus with vertices
1,\tau,\-1,-\tau
as shown in fig.
B.1
.

Figure B.1:
Plot of
f_{\tau}(z)
for
\tau=1/2+i
and
n=1
. The white spots represent the poles of the meromorphic function. For
n\rightarrow\infty
the number of poles inside the contour
\mathcal{C}
goes to infinity.

Then, integrating over
\mathcal{C}
, by the residues theorem we get

\displaystyle\frac{\pi i}{12}\left(\tau+\frac{1}{\tau}\right)+\frac{1}{8}\int_{\mathcal{C}}f_{\nu}(z)dz

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}\left(\cot\pi k\tau+\cot\pi k/\tau\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}\left[\frac{1}{e^{-2\pi ik\tau}-1}-\frac{1}{e^{2\pi ik/\tau}-1}\right]\,.

(B.9)

On the other hand, as
n\rightarrow\infty
the function
g(\nu z)
is uniformly bounded on
\mathcal{C}
and has on the four sides, excluding the vertices, the limit values
1,-1,1,-1
. Hence

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathcal{C}}f_{\nu}(z)dz=\left(\int_{1}^{\tau}-\int_{\tau}^{-1}+\int_{-1}^{-\tau}-\int_{-\tau}^{1}\right)\frac{dz}{z}=4\log\frac{\tau}{i}\,,

(B.10)

which gives the desired result.
